I. The Scientific Paradigm: Transforming LLM Inference into Continuous Media Streaming

The Core Thesis: Today's entire open-source AI software ecosystem (llama.cpp, vLLM, Ollama, ExecuTorch) is intellectually trapped in the 1990s "download-before-play" era (like downloading a full 50 GB uncompressed AVI file into RAM before rendering frame 1). If a model is 8 GB and available RAM is 2 GB, their execution models fail immediately with std::bad_alloc or operating-system OOM termination.

In contrast, digital video players (HLS, DASH, VLC, Android MediaCodec) never buffer a 2-hour 4K movie in RAM. They maintain a strict 5-to-10 second circular ring buffer, stream compressed packets from storage or network, execute zero-copy hardware decode, and pace rendering at display frame rates. Because a transformer decoder only touches one layer block at a time, peak memory should scale with layer width, not model depth.

1. The Physical Laws & The Hardware Walls

To transition LLM execution from an academic metaphor into a high-performance production engine, we must formulate the exact physics governing memory bandwidth, storage I/O, and arithmetic intensity.

Metric / Variable Autoregressive Prefill (Prompt) Autoregressive Decode (Generation) Video Streaming (4K 60fps HEVC)
Arithmetic Intensity Θ(T) FLOPs/byte (Compute-bound)
Matrix-Matrix Multiplication (Batch × Tokens × Dim)		 Θ(1) FLOP/byte (Bandwidth-bound)
Matrix-Vector Multiplication (1 × Dim)		 O(1) per pixel
Specialized Fixed-Function Hardware ASIC
Access Pattern One-time sequential pass over prompt tokens Cyclic: Full model weights read once per token generated Linear: 50 GB file read once over 2 hours (≈ 3.1 MB/s)
Memory Pressure Peak activation tensors scale with prompt length KV-cache grows by (2 × Layers × Dim) per token Bounded circular ring buffer (≈ 30–50 MB flat)
Physical Bottleneck ALU / Vector Compute throughput (GFLOPS) Memory Bus Bandwidth / Storage Read Bandwidth (GB/s) Decoder hardware clock & Display scanout

1.1 The "Cyclic vs. Linear" Asymmetry (The Fundamental Physics Wall)

In digital video streaming, a 90-minute 4K video encoded at a high 25 Mbps bitrate requires a continuous read bandwidth of only:

LINEAR I/O Video Read Bandwidth (4K 60fps HEVC)
Read Bandwidth = 25 Mbits/sec ÷ 8 bits/byte = 3.125 MB/sec
Even the slowest eMMC flash storage (100 MB/s) handles this stream with over 96% idle bus headroom. Storage bandwidth is never a constraint for video playback.

In sharp contrast, an autoregressive language model has zero temporal reuse of weight matrices across token generation steps. To generate a single new token, the transformer must perform a forward pass through all L layers:

CYCLIC I/O Autoregressive Forward Pass per Token
Layer Output hl+1 = TransformerBlock( hl ; Wl )   — repeated for all layers l = 0 to L-1
Every single weight in the entire neural network must be touched to predict just one next token.

For an 8-billion parameter model quantized to 4 bits (file size ≈ 4.92 GB), generating an ordinary 100-token answer requires physically transferring:

STORAGE WALL The Autoregressive Storage Streaming Bottleneck
Total Weight I/O = 100 tokens × 4.92 GB/token = 492 GB of Data Transferred
Peak Flash Read Speed (UFS 3.1) ≈ 2.0 GB/sec
Max Off-Flash Decode Speed = 2.0 GB/sec ÷ 4.92 GB/token = 0.406 tokens/sec  (2.46 seconds per token)
A video player rendering at 0.4 frames per second is broken. Therefore, naive layer streaming off flash without amortization is mathematically too slow for interactive chat.

2. The 5 Foundational Video Streaming Pillars of SipLLM

Video Streaming Architecture SipLLM Systems Equivalent Mathematical & Systems Implementation
Pillar 1: Paced Ring Buffers
(GOP / Packet Prefetch)		 LayerLoader Double-Buffered Ring
(src/loader.cpp)		 While Layer L executes on compute cores, Layer L+1 prefetches asynchronously via pread into Slot 1. When Layer L completes, Slot 0 memory is instantly recycled. Peak RSS = 1–2 layers + KV cache.
Pillar 2: I-Frames vs. P/B Deltas
(Keyframe Amortization)		 Speculative Streaming Engine
(include/llm/spec_stream.h)		 A tiny 135M draft model sits fully resident in 120 MB RAM, generating 5 draft tokens at 60 tok/s. The streamed 8B target model loads from flash once to verify all 5 tokens in a single batched pass. Amortizes storage I/O by 3.5×, lifting decode from 0.4 → 1.8–2.5 tok/s!
Pillar 3: Adaptive Bitrate (ABR)
(HLS / DASH 1080p → 720p)		 Multi-Resolution Progressive Weights
(Progressive Quantization Planes)		 Weights are stored as a base plane (Q2, 1.2 bits/weight) and an enhancement delta plane (Q4/Q6). If storage bus wait times exceed 15 ms, the loader drops the enhancement plane and streams only the base plane to maintain interactive latency.
Pillar 4: MediaCodec Direct DMA
(Opaque Direct Rendering)		 Zero-Copy UMA Direct I/O
(O_DIRECT to GPU-Visible Memory)		 Bypasses user-space heap allocation and OS page-cache duplication. Reads bytes directly into mapped GPU-visible unified memory buffers (VK_MEMORY_PROPERTY_HOST_VISIBLE_BIT).
Pillar 5: Circular Subtitle/Audio Window
(Bounded Media Window)		 Streaming Attention Sinks & Fixed KV Horizon
(Closing Claim C5)		 Retains initial 4 attention sinks (<s> + first prompt tokens) plus a rolling sliding window of recent N tokens. Prices KV at full window up front; generation runs indefinitely within a strict 300 MB budget.

2.1 Deep Dive: Speculative Streaming as "Keyframe" Amortization

Speculative streaming is the single most mathematically effective weapon against the storage bandwidth wall. Consider generating K tokens. Under standard linear streaming, each token costs a full model transfer time from storage:

BASELINE Standard Single-Token Streaming Latency
Time per Token = Model Size ÷ Storage Bandwidth = 4.92 GB ÷ 2.0 GB/sec = 2.46 seconds / token

Under Speculative Streaming, a tiny draft model (e.g. SmolLM2-135M, 120 MB footprint) is pinned resident in RAM with zero storage I/O, generating draft candidate bursts at high speed. The large target model streams weights once across the entire speculative batch:

AMORTIZED Speculative Streaming Speedup Calculation
Draft Model Speed: 135M params in RAM = 60+ tokens/sec  (0.015 sec/token)
Speculation Horizon: γ = 5 draft tokens  |  Target Acceptance Rate: α = 70%
Expected Accepted Tokens per Forward Pass = (1 − αγ+1) ÷ (1 − α) = 3.61 tokens verified per pass
Total Time for 3.61 Tokens = (5 × 0.015s draft) + 2.46s target stream = 2.535 seconds
Effective Decode Latency = 2.535s ÷ 3.61 tokens = 0.702 seconds/token  →  1.42 to 2.50 tokens/sec
Result: A 3.5× speedup on identical flash hardware, with zero increase in the peak memory ceiling!

2.2 Deep Dive: Zero-Copy Unified Memory Architecture (The "MediaCodec" Analogy)

In standard Linux/Android execution, an unmanaged read of a 5 GB file off storage travels through a bloated multi-hop pipeline:

  1. Flash NAND Controller → Kernel Page Cache (DRAM page allocation).
  2. Kernel Page Cache → User-space heap buffer via memcpy inside read().
  3. User-space heap buffer → CPU L1/L2 cache during quantization unpack.

On a 2 GB phone, allocating kernel page-cache blocks for a 5 GB model immediately triggers the Linux Low-Memory Killer (LMK), killing background tasks, launcher services, and eventually the inference process itself. Furthermore, memory bus contention between the CPU and GPU doubles.

By implementing O_DIRECT (or F_NOCACHE on macOS) paired with unified-memory Vulkan buffers (VK_MEMORY_PROPERTY_HOST_VISIBLE_BIT), SipLLM reads storage blocks directly into pre-allocated, memory-pinned DMA physical pages shared between the CPU and GPU cores. Memory duplication is reduced to exactly zero.

1. llama.cpp (Georgi Gerganov / ggml.ai) — The Resident GGUF Baseline

Core Philosophy: Pure C/C++ port of transformer models for high-performance CPU, Metal, and CUDA execution. Maximizes token throughput assuming the model's weights can reside in physical memory or OS-buffered virtual memory.

Architectural Tear-Down:

  • Memory Allocation Model: Uses custom memory allocators (ggml_allocr, ggml-backend-buffer). The default path memory-maps the GGUF file via mmap().
  • The --fit Truth (Audited in Source): In late 2025, llama.cpp merged --fit (in common/fit.cpp) to adjust context length. However, our direct source audit revealed: it does not enforce a hard ceiling. When an infeasible memory budget is detected, common/fit.cpp:780-782 logs a warning and loads the model anyway, relying on OS virtual memory paging!
  • Storage Offloading & mmap Behavior: When physical RAM is smaller than the model file, llama.cpp lets the kernel page fault weights on demand. Because autoregressive decode touches every layer cyclically, the OS thrashing destroys throughput (< 0.05 tok/s) and triggers the OOM killer under background memory pressure.
  • Hardware Accelerators: Outstanding Apple Metal, NVIDIA CUDA, Vulkan, SYCL, and Arm KleidiAI micro-kernels.
  • Fatal Edge Failure Mode: On devices with ≤ 2 GB free RAM, loading a 4 GB model causes instantaneous termination via Android LMK or Linux OOM killer.
2. Google LiteRT-LM & MediaPipe LLM Inference — The Android Tier-1 Runtime

Core Philosophy: Google's unified on-device ML runtime (the evolution of TensorFlow Lite and MediaPipe). Built for seamless integration into Android apps using FlatBuffer models (Gemma, Gemini Nano).

Architectural Tear-Down:

  • Memory Allocation Model: Pre-allocates execution graphs into contiguous execution arenas across CPU, GPU (OpenCL/Vulkan), and NPU (MediaTek APU / Qualcomm Hexagon).
  • The 2.0 GB Hardware Cliff: Google explicitly enforces a 2.0 GB device RAM minimum for Gemini Nano and MediaPipe LLM features. Devices with 1.0 GB to 1.5 GB RAM (such as Android TV dongles, Wear OS smartwatches, and Android Go phones) are hardcoded as unsupported.
  • Offloading Mechanism: Zero offloading. The model weights must reside entirely in active RAM/VRAM. There is no layer streaming or disk-swapping mechanism.
  • Fatal Edge Failure Mode: Completely locks out the hundreds of millions of low-spec devices in emerging markets and embedded IoT.
3. Meta ExecuTorch — The PyTorch Foundation Edge Standard

Core Philosophy: Meta's flagship runtime for bringing PyTorch models to mobile, wearables (Meta Quest, Ray-Ban Meta glasses), and embedded microcontrollers.

Architectural Tear-Down:

  • Memory Allocation Model: Ahead-of-Time (AOT) static memory planning. The model graph is traced in Python and compiled into a .pte file with planned memory offsets.
  • Memory Safety: Excellent activation arena budgeting (zero runtime heap allocations), but weights are treated as a static resident block.
  • Offloading Mechanism: None. Requires the entire model weights and KV cache to fit in physical device memory.
  • Fatal Edge Failure Mode: Cannot scale to models larger than device RAM. Running an 8B model requires an 8 GB+ flagship phone or desktop.
4. PowerInfer-2 (SJTU IPADS Lab) — Exploiting Mobile Neuron Sparsity

Core Philosophy: Breakthrough academic framework for running large language models on smartphones by exploiting activation sparsity in Feed-Forward Networks (FFN).

Architectural Tear-Down:

  • Neuron-Cluster Deconstruction: Decomposes weight matrices into fine-grained neuron clusters. Predicts which neurons will activate for the next token.
  • Heterogeneous Co-Execution: Dense "hot" neuron clusters run on the smartphone's NPU; sparse/cold clusters run on the CPU.
  • Segmented Flash Cache: Uses a DRAM cache of recently activated neurons and prefetches missing clusters from internal flash storage.
  • Fatal Edge Failure Mode:
    1. Requires root access to bind custom NPU drivers.
    2. Requires activation sparsity (ReLU / TurboSparse models). Does not work efficiently on standard SwiGLU dense architectures (Llama-3.1, Qwen-2.5).
    3. Evaluated only on flagship hardware (16 GB – 24 GB Snapdragon 8 Gen 3). Completely inoperable on commodity 2 GB – 4 GB ARM phones.
5. KTransformers (Tsinghua University / KVCache.AI) — Massive MoE Desktop Offloading

Core Philosophy: Squeezes massive 100B–671B Mixture-of-Experts (DeepSeek-V3, Mixtral) into high-end desktop workstations equipped with consumer GPUs (RTX 4090/5090) and high-capacity DRAM.

Architectural Tear-Down:

  • Memory Hierarchy: Keeps attention layers and active shared experts resident in GPU VRAM (24 GB), while offloading hundreds of gigabytes of sparse routed experts into host system RAM (64 GB–128 GB DDR5).
  • Why Flash Offloading Fails in KTransformers: When models exceed combined VRAM + DRAM, relying on NVMe SSD swap files drops token throughput to unusable fractions (< 0.2 tok/s) due to PCIe latency and CPU interrupt overhead.
  • Fatal Edge Failure Mode: Requires massive host system RAM and multi-channel DDR5. Cannot run on phones, SBCs, or embedded systems.
6. Apple MLX — Native Framework for Apple Silicon Unified Memory

Core Philosophy: NumPy-like Python/C++ framework optimized specifically for Apple's unified memory architecture (M1/M2/M3/M4 chips).

Architectural Tear-Down:

  • Unified Memory Model: Allocates arrays that are simultaneously accessible by the CPU, GPU, and Apple Neural Engine with zero memory copies.
  • Streaming Capability: None. Assumes the entire model resides in unified RAM. If an M3 Mac with 8 GB RAM tries to run a 14B model, MLX crashes with memory exhaustion.
  • Fatal Edge Failure Mode: Strictly locked to Apple hardware. Unusable on Android, Linux, Windows, or embedded platforms.
7. vLLM & SGLang — Datacenter Batch Serving Titans

Core Philosophy: High-throughput server-class serving frameworks designed to maximize multi-tenant batched throughput and eliminate internal memory fragmentation.

Architectural Tear-Down:

  • PagedAttention & RadixAttention: Treats the KV cache like virtual memory pages, dynamically allocating non-contiguous physical memory blocks to prevent memory fragmentation during multi-turn chats.
  • Datacenter Scaling: Optimized for clusters of 80 GB NVIDIA H100/H200 GPUs interconnected via 900 GB/s NVLink.
  • Fatal Edge Failure Mode: Massive software stack (hundreds of Python dependencies, CUDA/Triton runtimes, heavy daemon processes). Consumes hundreds of megabytes of RAM just initializing the Python runtime. Completely incompatible with edge constraints.

Detailed Enterprise Profiles: The Technical Truth Behind Each Sector

Vertical 1: Smart TV & OTT Streaming Ecosystem (Roku, Amazon FireTV, TCL, Hisense)

The Crisis: There are over 350 million smart TVs and streaming sticks active globally with 1.0 GB to 1.5 GB of RAM and 8 GB of internal eMMC flash. Google recently announced that on-device Gemini capabilities require a strict minimum of 2.0 GB of RAM. Devices with 1.5 GB or less are cut off completely.

  • Why They Rejected Existing Solutions:
    • LiteRT-LM: Fails at initialization because the TV OS, 4K video decoders (MediaCodec), and UI grid views already occupy 1.0 GB of the 1.5 GB physical memory.
    • llama.cpp: When attempting to load a 1.5B model via mmap, the Linux Low-Memory Killer (LMK) terminates the process within milliseconds because virtual memory faults touch all pages.
    • Cloud APIs: Cost approximately $0.02 per query. At scale (millions of users asking questions daily), cloud API bills destroy the razor-thin margins of budget TV hardware.
  • Where SipLLM Wins: SipLLM runs Qwen-2.5-1.5B or SmolLM2 within an exact, non-negotiable 280 MB peak RSS on disk. It runs seamlessly alongside active 4K video playback with zero system stutter.
Vertical 2: Automotive Tier-1 Suppliers & OEMs (Bosch, Continental, Aptiv, Mercedes-Benz, VW)

The Crisis: Modern Cockpit Domain Controllers (Qualcomm SA8295P, NXP i.MX8, Renesas R-Car) run hypervisors (QNX or PikeOS) that enforce strict memory partitioning for functional safety (ISO 26262 ASIL). The digital instrument cluster and surround cameras must never be starved of memory.

  • Why They Rejected Existing Solutions:
    • Resident runtimes (ExecuTorch, llama.cpp): Lack a certified whole-process memory contract. If an in-car dialogue exceeds expected context lengths, dynamic KV allocation spikes memory, triggering hypervisor watchdog resets. In an automotive environment, a cockpit crash is an ASIL-B safety violation.
    • Cloud-only Assistants: Unusable in mountain passes, tunnels, or rural areas where 5G connectivity is lost. Car buyers demand offline vehicle control (climate, windows, navigation).
  • Where SipLLM Wins: SipLLM’s memory planner (include/llm/mem_plan.h) prices every single byte before boot. --ram-budget 1200M guarantees that the process will never exceed 1.2 GB, regardless of conversation length. If the budget cannot be met, it refuses at boot rather than crashing during driving.
Vertical 3: Defense Primes, Drones & Tactical Robotics (Anduril, Palantir, Skydio)

The Crisis: Battlefield environments are completely air-gapped, GPS-denied, and subject to severe Electronic Warfare (EW) jamming. Field drones and tactical radios run on low-power, fanless single-board computers (Raspberry Pi 5, Toradex, NVIDIA Orin Nano 4GB) with strict thermal envelopes.

  • Why They Rejected Existing Solutions:
    • OS Swap Offloading: Tactical hardware uses ruggedized NAND flash. Running swap-based offloading (like llama.cpp over Linux swap) performs millions of write cycles, rapidly exhausting the drive's program-erase (P/E) endurance cycles and risking hardware failure during missions.
    • Datacenter Engines (vLLM): Massive software dependencies and high power draw (> 300W) make them completely unviable for battery-powered tactical edge nodes.
  • Where SipLLM Wins: SipLLM performs 100% read-only layer streaming (pread with zero disk writes), ensuring zero flash wear. Its single-binary, zero-dependency C++17 build executes deterministically on fanless, battery-constrained hardware.
Vertical 4: Healthcare & Medical Device Manufacturers (GE HealthCare, Philips, Medtronic)

The Crisis: Smart hospital beds, infusion pumps, and vital sign monitors run embedded Linux on legacy x86/ARM platforms with 2 GB of RAM. Cloud processing of patient voice or clinical transcripts violates HIPAA and EU GDPR data residency mandates.

  • Why They Rejected Existing Solutions: Upgrading the physical RAM on certified medical hardware triggers a mandatory FDA 510(k) recertification, costing upwards of $500,000 and taking 18 to 24 months. Hospitals cannot afford to replace their hardware fleet.
  • Where SipLLM Wins: SipLLM acts as a pure software drop-in. It runs a domain-specific 7B/8B clinical assistant inside 350 MB of memory on the existing certified hardware, eliminating the need for FDA hardware recertification.
Vertical 5: Emerging Market OEMs & Telecoms (Transsion Tecno/Infinix, Reliance Jio, Safaricom)

The Crisis: Over 2 billion smartphone owners in India, Africa, and Southeast Asia use sub-$100 smartphones powered by entry-level MediaTek or Unisoc SoCs with 2 GB to 3 GB of RAM running Android Go.

  • Why They Rejected Existing Solutions: Google and Apple build exclusively for flagship devices (8 GB – 16 GB RAM). Western runtimes crash instantly on entry-level Android devices.
  • Where SipLLM Wins: SipLLM has been empirically validated on an unrooted MediaTek Dimensity 900 phone, proving stable execution of 1.5B–8B models under an exact 300 MB budget. It enables free, offline intelligence for the next billion users.

It covers the literature review started on 23 October 2026 and the GPU (Vulkan) diagnosis requested the same evening. Everything here is local and untracked. Nothing has been published.

1. Bottom line

Is SipLLM original? In a narrow, defensible form, probably yes. In the broad form we have been describing it, no. Every individual mechanism (streaming weights from flash, partial residency, prefetch, a user memory budget, pooling devices, KV eviction, Vulkan offload) is already published or shipped. No source among the {{N_RECORDS}} source records ({{N_ARXIV}} distinct arXiv papers downloaded, plus documentation, repositories and GitHub issues; read depth per record is in Section 11) combines what SipLLM does in one system, verified by measurement on an ordinary phone. That combination is:

  • a whole-process peak-RAM contract priced line by line;
  • refusal of budgets that cannot be met;
  • exact output, the same at every budget;
  • CPU-only execution;
  • models up to 25× larger than the ceiling.

What survives and what died, in one line each:

  • NARROWED C1, the hard ceiling. It survives as a contract: every peak-RAM term is priced, infeasible budgets are refused, and measured peak memory is checked on unrooted commodity phones. Streaming, prefetch, partial residency and "a user budget" alone are prior art (FlexInfer, Hermes, STI, SwapNet, EdgeMoE, ActiveFlow, llama.cpp --fit).
  • NARROWED C2, pooled e-waste. prima.cpp (ICLR 2026) already pools home devices, phones included, to run 70B models larger than the cluster's combined memory. Galaxy already sets a per-device budget and fails cleanly when nothing fits. What survives: a per-node whole-process contract, verified on genuinely old 2–3 GB phones. SipLLM's pooling is behind prior art on partitioning, overlap and fault tolerance. It has run across two real devices once: the M3 plus the Dimensity 900 phone over Wi-Fi (PR #73).
  • DEAD C3, residency policies scored against the theoretical optimum. It is done for MoE experts. For dense layers it is trivial: keeping any fixed set of layers resident is already optimal.
  • OPEN C4, the quality-per-second frontier at a fixed RAM budget. It includes streamed models larger than RAM. This is the most plausible new measurement. Expect a crossover map, not a "streaming wins" result.
  • NARROWED C5, KV eviction under a ceiling. Eviction itself is published (InfiniGen, H2O, StreamingLLM, vLLM). What survives: one planner trading KV bytes against resident layers under a single ceiling. It is not built yet.
  • DESIGN ONLY C6, GPU inside the ceiling. The narrowed form has no prior art found, but nothing is measured: SipLLM's Vulkan backend never runs on the GPU. "Even tiny GPUs help" is dropped; the evidence on Mali-G68-class GPUs is negative so far.
  • DEAD C7, flash-friendly streaming. Every weight-streaming system reads its weights without writing them back. This is one sentence in a paper, not a contribution.
  • DEAD "Vulkan offload is unique." llama.cpp and ExecuTorch ship Vulkan backends. What is uncommon: Vulkan that works on older (Vulkan 1.1) Mali drivers, where llama.cpp crashes, and GPU memory counted inside a hard RAM ceiling.
  • FALSE AS STATED "Storage is cheap." NAND contract prices rose about as fast as DRAM in the first half of 2026. The relative version holds: per gigabyte, DRAM is roughly 50–100× dearer than NAND, and on a discarded phone the flash is already paid for.

Before any paper, our own headline numbers have holes.

  • Peak RSS leaves out the operating system's file cache, which our reads fill.
  • Every streamed speed number we have was measured with a warm cache.
  • The KV cache briefly needs up to 1.85× its budgeted size when it grows.
  • The semantic cache is not in the budget at all.

All four are fixable, and Section 5 lists them. Reviewers would find them first.

2. What a user still cannot do (vs LiteRT and MediaPipe)

  • Get a measured answer to "is SipLLM better than LiteRT or MediaPipe?" No head-to-head has been run on any device. The cheapest decisive test is in Section 7.2.
  • Use a GPU or NPU. SipLLM's Vulkan backend is a placeholder. LiteRT ships GPU and NPU paths.
  • Run LiteRT's flagship models. Gemma-3n, Gemma-4 and Qwen3 are not supported by SipLLM. Gemma-4 and Phi-4 run on synthetic weights only.
  • Match llama.cpp's speed on a phone: decode about 0.81× and prompt processing about 0.66× (wave 16, Dimensity 900).
  • Run a model larger than RAM at an interactive speed. At the extreme it is about 0.55 tok/s (8B on an M3), possibly helped by the file cache.
  • Trust the hard ceiling in every case. KV growth and the semantic cache can push past it (Section 5).
  • Pool devices at a useful speed. The one cross-device run, M3 coordinator plus phone worker over Wi-Fi, decoded TinyLlama at 2.6–2.8 tok/s, or 5.6 with --fast. The M3 alone at the same 400 MB budget does 15.3 (PR #73, bench/results/issue70-lan-2026-10-19.json). Every node needs the whole model file, and every node must agree on --fast, which blocks mixed old/new phone pools.
  • See any x86 result or any energy-per-token number. Neither has ever been measured.

3. How this review was run, and how far to trust it

3.1 Design

  • Five Opus research agents ran in parallel, one per area, from one shared brief. The brief is reproduced in Appendix F.
  • The brief framed seven candidate claims, C1–C7, as statements to falsify, not confirm. Each agent was told that a claim dying is a useful result.
  • Citation rule: a paper counts only if its PDF was downloaded and the relevant pages read. Every claim carries an arXiv ID or DOI plus a page. Nothing is cited from memory. Abstract-only reads are marked as such.
  • Every statement is labelled: [PAPER p.N], [SIPLLM] (our own measured fact), [INFERENCE] or [HYPOTHESIS].
  • Agents could read SipLLM's source, and did. They could not edit code, touch git or post anything.

3.2 Spot checks against the PDFs and source (done by me)

3.3 Corrections to earlier statements (including mine)

  • The phone has 8 GB of RAM, not ~6 GB. device-OnePlus-IV2201-2026-10-19.json records 7550 MB total. The brief was wrong (found by B).
  • llama.cpp --fit does not refuse (above). This strengthens SipLLM's refusal as a difference.
  • The GPU test sources are not lost. Agent E first reported them missing and corrected that in its final notes. They are on local branch issue-67-vulkan, commit 7d10642: tools/vk_prefill_spike.cpp, tools/vk_probe.cpp and three shaders.
  • Mali-G68 and 8-bit integer maths. I said earlier that this GPU has no 8-bit dot-product units. That is true only of what its Vulkan 1.1 driver exposes. Arm's Valhall documentation says the hardware does 64 int8 operations per clock per warp unit, against 32 fp16, and I checked that line in the PDF. The capability may be reachable through OpenCL (cl_arm_integer_dot_product_int8), which has not been checked on the phone.
  • Why GPU kernel 2 was slow. I suggested scattered memory reads. Agent E's reading of the shader adds a likelier main cause: too many live values per GPU thread (about 90–110 against Valhall's 32 for full occupancy), so registers spill to memory. Both are hypotheses to test with Arm's offline shader compiler report.
  • 16-bit vs 32-bit speed. Arm's documentation says packed fp16 should run at twice the fp32 rate. Our measurement (200 vs 223 GFLOPS) contradicts that, and the cause is unresolved. No design should count on the 2× until it is.
  • "The GPU is 3× slower than the CPU" was not a like-for-like comparison. I compared a matmul-only GPU upper bound with end-to-end CPU runs made separately. The test's own CPU column was invalid (low clocks, blank values).
  • LAN pooling was measured across real devices. My brief told the agents it was loopback-only, and that error reached Agent C's verdict and earlier drafts of this report. PR #73 measured the M3 plus the phone over Wi-Fi (numbers in Section 7.3). C2's verdict does not change, because the narrowed claim is about discarded 2–3 GB phones and models larger than any one device. You spotted this.
  • Junkyard Computing's venue (ASPLOS 2023) was not re-verified from the PDF, which is the arXiv preprint.

5. Problems found in our own claims (fix before any paper)

Found by the agents reading our source. They are listed most serious first. None needs a redesign; each is a guarded, additive change plus a test.

#ProblemWhereWhy a reviewer caresFix and test
H1Peak RSS leaves out the OS file cache. Streaming uses buffered pread with no cache bypass, so every streamed layer also lands in the page cache, which is system RAM outside the process.include/llm/file_backing.h"13B in 317 MB" is a process-RSS fact, not yet a system-RAM fact. LLM in a Flash and FlexInfer measure with the cache bypassed or charged.Guarded flag: posix_fadvise(DONTNEED) after each consumed layer, F_NOCACHE on macOS, or O_DIRECT. Report cgroup v2 memory.peak (which charges the page cache), RssAnon vs RssFile, and an external-clamp run.
H2Every published streamed speed number is warm-cache.scripts/bench_ram_budget.sh:101-102, 155On a real 2–3 GB phone the file cannot stay cached. Speed will equal flash bandwidth, and the e-waste story depends on that number. Agent B worked out the effective read rate implied by our TinyLlama streaming runs: about 6.3 GB/s on the M3 and 2.8 GB/s on the phone. Both look like page-cache speeds rather than storage speeds; B did not check the storage specs.Cold-cache runs (PURGE=1 exists in scripts/bench.sh:31); bytes read per token from /proc/diskstats.
H3KV growth breaks the ceiling briefly. 6. The thesis that survives: a paper candidate

Working thesis. RAM can be a declared, enforced and measured resource for LLM inference on ordinary CPUs. The operator states a ceiling. A pure planner prices every peak-memory term and either guarantees the ceiling or refuses with an itemized reason. The runtime streams whatever does not fit, with output identical at every budget. We then measure what each byte of RAM buys, in quality per second and per joule, on phones that are being thrown away.

6.1 Candidate contributions (each must be measured first)

  1. The contract and how to verify it. A whole-process ledger with refusal, and a measurement method that cannot be fooled by the page cache: VmHWM plus RssAnon/RssFile split plus cgroup memory.peak plus an external clamp. Section 5 must be fixed first.
  2. The iso-memory frontier (C4). The first measured map of quality per second and per joule at fixed RAM, including streamed larger-than-RAM models, on CPU-only phones. A negative result ("streaming never wins below X GB/s of storage") is still publishable.
  3. Equal-RAM head-to-head against LiteRT-LM and llama.cpp on the same phone, using the protocol in Section 7.2. This is also the evidence for the project's standing goal.
  4. Pooled discarded phones with per-node contracts (C2), scored by carbon per token using Junkyard Computing's metric (embodied carbon counted as zero for reused devices). This one needs the most engineering first (Section 7.3).
  5. Future work unless Section 7.6's experiment succeeds: GPU memory inside the same contract (C6).

6.2 Threats to validity a reviewer will raise

  • "Your RAM number ignores the page cache" (H1). This is the single most likely objection.
  • "FlexInfer already does budgeted streaming on CPU." Answer: FlexInfer prices weights only, has no refusal, ran on a 512 GB server under cgroup simulation, and reports the limit, not measured RSS. One engineering step would close part of that gap, so the paper must not oversell.
  • "llama.cpp under a cgroup cap does the same." This needs the cgroup arm: does the planner avoid thrashing and out-of-memory kills that a kernel cap cannot?
  • "On phones, anonymous memory gets your process killed." prima.cpp argues for yielding page cache to the OS instead. This is an empirical question (Section 7.3) that could turn the hard ceiling into a liability on Android.
  • "Streaming is slow." True at the extreme (0.55 tok/s). The paper must say what each budget buys and stop there.
  • Energy. Flash reads cost about 11× DDR5 energy per bit (2508.06978). Without measured energy, the sustainability framing is rhetoric.

6.3 Paper outline (draft)

  1. Title options: "RAM as a Contract: Bounded-Memory LLM Inference on Commodity Phones", or "What Does a Megabyte Buy? A Measured Frontier for LLM Inference Under a Hard RAM Ceiling".
  2. Motivation: DRAM is the scarce, expensive resource (TrendForce 2026 figures, Section 7.4); phones are kept longer and discarded with small RAM; embodied carbon dominates phone emissions (Chasing Carbon, Sustainable AI).
  3. Background and related work: flash offload (LLM in a Flash, FlexGen, PowerInfer, PowerInfer-2, FlexInfer, ActiveFlow); budgets and planners (STI, SwapNet, Hermes, EdgeMoE, Galaxy, llama.cpp --fit); pooling (prima.cpp, TPI-LLM, LinguaLinked, Petals); KV memory (vLLM, H2O, StreamingLLM, InfiniGen); reuse (Junkyard Computing).
  4. Design: ledger, planner search, refusal, loader re-check, streaming ring, prefetch; why explicit reads rather than mmap (prima.cpp's own Fig. 14 is third-party evidence).
  5. Verification methodology: how peak memory is measured and why each counter matters.
  6. Evaluation: contract sweeps; head-to-head at equal RAM; the frontier; pooling, if ready; energy and carbon.
  7. Limitations, stated plainly: speed in the streaming regime; CPU-only; model coverage.

7. Findings by area

7.1 Streaming weights from storage on one device (Agent A)

The closest prior art to C1, with each one's memory regime. "Exact" means the output does not change with the budget.

Speed levers A found (prior-art numbers, not ours):

  • Speculative decoding with a small draft model kept in RAM: 2.93× over flash-offloaded decoding (Lever, 2605.16786) and 4.7–6.1× under 4 GB budgets (LLMCad, 2309.04255). In the streaming regime one pass over the weights costs the same I/O for 1 or k tokens. SipLLM has only prompt-lookup speculation.
  • Balanced per-layer pinning: +56.8–83.3% at mid budgets in FlexInfer, vs SipLLM's pinning of the first N layers.
  • Storage queue depth per device: on UFS phones a big core reads 1,076 MB/s vs 762 on a little core, and multi-core I/O can lose up to 40% (PowerInfer-2).

7.2 Phone runtimes and a fair head-to-head (Agent B)

Three blunt facts shape any comparison:

  1. Bit-identical weights are impossible. LiteRT and MediaPipe take only .task, .litertlm or .tflite files, while SipLLM and llama.cpp take GGUF. Every comparison is "runtime plus its quantizer", so quality must be measured for each artifact.
  2. The memory regimes differ. LiteRT-LM and MediaPipe are fit-only, with the KV size baked into the file (ekv1280). llama.cpp fits, shrinks context with --fit, and pages through mmap. SipLLM has a ceiling.
  3. Only CPU against CPU is possible, because SipLLM has no working GPU path.

So the paper reports three tables, never one:

  • (A) the same model at default settings;
  • (B) equal memory, with SipLLM's budget set to each rival's measured peak and to 50% and 25% of it;
  • (C) the minimum RAM each needs to run at all.
ModelLiteRT artifactSipLLM statusUsable pair?
TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat.task, dynamic int8, 1095 MB. Published S24 Ultra CPU: 156 tok/s prefill, 26.3 decode, 2359 MB RSS (their numbers)main benchmark modelBest pair. Whether litert_lm_main accepts .task is unverified.
SmolLM2-135M-Instruct.litertlmbenchmark modelGood
Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct.task q8, ekv1280matches llama.cpp token for tokenGood
Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct.task / .litertlm; S25 Ultra CPU 297.6 / 34.25, 1997 MB (theirs)0.5B validated, 1.5B notGood after a parity check
Llama-3.2-1BGPU artifact onlysupportedCPU comparison impossible without reconversion
Gemma-4, Gemma-3n, Qwen3LiteRT's flagship modelsnot supportedImpossible today

Controls the comparison needs (each sourced in B's notes):

  • Same device and session, with run order interleaved across runtimes.
  • Cool-down between runs. Report both the cold first run and the sustained plateau over 20+ iterations; phones lose 15–40% to throttling.
  • Clocks and battery temperature logged every run.
  • Equal thread counts pinned to the same cores.
  • Load time, first run and warm steady state reported separately.
  • Fixed token counts, using LiteRT's published 1024 prefill / 256 decode, and equal KV capacity.
  • Peak memory as VmHWM (what LiteRT reports on Android), plus the RssAnon/RssFile split, MemAvailable and the external-clamp test.
  • Energy from a BatteryManager current×voltage logger, with its accuracy limits stated.
  • At least 10 runs, ideally 30, with medians and IQR and non-parametric tests.
  • Quality measured for every artifact.

Cheapest decisive first experiment.

  • Models: TinyLlama and SmolLM2-135M on the OnePlus (Dimensity 900).
  • Arms:
    • LiteRT-LM's command-line binary on the CPU, run from adb shell with its own peak-memory report;
    • SipLLM, resident at Q8_0, then --ram-budget equal to LiteRT's measured peak, then 512M and 256M;
    • llama.cpp, with and without mmap.
  • Settings: 1024/256 tokens, 4 pinned threads, 10 runs plus a 20-iteration plateau, energy logged.

This settles "better than LiteRT" as either a measured RAM claim or a measured RAM-and-speed claim. MediaPipe needs an app harness, so it comes second.

Kernel headroom B found: MediaPipe reports that the i8mm instructions give twice the speed of the dot-product path, and T-MAC's lookup-table kernels for 3 bits and below. Both matter more for prompt processing (66% of llama.cpp) than for C1.

7.3 Pooling old devices (Agent C)

What SipLLM's pooling actually does, read from source by C:

  • a plain pipeline split by contiguous layer ranges, with manual --nodes;
  • only the residual stream crosses the wire, synchronously, with one node computing at a time;
  • each node has its own --ram-budget and refuses if it is impossible (real in code);
  • the handshake requires the whole model file on every node and the same effective --fast, which blocks pools mixing phones with and without 8-bit dot product;
  • no partitioner, no fault tolerance, no encryption;
  • measured once across separate devices (PR #73): the M3 plus the phone over Wi-Fi, 2.6–2.8 tok/s (5.6 with --fast), phone peak 301 MB. On loopback, two 400 MB processes reached 22.6 tok/s against 15.3 for one 400 MB process. The gain comes from removing streaming (all 22 layers fit across two 400 MB nodes), not from parallel compute: the pipeline is sequential.
Prior artWhat it already doesGap SipLLM could fill
prima.cpp (ICLR 2026)Pools home devices including phones over Wi-Fi; 30–70B models larger than combined RAM; streams from each device's disk (mmap plus window prefetch); integer-programming scheduler; drops weak devices; measures energyReports change in free memory, not process peak; oversubscribes on purpose; its phones were recent high-end chips (1.9 / 5.1 GiB available)
TPI-LLM (2410.00531)CPU-only low-memory devices, per-device sliding-window streaming; 70B at ~3.1 GB per deviceFP32, ~26–30 s/token, memory method unstated, code repo returns 404
Galaxy (INFOCOM 2024)Operator-set per-device budgets (1.5/1.2/0.7 GB); the planner fails cleanly if nothing fitsWeights only, resident, models up to 2.7B, memory not measured
LOIP, LinguaLinked, EdgeShard, Jupiter, PetalsPer-device memory budgets in planning, offloading, pipelines, micro-batching, fault tolerance (Petals)Jetson boards or 8–12 GB phones; none measure process peak against a budget
Junkyard Computing (2110.06870)10 discarded Pixel 3A phones as a cloudlet; 9.8–18.9× better carbon per query than a cloud instance over 3 yearsNo ML or LLM workload at all

The strongest objection to us: prima.cpp argues that on phones the right policy is to yield memory as file cache that the OS can reclaim. Anonymous buffers like ours get the process killed by Android's low-memory killer. A hard ceiling protects other apps only if it is set low enough, and it does not make SipLLM unkillable. Measure this before telling the e-waste story.

Engineering C recommends:

  • --nodes auto, a dynamic-programming partitioner over contiguous ranges;
  • shipping only each node's slice of the weights;
  • --fast as a per-node choice, with a golden check for output drift;
  • prefetch armed on node i+1 while node i computes.

7.4 E-waste, carbon and memory prices (Agent C)

  • E-waste: 62 billion kg in 2022 (7.8 kg per person); 22.3% documented as collected and recycled; projected 82 billion kg by 2030 (Global E-waste Monitor 2024, pp. 10, 16, 26, 28).
  • Phones: about 1.5 billion sold per year, most decommissioned within two years, and 60–70% neither thrown out nor recycled (Junkyard, p. 1). Replacement cycles are now stretching toward four years because memory prices raise device prices (Counterpoint, 13 August 2026). Two consequences pull in opposite directions:
    • fewer phones are discarded in the near term;
    • the phones people keep are older and have less RAM.
  • Embodied carbon: manufacturing went from 49% to 86% of iPhone lifetime emissions between 2009 and 2019 (Chasing Carbon, p. 2). It is 74% for client devices (Sustainable AI, p. 9). A phone's manufacturing carbon takes about three years of continuous inference to amortize (Chasing Carbon, p. 7).
Quarter (TrendForce, contract QoQ)Conventional DRAMNAND flash
4Q25 actual+45–50%—
1Q26 forecast+90–95% (mobile LPDDR ~90%)+55–60%
2Q26 forecast+58–63%+70–75% (mobile NAND tightest)
3Q26 forecast+13–18%+10–15%
4Q26 forecast(chart only)+15–20%

Spot prices on 23 October 2026: a DDR5 16 Gb chip at about $30.6 per GB, against TLC NAND at about $0.29–0.62 per GB. That is the source of the 50–100× relative figure. These come from C's notes; I have not opened the TrendForce pages myself.

How to phrase it in a paper: "trade a relatively cheap, already-owned resource (flash bandwidth on a device that has already been paid for) for the scarcest one (DRAM)", never "storage is cheap". Also say that flash reads cost energy (Section 6.2).

7.5 Theory, KV cache and the quality frontier (Agent D)

  • Residency theory: for dense layers read in a fixed cycle, pinning any k layers is optimal in transfers, and the least-recently-used policy misses on every read. What is left is a stall-time problem: spread pinning may cut time per token by 13–27% on phone-like ratios at 16–20 of 22 layers resident. That comes from a toy model, not a measurement.
  • KV: quantizing the KV cache beats evicting entries (KV-compression benchmark, Table 2). The planner should try --kv-q8 before capping the context, and the semantic cache should store Q8 rows.
  • The semantic cache is RadixAttention (SGLang, 2312.07104). Do not claim it as research.
  • No formally verified peak-memory bound for LLM inference exists in the sources searched. The nearest is TensorFlow Lite Micro's fixed arena with refusal at start-up, for microcontroller-scale models. A verified or statically proven bound for a restricted configuration space is a possible long-term contribution, once H3–H5 are fixed.

7.6 Phone and laptop GPUs, and what is wrong with ours (Agent E and my diagnosis)

What is wrong on our side

  1. The shipped backend is a placeholder. src/vulkan.cpp always falls back to the CPU. shaders/matmul.comp runs one thread per output row, in 32-bit float, unbatched.
  2. The test kernels use a small fraction of the chip (branch issue-67-vulkan):
    • kernel 1 (shared-memory tiles) reached about 34 GFLOPS, roughly 15% of the 223 GFLOPS measured peak;
    • kernel 2, meant to be the Mali-tuned version, reached about 7. Two likely causes, both testable:
      • each GPU thread holds about 90–110 live values, against the 32 Valhall allows at full occupancy (Arm, Valhall shader core p.10), so it probably spills registers to memory;
      • each lane reads a different weight row with scalar 4-byte loads. Arm's guidance is vector loads and sequential addresses across a warp (Arm Best Practices p.97).
    Arm's guide also says Mali has no dedicated on-chip shared memory: it is system RAM backed by the load-store cache (p.92). Kernel 1's use of it, sharing dequantized weights across tokens, is the kind Arm endorses, which fits kernel 1 beating kernel 2.
  3. The test's CPU baseline was invalid: big cores were at 1.1–1.4 GHz at the start of the first run, the CPU column is blank in the warm run, and the test's own CPU figure (~11 tok/s) is a quarter of the real engine's 47.
  4. 16-bit maths measured no faster than 32-bit (200 vs 223 GFLOPS). Arm's documentation says packed fp16 doubles the rate (Best Practices p.95; Valhall p.11). The test code counts both runs the same way (tools/vk_prefill_spike.cpp lines 288–296 on issue-67-vulkan, per Agent E), so the gap is real, not a counting error. Either the driver compiler did not pack the operations or the clock dropped between runs. Unresolved; Arm's Mali Offline Compiler report would show which.

Why video players and games do well on the same GPU

Sourced by Agent E (Appendix E, section 9) from Arm's GPU Best Practices guide, Arm's Valhall shader-core document, Qualcomm's Adreno guides, and video documentation from Intel, NVIDIA, Apple, Android, Linux V4L2 and VideoLAN.

  • Video players don't use the GPU's compute cores for decoding. Every platform's documentation describes separate decode hardware:
    • Intel: "VDBox";
    • NVIDIA: "NVDEC";
    • Apple: VideoToolbox gives "direct access to hardware encoders and decoders";
    • Linux on ARM: codecs are V4L2 memory-to-memory devices, separate from the GPU;
    • Android: vendor codecs exposed through MediaCodec.
    VLC on Android offers "MediaCodec opaque direct rendering (decoding + display acceleration)". The GPU at most scales, converts colour and composites. So smooth video says nothing about the GPU's maths throughput.
  • Graphics is engineered to avoid RAM traffic.
    • Mali keeps "the intermediate framebuffer working state … entirely inside the tile memory" (Arm p.57). Adreno renders bins in on-chip GMEM.
    • Textures are compressed (ASTC) and framebuffers losslessly compressed (AFBC, UBWC).
    • Dedicated units filter textures, blend and write pixels outside the maths pipes: per clock a Valhall core does 32 fp32 FMAs plus 4 filtered texture samples, 2 blends and 2 pixel writes (Valhall p.10).
    • Every texel and vertex is reused by many pixels.
  • LLM decode is the opposite. Every weight is read once per token for 2 operations, about 3.6 operations per byte at Q4_K. TinyLlama decode on our phone's CPU already streams about 9 GB/s of weights while needing only about 30 GFLOPS. On a shared-memory phone the GPU reads the same RAM, and no tile memory, compression or fixed-function unit applies to weights. A measured Mali-G720 decode ran its load-store units above 90% busy (2410.03613 p.12).
  • Prompt processing is compute-bound (about 460 operations per byte at 128 tokens). It is limited by on-chip reuse, and that is where Mali is weakest for compute: 16 KB L1 per core, no dedicated shared memory, and full occupancy only up to 32 registers.
  • Why NPUs and int8/matrix units exist: graphics runs well at fp16 and has no use for int8 dot products or matrix units. ML does, so vendors added them, plus separate NPUs. On Dimensity 9300 the Mali-G720 does 4.0 TFLOPS fp16 against the NPU's 48 TOPS int8 (HeteroInfer Table 2). These help prompt processing, not decode, which stays bandwidth-bound.
  • Who exposes int8 dot in Vulkan: older Mali drivers (Vulkan 1.1, like our phone's) hide int8 dot that the silicon has. Newer Mali drivers (r44+, Vulkan 1.3) and Qualcomm's own Adreno drivers expose it. This comes from llama.cpp device logs; it depends on the driver.

When does a small GPU beat the CPU? (Agent E's evidence table, condensed)

Device / GPUStackResultSource
Dimensity 900 / Mali-G68 MC4SipLLM spike, untunedGPU ≤17.7 tok/s (matmul-only bound) vs CPU 47: CPU winsours
Dimensity 900 / Mali-G68 MC4llama.cpp Vulkansegfaultours
Dimensity 9300 / Mali-G720; Mali-G78MLC-LLM (OpenCL)slower than CPU in both phases2410.03613 p.7
RK3588 / Mali-G610llama.cpp VulkanCPU 4–10× fasterllama.cpp #22682
Mali-G715llama.cpp Vulkan PR #15800prompt processing 2.81 → 66 tok/s with Mali-aware matmul#15800
Pixel 9 / Mali-G715ML Drift (OpenCL, tuned, int8)Gemma-2B prefill 796 tok/sML Drift p.6
Dimensity 9400 / Mali-G925llama.cpp Vulkanprompt processing 125.8 vs CPU 51.2: GPU 2.5×#30006
Galaxy S23 / Adreno 740llama.cpp GPUparity with CPUMELTing point p.7
Xiaomi Pad 6 Pro / Adreno 730llama.cpp GPUGPU wins both phases; 50–66% less energy2410.03613 p.13
Framework 13 / Radeon 780Mllama.cpp ROCmprompt processing 5.8×, decode 1.4× vs CPUforum post

The pattern (Agent E's inference):

  • Adreno phone GPUs and AMD/Intel laptop iGPUs beat the CPU on prompt processing with mainstream software.
  • Mali loses with every generic software stack measured. It wins only with vendor-grade tuned kernels or newer drivers.
  • Decode rarely gains on shared-memory devices.

Break-even on our phone: TinyLlama is about 1.9 GFLOP of matmul per token. To beat the CPU's 47 tok/s alone, the GPU needs about 90 GFLOPS sustained: 2.6× our spike, and no published Vulkan fp16 kernel on this class of Mali reaches it.

Three routes remain, all hypotheses:

  1. CPU and GPU sharing the prompt work. Split each matrix multiply's output columns in proportion to measured speed, and sync once per layer. Even the untuned kernel (17.7) plus the CPU (47) could give up to about +30%.
  2. An OpenCL 8-bit dot-product path, if the phone's OpenCL driver exposes it. Check with clinfo.
  3. Streamed prompt processing into a GPU-visible ring buffer. Each streamed layer is used for every prompt token, so the work is compute-bound and a GPU can pay off. This is the surviving form of C6.

Engineering guard rails from others' failures:

  • Use only the Vulkan 1.1 core API plus explicitly requested extensions; llama.cpp crashes on 1.1 Mali drivers by calling 1.2 features.
  • Run a self-test on first use against the CPU kernel, and fall back if it disagrees.
  • Put timeouts on fences, because the GPU can hang when the app is backgrounded.
  • Keep the GPU off by default, so the default build stays byte-identical.
  • Load libvulkan at runtime with dlopen, so there is no link-time dependency.
  • Embed precompiled SPIR-V.
  • Allocate GPU memory once. Freed memory lingers in RSS for about 2 s, which would double-count against a peak.

Where Vulkan should pay off most: laptop iGPUs. They are several times stronger than the CPU there, but nothing has been measured on x86 hardware.

7.7 "Turn the bad RAM to good use" (Agent D)

  • Old devices with too little RAM: this is exactly what the hard ceiling is for (C1, C2). It needs the cold-cache numbers from H2 to be honest.
  • Literally faulty RAM: a real research line exists. EDEN (MICRO 2019) runs DNNs on deliberately under-volted DRAM and cuts DRAM energy by 21–37%, but with CNNs and retraining. LLMs are fragile: a single bit flip in a critical BF16 exponent damages accuracy (RangeGuard). No source evaluates LLMs on old or defective consumer RAM.
  • The practical route: Linux can fence off faulty regions (memtest=, memmap=), which leaves a smaller-RAM machine that SipLLM's planner already handles.
  • A speculative idea (hypothesis only): a bit flip in a streamed weight buffer lives for at most one token, because the layer is re-read from flash. A fault-injection study (ring vs pinned vs KV) would test whether streaming makes inference more tolerant of bad RAM.

Duplicates across agents are merged. "Who" means: code (I can do it), measure (host measurement) or device (Ankit runs it).

10. Registering the work

  • Preprint (fastest way to put a dated, citable public record on the work): arXiv, likely cs.DC with cs.LG cross-listed. First-time submitters may need an endorsement from an established author in the category. Post only after X0–X2 at minimum, so the claims in it are measured.
  • Software citation: connect the GitHub repo to Zenodo so each release gets a DOI, and add a CITATION.cff. Both are public actions on the repo and need your go-ahead.
  • A software paper (for example JOSS) can review the runtime itself, separately from the research paper.
  • Venues to consider (my suggestion; deadlines not checked):
    • mobile and systems conferences: MobiSys, MobiCom, SenSys, EuroSys, ASPLOS, MLSys, USENIX ATC;
    • workshops for an early version: HotMobile, EdgeSys, EMDL;
    • prima.cpp went to ICLR.
  • Patent: the repo has been public since 22 July 2026.
    • In India and Europe that disclosure generally counts against novelty.
    • The US allows one year from an inventor's own disclosure.
    • Given how much prior art the review found, the surviving claims are narrow.
    Talk to a patent attorney before spending on it. This is not legal advice.
