2. Per-paper records (most relevant to C1 first)

Conjunct table used for every C1 assessment [INFERENCE: C1 is a conjunction, so each paper is scored per conjunct rather than yes/no]: (a) budget is an operator input vs device capacity; (b) what the budget covers (weights only / weights+KV / whole process); (c) enforcement (own accounting / external cgroup / "it happened to fit"); (d) behaviour when infeasible (refuse / OOM / degrade quality / silently exceed); (e) CPU-only?; (f) how memory was measured (RSS tool / cgroup limit / profiler / computed from tensor sizes); (g) model-size / budget ratio.

FlexInfer — Breaking Memory Constraint via Flexible and Efficient Offloading for On-Device LLM Inference
  • Cite: H. Du, S. Wu, A. Kharlamova, et al., EuroMLSys 2025 (per arXiv), arXiv:2503.03777, https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03777. Read: FULL (9 pp.)
  • Problem: llama.cpp's mmap path collapses when available memory < model size; existing compression/offloading methods cannot adapt to a varying, user-specified memory budget [PAPER p.1-2].
  • Mechanism:
    • Asynchronous prefetching over a "prefetch window" of k layers; memory freed right after a layer is used; footprint ~ n/k reduction vs the model, default k = 3 [PAPER p.3, p.5].
    • Tensor-level multi-threaded I/O: each I/O thread loads one tensor (W_Q, W_K, ...), to avoid small transfers and use storage bandwidth [PAPER p.3].
    • "Balanced memory locking": spare memory locks a SLICE OF EVERY LAYER (each layer split into a fixed part and a prefetched part) instead of locking the first N whole layers, so per-layer I/O time stays uniform and I/O/compute overlap stays balanced [PAPER p.3-4, Fig. 3].
    • "Flexible tensor preservation" (Algorithm 1): given attention/FFN tensor sizes, N layers and the memory budget, choose which tensor types to lock across all layers (FFN-first when memory is large, attention-first when small) [PAPER p.4].
    • Direct I/O to bypass the page cache for the I/O threads; 828 lines of C/C++ on llama.cpp; "additional parameters were introduced to control the available memory" [PAPER p.4-5].
  • Memory regime: (a) operator-set: YES — "user-specified resource budgets" [PAPER p.1, p.2, p.6]. (b) covers WEIGHTS ONLY: Algorithm 1 inputs are tensor sizes + budget; embeddings kept resident outside the scheme; KV cache, activations and process base are not priced [PAPER p.3-4; INFERENCE from Alg. 1]. (c) evaluation enforcement is EXTERNAL: cgroup limits memory, taskset limits cores [PAPER p.5]. (d) infeasible budgets: not discussed [PAPER, absent]. (e) CPU-only: YES, "This paper focuses on CPU inference since resource-constrained devices usually don't have powerful GPUs" [PAPER p.3 fn.1]. (f) memory axis = cgroup limit, no measured peak RSS reported [PAPER p.5, Fig. 4]. (g) Llama2-70B Q4 (36.4 GB) evaluated down to ~5 GB (~7x) [PAPER p.2 Table 1, p.5 Fig. 4d].
  • Hardware: Ubuntu server, AMD 7995WX, 512 GB RAM, devices SIMULATED with cgroup + taskset [PAPER p.5]. No phone, no laptop.
  • Headline numbers (theirs): llama.cpp mmap under cgroup gives 0.08-0.67 tok/s; FlexInfer 5.2-12.5x (7B), 5-11.8x (13B), 4.2-10.6x (34B), 5-11x (70B) over mmap [PAPER p.5]. Sync multi- thread large reads alone 2.6-3x over mmap; prefetch +34.8-59.4%; balanced locking +9.2-11.1% at minimum memory, up to +56.8-83.3% with more memory [PAPER p.5-6]. Full-memory 70B baseline 31.14 tok/s on that server [PAPER p.2 Table 1].
  • Does NOT do: no whole-process ledger, no KV pricing, no refusal path, no measured RSS, no real edge device, no quality evaluation (weights are exact so none needed), no MoE. [PAPER; INFERENCE]
  • Pre-empts SipLLM claims:
    • C1 PARTIAL (the strongest dense-model hit found): operator-set budget, CPU-only, models up to ~7x the budget, async layer prefetch + residual pinning — this is SipLLM's mechanism set. What survives: whole-process pricing incl. KV at full ctx, refusal with itemized report, measured peak RSS on real phones/laptops.
    • C3 NONE (static heuristic, no optimum comparison). C4 NONE. C7 NONE (no discussion of wear).
  • Lessons for SipLLM:
    1. src/loader.cpp plan_and_pin_layers() pins contiguous whole layers [0, n_pinned) — exactly FlexInfer's "Flex. w/o Balance" baseline ("locks the model parameters by layer order"), which loses up to 56.8-83.3% vs balanced locking at mid budgets [PAPER p.5-6; SIPLLM loader.cpp:192-230]. Pin a per-layer slice (e.g. the same tensor roles in every layer) so every streamed layer costs the same I/O and the prefetch never stalls on a "big" layer.
    2. src/loader.cpp worker_loop()/fill_slot() is ONE thread issuing serial per-role preads (queue depth 1) [SIPLLM loader.cpp:320-355]. FlexInfer uses one I/O thread per tensor; LLM in a Flash uses 32 threads. Parallel per-role reads are a cheap experiment.
    3. FlexInfer's direct I/O bypasses the page cache; SipLLM's FileBacking::pread_exact does not (include/llm/file_backing.h, no O_DIRECT/F_NOCACHE) — see measurement-methodology lesson in §4.
  • Contribution opportunity: [HYPOTHESIS] SipLLM's whole-process contract (KV at full ctx + scratch
    • base) holds measured peak RSS <= budget where a weights-only budget (FlexInfer-style) overshoots by the KV+activation term at long ctx. Experiment: same model, budgets 0.5/1/2 GB, ctx 512/4096, record /usr/bin/time peak RSS and cgroup memory.peak for SipLLM vs a weights-only budget mode (emulate by excluding KV from the ledger); report overshoot.
ActiveFlow — Scaling Up On-Device LLMs via Active-Weight Swapping Between DRAM and Flash
  • Cite: F. Jia, Z. Wu, S. Jiang, et al. (Microsoft Research / Tsinghua / CSU), arXiv:2504.08378 v2 (Sep 2025), https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.08378. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-2, 4-13; skipped distillation details)
  • Problem: scale deployable LLM size on phones by keeping only "active" weights (Top-K activation sparsity, no ReLU needed) in DRAM and swapping the rest from flash [PAPER p.1-3].
  • Mechanism:
    • Cross-layer active-weight preloading: current-layer activations predict the next N layers' active channels (N=4 overlaps fully); misses (~5%) loaded on demand [PAPER p.5].
    • Flash layout reordered across a layer group (channel, layer, operator) so one read fetches the same channel of 4 layers, raising minimum chunk size; peak UFS throughput needs chunks > 64 KB while a channel is ~4 KB [PAPER p.5, Fig. 7, Fig. 9].
    • Cost model with a HARD memory constraint M <= M_max where M = M_cl + M_cache + M_kv (KV "only the fixed-size case"); greedy search sets sparsity sp = 1 - M_max/S_m, then group size N, then cache size; cache resized online if the budget changes [PAPER p.6-7].
    • LFU-style per-tensor channel cache [PAPER p.7]; io_uring reads; loader thread pinned to a little core [PAPER p.9]; sparsity-aware self-distillation to recover accuracy [PAPER p.8].
  • Memory regime: (a) operator-set: YES, M_max is an input ("for a given mobile device (i.e., with specific computational power and memory budget)") [PAPER p.6]. (b) covers active weights + weight cache + fixed-size KV [PAPER p.6 Eq. 8]; activations/process base not in the model [INFERENCE]. (c) own accounting via the cost model. (d) infeasible budget is met by RAISING SPARSITY, i.e. degrading model quality (sp = 1 - M_max/S_m) — the budget is never refused [PAPER p.6]. (e) CPU only (llama.cpp-based, big.LITTLE) [PAPER p.9]. (f) memory measured with Android Studio Profiler [PAPER p.9]. (g) Mixtral-8x7B (24.6 GB) at 2.9-4.4 GB (~6-8x) but at 50-80% sparsity [PAPER p.10].
  • Hardware: OnePlus 12 (16 GB, UFS 4.0), Pixel 6 (8 GB, UFS 3.1), Infinix ZERO 30 (8 GB, UFS 2.2) [PAPER p.10 Table 2].
  • Headline numbers (theirs): LLaMA-2-7B same speed as full-weight at 40% less memory on Dev 2/3; 1.9x/1.5x faster than full-weight at 75% less memory [PAPER p.10]. Mixtral-8x7B 1.3/1.0/0.4 tok/s at 4.3 GB and 2.3/1.8/0.8 tok/s at 2.9 GB on the three phones [PAPER p.10]. Average power -27.34% vs llama.cpp, energy/token up to -53% at 1.3 GB [PAPER p.12, Fig. 20]. Top-K 70% sparsity costs accuracy (e.g. MMLU 65.16% -> 36.53% for plain Top-K; their distillation recovers part) [PAPER p.11 Table 4].
  • Does NOT do: output is NOT invariant to the budget (sparsity, hence quality, is a function of M_max); requires per-model self-distillation (~10 h on 4xA100) [PAPER p.9]; no refusal; KV not priced at a chosen ctx (fixed-size assumption).
  • Pre-empts: C1 PARTIAL — operator-set hard memory constraint on real phones, CPU-only, with a cost model; differs in that it meets the budget by lowering quality and does not price the whole process. C4 PARTIAL — it plots a quality-vs-memory Pareto frontier at fixed memory (Fig. 1, Fig. 15) but only along the sparsity axis of one model, not streamed-large vs resident-small. C3 NONE (LFU, no optimum). C7 NONE.
  • Lessons: (1) Energy can be measured on unrooted Android from voltage_now/current_now at 0.5 s sampling [PAPER p.9] — closes SipLLM's "energy never measured" gap with no new dependency (bench script, not runtime). (2) Chunk > 64 KB to reach peak UFS bandwidth [PAPER p.5]: SipLLM reads whole tensors (MBs), so it is already in the large-chunk regime — but its windowed embedding reads (read_raw_at of one row, loader.cpp:453) and LM-head windows should be checked against this threshold. (3) io_uring + loader thread on a little core [PAPER p.9] — relevant to SipLLM's big.LITTLE "fixed16" scheduling.
  • Contribution opportunity: [HYPOTHESIS] at equal M_max, exact dense streaming (SipLLM, bit- identical output) of a model one size up beats ActiveFlow-style sparse swapping in quality per second for tasks sensitive to sparsity (GSM8K, MMLU). Experiment: fixed 2 GB budget, Llama-3-8B streamed dense vs 70%-sparse, measure tok/s and GSM8K/MMLU.
LLM in a flash — Efficient Large Language Model Inference with Limited Memory
  • Cite: K. Alizadeh, I. Mirzadeh, D. Belenko, et al. (Apple), ACL 2024, arXiv:2312.11514, https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.11514. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-8, 16)
  • Problem: run LLMs larger than available DRAM by keeping weights in flash and loading only what each token needs [PAPER p.1].
  • Mechanism:
    • Exploit FFN activation sparsity (ReLU / relufied models) with a low-rank predictor; attention weights (~1/3 of the model) kept resident [PAPER p.3-4].
    • Sliding window: keep neurons active in the last k tokens; load only the delta each token [PAPER p.4-5].
    • Row-column bundling: store up-proj column i and down-proj row i contiguously, doubling chunk size [PAPER p.5].
    • Preallocated per-layer matrix with swap-to-end deletion to avoid realloc/copy [PAPER p.5-6, Fig. 6].
    • 32-thread parallel reads; flash throughput rises with chunk size and thread count; ">= 32 KiB random reads across multiple threads" suffice for sparse inference [PAPER p.2 Fig. 2b, p.3, p.7].
  • Memory regime: fixed ASSUMPTION, not enforcement: "tested under the condition that approximately half of the model size is available in DRAM" and "we assume nearly half of the total memory (DRAM and GPU) is allocated for model computations" [PAPER p.6, p.7]. KV gets "a specific portion of DRAM" not modelled [PAPER p.6]. Baseline I/O latencies are "best theoretical possible numbers" [PAPER p.7]. No measured RSS. (a) assumption ~50% of model; (b) weights; (c) none; (d) n/a; (e) CPU, Metal GPU and RTX 4090; (f) computed; (g) 2x.
  • Hardware: Apple M1 Max (1 TB SSD), M2 Ultra (2 TB SSD), Linux + RTX 4090 [PAPER p.7].
  • Headline numbers (theirs): models up to 2x DRAM; 4-5x (CPU) and 20-25x (GPU) faster than naive loading [PAPER p.1-2]. OPT-6.7B I/O latency 2196 ms naive -> 87 ms with predictor+window+bundling at half memory (Table 2) [PAPER p.7]. Sparse scattered reads 1.25 GiB/s vs 6.1 GiB/s dense [PAPER p.7].
  • Page cache (important for SipLLM): "the operating system typically caches the blocks ... this caching mechanism consumes additional memory in DRAM beyond what is allocated for the model"; benchmarks use F_NOCACHE (macOS/iOS) and DirectIO (Linux) plus purge [PAPER p.16].
  • Does NOT do: no hard ceiling, no planner, requires sparse (ReLU) models and a trained predictor; PyTorch/HF implementation, not a deployable runtime [PAPER p.6].
  • Pre-empts: C1 NONE on the ceiling conjuncts (fixed 50% assumption, no enforcement, no measured memory); PARTIAL on "model larger than DRAM via flash" (2x vs SipLLM's 25x). C7 PARTIAL: it frames flash as the large, cheap tier and discusses bandwidth/energy of reloading [PAPER p.2], but says nothing about write endurance. C3 NONE.
  • Lessons: (1) Page cache is real memory: SipLLM's RSS numbers (pread through the page cache, warm-cache benchmarks per docs/product/benchmarks.md:135) exclude it. Add an F_NOCACHE (macOS) / O_DIRECT or POSIX_FADV_DONTNEED (Linux/Android) option to FileBacking (include/llm/file_backing.h) and report cold-cache, cache-bypassed numbers. (2) Parallel reads (32 threads) to raise flash throughput — see FlexInfer lesson 2. (3) "Read more than needed in larger chunks then discard" [PAPER p.3] applies to SipLLM's LM-head/embedding windows.
  • Contribution opportunity: [HYPOTHESIS] dense exact streaming at 25x model/RSS beats the 2x regime of sparsity-based loading for models without ReLU sparsity. Test: Llama-3.1-8B (SwiGLU, no ReLU) under budgets 0.25x-1x the file, SipLLM measured; LLM-in-a-flash cannot run it without relufication.
FlexGen — High-Throughput Generative Inference of Large Language Models with a Single GPU
  • Cite: Y. Sheng, L. Zheng, B. Yuan, et al., ICML 2023, arXiv:2303.06865, https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.06865. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-8, 15-17)
  • Problem: throughput-oriented batch inference of OPT-175B on one 16 GB GPU by offloading weights, activations and KV across GPU/CPU/disk [PAPER p.1-2].
  • Mechanism:
    • Zig-zag block schedule (proved within 2x of I/O-optimal; diagonal schedule asymptotically optimal) [PAPER p.5, Thm 4.1; p.15].
    • Placement percentages for weights (layer granularity), activations and KV (tensor granularity) across three tiers [PAPER p.5].
    • Analytical cost model + two-level search: enumerate (block size, GPU batch), then a 9-variable LP minimizing T/bls subject to gpu/cpu/disk PEAK MEMORY < CAPACITY [PAPER p.6 Eq. 1]; peak-memory terms price weights, activations, KV and attention working buffers per phase (prefill/decode) [PAPER p.17 "Peak Memory Constraints"].
    • 4-bit group-wise quantization of weights and KV; CPU computes attention when KV is on CPU [PAPER p.5-7].
  • Memory regime: (a) capacities are HARDWARE SPECS fed to the LP (Table 1: T4 16 GB, 208 GB DRAM, 1.5 TB SSD) — the formulation accepts any number, so an operator could set it lower, but the paper never uses it as an operator ceiling [PAPER p.6-7; INFERENCE]. (b) GPU+CPU+disk peaks incl. weights, KV, activations [PAPER p.17]. (c) own cost model. (d) NOT a guarantee: "Due to our relaxation and the hardness of accurately modeling peak memory usage (e.g., fragmentation), sometimes a strategy from the policy search can run out of memory. In this case, we manually adjust the policy slightly" [PAPER p.6]. (e) GPU required. (f) not reported as measured RSS. (g) OPT-175B (~325 GB fp16) on 16 GB GPU + 208 GB DRAM.
  • Hardware: Google Cloud T4 instance, SSD read ~2 GB/s, write ~1 GB/s [PAPER p.7].
  • Headline (theirs): 1 token/s OPT-175B at effective batch 144 [PAPER p.1]; up to 100x throughput over DeepSpeed ZeRO-Inference / Accelerate in throughput mode [PAPER p.1-2].
  • Does NOT do: no CPU-only mode, no latency-oriented single-stream use (batch 1 is its weak case), no guarantee; WRITES activations and KV to SSD (ctod bandwidth terms) [PAPER p.5-6] — an endurance cost SipLLM's read-only weight streaming avoids.
  • Pre-empts: C1 PARTIAL — this is the prior art for "a planner that prices every peak-memory contributor (weights + activations + KV) against a capacity and searches knobs". SipLLM's distinguishing conjuncts: operator-set ceiling below device capacity, CPU-only single-stream, a guarantee (refuse instead of OOM-then-hand-tune), loader re-check. C7 PARTIAL-contrast — FlexGen writes KV/activations to disk; SipLLM does not (except explicit session save). C3 NONE. C4 NONE.
  • Lessons: (1) FlexGen's per-phase peak-memory equations (prefill vs decode separately) [PAPER p.17] are a checklist for src/mem_plan.cpp: confirm the prefill-phase peak (residual streams x ctx, attention scores) is priced at the worst prompt length, not only decode. (2) A published I/O- complexity bound (Thm 4.1) is the kind of formal statement SipLLM's planner lacks; a proof that the ledger upper-bounds RSS (modulo allocator slack) would strengthen C1.
  • Contribution opportunity: [HYPOTHESIS] FlexGen-style LP placement degrades to SipLLM's greedy order (head-stream -> ctx cap -> ring -> pin) when there is one compute device and batch 1; i.e. the greedy is optimal in that regime. Test: brute-force all (ctx, head mode, ring, n_pinned) for 3 models x 5 budgets and compare to the greedy's choice on measured tok/s.
Hermes (PIPELOAD) — Memory-Efficient Pipeline Inference for Large Models on Edge Devices
  • Cite: X. Han, Z. Cai, Y. Zhang, et al. (SJTU / Melbourne), ICCD 2024 per arXiv comments [not verified], arXiv:2409.04249, https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.04249. Read: FULL (8 pp.; pp.3-8 read as page images because text extraction was empty)
  • Problem: layer-wise load/compute pipelines on edge CPUs stall because loading a layer is ~10x slower than computing it, and they do not reduce memory [PAPER p.2-3, Fig. 3].
  • Mechanism:
    • PIPELOAD: m "Loading Agents" read layers from disk in parallel (agent i loads layers i, i+m, i+2m, ...), an Inference Agent computes in order, a Daemon Agent destroys each layer's memory right after use [PAPER p.4, Fig. 4-5].
    • When memory "is about to exceed or has exceeded the memory constraints", the Daemon Agent sends a STOP signal pausing loaders until memory frees [PAPER p.4] — reactive throttling, not a plan.
    • Layer Profiler (per-layer load time, compute time, memory) -> Pipeline Planner maps a memory constraint (MB) to the number of Loading Agents (e.g. 1000 MB -> 3, 4000 MB -> 8) by pre-running candidates -> Execution Engine picks the schedule "based on the current memory constraints of the edge device" [PAPER p.5, Fig. 6].
    • For GPT-style models, every token reloads every layer ("one loading and inference operation for each token") [PAPER p.7].
  • Memory regime: (a) operator-set "memory constraint" input to the planner: YES [PAPER p.5 Fig. 6b]. (b) covers model layer memory; embeddings/other layers outside the scheme [PAPER p.4]; no KV discussion. (c) external docker --memory limit + daemon stop signal [PAPER p.6, p.4]. (d) infeasible: not discussed. (e) CPU-only: YES ("do not require GPU usage") [PAPER p.7]. (f) "maximum memory occupation by the model" [PAPER p.6] — tool not named. (g) GPT-J FP32 12,354 MB run at 1,668.6 MB (0.135 ratio, ~7.4x) with 2 agents [PAPER p.6 Table III].
  • Hardware: Intel Xeon Gold 6248R server, docker limits 8 cores + memory [PAPER p.6]. No real edge device.
  • Headline (theirs): up to 4.24x faster and 86.7% less memory than PipeSwitch (BERT/ViT), 2.58x and 90.3% (GPT-style) [PAPER p.1]. GPT-J (4-token prompt, 8 output tokens) 51,003 ms at 2,000 MB -> 29,074 ms at 7,000 MB [PAPER p.7]; baseline (load-then-run) 31,331 ms [PAPER p.6 Table II].
  • Does NOT do: KV/whole-process pricing, refusal, quantized weights, long generations, real devices.
  • Pre-empts: C1 PARTIAL — CPU-only, operator memory constraint -> planner -> per-token layer streaming at ~7x model/footprint, with measured footprint. This is the closest *dense, CPU-only* match after FlexInfer. Survives: whole-process ledger, refusal, quantized GGUF, real phones, 25x ratio, decode-length workloads. C3/C4/C7 NONE.
  • Lessons: Parallel loaders striped across layers (i, i+m, ...) [PAPER p.4] is another way to raise storage queue depth — compare against per-tensor threads (FlexInfer) in src/loader.cpp; but see PowerInfer-2's warning that UFS has a single command queue.
  • Contribution opportunity: [HYPOTHESIS] planned (ledger) enforcement gives a tighter, stall-free ceiling than reactive throttling (Hermes' stop signal). Experiment: same budget, record RSS time series (100 ms sampling) — SipLLM flat vs reactive sawtooth.
NeuroPrefetcher — Storage-Aware Sparse LLM Inference via Delta Prefetching
  • Cite: N. Dhar, M. R. Islam, X. Zhang, et al. (Kennesaw State / Samsung), ICPP 2026, arXiv:2608.22643, DOI 10.1145/3832810.3832862. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-4, 6-10 skimmed for setup/results)
  • Problem: the "model-exceeds-memory" regime where storage is on the critical path; llama.cpp's demand paging thrashes [PAPER p.1-2].
  • Mechanism: one GPU predictor after layer 0 predicts MLP active sets for all later layers; the runtime fetches only the delta rows vs the resident set (82-85% of active neurons persist token-to-token) via io_uring into a staging buffer; dense prefix of d resident layers + sparse suffix with fixed K_max row buffers, d chosen as the largest prefix that fits the budget; weights repacked into 24.5 KiB gate/up/down records [PAPER p.1-4, p.6].
  • Memory regime: (a) budget = system memory constrained by the kernel mem= boot parameter, seven levels 11-17 GiB; "the memory available to inference is a deployment parameter rather than a hardware constant" [PAPER p.7]. (b) whole system. (c) external (boot parameter). (d) quality traded (sparsity) as memory tightens; 92-96% of dense accuracy [PAPER p.3, p.10]. (e) GPU (Jetson AGX Orin unified memory). (f) "memory for our process ranges from 9.0 GiB to 14.8 GiB" measured CUDA availability [PAPER p.7]. (g) ~1.2-1.5x (FP16 Mistral-7B 13.5 GiB vs 9-12 GiB available) [INFERENCE].
  • Headline (theirs): 7.9-12.0x over llama.cpp; 3.22 vs 0.34 tok/s at mem=14G; at 14 GiB cap of 20 inference systems only 4 produce output (NeuroPrefetcher 3.22, llama.cpp FP16 0.34, FlexGen all-disk 0.25, Ollama 0.07 tok/s); 6 fail with allocation failure [PAPER p.7 Table 4]; llama.cpp at mem=14G: 11,453 major faults/s, 77% iowait [PAPER p.2 Fig. 1].
  • Pre-empts: C1 NONE-to-weak (GPU, system-level boot limit, ~1.2x ratio, quality traded). Useful as independent evidence that llama.cpp mmap collapses under a memory cap and that most runtimes cannot run at all below model size [PAPER p.7]. C3 NONE. C4 PARTIAL (accuracy vs memory via d and sparsity).
  • Lessons: the 20-system "capability study at a memory cap" is a reviewer-proof evaluation format SipLLM should copy for its own paper (llama.cpp, MLC, ExecuTorch, MNN, LiteRT-LM under one cap).
PowerInfer-2 — Fast Large Language Model Inference on a Smartphone
  • Cite: Z. Xue, Y. Song, Z. Mi, et al. (SJTU IPADS), arXiv:2406.06282 v3 (Dec 2024), https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.06282. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-10)
  • Problem: phone inference of models larger than DRAM; UFS is far weaker than NVMe [PAPER p.1-2].
  • Mechanism: neuron-cluster abstraction; offline planner produces neuron plan + hardware plan; NPU for dense/hot clusters, CPU for sparse/cold; segmented neuron cache (fixed region for attention
    • KV, plus hot/cold regions); Gate-Up-Down bundles read as 24 KB (fp16) or 2x4 KB (int4) reads; neuron-cluster pipeline overlaps I/O with compute [PAPER p.5-8].
  • Memory regime: (a) "available memory" configured per experiment: FFN weights in DRAM limited to 50% (75% offload for 47B on Ace 2) [PAPER p.9]; sweep 7-19 GB for TurboSparse-Mixtral-47B [PAPER p.10]. (b) ITEMIZED at 7 GB: 1 GB non-FFN weights + 2.6 GB predictors + 2.7 GB FFN quant scales + 300 MB runtime = 6.6 GB, 400 MB neuron cache (1.8% of FFN) [PAPER p.10]. (c) own allocation. (d) not discussed. (e) NO — NPU+CPU. (f) allocation accounting, not RSS. (g) 47B (TurboSparse, ~sparse ReLU-fied) in 7 GB, ~3-4x [INFERENCE from model size].
  • Hardware: OnePlus 12 (SD 8 Gen 3, 24 GB) and OnePlus Ace 2 (SD 8+ Gen 1, 16 GB) [PAPER p.2, p.8].
  • Headline (theirs): 11.68 tok/s TurboSparse-Mixtral-47B; 24.6x over llama.cpp and 3.84x over LLMFlash at 50% FFN offload on OnePlus 12 [PAPER p.2, p.9]; 2.13 tok/s at 7 GB [PAPER p.10]. Their re-implementation of PowerInfer/LLMFlash at 50% FFN offload loses 89%/82% of decode speed [PAPER p.4 Table 2].
  • UFS facts (theirs): sequential 450 MB/s at 4 KB -> 4 GB/s at 512 KB; 4 KB random 1 GB/s within a 128 MB range, < 850 MB/s across 512 MB; I/O thread on big core 1,076 MB/s vs little core 762 MB/s; "UFS storage has a single command queue ... using multiple cores for I/O operations can degrade performance by up to 40%" [PAPER p.4 Table 1].
  • Pre-empts: C1 PARTIAL (phone, memory-capacity sweep, itemized memory breakdown) but needs NPU and a sparsified model, no operator ceiling/refusal. C3 NONE. C4 NONE. C7 NONE.
  • Lessons: (1) Do NOT blindly add I/O threads on phones: UFS single queue -> contention; pin the SipLLM prefetch thread (src/loader.cpp worker_) to a BIG core on Android and measure [PAPER p.4; HYPOTHESIS for SipLLM]. ActiveFlow does the opposite (little core) [ActiveFlow p.9] — a measurable disagreement in prior art. (2) Their 6.6 GB itemization is a precedent for SipLLM's ledger — cite it as related, not as a planner.
SwapMoE — Serving Off-the-shelf MoE-based Large Language Models with Tunable Memory Budget
  • Cite: R. Kong, Y. Li, Q. Feng, et al., ACL 2024, arXiv:2308.15030 v4, https://arxiv.org/abs/2308.15030. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-6)
  • Problem: serve off-the-shelf MoE models under a per-app memory budget without retraining [PAPER p.1].
  • Mechanism: keep a small set of important "Virtual Experts" in memory, route all tokens to them via masked gating; importance score from gate weights x activations; amortized async expert swaps; profiling-guided planner (genetic algorithm) maximizing accuracy s.t. E_memory(config) <= LIMIT_memory [PAPER p.3-5, Eq. 3].
  • Memory regime: (a) operator-set "tunable memory budget": YES [PAPER p.1, Eq. 3 p.4]. (b) expert memory per layer + model [PAPER p.4]. (c) planner + profiling. (d) budget met by APPROXIMATION (fewer experts used) — accuracy drops, never refused [PAPER p.3-5]. (e) GPU (Jetson Nano, AGX Orin) [PAPER p.5]. (f) "peak memory (GiB)" [PAPER p.2]. (g) Switch Transformer 14.2 GiB -> 4.7 GiB (3x) with Rouge-2 drop 0.041 [PAPER p.2].
  • Pre-empts: C1 PARTIAL for MoE (operator-tunable budget + planner), but approximate output and GPU. C3 NONE. C4 PARTIAL (accuracy vs memory budget curves, Fig. 8).
  • Contribution opportunity: [HYPOTHESIS] exact expert streaming under the same budget (SipLLM's Mixtral path: FfnGateExps/UpExps/DownExps roles in include/llm/loader.h) gives equal-quality output at lower tok/s; the frontier between "approximate but fast" and "exact but slow" is unmapped.
EdgeMoE — Empowering Sparse Large Language Models on Mobile Devices
  • Cite: R. Yi, L. Guo, S. Wei, et al., IEEE TMC (arXiv v2 Mar 2025), arXiv:2308.14352, https://arxiv.org/abs/2308.14352. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-4, 7-11)
  • Problem: MoE LLMs on edge devices — non-expert weights resident, experts on storage [PAPER p.1].
  • Mechanism: expert-wise bitwidth adaptation under a user accuracy-loss target P; predict-then-preload of next-layer experts; expert cache with an eviction score using frequency + layer index (beats FIFO/LRU/LFU in their Fig. 14) [PAPER p.2, p.4, p.7, p.10-11].
  • Memory regime: (a) "a memory budget M, specified either by users or the OS. The budget ranges from 1.5GB-3GB" [PAPER p.4]; "takes a memory budget and a tolerable accuracy loss as input from developers" [PAPER p.2]. (b) M sizes the EXPERT CACHE only ("a cache for expert weights in memory, sized by the memory budget M") [PAPER p.7]; non-expert weights are additional. (c) own buffer sizing. (d) not discussed; accuracy is a "soft goal" [PAPER p.4]. (e) BOTH: Raspberry Pi 4B CPU (8 GB, SD card storage) and Jetson TX2 GPU; Xiaomi 14 phone for MiniCPM-MoE-8x2B [PAPER p.8, p.11]. (f) peak memory footprint reported [PAPER p.8-9]. (g) memory savings 2.1x-38.6x vs IO-FREE [PAPER p.9] (the abstract's "1.05x-1.18x" [PAPER p.2] is inconsistent with p.9 — flagged, not resolved).
  • Pre-empts: C1 PARTIAL — an operator/OS budget on a CPU edge board (Raspberry Pi) with storage streaming; differs: budget is the expert cache only, quality is traded via bitwidth, MoE-only. C3 PARTIAL — custom eviction policy compared with FIFO/LRU/LFU, NOT with Belady [PAPER p.10-11]. C7 NONE.
  • Lessons: SD-card storage on a Pi is exactly the "e-waste-class storage" regime; EdgeMoE shows it works for experts. SipLLM should benchmark on slow storage (eMMC/SD) as well as NVMe/UFS.
STI — Turbocharge NLP Inference at the Edge via Elastic Pipelining
  • Cite: L. Guo, W. Choe, F. X. Lin (UVA), ASPLOS 2023, arXiv:2207.05022, https://arxiv.org/abs/2207.05022. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-4, 8-9)
  • Problem: BERT-class models (100s of MB) on phones: holding the model resident inflates an app's footprint (apps usually < 100 MB) and makes it a low-memory-kill victim; loading on demand is slow [PAPER p.1-3].
  • Mechanism: shard each layer into M shards x K bitwidth versions on flash; a small preload buffer holds bottom-layer shards; a two-stage planner picks a submodel meeting a target latency T and allocates per-layer I/O budgets by shard importance [PAPER p.2-4].
  • Memory regime: (a) operator chooses preload buffer |S| and target latency [PAPER p.4]. (b) preload buffer only (working layer separate). (c) planner. (d) accuracy reduced to meet latency. (e) CPU (Odroid-N2+) and GPU (Jetson Nano) [PAPER p.8 Table 2]. (f) preload memory 1 MB / 5 MB [PAPER p.9]. (g) model memory reduced by 1-2 orders of magnitude [PAPER p.1].
  • Pre-empts: C1 PARTIAL in spirit (operator memory knob + planner + layer streaming on edge) for encoder NLP models; no LLM decode, no whole-process ceiling, quality traded. C7 NONE.
  • Lessons: the low-memory-killer argument (big resident apps get recycled) is the strongest user-facing motivation for an operator ceiling on Android — cite STI p.1-3 for it.
SwapNet — Efficient Swapping for DNN Inference on Edge AI Devices Beyond the Memory Budget
  • Cite: K. Wang, J. Cao, Z. Zhou, Z. Li, IEEE TMC (arXiv Jan 2024), arXiv:2401.16757, https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.16757. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-8)
  • Problem: run DNNs larger than the per-task memory budget on Jetson-class devices [PAPER p.1-2].
  • Mechanism: partition a DNN into n blocks and swap them in order; choose n from the budget (m x s/n <= b for m blocks in flight); zero-copy swap-in that bypasses the page cache using unified memory addressing; model assembly by reference; multi-DNN budget allocation [PAPER p.4-7].
  • Memory regime: (a) per-DNN budget b allocated by a scheduler from a device budget [PAPER p.7]. (b) block weights in flight. (c) own partitioning. (d) n increases until it fits. (e) GPU (Jetson, unified memory). (f) memory usage measured on device. (g) DNNs needing 2.32x-5.81x the budget run at near-unconstrained latency [PAPER p.1].
  • Pre-empts: C1 PARTIAL for CNN/BERT-class DNNs on Jetson (operator budget -> block count) — the generic "run beyond the memory budget by swapping blocks" idea is not new. Not LLM decode, not CPU. C6 PARTIAL-related: on unified memory it avoids double-buffering via zero-copy (the page cache plus a GPU copy "doubles the peak memory cost per block") [PAPER p.4-6].
  • Lessons: page-cache double counting (read() copies into page cache then into the buffer) is identified as a peak-memory hazard [PAPER p.4]; same issue as LLM-in-a-flash p.16 — supports adding cache-bypass reads to FileBacking.
llama.cpp (practical baseline) — mmap loading and the --fit memory fitter
  • Cite: ggml-org/llama.cpp, master as fetched 2026-10-23. Files read: src/llama-mmap.cpp (via raw.githubusercontent, summarized by WebFetch with verbatim quotes), common/arg.cpp, common/common.h, common/common.cpp, common/fit.cpp (last commit touching it: 2026-09-26 per GitHub API), and tools/fit-params/README.md. Local copies: .../A-storage-offload/repo_snapshots/. Read: the cited files.
  • mmap path: mmap(NULL, size, PROT_READ, MAP_SHARED, fd, 0), MAP_POPULATE on Linux when prefetch is on; posix_fadvise(..., POSIX_FADV_SEQUENTIAL); POSIX_MADV_WILLNEED over eager ranges, POSIX_MADV_RANDOM over lazy ranges / NUMA; optional mlock (--mlock) with RLIMIT_MEMLOCK warnings; unmap_fragment unmaps unused ranges. Residency is decided by the kernel page cache: there is no process-level ceiling [repo src/llama-mmap.cpp].
  • --fit (DEFAULT ON: bool fit_params = true; // whether to fit unset model/context parameters to free device memory) [repo common/common.h:483]. Flags: -fit/--fit, -fitp/--fit-print, -fitt/--fit-target "target margin per device for --fit" (default 1024 MiB), -fitc/--fit-ctx "minimum ctx size that can be set by --fit option" (default 4096) [repo common/arg.cpp:2882-2940]. Implementation: a dry run (no_alloc = true) builds model + context and reads llama_get_memory_breakdown (model, context = KV, compute buffers) per device incl. host; for a CPU-only build (nd == 0) it compares projected host use with total host memory minus the margin, reduces n_ctx by interpolation down to the minimum, and otherwise throws "was unable to fit model into system memory by reducing context, abort" [repo common/fit.cpp:56-153, 316-327, 405-461]. With GPUs it also sets -ngl and moves expert tensors to CPU via -ot [repo tools/fit-params/README.md].
  • Memory regime: (a) budget = device/host TOTAL minus an operator margin — an operator can lower the effective ceiling by raising --fit-target, but it is expressed as headroom, not a ceiling. (b) model + KV + compute buffers (dry-run measured). (c) own accounting (dry run). (d) REFUSES (abort) if reducing ctx cannot fit. (e) CPU-only supported. (f) projected allocator sizes, not measured RSS. (g) model must FIT — weights are counted fully resident, so a model larger than the budget is refused rather than streamed.
  • Pre-empts: C1 PARTIAL and important — "price model+KV+compute, cut ctx, refuse with abort" is now mainline llama.cpp behaviour, default on, CPU-only included. What survives for SipLLM: a ceiling far BELOW model size, made feasible by streaming/partial residency, with an itemized report and measured RSS. Any SipLLM paper must cite --fit and must not claim "no runtime refuses an infeasible memory configuration".
  • Lessons: (1) llama.cpp prices via a dry-run allocation instead of formulas — this is more robust to arch drift than SipLLM's hand-written ledger in src/mem_plan.cpp (e.g. the hard-coded fixed.base = 24 * 1024 * 1024 at mem_plan.cpp:52). A dry-run "measure the allocator" pass at plan time would make the guarantee less dependent on constants. (2) Benchmark SipLLM vs llama.cpp with --fit on and --no-mmap, and with --mlock, to show the specific regime where llama.cpp aborts and SipLLM runs.
TPI-LLM — Serving 70B-scale LLMs Efficiently on Low-resource Edge Devices
  • Cite: Z. Li, W. Feng, M. Guizani, H. Yu (MBZUAI), arXiv:2410.00531 v1, https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.00531. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-2, 6-10, 15). Overlaps cluster C (multi-device).
  • Mechanism: tensor parallelism across home devices plus a per-device "sliding window memory scheduler" on a daemon thread that loads/unloads layer weights from disk, overlapping disk I/O with compute and allreduce; closed-form steady-state peak memory (Proposition 5, depends on window size w) [PAPER p.6-7].
  • Memory regime: window size w is the operator knob (default 2) [PAPER p.8]; peak footprint is analytic, with the caveat that "precise quantification" is "challenging" [PAPER p.15]; FP32 weights; CPU laptops; Llama 2-70B at 3.1 GB per device across 8 devices [PAPER p.1-2, p.8 Table 1-2]; a single device "makes it challenging to run even 3B models in a standalone mode" [PAPER p.9].
  • Pre-empts: C1 PARTIAL (bounded per-device memory via weight streaming, analytic peak) but multi-device and window-based, no operator byte ceiling/refusal. C2 — for cluster C.
  • Lesson: an analytic peak-memory proposition (their Prop. 5) is the paper-ready form SipLLM's ledger should take — "peak RSS <= sum(ledger) + epsilon" with epsilon measured.
Edge0 — The Other Half of the Memory Wall: Serving 35B MoEs from SSD with Trained Routing Prediction
  • Cite: Y. Lin, Y. Wang, R. Cai, et al. (AutoArk), arXiv:2609.18063 v2 (Sep 2026). Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-5, 7-11)
  • Mechanism: per-layer "prerouter" trained to predict next-layer routing one token ahead and used AS the routing (so staged set = routed set); experts mmap-streamed into a bounded pool; recovery LoRA restores quality lost to int4 + routing replacement [PAPER p.1-4].
  • Memory regime: no enforced limit — "No artificial memory limit is imposed (no mlock, no wired pages, no cgroup cap, no page-cache purge)" [PAPER p.7]. Reports BOTH "peak active memory" (MLX allocator peak) 2.9 GiB AND process RSS 4.91-6.39 GiB (RSS "counts the file-backed expert pages as well as the allocator"); "the rest of the machine is page cache, which is reclaimable" [PAPER p.8].
  • Hardware: Mac mini M4 Pro 24 GB (GPU via MLX), MacBook M2 16 GB [PAPER p.5, p.7].
  • Headline (theirs): 35B MoE (19.5 GB int4) at ~20 tok/s in 2.9 GiB active vs mlx-lm resident 3.9 tok/s at 18.2 GiB [PAPER p.5, p.10]; run-to-run spread +-40%, up to 2.3x across sessions on 16 GB [PAPER p.7].
  • Pre-empts: C1 NONE (no ceiling); C4 PARTIAL — shows a streamed BIG MoE beating a resident one at fixed hardware, i.e. a point on the iso-memory frontier (GPU, MoE, modified model).
  • Lessons: (1) honest dual reporting (allocator peak vs RSS vs page cache) is the standard SipLLM should meet; (2) +-40% variance under page-cache dynamics argues for SipLLM's explicit pread ring over mmap — a measurable advantage [HYPOTHESIS]: SipLLM's tok/s variance across sessions at a fixed budget is much lower than mmap-streaming engines.
mzCache — On-Device LLM Memory Management under Multitasking
  • Cite: H. Yu, M. Kim, J. Park, K. Lee (SNU / Berkeley), MobiCom 2026, arXiv:2609.01338, DOI 10.1145/3795866.3844495. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-4)
  • Problem/mechanism: Android multitasking evicts LLM weights/KV (5-7 GB footprint); mzCache splits LLM memory into fine-grained shared buffers, evicts later layers first, restores on CPU while GPU runs on resident front layers; llama.cpp-based Android app; 2.1-5.5x TTFT reduction [PAPER p.1-2]. Notes that mlock'd system services (e.g. Gemini Nano) avoid this at the cost of permanently occupied RAM [PAPER p.4].
  • Pre-empts: C1 NONE directly; it argues the memory available on phones is DYNAMIC (OS-driven), which challenges a static operator ceiling. [INFERENCE] SipLLM's fixed ceiling is complementary: a small, never-exceeded footprint is less likely to be evicted/killed; but SipLLM cannot shrink at runtime. [HYPOTHESIS] adding an online re-plan (lower budget -> unpin layers) would cover this.
Lever — Speculative LLM Inference on Smartphones
  • Cite: T. Wang, F. Li, Y. Sun, et al. (Tsinghua), arXiv:2605.16786 (May 2026). Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-4)
  • Mechanism: small resident draft model + flash-offloaded target; I/O- and compute-aware token-tree drafting; NPU batching [PAPER p.1-2]. Headline: 2.93x over flash-offloaded autoregressive inference, 1.50x over conventional speculative decoding [PAPER p.1-2].
  • Pre-empts: C4 PARTIAL (a resident small model + streamed large model combination at fixed memory).
  • Lesson (high impact): in SipLLM's streaming regime one weight pass costs the same I/O whether it verifies 1 or k tokens. SipLLM ships only prompt-lookup drafting (include/llm/spec_decoder.h, PromptLookupDecoder); a draft-model SpecDecoder priced as one more ledger line in src/mem_plan.cpp is the most direct way to lift 0.55 tok/s. LLMCad (below) is the 2023 precedent.
LLMCad — Fast and Scalable On-device Large Language Model Inference
  • Cite: D. Xu, W. Yin, X. Jin, et al. (PKU), arXiv:2309.04255, https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.04255. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-3, 9-11)
  • Mechanism: resident small "generator" LLM builds a token tree; large target LLM loaded under a memory budget verifies non-autoregressively; speculative generation runs only while target loading is below the budget [PAPER p.1-3, p.9-10]. llama.cpp backend on phones, PyTorch on IoT [PAPER p.10].
  • Memory regime: memory budgets swept 4-8 GB on Xiaomi 10 and 6/4 GB on Jetson TX2 [PAPER p.10]; enforcement mechanism not described in the parts read.
  • Headline (theirs): under a 4 GB budget on TX2, 6.12x/1.91x/1.25x over StdPL/SP/BLD; 4.72x over StdPL on Xiaomi 10 at 4 GB [PAPER p.10].
  • Pre-empts: C1 PARTIAL (budget-swept on phones, larger-than-memory target) — budget is an experimental knob, not a priced contract. C4 PARTIAL (small+large model collaboration at a budget).
SSD offload energy — SSD Offloading for LLM Mixture-of-Experts Weights Considered Harmful in Energy Efficiency
  • Cite: K. Kyung, S. Yun, J. H. Ahn (SNU), arXiv:2508.06978 (IEEE CAL-style, 4 pp.). Read: FULL
  • Finding: flash read energy ~102.4 pJ/b vs DDR5-7200 9.2 pJ/b vs HBM3 4.2 pJ/b; SSD-resident MoE weights make DeepSeek-R1 decode ~4.9x (up to ~12x) more energy per token than HBM and 3.1x more than CPU memory; SSD weight access ~80% of per-token energy; prefetching hides latency but not energy; SSD offload becomes energy-viable only if flash read energy improves ~10x [PAPER p.1-2]. Analytical model, datacenter config (GH-style NVLink, batch 1024) [PAPER p.1-2].
  • Pre-empts: C7 — directly CHALLENGES the "cheap storage reads for scarce RAM" framing: reads are cheap in $ and wear, not in joules. [INFERENCE] SipLLM's stream-every-layer regime reads the whole model per token (8B Q4 ~4.9 GB/token); at their 102.4 pJ/b that is ~4 J/token for flash reads alone — a datacenter-NVMe estimate, phone UFS unmeasured. [HYPOTHESIS] energy/token rises sharply as the budget shrinks; must be measured (ActiveFlow's voltage_now/current_now method) before any e-waste/sustainability claim.
LLM Inference in a Flash! (compute-in-flash)
  • Cite: S. Zhao, M. Kim, C. Hooper, et al. (UC Berkeley), arXiv:2609.16161 (Sep 2026). Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-5)
  • Relevant content: states that flash is suitable for static weights but has "limited write endurance (which is unsuitable for KV cache activations)"; quotes 143 TB of KV writes for a 65B model at 3 tok/s over 5 years and an SSD-KV system writing 1.98 TiB/day vs a 750 TBW drive rating; designs a read-only dictionary to cut KV writes [PAPER p.1-3, p.5].
  • Pre-empts: C7 PARTIAL-to-FULL on the insight: "weights read-only => no wear; KV writes => wear" is stated explicitly in 2026 prior art (and is textbook flash behaviour). SipLLM's read-only streaming is a correct engineering property, not a novel claim.
C3 group — residency policies evaluated against Belady
Towards MoE Deployment — Mitigating Inefficiencies in Mixture-of-Expert (MoE) Inference
  • Cite: H. Huang, N. Ardalani, A. Sun, et al. (Meta), arXiv:2303.06182 v2 (2023). Read: PARTIAL (p.9 + abstract)
  • "Expert Buffering": a per-GPU expert cache; "We further compare our caching policy with Belady's MIN, the theoretical optimal policy that requires information from the future ... the LIFO policy is better than FIFO, and with the load balancing ... our policy can obtain a cache miss rate very close to Belady's MIN" [PAPER p.9, Fig. 12b]. GPU serving (machine translation MoE).
  • Pre-empts: C3 FULL for "expert residency policy vs Belady" (2023, GPU).
Read-ME — Refactorizing LLMs as Router-Decoupled Mixture of Experts with System Co-Design
  • Cite: R. Cai, Y. Ro, G.-W. Kim, et al. (UT Austin / Qualcomm), NeurIPS 2024 per arXiv comments [not verified], arXiv:2410.19123. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-5, 9)
  • A pre-gating router decoupled from the backbone computes future expert references, enabling "Belady-inspired caching" that evicts argmax next-use (exact Belady when lookahead is complete); Table 4 compares Belady-inspired vs other policies' hit ratios across cache capacities [PAPER p.5, p.9].
  • Pre-empts: C3 FULL (Belady-optimal expert caching made realizable by architecture).
FlashMoE — Reducing SSD I/O Bottlenecks via ML-Based Cache Replacement for MoE Inference on Edge Devices
  • Cite: B. Kim, J. Lee, D. Han, et al. (KAIST), arXiv:2601.17063 v2 (Oct 2026). Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-3)
  • Experts offloaded to SSD under limited RAM on a user-grade desktop; ML cache policy blending recency and frequency "to approximate Belady's optimal policy"; LRU vs Belady heatmaps; up to 51% higher hit rate than LRU/LFU, up to 2.6x speedup [PAPER p.1-3]. Notes desktop DRAM is 16-64 GB and "available memory may be further limited" [PAPER p.2].
  • Pre-empts: C3 FULL for SSD-offloaded experts on edge-class hardware (hit rate vs Belady).
Kernel-managed expert tiering — Who Should Own the Expert Cache? Kernel-Managed Tiering for Trillion-Parameter MoE Inference
  • Cite: Y. Si, Y. Lin, D. Li, J. Zhang (Waterloo et al.), arXiv:2608.12103 v2 (Aug 2026). Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-2)
  • Finding: with capacity enforced three ways (balloon, cgroup wall, mem= boot), kernel page-cache recency serves essentially the same demand as an ORACLE static-frequency pinning policy; LRU reaches ~70% of Belady's optimum at trillion scale; "Belady bounds the remaining miss-reduction potential at roughly one third of the misses LRU still incurs"; balloon-based sizing "can overstate low-capacity device traffic by approximately 2x"; pread-based oracle-pinned arena 1.09-1.11x faster than page-cache path [PAPER p.1-2].
  • Pre-empts: C3 FULL (competitive comparison to Belady and to an oracle, for MoE experts) and it is an adversarial result for SipLLM's anti-mmap stance: the kernel page cache is near-oracle for expert residency, and explicit pinning wins only ~10%.
  • Lessons: (1) capacity-enforcement mechanism changes results (balloon vs cgroup vs mem=) — SipLLM's evaluation under caps must state and ideally cross-check the mechanism; (2) for SipLLM's MoE path, test --mmap vs pread under a cgroup cap before claiming pread residency is better.
Compact records — GPU-centric or peripheral (read for mechanism + headline only)
  • Mixtral offloading (A. Eliseev, D. Mazur, arXiv:2312.17238, PARTIAL pp.1-3): Mixtral-8x7B on T4 / RTX 3060 / RTX 3080 Mobile with experts in system RAM, LRU expert cache + speculative expert loading + mixed quantization, 2-3 tok/s [PAPER p.2]. C1 NONE (GPU memory fixed by device), C3 PARTIAL (LRU, no optimum comparison).
  • MoE-Infinity (L. Xue et al., arXiv:2401.14361 v3, ABSTRACT-ONLY + p.1 table): sparsity-aware expert cache for batch-1 personal machines; 3.1-16.7x TPOT improvement on an A5000 24 GB [PAPER p.1]. GPU. C1 NONE.
  • Fiddler (K. Kamahori et al., ICLR 2025, arXiv:2402.07033 v3, ABSTRACT-ONLY): CPU-GPU orchestration, computes experts on CPU instead of moving weights; 1.26x single-batch, 11.57x beam search [PAPER p.1]. GPU required. C1 NONE.
  • HOBBIT (P. Tang et al., arXiv:2411.01433, ABSTRACT-ONLY): mixed-precision expert offloading (load low-precision copies of cache-miss experts) for Jetson AGX Orin-class devices [PAPER p.1]. C1 NONE; quality traded on misses.
  • AdapMoE (S. Zhong et al., arXiv:2408.10284, ABSTRACT + p.5): sensitivity-based adaptive number of activated experts (-25% experts, 1.35x), prefetching, and cache allocation across layers solved as a knapsack by dynamic programming [PAPER p.1, p.5]. C3 PARTIAL (optimal cache *allocation*, not replacement vs Belady).
  • ProMoE (X. Song et al., arXiv:2410.22134 v3, ABSTRACT-ONLY): proactive expert prefetching from intermediate results; 2.20x prefill / 2.07x decode over offloading baselines [PAPER p.1]. GPU edge.
  • FluxMoE (Q. Liu et al., arXiv:2604.02715 v3, ABSTRACT-ONLY): streams expert weights while keeping the non-expert backbone GPU-resident; budget-aware residency planner (per search snippet; not read) — datacenter GPUs. C1 NONE.
  • SelectInfer (H. S. Kabakibo et al., arXiv:2607.18081, ABSTRACT-ONLY): offline-profiled task-specific neuron subsets loaded selectively — memory reduced at a quality cost. C1 NONE.
  • Neuralink (formerly Ripple) (T. Wang et al., arXiv:2410.19274 v3, ABSTRACT-ONLY): offline co-activation-aware neuron placement in flash for continuous reads on smartphones; 1.49x average end-to-end over prior flash-sparsity methods [PAPER p.1]. C7-adjacent (layout for storage). Lesson: storage layout is a first-class lever; SipLLM's IL4 repacking (interleave_q4k) is a compute layout — a storage-order layout (e.g. ActiveFlow's cross-layer channel order) is untested.
  • PowerInfer (Y. Song et al., arXiv:2312.12456 v2, PARTIAL p.1-2, p.9): hot neurons on GPU, cold on CPU; ILP placement with per-unit memory capacity constraint (Ineq. 6) [PAPER p.9]; up to 11.69x over llama.cpp on RTX 4090 [PAPER p.1-2]. Capacity = device memory, GPU required. C1 NONE.
  • Deja Vu (Z. Liu et al., ICML 2023, arXiv:2310.17157, ABSTRACT-ONLY): contextual sparsity predictors for attention heads/MLP; GPU latency focus, no memory ceiling. Background for sparsity- based offloading only. C1 NONE.
  • InfiniGen (W. Lee et al., OSDI 2024, arXiv:2406.19707, PARTIAL (p.1, p.11)): speculative KV prefetch from CPU memory for offloaded KV; evaluated under "80% memory limit of a full KV cache" with FIFO / LRU / counter-based victim selection compared [PAPER p.11]. C5 territory (cluster with KV) — noted, not assessed.
  • ZeRO-Infinity (S. Rajbhandari et al., SC 2021, arXiv:2104.07857, ABSTRACT-ONLY): NVMe offload for TRAINING extreme-scale models on GPU clusters. Peripheral; cited only as the origin of NVMe-tier offloading. C1 NONE.
  • LOIP (M. Sun et al., arXiv:2512.21835 v3): multi-device offloading pipeline on edge — downloaded, title verified, left to cluster C (C2).
k-bit scaling laws — The case for 4-bit precision: k-bit Inference Scaling Laws (for C4)
  • Cite: T. Dettmers, L. Zettlemoyer, ICML 2023, arXiv:2212.09720 v2. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-2)
  • 35,000+ experiments, 19M-176B params, 3-8-bit: "4-bit precision is almost universally optimal for total model bits and zero-shot accuracy" — e.g. "a 30B 8-bit model and a 60B 4-bit model have the same number of bits but may have very different zero-shot accuracies" [PAPER p.1-2].
  • Pre-empts: C4 PARTIAL — the iso-memory frontier over (model size x bit-width) exists for RESIDENT weights, scoring accuracy only. It does not include residency/streaming or time.
  • Contribution opportunity: [HYPOTHESIS] once streaming decouples model bits from RAM, the optimal point at a fixed RSS moves from "largest 4-bit model that fits" to "largest model whose streamed tok/s clears the task's latency floor"; measure accuracy and tok/s for {1B, 3B, 8B, 13B} x {Q4_K, Q8_0} x budgets {0.25, 0.5, 1, 2 GB} on one phone and one laptop.
3. Claim verdicts (cluster A scope: C1 primary, C7, plus C3 and C4)
C1 — Hard ceiling — verdict: NARROWED

Every individual conjunct of C1 has prior art; the conjunction was not found.

ConjunctClosest prior art (evidence)Status

KV and activationsFlexGen LP over gpu/cpu/disk peaks incl. weights, KV, activations [2303.06865 p.6, p.17]; llama.cpp --fit dry-run of model + context(KV) + compute [repo common/fit.cpp:56-153]; ActiveFlow M = M_cl + M_cache + M_kv [p.6]; PowerInfer-2 itemization [p.10]DONE before (FlexGen on GPU; llama.cpp on CPU but only for models that fit)

Surviving form (state exactly this): an exact (budget-invariant output), CPU-only runtime that takes an operator-set ceiling on WHOLE-PROCESS peak memory (weights + KV at the full chosen context

  • scratch + logits + base), plans every knob to it with an itemized ledger, refuses infeasible

budgets with an itemized report, and runs models up to ~25x larger than the ceiling — validated by measured peak RSS on real commodity phones and laptops. Prior art covers each piece; none found combines weights-below-budget streaming with whole-process pricing and refusal. Threats to the surviving form: (i) FlexInfer is one engineering step away (add KV/base terms to Algorithm 1); (ii) llama.cpp --fit already prices and refuses on CPU, so the novelty is only the "model much larger than the ceiling" regime; (iii) SipLLM measures RSS, which excludes the page cache its preads populate (LLM in a flash p.16 explicitly calls block-cache memory "additional memory in DRAM beyond what is allocated for the model"); a reviewer will ask for cgroup memory.peak (which charges page cache) or cache-bypassed reads; (iv) the ledger's process base is a hard-coded 24 MB (src/mem_plan.cpp:52) — the "guarantee" is only as good as that constant per OS.

C7 — Flash-friendly read-only streaming — verdict: DEAD as a research claim (survives as an engineering property)
  • Closest prior art: "LLM Inference in a Flash!" states flash suits static weights but has "limited write endurance (which is unsuitable for KV cache activations)" and designs around KV writes [2609.16161 p.1-2, p.5]; the property is basic NAND physics. Counter-examples that DO write: FlexGen spills KV/activations to SSD (ctod bandwidth in its cost model) [2303.06865 p.5-6].
  • Counter-evidence to the economic framing: flash reads cost ~102.4 pJ/b vs 9.2 pJ/b for DDR5; SSD-resident weights raise decode energy/token ~4.9x (up to ~12x) [2508.06978 p.1-2]. "Storage is cheap" holds for $/GB and wear, not for joules/token.
  • What survives: SipLLM's inference path performs zero storage writes (except explicit session save) — verifiable with iostat//proc/diskstats write counters during a run. Worth one sentence in a paper, not a contribution.
C3 — Residency science vs Belady — verdict: DEAD for expert caches; trivially closed for dense layers
  • Closest prior art: expert caches compared with Belady's MIN [2303.06182 p.9]; Belady-inspired caching via pre-gating [2410.19123 p.5, p.9]; ML policy approximating Belady for SSD-offloaded experts on edge [2601.17063 p.1-3]; LRU ~70% of Belady and near-oracle kernel recency for experts under three capacity-enforcement mechanisms [2608.12103 p.1-2]; EdgeMoE custom eviction vs FIFO/LRU/LFU [2308.14352 p.10-11].
  • Dense layers: SipLLM streams layers in a strict cycle. [INFERENCE + simulation] On a cyclic reference string with k weight slots, LRU hits 0%, Belady hits ~(k-1)/(n-1), and static pinning of k-1 layers + 1 streaming slot hits (k-1)/n — within a factor n/(n-1) of optimal (simulated: n=32,k=8: MIN 0.222 vs static 0.219; script /Users/mobile/Desktop/Dev/sipllm/.claude-artifacts/research/A-storage-offload/scripts/belady_cyclic.py). This is the textbook looping-pattern result; it gives SipLLM's static pinning a one-line optimality argument, but no research contribution.
  • Narrow residue (weak): competitive analysis where the residency cache competes with KV/ctx inside one whole-process ceiling (cache bytes vs context length). Not found, but likely judged incremental.
C4 — Iso-memory frontier — verdict: OPEN in the specific form; PARTIAL frontiers exist
  • Closest prior art: k-bit scaling laws (iso-model-bits accuracy frontier, resident only) [2212.09720 p.1-2]; ActiveFlow quality-vs-memory Pareto along the sparsity axis of one model [2504.08378 p.2, p.10-11]; SwapMoE / NeuroPrefetcher accuracy-vs-budget curves [2308.15030 Fig. 8; 2608.22643 p.9-10]; Edge0's streamed-35B-MoE vs resident point on one machine [2609.18063 p.5]; LLMCad / Lever small+large model collaboration at a budget [2309.04255 p.10; 2605.16786 p.1-2].
  • Not found: a frontier at FIXED measured RSS across model size x quantization x residency (streamed large vs resident small), scored as task quality per second, on CPU-only edge hardware. Searched: queries 1, 4, 7, 9 in §1.2 plus the papers above. Cluster B (phone runtimes) may find more.
4. Top lessons for SipLLM (ranked by expected impact)
  1. Speculative decoding with a resident draft model — in the streaming regime one weight pass costs the same I/O for 1 or k tokens; Lever reports 2.93x over flash-offloaded decoding [2605.16786 p.1-2], LLMCad 4.7-6.1x under 4 GB budgets [2309.04255 p.10]. SipLLM only has PromptLookupDecoder (include/llm/spec_decoder.h). Add a draft-model SpecDecoder, priced as a ledger line in src/mem_plan.cpp. Biggest lever on the 0.55 tok/s regime. [HYPOTHESIS for SipLLM]
  2. Balanced (per-layer-slice) pinning instead of contiguous whole layers — plan_and_pin_layers() (src/loader.cpp:192-230) pins layers [0, n) = FlexInfer's "w/o Balance" baseline; balanced locking gave +56.8-83.3% at mid budgets [2503.03777 p.5-6]. Pin the same tensor roles in every layer so each streamed layer costs equal I/O and the prefetch never stalls.
  3. Make the RSS claim robust to the page cache — add a cache-bypass mode to FileBacking (include/llm/file_backing.h: F_NOCACHE on Apple, O_DIRECT or POSIX_FADV_DONTNEED after read on Linux/Android) and report cold-cache numbers plus cgroup v2 memory.peak alongside /usr/bin/time RSS [2312.11514 p.16; 2401.16757 p.4-6; 2609.18063 p.8]. State the capacity- enforcement mechanism (cgroup vs mem=), since balloon-style caps can overstate traffic ~2x [2608.12103 p.2]. This directly defends C1.
  4. Storage queue depth, device-aware — the prefetch worker is one thread issuing serial per-role preads (src/loader.cpp worker_loop/fill_slot). NVMe/SSD: parallel per-tensor reads help (FlexInfer one thread per tensor [p.3]; LLM in a flash 32 threads [p.7]; io_uring in ActiveFlow [p.9]). UFS phones: single command queue, multi-core I/O can lose up to 40%, and a big core reads 1,076 vs 762 MB/s on a little core [2406.06282 p.4]. Experiment: threads {1,2,4} x core {big,little} on the Dimensity 900 and M3.
  5. Measure energy/token before any sustainability claim — flash reads are ~11x DDR5 energy per bit [2508.06978 p.2]; ActiveFlow measures on unrooted Android via voltage_now/current_now at 0.5 s [2504.08378 p.9]. Add to scripts/bench*.sh (no runtime dependency).
  6. Prefill-phase and dry-run pricing — check src/mem_plan.cpp against FlexGen's per-phase peak equations [2303.06865 p.17]; consider llama.cpp-style dry-run measurement of the allocator [repo common/fit.cpp:56-153] to replace hand-set constants such as the 24 MB base.
  7. Evaluation format — copy NeuroPrefetcher's "capability study at a memory cap" across many runtimes [2608.22643 p.7 Table 4], with llama.cpp --fit on/off, --mlock, --no-mmap.
  8. Slow-storage tier — test on SD/eMMC as EdgeMoE did on a Raspberry Pi [2308.14352 p.8]; this is the honest "e-waste" storage regime.
5. Open questions (unresolved)
  1. Does any 2025-2026 paper outside this cluster combine whole-process pricing + refusal with below-model-size streaming? Not found here; cluster B (phone runtimes: MNN, ExecuTorch, LiteRT-LM, MLC) should check whether any of them prices KV and refuses.
  2. FlexInfer's KV handling: the paper never mentions KV; whether its implementation counts KV in the budget could only be settled from its code (not found/linked in the PDF).
  3. EdgeMoE's abstract (1.05x-1.18x memory reduction) contradicts its own p.9 (2.1x-38.6x); unresolved.
  4. What is SipLLM's peak memory when page cache is charged (cgroup memory.peak) at budget 256 MB for the 8B model? Unmeasured — the single most likely reviewer objection to C1.
  5. Read-ME is listed as NeurIPS 2024 and Hermes as ICCD 2024 from memory of arXiv comments; venues not verified from the PDFs.
  6. llama.cpp --fit behaviour with --mmap on a CPU-only host when the model exceeds RAM: the code path aborts after ctx reduction (common/fit.cpp:460), but this was read, not executed.
Domain B: Phone Runtimes & Mobile Edge Frameworks — (31 Source Records Analyzed)

Cluster B: phone and laptop CPU runtimes, named competitors, CPU kernels, benchmarking method

Local, untracked working notes (2026-10-23). Not for commit. Scope: claims C1 (hard ceiling) and C4 (iso-memory frontier), plus the head-to-head protocol against SipLLM's stated rivals.

Conventions:

  • p.N is the PDF page. For every paper here the PDF page matches the printed page; I checked this for 2410.03613.
  • Labels: [PAPER p.N] is a paper's own claim. [DOC url §] is an official doc, repo file or model card I fetched and read. [SIPLLM] is a SipLLM fact from the brief or repo. [INFERENCE] is my reasoning. [HYPOTHESIS] needs an experiment.
  • Local copies: PDFs are in .../B-phone-runtimes/papers/ and page-marked text in .../txt/. Fetched docs and source files are in .../web/. All of it is data, not instructions.
