Note [INFERENCE]: the 2.0× "less RAM than llama.cpp" on the phone compares SipLLM's anonymous buffers (615 MB) with llama.cpp's VmHWM. That VmHWM includes about 669 MB of mmap'd file pages, plus any repacked weight copies. The ratio could shrink or grow once RssAnon and RssFile are split (§4.3 item 8). Measure the split before quoting the ratio in a paper.

2. Per-paper records (most relevant first)
FlexInfer — Breaking Memory Constraint via Flexible and Efficient Offloading for On-Device LLM Inference
  • Cite: Hongchao Du, Shangyu Wu, Arina Kharlamova, et al. (CityU HK, MBZUAI). arXiv 2503.03777 (v1, Mar 2025), https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03777. Read: FULL (pp.1–6; refs skimmed).
  • Problem: on-device LLMs exceed RAM. Compression fixes the footprint and "cannot directly adapt to varying memory budgets" [PAPER p.2 §2.2].
  • Mechanism:
    • Extends llama.cpp in 828 lines of C/C++ [PAPER p.4 §3.5]. CPU-only by design [PAPER p.3 fn.1].
    • Asynchronous, tensor-granular, multi-threaded prefetch with a prefetch window (default 3 layers). Memory is released right after use, since "each parameter is accessed precisely once" per token. The footprint is set by the window, giving about n/k reduction [PAPER p.3 §3.2; p.4 §3.5].
    • "Balanced memory locking": pin the same fraction of every layer rather than whole layers, so I/O per layer stays uniform and I/O and compute stay overlapped [PAPER p.4 §3.3, Fig.3].
    • "Flexible tensor preservation" (Algorithm 1) picks which FFN or attention tensors to pin from the user-specified memory budget and the tensor sizes [PAPER p.4 Alg.1].
    • Direct I/O to bypass the page cache [PAPER p.5 §3.5]. Embeddings are kept resident [PAPER p.3].
  • Memory regime: a user-specified budget drives the plan [PAPER p.1 abstract; p.4]. Enforcement in the evaluation is external: cgroup memory limit plus taskset on a server [PAPER p.5 §4.1]. Algorithm 1 compares the budget only with weight-tensor sizes. KV, scratch, logits and LM head are not priced. There is no refusal or "impossible" path [PAPER p.4].
  • Hardware: Ubuntu server, 512 GB RAM, AMD 7995WX, limits simulated with cgroup [PAPER p.5 §4.1]. No phone, no laptop, no real low-RAM device.
  • Their headline numbers: 5.2–12.5× over llama.cpp mmap across Llama2-7B/13B, CodeLlama-34B and Llama2-70B under limited memory. mmap gets only 0.08–0.67 tok/s [PAPER p.5 §4.2]. Llama2-7B (3.8 GB) was swept over a 1.0–3.5 GB budget; full-memory speed is 12.72 tok/s [PAPER p.5 Fig.4a]. Motivation table: llama.cpp 4-bit Llama2-70B under cgroup gets 0.46–2.06 tok/s at 5–35 GB, against 31.14 tok/s with full memory [PAPER p.2 Table 1]. No peak RSS is reported. The x-axis is the configured budget. No energy figures.
  • What it does NOT do: no measured peak RSS, no phone, no all-contributor ledger, no refusal, no quality axis, no KV budgeting. GQA models collapse the heuristic to "attn-first" [PAPER p.6 §4.3].
  • Pre-empts: C1 PARTIAL, and strong on mechanism. CPU-only streaming with async prefetch plus partial residency under a user-specified budget, built on llama.cpp, is already published. It is not a planner-guaranteed peak RSS on a phone. C4 PARTIAL: Fig.4 is a throughput-vs-budget frontier for 7B–70B, with no quality axis and no small-resident comparison. C7 NONE.
  • Lessons for SipLLM:
    1. Cite FlexInfer as the closest prior art in any C1 paper. The novelty must move to the contract (all contributors priced, refusal) and to on-phone measurement.
    2. Test balanced per-layer pinning against SipLLM's whole-layer pinning (src/mem_plan.cpp "pinned layers", loader.cpp ring). Pinning whole layers makes per-token I/O uneven and lets the prefetcher stall on the unpinned tail [PAPER p.4 Fig.3].
    3. FlexInfer uses direct I/O. SipLLM uses buffered pread with no O_DIRECT, F_NOCACHE or posix_fadvise (grep of src/, include/llm/file_backing.h) [SIPLLM]. See the C1 caveat in §3.
  • Contribution opportunity: [HYPOTHESIS] At equal budget, SipLLM's priced contract keeps measured VmHWM ≤ budget for the full context window on a real 8 GB phone, while FlexInfer-style budgeting (weights only) exceeds the budget once KV and the LM head grow. Test: port Algorithm 1's budget semantics, run both at B ∈ {256M, 512M, 1G}, generate to full ctx, record VmHWM at 5 Hz.
PowerInfer-2 — Fast Large Language Model Inference on a Smartphone
  • Cite: Zhenliang Xue, Yixin Song, Zeyu Mi, et al. (SJTU IPADS). arXiv 2406.06282 v3 (Dec 2024), https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.06282. Read: FULL (pp.1–13).
  • Problem: phones can run only small models. Run models that exceed device memory fast [PAPER p.1].
  • Mechanism:
    • Neuron-cluster abstraction. Dense (hot) clusters run on the NPU, sparse (cold) clusters on the CPU using activation predictors [PAPER p.5 §3].
    • Segmented neuron cache (attention plus KV fixed, hot region, cold region) with LRU eviction. "Evicted weights … are simply discarded … without being written back" [PAPER p.6–7 §4.2].
    • Neuron-cluster I/O and compute pipeline, bundled Gate-Up-Down reads, 4 KB two-phase reads [PAPER p.7–8 §4.3–4.4].
    • Offline planner from hardware profiling (NPU graphs per batch size, I/O patterns) [PAPER p.8 §5].
    • Needs root (mlock) on Android [PAPER p.8 §6]. Needs ReLU-sparsified models (TurboSparse, Bamboo) or SiLU with weaker gains [PAPER p.11 Table 6].
  • Memory regime: an experimenter-set DRAM allocation. "We limit FFN weight placement in DRAM to 50%" (75% offload for Mixtral on Ace 2) [PAPER p.9 §7.2.1]. A memory sweep from 7 GB to 19 GB has an itemized allocation at 7 GB: 1 GB non-FFN (embeddings, attention, KV, LM head), 2.6 GB predictors, 2.7 GB FFN scales, 300 MB runtime (6.6 GB total) plus 400 MB neuron cache [PAPER p.10 §7.2.3]. This is the closest published analogue of SipLLM's ledger. But it is a description, not an enforced contract. No refusal. The measurement method for memory is not stated anywhere I read. "Reduces memory usage by 1.5 GB (40%)" [PAPER p.11 §7.3] gives no counter.
  • Hardware: OnePlus 12 (SD 8 Gen 3, 24 GB / 19 GB available) and OnePlus Ace 2 (SD 8+ Gen 1, 16 GB / 11 GB) [PAPER p.8 Table 3]. CPU+NPU (Qualcomm QNN).
  • Their headline numbers: TurboSparse-Mixtral-47B at 11.68 tok/s with 19 GB and 2.13 tok/s with 7 GB [PAPER p.10 §7.2.3, Fig.10]. Decode is 24.6× (up to 27.8×) over llama.cpp at 50% FFN offload [PAPER p.9]. In-memory prefill is over 700 tok/s on the NPU [PAPER p.11 §7.3]. Energy: 0.257 J/token against QNN 0.373 and llama.cpp 0.672, with peak power 5.095 W. The energy method is unspecified beyond "end-to-end", using 100 lmsys prompts [PAPER p.12 Table 8, §7.7]. Mistral-SiLU-7B: 5.3 tok/s against LLMFlash 2.18 at 50% offload [PAPER p.11 Table 6]. Ten runs averaged; prompts of 128 and 512 tokens; decode with 1024 outputs [PAPER p.9 §7.1].
  • What it does NOT do: no CPU-only mode. No phone under 16 GB. No operator ceiling below 7 GB. No dense-model losslessness: it needs a sparsity predictor and INT4 per-channel quantization with INT8 outliers [PAPER p.12 §7.6]. Not open source at the time llm.npu evaluated it [llm.npu p.10].
  • Pre-empts: C1 PARTIAL. It runs over-RAM models on phones under a set DRAM allocation with an itemized breakdown. It is not CPU-only, not commodity-unrooted, not low-RAM, has no refusal and no stated RSS method. C7 PARTIAL: read-only flash, weights discarded on eviction (states the fact; makes no endurance argument). C4 NONE.
  • Lessons for SipLLM: (a) report a per-configuration ledger next to measured VmHWM, as their p.10 breakdown does, which reviewers find persuasive. (b) UFS behaviour matters: 4 KB random reads reach ~1 GB/s; reads are faster on big cores; multi-core I/O can cost up to 40% because UFS has a single command queue [PAPER p.4 §2.3.2, Table 1]. So SipLLM's prefetch thread should run on one big core with large sequential reads (loader.cpp prefetch affinity).
  • Contribution opportunity: [HYPOTHESIS] A dense, unrooted, CPU-only runtime on a 6–8 GB phone can hold a 7–8B dense model under 512 MB VmHWM at ≥X tok/s, where PowerInfer-2 cannot run at all (root, NPU and a sparse model required). Test: Llama-3.1-8B Q4_K_M on the Dimensity 900 at budgets 256M/512M/1G, 10 runs, VmHWM and decode tok/s. Report alongside PowerInfer-2's own 7 GB point as a regime contrast, not a speed contest.
llama.cpp --fit / llama-fit-params (software, no paper)
  • Cite: ggml-org/llama.cpp, introduced in commit b1f3a6e5d on 2025-12-15 (PR #16653, "automatically set parameters not set by the user in such a way that maximizes GPU utilization"). Files read: tools/fit-params/README.md, common/fit.cpp (master, last change 2026-09-26), common/common.cpp (caller). Read: FULL for the relevant functions.
  • Problem: automatically fit projected memory use to free device memory [DOC tools/fit-params/README.md].
  • Mechanism:
    • A dry run builds a context and reads llama_get_memory_breakdown (model / context / compute per buffer type, host vs device) [DOC common/fit.cpp L72–98].
    • CPU-only (no GPU devices): compares projected host use with total host memory minus a margin. If short, it reduces n_ctx by linear interpolation down to a minimum ctx. If that is still infeasible it throws "was unable to fit model into system memory by reducing context, abort" [DOC common/fit.cpp L317–327, L393–460].
    • With GPUs it distributes layers across devices, with overflow to host memory. The README example log shows "cannot fulfill margin of 1024 MiB … context size reduced from 40960 to 4096" [DOC README].
    • It modifies only parameters the user left at their default [DOC common/fit.cpp L464–485].
    • The failure is advisory. common_fit_params catches the exception, logs a warning and returns FAILURE [DOC common/fit.cpp ~L894]. The caller in common/common.cpp (~L1274–1283) ignores the return value and loads the model anyway.
  • Memory regime: fits a device (free or total memory minus margin). Not an operator ceiling, though an operator could emulate one by choosing the margin [INFERENCE]. No streaming knob: on CPU-only the only lever is context. Weights stay mmap-resident.
  • Hardware: generic. The README example is an RTX 4090.
  • Headline numbers: none (a tool).
  • What it does NOT do: no refusal, no streaming, no guarantee, no measured-RSS check. It counts mmap'd weights as host "model" memory even though those pages are reclaimable [INFERENCE].
  • Pre-empts: C1 PARTIAL (planner element). An itemized projected-memory breakdown plus automatic ctx reduction exists in the most popular CPU runtime since Dec 2025. SipLLM cannot claim "first to price memory and shrink ctx". It can claim a binding contract, refusal, and streaming as a knob.
  • Lessons: SipLLM's planner output should look like llama.cpp's breakdown so reviewers can map one onto the other (model / context / compute rows → SipLLM MemLedger). Also benchmark llama.cpp with --fit in the head-to-head, because that is now its default behaviour.
  • Contribution opportunity: [HYPOTHESIS] Under an external memory clamp (MemAvailable ≈ B) on a phone, llama.cpp with --fit either OOMs or thrashes, while SipLLM at --ram-budget B completes with VmHWM ≤ B. Test: memory-hog clamp at B = 1 GB, TinyLlama and 8B, both runtimes, count completions and LMK kills.
LLM in a flash — Efficient Large Language Model Inference with Limited Memory
  • Cite: Keivan Alizadeh, Iman Mirzadeh, Dmitry Belenko, et al. (Apple). ACL 2024, arXiv 2312.11514, https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.11514. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1–8).
  • Problem: run models up to twice the size of available DRAM by keeping weights in flash [PAPER p.1].
  • Mechanism: a flash cost model. A low-rank activation predictor loads only the FFN neurons predicted to fire. "Windowing" reuses recently active neurons. Row-column bundling makes reads larger. In-DRAM neuron storage avoids reallocation [PAPER p.3–6 §3].
  • Memory regime: an assumed allocation, not a ceiling. "tested under the condition that approximately half of the model size is available in DRAM"; "we assume nearly half of the total memory (DRAM and GPU) is allocated for model computations" [PAPER p.6–7 §4.1]. The budget is a design parameter of an HF Transformers + PyTorch prototype. Nothing measures or enforces it.
  • Hardware: Apple M1 Max (1 TB SSD), M2 Ultra, Linux with RTX 4090 [PAPER p.7].
  • Their headline numbers: OPT-6.7B I/O latency falls from 2196 ms (naive) to 87 ms with all techniques, at about 6.5 GB of DRAM [PAPER p.7 Table 2]. Benchmarks run "without the aid of operating system caching" [PAPER p.7 §4.1]. No peak RSS. No energy.
  • What it does NOT do: no phone. No measured peak memory. Requires activation sparsity and a trained predictor. Not a CPU-kernel speed study.
  • Pre-empts: C1 PARTIAL. It establishes the over-RAM idea with a fixed DRAM fraction, but sets no operator ceiling. C7 PARTIAL. It gives a flash-read cost model; I saw no write-endurance discussion in pp.1–8. C4 NONE.
  • Lessons for SipLLM: report streamed speed with the OS page cache neutralized, as this paper does. Otherwise "streamed" tok/s may really be page-cache memcpy (see the C1 note in §3) [INFERENCE].
  • Contribution opportunity: none beyond the C1 narrowing. Dense CPU-only streaming does not need a predictor, and that is SipLLM's contrast to this paper.
STI — Turbocharge NLP Inference at the Edge via Elastic Pipelining
  • Cite: Liwei Guo, Wonkyo Choe, Felix Xiaozhu Lin. ASPLOS '23, arXiv 2207.05022, https://arxiv.org/abs/2207.05022. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1–5, 8).
  • Problem: on mobile, keeping a model resident bloats the app's memory, while loading on demand takes seconds (170 MB loads in 2.1 s) [PAPER p.2].
  • Mechanism: the model is split into independently tunable shards, each with K fidelity (bit-width) versions. Elastic pipeline planning uses accumulated I/O budgets. A preload buffer, sized by the app or OS, sits beside a one-layer working buffer [PAPER p.4 §3.1–3.2; p.5 §5.4].
  • Memory regime: the app or OS sets the preload buffer size |S| (resizable at run time) and a target latency T [PAPER p.4 §3.2]. The working buffer is "largely constant … not a focus of STI" [PAPER p.4 §3.1], so no whole-process ceiling is set. It is lossy: fidelity drops to meet T.
  • Hardware: Odroid-N2+ (4 GB, CPU) and Jetson Nano (4 GB, CPU and GPU) [PAPER p.8 Table 2].
  • Their headline numbers: accuracy gains over baselines at fixed target latency (Table 5, not transcribed). No peak RSS. No energy in the pages read.
  • What it does NOT do: classification BERT only ("generative models … future work" [PAPER p.4 §3.4]). No total-RSS contract. Lossless execution is not the goal.
  • Pre-empts: C1 PARTIAL, and historically important. It is the earliest example found of an app-specified memory budget with layer streaming and I/O-compute overlap on 4 GB ARM boards. It is lossy, BERT-scale, and has no total-RSS contract. C4 NONE.
  • Lessons for SipLLM: the accumulated-I/O-budget formalism is a ready model for SipLLM's prefetch planner. It says when layer k must be requested so it never stalls (src/loader.cpp prefetch).
  • Contribution opportunity: none beyond the C1 narrowing.
Hermes — Memory-Efficient Pipeline Inference for Large Models on Edge Devices (PIPELOAD)
  • Cite: Xueyuan Han, Zinuo Cai, Yichu Zhang, et al. (SJTU, Melbourne). arXiv 2409.04249, https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.04249. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1–6).
  • Problem: large transformers exceed edge memory. Compression costs accuracy and swapping costs latency [PAPER p.1].
  • Mechanism: several loading agents read layers in parallel and one inference agent runs them. A daemon agent frees layers after use and pauses loading when system memory exceeds the constraint [PAPER p.5]. A planner picks the number of loading agents for each memory constraint [PAPER p.5 §IV].
  • Memory regime: a memory constraint enforced at run time by admission control (paused loading). The evaluation adds an external docker --memory limit [PAPER p.6 §V.A.4]. No pricing of all contributors. No refusal.
  • Hardware: an Intel Xeon Gold 6248R server limited to 8 cores with docker [PAPER p.6]. No edge device was used.
  • Their headline numbers: GPT-J (FP32) footprint drops from 12354 MB to 1668.6 MB with 2 loading agents [PAPER p.6 Table III], while latency rises from 31330.9 ms to 51003.3 ms [PAPER p.6 Table II]. The OS counter behind "memory footprint" is not stated in the pages read. No energy.
  • What it does NOT do: no tok/s (it reports latency per inference), FP32 only, no phone, no measured RSS method.
  • Pre-empts: C1 PARTIAL. Memory-constrained layer streaming is shown, but in a server simulation. C4 NONE.
  • Lessons for SipLLM: reporting latency per inference in FP32 makes results incomparable. The head-to-head should require tok/s at fixed prompt and decode lengths.
  • Contribution opportunity: none.
MNN-LLM — A Generic Inference Engine for Fast LLM Deployment on Mobile Devices
  • Cite: Zhaode Wang, Jingbang Yang, Xinyu Qian, et al. (Alibaba). MM Asia 2024 (per its footnote), arXiv 2506.10443, https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10443. Read: FULL (7 pp.).
  • Problem: memory use and inference speed limit LLMs on phones [PAPER p.1].
  • Mechanism: DRAM-Flash hybrid storage keeps the embeddings in flash. They are about 15% of parameters, and the paper says Qwen-7B saves about 2.18 GB in bf16. KV beyond a threshold spills to flash with prefetch [PAPER p.2–3 §4.1]. Combined quantization uses int4 or int8 layers, an int8 LM head and an fp8 V cache [PAPER p.3–4 §4.2]. Hardware-driven tiling (sdot or i8mm), big.LITTLE load balancing and mixed fp16/fp32 complete the design [PAPER p.4–5 §5].
  • Memory regime: just reduces memory. No budget parameter, no ceiling.
  • Hardware: Xiaomi 14 (Snapdragon 8 Gen 3), CPU with 4 threads, and the GPU through OpenCL [PAPER p.6 §6].
  • Their headline numbers: CPU prefill up to 8.6× and decode 2.3× faster than llama.cpp [PAPER p.6]. No memory measurement at all, despite the memory claims. Decode is capped at 16 tokens [PAPER p.6]. No energy.
  • What it does NOT do: no memory numbers, no low-RAM device, no ceiling.
  • Pre-empts: C1 NONE (it only reduces memory). C4 NONE. Embeddings in flash and KV spilled to flash overlap SipLLM's windowed embeddings, which is not a C1/C4 claim.
  • Lessons for SipLLM: MNN's i8mm tile (ARM i8mm with ep=10, hp=8, lp=8 [PAPER p.4 Table 2]) is a concrete reference for SipLLM's missing i8mm path (src/neon_sdot.cpp is SDOT-only). A 16-token decode cap is a methodology flaw to avoid.
  • Contribution opportunity: none for C1/C4.
LiteRT-LM (Google; software plus docs) — named rival
  • Cite: [DOC https://developers.google.com/edge/litert-lm/overview (fetched 2026-10-23)]; [DOC github.com/google-ai-edge/LiteRT-LM README v0.18.0]; [DOC docs/getting-started/build-and-run.md]; [DOC HF model cards: litert-community/gemma-4-E2B-it-litert-lm, Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct, TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat-v1.0, SmolLM2-135M-Instruct]. Read: FULL for these pages.
  • Problem: production cross-platform LLM execution on CPU, GPU and NPU [DOC overview].
  • Mechanism: an orchestration layer over LiteRT, using XNNPACK on CPU, "ML Drift" on GPU and NPU on Android [DOC gemma-4 card]. The Gemma 4 card says "the weight footprint in memory can be as low as 0.8 GB while the runtime uses memory mapping to support the 1.12GB of embedding parameters". Vision and audio towers "are loaded on demand" [DOC gemma-4 card, line 19 of the saved README]. NPU has "dynamic on-demand KV cache growth" [DOC README v0.18.0]. Legacy .task artifacts carry the KV size in their filenames (ekv1280, ekv4096) [DOC HF file lists].
  • Memory regime: fits the device; there is no budget knob. The benchmark binary has a --report_peak_memory_footprint flag [DOC build-and-run.md, flag table].
  • Hardware: CPU on Android, iOS, macOS, Windows, Linux and IoT. GPU on all of these except IoT. NPU on Android, with Windows in preview [DOC overview, "Supported Backends"]. Used in production in "Chrome, Chromebook Plus, Pixel Watch" [DOC README]. No minimum-RAM statement. Every published device is a flagship or a 16 GB Raspberry Pi 5 [DOC overview tables].
  • Their headline numbers (vendor-published, not measured by me):
    • Gemma-4-E2B (2583 MB file; 1024 prefill / 256 decode tokens; ctx 2048; XNNPACK, 4 threads; warm caches): [DOC gemma-4 card]. Memory counters: "rusage::ru_maxrss on Android, Linux and Raspberry Pi, task_vm_info::phys_footprint on iOS and MacBook and process_memory_counters::PrivateUsage on Windows". The card adds: "During the first run, the latency and memory usage may differ".
      Device (CPU)Prefill tok/sDecode tok/sCPU memory
      S26 Ultra55746.91733 MB
      iPhone 17 Pro53225.0607 MB
      M4 Max90141.6736 MB
      Linux Arm26035.01628 MB
      Raspberry Pi 51337.61546 MB
    • Qwen2.5-1.5B dynamic_int8 (1598 MB file), S25 Ultra CPU: 297.6 prefill / 34.25 decode tok/s with peak RSS 1997 MB at ctx 1280; 162.7 / 26.06 and 2216 MB at ctx 4096 [DOC card].
    • TinyLlama-1.1B dynamic_int8 (1095 MB file), S24 Ultra CPU, KV 1280: 156.1 prefill / 26.34 decode tok/s, "Memory (RSS in MB)" 2,359 [DOC card].
    • No energy figures anywhere.
  • What it does NOT do: no RAM ceiling, no streaming of main weights, no published low-RAM device. The Windows CPU figure (3505 MB) uses a different counter.
  • Pre-empts: C1 NONE (fit regime). C4 NONE.
  • Lessons for SipLLM:
    1. TinyLlama and SmolLM2-135M, SipLLM's two main benchmark models, both have official LiteRT artifacts. That makes a head-to-head cheap (§4).
    2. On Android, LiteRT's ru_maxrss is the same kernel counter as SipLLM's VmHWM, so the two are comparable. On macOS they are not (phys_footprint vs /usr/bin/time -l maximum RSS) [INFERENCE].
    3. litert_lm_main builds for android_arm64 and runs from adb shell under /data/local/tmp. It accepts --benchmark --benchmark_prefill_tokens=1024 --benchmark_decode_tokens=256 --async=false, and the docs advise "use taskset to pin … e.g. taskset f0" [DOC build-and-run.md].
  • Contribution opportunity [HYPOTHESIS]: on the same phone, resident SipLLM TinyLlama Q8_0 uses less VmHWM than LiteRT dynamic_int8, and SipLLM with --ram-budget set to LiteRT's measured peak decodes at ≥70% of LiteRT's tok/s. Test: the §4 protocol.
MediaPipe LLM Inference API (Google; docs) — named rival
  • Cite: [DOC https://developers.google.com/edge/mediapipe/solutions/genai/llm_inference]; [DOC …/llm_inference/android]; [DOC developers.googleblog.com, "Large Language Models On-Device with MediaPipe and TensorFlow Lite", Mar 2024]. Read: FULL.
  • Problem: running text-to-text LLMs fully on device on Android, iOS and Web [DOC guide].
  • Mechanism: the prefill and decode contexts share weights and the KV cache. On GPU it uses "fixed operations with a predefined maximum cache size". XNNPACK dynamic-range int8/int4 fully connected layers. I8MM gives "twice the speed of the NEON dot product-based implementation" [DOC blog 2024]. Models are .task or .litertlm bundles from LiteRT Torch. The converter "only converts for CPU and requires a Linux machine with at least 64 GBs of RAM" [DOC guide]. No GGUF.
  • Memory regime: fits the device. No ceiling. For Gemma-3 1B, maxTokens (input plus output, default 512) "must match the context size built into the model" [DOC guide, config table and the Gemma-3 1B section]. That is stated for that model only.
  • Hardware: "optimized for high-end Android devices, such as Pixel 8 and Samsung S23 or later", and "does not reliably support device emulators" [DOC Android guide]. The 2024 launch called Android use "experimental and research use only", and said "Gemma 2B (int4) was the only model we could run on iOS due to its memory constraints" [DOC blog 2024].
  • Their headline numbers: none read beyond the 2024 blog's benchmark chart (1024-token prompt, max 1280 tokens, int8; values not transcribed). No memory or energy numbers in the docs read.
  • What it does NOT do: it is "now in maintenance-only mode. New features and optimizations will be focused on LiteRT-LM" [DOC guide banner]. No low-RAM support, no shell binary documented (Android use goes through the AAR / LlmInference API).
  • Pre-empts: none.
  • Lessons for SipLLM: run it on the Dimensity 900, which is below the stated Pixel 8 / S23 floor, and record a failure as a result, not a missing cell. It needs an APK harness, which makes it the weakest-controlled leg of any comparison.
  • Contribution opportunity: none beyond the head-to-head.
ExecuTorch — A Unified PyTorch Solution to Run AI Models On-Device
  • Cite: Mergen Nachin, Digant Desai, Sicheng Stephen Jia, et al. (Meta). MLSys 2026 Industry Track, arXiv 2605.08195, https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08195. Read: FULL (pp.1–12).
  • Problem: on-device deployment is fragmented across conversion-based, model-specific and vendor-specific runtimes [PAPER p.1].
  • Mechanism: torch.export, then Edge Dialect, then backend delegation (XNNPACK, Vulkan, QNN, CoreML and others) [PAPER p.3–7]. Ahead-of-time memory planning places tensors in fixed-size arenas by lifetime, using greedy best-fit [PAPER p.4 §4.2]. The core runtime does no dynamic allocation; all memory comes through MemoryManager [PAPER p.6 §5.1]. "Deterministic memory behavior follows from ahead-of-time memory planning" [PAPER p.6 §5.3]. Delegate blobs can be freed after init, and segments can be mmap'd [PAPER p.5 §4.5].
  • Memory regime: an activation arena planned at export time. Weights are handled separately. There is no operator ceiling and no refusal for LLM weights.
  • Hardware: Galaxy S25 Ultra (16 GB), Pixel 9 Pro XL (16 GB), iPhone 15 Pro [PAPER p.9 §11], and a Raspberry Pi Pico 2 microcontroller (520 KiB SRAM) [PAPER p.8 §9.3].
  • Their headline numbers (S25 Ultra CPU, group size 32) [PAPER p.10 Table 5]: LiteRT has no Phi-4-mini row, and "For LiteRT, we observed a segmentation fault … with GPU acceleration" [PAPER p.11]. No peak memory is reported for any LLM. Measured MCU RAM is about 11 KiB for int8 [PAPER p.9 Table 3]. No energy.
    Model (decode tok/s)ExecuTorchllama.cppLiteRT
    Llama-3.2-1B65.9–67.165.6–66.531.4–33.0
    Qwen3-0.6B72.3–72.797.9–100.811.9–12.5
  • What it does NOT do: no LLM memory measurement, no ceiling. It is vendor-authored. LiteRT's CPU decode here is far below Google's own card figures for similar models [INFERENCE], so remeasure rather than quote.
  • Pre-empts: C1 NONE. Static activation planning is a precedent for "priced memory", but not for weights or ceilings. C4 NONE.
  • Lessons for SipLLM (the only published LiteRT head-to-head on phones):
    1. Copy its protocol skeleton. Quantization was "standardized as much as possible" (4-bit groupwise weights with dynamic 8-bit activations; llama.cpp Q4_0 with a 6-bit LM head). It used 256 prompt plus 256 generated tokens, 3 runs reporting min/max, max ctx 2048, a 60 s cooldown, threads equal to the performance cores (8 or 4), one warm-up run, and reports artifact sizes [PAPER p.9].
    2. Its missing memory column is the gap SipLLM can fill.
    3. Cite its AOT arenas as related work for MemLedger scratch, attention and residual pricing.
  • Contribution opportunity: a LiteRT head-to-head *with* peak memory and energy (§4).
MLC-LLM (software; no dedicated paper)
  • Cite: [DOC github.com/mlc-ai/mlc-llm python/mlc_llm/serve/config.py, the EngineConfig docstring]. Read: FULL for the memory fields.
  • Problem: GPU-accelerated LLM serving and on-device chat through TVM compilation (repo purpose).
  • Mechanism: gpu_memory_utilization is described as "fraction of GPU memory used … in total. It is used to infer to maximum possible KV cache capacity. … defaults to 0.85" [DOC config.py L66–69]. Modes (local, interactive, server) set batch size and maximum sequence length [DOC L25–37].
  • Memory regime: an operator-set GPU-memory fraction that sizes the KV cache. It is explicitly not a guarantee: "Under mode 'server', the actual memory usage may be slightly larger than this number" [DOC config.py L70–72]. Models must fit in memory [PowerInfer-2 p.12 §8].
  • Hardware: Android OpenCL, iOS Metal, CUDA (per MELT p.6 Table 3 and PalmBench p.14).
  • Their headline numbers: none read in the docs. MLC appears as a baseline in MELT, PalmBench, 2410.03613, PowerInfer-2 and llm.npu.
  • What it does NOT do: no CPU path (MNN-LLM p.6: "MLC-LLM does not accommodate CPU-based inference"), no weight streaming, no guarantee.
  • Pre-empts: C1 NONE. It is a precedent for an operator-set memory fraction, without a guarantee and on GPU only. C4 NONE.
  • Lessons for SipLLM: if SipLLM ever budgets Vulkan buffers (C6), avoid MLC's "may be slightly larger" caveat by pricing buffers inside the same ledger.
  • Contribution opportunity: none.
MELT — MELTing point: Mobile Evaluation of Language Transformers
  • Cite: Stefanos Laskaridis, Kleomenis Katevas, Lorenzo Minto, Hamed Haddadi (Brave, Imperial). MobiCom '24 (the preprint header says accepted), arXiv 2403.12844. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1–12).
  • Problem: a systematic measurement of on-device LLM performance, energy, memory, thermals and accuracy [PAPER p.1–2].
  • Mechanism: a device farm with a Raspberry Pi coordinator. A Monsoon AAA10F power monitor with battery bypass, sampling at up to 5 kHz. USB power lanes are cut while measuring, and a FLIR thermal camera watches the device. OS updates and adaptive brightness are off, dark mode is on, and brightness is fixed at 25%. Bluetooth and Wi-Fi baselines are subtracted [PAPER p.3–4 §3.1]. Clocks are synced and runs are separated by a configurable sleep [PAPER p.5 Alg.1]. Memory comes from "the benchmark binaries", i.e. the framework's own counter [PAPER p.6 §4.2].
  • Memory regime: measures memory only (fit). Out-of-memory failures are recorded.
  • Hardware: Galaxy S23 (8 GB), Pixel 6a (listed as 8 GB), iPhone 14 Pro (6 GB), iPhone SE (4 GB), Jetson AGX Orin and Orin Nano [PAPER p.3 Table 1]. Frameworks: MLC-LLM, llama.cpp, LLMFarm [PAPER p.6 Table 3].
  • Their headline numbers: 3 repetitions, mean ± std [PAPER p.7 §5.1]. Zephyr-3B 4-bit costs 0.16–0.21 mWh/token [PAPER p.8]. iPhones draw up to 13.8 W sustained and over 18 W peak [PAPER p.8]. The llama.cpp GPU path on Android adds nothing: TinyLlama 4-bit on S23 runs at 13.61 tok/s on CPU vs 13.22 on GPU [PAPER p.7 fn.8]. Out-of-memory runs appear as missing bars [PAPER p.8 Fig.4 caption]. Out-of-memory errors and device restarts count as quality-of-experience failures [PAPER p.9 §5.2.3]. Surface temperature reaches 47.9 °C [PAPER p.9]. Fig.10 plots model size (GB) against accuracy across quantizations [PAPER p.11].
  • What it does NOT do: no OS-level memory counter, no ceiling, no streaming.
  • Pre-empts: C1 NONE. C4 PARTIAL: a size-vs-accuracy view for resident models on phones, with no throughput at fixed RAM.
  • Lessons for SipLLM: Monsoon with battery bypass is the gold-standard energy setup. MELT's framework-reported memory is weaker than SipLLM's VmHWM; cite that difference.
  • Contribution opportunity: run MELT's out-of-memory configurations (7B/13B on 4–6 GB phones) under a SipLLM ceiling and report VmHWM [HYPOTHESIS: they complete].
Understanding Large Language Models in Your Pockets: Performance Study on COTS Mobile Devices
  • Cite: Jie Xiao, Qianyi Huang, Xu Chen, Chen Tian. arXiv 2410.03613 v5 (Feb 2026). Read: PARTIAL (pp.1–7, 13, 16).
  • Problem: the performance of on-device LLMs on commercial phones, from both the user's and the developer's point of view [PAPER p.1].
  • Mechanism: a measurement study with llama.cpp on CPU and MLC on GPU, profiled with Perfetto, Snapdragon Profiler, Arm Streamline and Xcode [PAPER p.4–5]. Prompts are 64 and 512 tokens with outputs of 128 and 256. Each test runs two rounds of 5 repetitions with a 10 s gap; for CPU the device is rebooted and rested 10 minutes between rounds. GPU tests get 5 runs with at least 10 minutes of rest [PAPER p.5 §III.B]. Energy comes from sysfs current and voltage on a rooted Xiaomi, and from OS app-level power data on unrooted devices, over 2 trials of 20 iterations [PAPER p.13 §C].
  • Memory regime: fit. Measured memory "closely corresponds to the size of the model weights" [PAPER p.6]. The counter is not stated in the pages read.
  • Hardware: seven devices, including Huawei Nova 7 (Kirin 985, 4 GB available of 8 GB), MatePad 11 Pro (5.5 of 8 GB), Xiaomi 14 Pro (12 of 16 GB) and an iPad Pro M1 [PAPER p.4 Table I].
  • Their headline numbers: about 3.8 GB to run Llama2-7B and under 1 GB for Gemma3-1B. Implication 1: "as long as a mobile device has more than 4GB available RAM, it is sufficient" [PAPER p.6]. About 20 mAh per round (64 + 128 tokens) on CPU [PAPER p.2]. Best prefill energy is 0.031 mAh/token [PAPER p.13]. An mmap cold start takes up to 20 s on HarmonyOS, against 1.2 s warm [PAPER p.16].
  • What it does NOT do: no ceiling, no streaming, no OS-level memory counter stated.
  • Pre-empts: none. It embodies the "fit" framing that C1 argues against, with "4 GB available" as the stated floor.
  • Lessons for SipLLM: report cold and warm runs separately, because an mmap cold start dominates on some operating systems. Its reboot-and-rest protocol is the strongest thermal reset seen.
  • Contribution opportunity [HYPOTHESIS]: a 7B model under a 512 MB ceiling on a device with 4 GB available directly falsifies Implication 1's floor.
Sustainability Is Not Linear: Quantifying Performance, Energy, and Privacy Trade-offs in On-Device Intelligence
  • Cite: Eziyo Ehsani, Luca Giamattei, Ivano Malavolta, Roberto Pietrantuono. arXiv 2603.26603 v2 (May 2026). Read: FULL (pp.1–13).
  • Problem: the joint energy, latency, memory and quality trade-offs of on-device LLMs on an unrooted phone [PAPER p.1–2].
  • Mechanism: the best unrooted protocol found. Galaxy S25 Ultra on Android 16, llama.cpp on CPU with 8 threads, ctx 512, greedy decoding, 100 output tokens. 30 repetitions, a 200 s cooldown, and the battery held at 80–100%. The screen stays on at minimum brightness, background services are isolated, and ADB runs wirelessly because USB charging corrupts discharge readings [PAPER p.5–6 §III.B–C]. Energy: a BatteryManager logging app samples at 100 ms, the mean power of a 2-hour idle baseline is subtracted, and the result is integrated with the trapezoidal rule [PAPER p.6–7 §III.D]. Statistics: Shapiro-Wilk, then Friedman plus Wilcoxon with Holm-Bonferroni [PAPER p.7–8].
  • Memory regime: fit. Peak memory "encompassing the model weights, the dynamic KV cache, and runtime buffers"; the counter is not named [PAPER p.7].
  • Hardware: Samsung Galaxy S25 Ultra only. "The evaluated LLMs failed to run reliably on mid- and low-end devices due to insufficient RAM and thermal headroom" [PAPER p.5].
  • Their headline numbers: eight models from 0.5B to 9B, in Q4_K_M and IQ4_XS. IQ4_XS wins on peak memory (p = 2.8e-41) but loses on speed and energy [PAPER p.10 Table III]. Gemma-2-9B costs over 300 J per run, Qwen2-0.5B about 50 J [PAPER p.10]. The authors call 3B the "sweet spot" [PAPER p.11].
  • What it does NOT do: one device, a single summarization task, no ceiling, no streaming.
  • Pre-empts: C4 PARTIAL: a measured speed, energy and quality frontier on a phone for resident models (§IV-C), with no fixed-RAM axis. C1 NONE. It documents the gap C1 targets, since low-RAM phones were excluded.
  • Lessons for SipLLM: adopt its energy pipeline wholesale for the missing energy-per-token line in scripts/device. Cite its exclusion of low-end phones as C1's motivation.
  • Contribution opportunity: the same pipeline on the 8 GB Dimensity 900 with streamed configurations (§3, C4 hypothesis).
A Systematic Evaluation of On-Device LLMs: Quantization, Performance, and Resources
  • Cite: Qingyu Song, Rui Liu, Wei Lin, et al. (Xiamen U., CUHK, Huawei). arXiv 2505.15030 v5 (Mar 2026). Read: PARTIAL (pp.1–4).
  • Problem: joint evaluation of capability, efficiency and resource use for quantized on-device LLMs [PAPER p.1].
  • Mechanism: llama-cpp-python running Qwen2.5 from 0.5B to 14B in seven PTQ formats (q2_k to q8_0, plus fp16). Inputs of 64, 128, 256 and 512 tokens, 1024 output tokens. Peak RSS from psutil's "wset" (the Windows working set), energy from Windows E3 [PAPER p.3 §3.3, §4].
  • Memory regime: fit (measurement only).
  • Hardware: a laptop (Core i7-1360P, 16 GB DDR5) and an EPYC 9654 server [PAPER p.3].
  • Their headline numbers: "heavily quantized large models consistently outperform smaller, high-precision models, with a performance threshold at ∼3.5 effective bits-per-weight" [PAPER p.1]. For example, 14B scores 89.08 on GSM8K at q5_k and 89.01 at q3_k, while 1.5B drops from 59.14 to 46.47 [PAPER p.4]. Throughput declines monotonically with model scale [PAPER p.4].
  • What it does NOT do: no phone, no streaming, no fixed-RAM axis (fixed bits instead).
  • Pre-empts: C4 PARTIAL, and strong for the resident form. At comparable resident memory, prefer a bigger model quantized down to about 3.5 BPW.
  • Lessons for SipLLM: C4 must not claim the resident iso-memory frontier. Only the streaming-enabled extension is open.
  • Contribution opportunity: extend its quality-per-BPW grid with streamed points at a fixed RAM budget (§3, C4).
The case for 4-bit precision: k-bit Inference Scaling Laws
  • Cite: Tim Dettmers, Luke Zettlemoyer. ICML 2023, arXiv 2212.09720. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1–3).
  • Problem: for a fixed total number of model bits, which (parameter count, precision) maximizes zero-shot accuracy? [PAPER p.1].
  • Mechanism: 35,000 zero-shot experiments, 3- to 16-bit weights, 19M–176B parameters across BLOOM, OPT, Pythia/NeoX and GPT-2. Data types and block sizes vary [PAPER p.1–3].
  • Memory regime: none. Total model bits stand in for memory for resident weights.
  • Hardware: not relevant (accuracy study).
  • Their headline numbers: "4-bit precision is almost universally optimal for total model bits and zero-shot accuracy" [PAPER p.1 abstract, Fig.1]. Accuracy at fixed bits improves from 16-bit down to 4-bit and worsens at 3-bit. Small blocks (64–128) and float or quantile data types help [PAPER p.2].
  • What it does NOT do: no throughput, no device, no streaming.
  • Pre-empts: C4 PARTIAL. Iso-bits equals iso-memory only when weights are resident.
  • Lessons for SipLLM: in SipLLM, model bits ≠ RAM. Frame C4 as "Dettmers' question once RAM and model bits come apart".
  • Contribution opportunity: see §3, C4.
PalmBench — A Comprehensive Benchmark of Compressed LLMs on Mobile Platforms
  • Cite: Yilong Li, Jingyu Liu, Hao Zhang, et al. (UW–Madison, AWS, Uber). ICLR 2025, arXiv 2410.05315. Read: PARTIAL (pp.4–7, 9, 14).
  • Problem: the resource efficiency (memory, power) and harmful output of compressed LLMs on mobile [PAPER p.1].
  • Mechanism: an automated benchmark over MLC and llama.cpp. Memory footprint is "main memory used by the LLM application", framework included [PAPER p.4 Table 2]. Measurements come from Android GPU Inspector and Xcode Instruments, with energy from PMU data through vendor tools [PAPER p.6 §3.2.5]. 10 repetitions, ctx 4096, devices factory-reset [PAPER p.6 §4.1].
  • Memory regime: fit. Models larger than phone memory are excluded, e.g. Vicuna-13B at 3 bits (6 GB) [PAPER p.6]. On phones "only 3-bit and 4-bit" models fit, because "Higher-bit models exceeded the devices' memory capacity" [PAPER p.7].
  • Hardware: includes the older Pixel 4 (Snapdragon 855, 6 GB) and Pixel 5a (Snapdragon 765G, 6 GB), plus Pixel 7, S22 Ultra, iPhones, Orange Pi 5 and Jetson Orin Nano [PAPER p.14 Table 6].
  • Their headline numbers: for devices with "less than 4GB of memory, 2-bit or 3-bit quantization can still provide an adequate user experience" on simpler tasks [PAPER p.9 §4.5]. The per-model memory and energy figures were not transcribed.
  • What it does NOT do: no ceiling. It drops whatever does not fit.
  • Pre-empts: C4 PARTIAL (the quantize-down-to-fit answer, judged on quality only). C1 NONE.
  • Lessons for SipLLM: its devices and its exclusion rule define the experiment. Run the excluded configurations (13B on a Pixel-4-class phone) under a ceiling.
  • Contribution opportunity [HYPOTHESIS]: Vicuna-13B 3-bit or Llama-2-13B Q4, excluded by PalmBench, completes on a 6 GB phone under a 512 MB ceiling.
MobileAIBench — Benchmarking LLMs and LMMs for On-Device Use Cases
  • Cite: Rithesh Murthy, Liangwei Yang, Juntao Tan, et al. (Salesforce). arXiv 2406.10290. Read: PARTIAL (pp.5, 9).
  • Problem: benchmarking LLMs and LMMs for on-device use on effectiveness, efficiency and safety [PAPER p.1].
  • Mechanism: desktop runs for quality, plus an iOS app on an iPhone 14 measuring RAM usage and battery drain rate [PAPER p.5].
  • Memory regime: fit (measurement). The counter is not checked.
  • Hardware: iPhone 14 (6 GiB) [PAPER p.9].
  • Their headline numbers: "even running the 4-bit quantized TinyLlama model (1B) on-device takes more than 50% of the overall memory" [PAPER p.9].
  • What it does NOT do: no Android, no ceiling, no stated counter.
  • Pre-empts: none. It supports the point that a ~1B model in the fit regime takes half a 6 GB phone. Contrast with SipLLM's 118 MB TinyLlama floor [SIPLLM].
  • Lessons for SipLLM: a useful quote for the motivation section.
  • Contribution opportunity: none.
LLM Inference at the Edge: Mobile, NPU, and GPU Performance Efficiency Trade-offs Under Sustained Load
  • Cite: Pranay Tummalapalli, Sahil Arayakandy, Ritam Pal, Kautuk Kundan (Conscious Engines). arXiv 2603.23640 v2. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1–6).
  • Problem: sustained (thermally limited) LLM inference across a phone, an NPU and a GPU [PAPER p.1].
  • Mechanism: the same base model (Qwen2.5-1.5B, 4-bit), but a different runtime and format on each device: GGUF Q4_0 via hailo-ollama, MLC q4f16_2, MLX Q4_0, GPTQ Int4 [PAPER p.4 Table 2]. Context is fixed at 2048 everywhere, with each stack's knob named [PAPER p.4 footnote]. Devices equilibrate 10 minutes at 22 ± 2 °C, one warm-up run is discarded, and thermal stability (a rise under 2 °C over 60 s) is required before 20 iterations [PAPER p.5 §3.4].
  • Memory regime: none measured.
  • Hardware: Raspberry Pi 5 with a Hailo-10H, Galaxy S24 Ultra (12 GB), iPhone 16 Pro (8 GB), and an RTX 4050 laptop [PAPER p.3 Table 1].
  • Their headline numbers: the iPhone 16 Pro loses about 40% of peak throughput within 3 iterations and plateaus near 23.7 tok/s. The S24 Ultra loses about 15% and plateaus near 10 tok/s [PAPER p.1]. Phone energy is not reported, because BatteryManager accuracy is caveated [PAPER p.5 §3.3].
  • What it does NOT do: no memory figures, no iso-format comparison.
  • Pre-empts: none.
  • Lessons for SipLLM: cross-runtime numbers are "hardware and software combined" [PAPER p.1], and a SipLLM paper must say the same. The thermal plateau, not the peak, is the honest phone metric.
  • Contribution opportunity: none.
llm.npu — Fast On-device LLM Inference with NPUs
  • Cite: Daliang Xu, Hao Zhang, Liming Yang, et al. (PKU). ASPLOS '25, arXiv 2407.05858. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1–3, 9–13).
  • Problem: on-device prefill is slow on CPU and GPU, so offload it to mobile NPUs [PAPER p.1–2].
  • Mechanism: chunk-sharing NPU graphs for variable-length prompts, shadow execution of outlier activations on the CPU, and out-of-order subgraph scheduling. Built on mllm and QNN; decoding runs on the mllm CPU backend [PAPER p.3; p.10 §4].
  • Memory regime: fit. The Hexagon NPU "typically access[es] limited memory regions (e.g., 4GB …)" [PAPER p.10].
  • Hardware: Redmi K70 Pro (24 GB) and K60 Pro (16 GB), Android 13 [PAPER p.10 §4.1].
  • Their headline numbers: 22.4× average prefill speedup and 30.7× energy savings [PAPER p.1]. Prefill memory at a 512-token prompt: Gemma-2B uses about 2.8 GB under llama.cpp-CPU, about 3.1 GB under TFLite CPU and GPU, and 3.7 GB under llm.npu. "llm.npu consumes up to 1.32× more memory than llama.cpp and TFLite" [PAPER p.13 §4.5, Fig.17]. The memory counter is not stated. Energy is read from /sys/class/power_supply every 100 ms on the only rootable device. 3 repetitions [PAPER p.10 §4.1]. PowerInfer-v2 figures are "reported data from its paper" [PAPER p.10].
  • What it does NOT do: no ceiling, no low-RAM device. Its decode path is unoptimized.
  • Pre-empts: none for C1/C4.
  • Lessons for SipLLM: it is a rare same-phone measurement of TFLite (MediaPipe's runtime) memory against llama.cpp. NPU prefill speed costs memory, the opposite trade to SipLLM's.
  • Contribution opportunity: none.
LLMS — LLM as a System Service on Mobile Devices
  • Cite: Wangsong Yin, Mengwei Xu, Yuanchun Li, Xuanzhe Liu (PKU). arXiv 2403.11805. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1–5, 9–11).
  • Problem: keeping many apps' LLM contexts (their KV caches) within a tight device memory budget while minimizing context-switch latency [PAPER p.1].
  • Mechanism: the KV cache is split into 16-token chunks. Chunks get tolerance-aware compression and are swapped through an I/O-recompute pipeline. Eviction uses LCTRU (least compression-tolerable, then least recently used), with swap-out done ahead of time [PAPER p.1–2].
  • Memory regime: a budget for KV contexts only (1, 2 or 3 GB); weights stay resident [PAPER p.11]. Llama2-7B in GPTQ 4-bit, KV in int8 [PAPER p.9].
  • Hardware: MI14 phone (8 GB), Jetson Orin NX and TX2 [PAPER p.9 table].
  • Their headline numbers: under a 10 ms switching-latency limit it handles 4.32, 10.72 and 16.32 contexts at 1, 2 and 3 GB, 1.99–2.85× more than baselines [PAPER p.11].
  • What it does NOT do: no whole-process ceiling, no weight streaming.
  • Pre-empts: C1 NONE. It belongs to C5 (another cluster): a KV cache managed as a buffer pool with eviction under a budget is published here, for multi-context switching.
  • Lessons for SipLLM: if SipLLM adds KV eviction (C5), LCTRU is the published baseline to beat.
  • Contribution opportunity: C5, in another cluster.
Neuralink (v1 title "Ripple") — Fast LLM Inference on Smartphones with Neuron Co-Activation Linking
  • Cite: Tuowei Wang, Ruwen Fan, Minxing Huang, et al. (Tsinghua, MSR). arXiv 2410.19274 v3 (Oct 2025; the PDF read carries the "Neuralink" title). Read: PARTIAL (pp.1–4).
  • Problem: sparsity-based flash offloading on phones is IOPS-bound [PAPER p.1–2].
  • Mechanism: an offline placement of neurons in flash by co-activation, so reads become contiguous, plus online access and caching strategies [PAPER p.1–4].
  • Memory regime: reduces memory through sparsity offload. No ceiling.
  • Hardware: OnePlus 12 and OnePlus Ace 2 [PAPER p.2–4].
  • Their headline numbers: I/O is 73.4–95.8% of latency when the FFN is offloaded [PAPER p.2 Table 1]. UFS has a shallow 32-entry command queue [PAPER p.3–4]. The paper reports up to 4.32× bandwidth gain (abstract; the evaluation section was not read).
  • What it does NOT do: no ceiling, no dense model, no low-RAM phone.
  • Pre-empts: C1 NONE. Relevant to C7 and I/O (another cluster).
  • Lessons for SipLLM: on UFS the limit is IOPS unless reads are contiguous. SipLLM's sequential whole-layer reads already follow the IOPS-friendly pattern [INFERENCE].
  • Contribution opportunity: none.
T-MAC — CPU Renaissance via Table Lookup for Low-Bit LLM Deployment on Edge
  • Cite: Jianyu Wei, Shijie Cao, Ting Cao, et al. (USTC, MSR). EuroSys '25, arXiv 2407.00088. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1–3, 10, 13).
  • Problem: mixed-precision GEMM for low-bit weights pays a dequantization cost on CPUs [PAPER p.1].
  • Mechanism: LUT-based GEMM without dequantization. Cost scales linearly with weight bits. Tables are kept in registers through axis reordering and tiling [PAPER p.1–3].
  • Memory regime: none (a speed lever).
  • Hardware: M2-Ultra, Raspberry Pi 5, Jetson AGX Orin, Surface Book 3; also Surface Laptop 7, OnePlus 12 and Jetson Orin NX [PAPER p.10; p.13 Table 7].
  • Their headline numbers: Llama-2-7B 2-bit on the OnePlus 12 CPU runs at 16.62 tok/s against 6.95 for llama.cpp [PAPER p.13 Table 7]. Its NPU figures are "sourced from official data released by Qualcomm" [PAPER p.13]. Energy falls 20.6–61.2% against llama.cpp on the M2 Ultra [PAPER p.10 §5.4]. Method: kernels get 10 warm-up runs and 100 timed runs; at model level, 64 tokens are generated 20 times [PAPER p.10 §5.1]. **Energy from powermetrics at 500 ms over at least 120 s of continuous generation, integrated** [PAPER p.10 §5.4].
  • What it does NOT do: no memory regime, and no measurement against a phone NPU (the NPU numbers are vendor figures).
  • Pre-empts: none.
  • Lessons for SipLLM: at 2–3 bits, LUT kernels beat SDOT. Its powermetrics method is the macOS energy recipe for SipLLM's CHANGELOG energy line.
  • Contribution opportunity [HYPOTHESIS]: if C4 favours larger streamed models, lower bit-widths also cut the bytes streamed per token, so LUT kernels and streaming compound.
bitnet.cpp — Efficient Edge Inference for Ternary LLMs
  • Cite: Jinheng Wang, Hansong Zhou, Ting Song, et al. (MSR, PKU, USTC, UCAS). ACL 2025, arXiv 2502.11880. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1, 7–8, 11–13).
  • Problem: fast, lossless inference for ternary (BitNet b1.58) models on edge CPUs [PAPER p.1].
  • Mechanism: TL (lookup-table) and I2_S (lossless multiply-accumulate) mpGEMM kernels, plus an element-wise LUT generalization [PAPER p.1].
  • Memory regime: none.
  • Hardware: Apple M2 Ultra and Intel i7-13700H only [PAPER p.7 §4.1.1].
  • Their headline numbers: up to 6.25× over full-precision baselines and 2.32× over low-bit baselines [PAPER p.1].
  • What it does NOT do: no phone, no memory study. Its weight formats are special GGUF types that SipLLM does not implement.
  • Pre-empts: none.
  • Lessons for SipLLM: ternary models are a C4 candidate (very few bits per weight) only if SipLLM adds these types. Low priority.
  • Contribution opportunity: none now.
Methodology source (not a paper): Android memory accounting
  • [DOC https://developer.android.com/topic/performance/memory-management], read in full:
    • Clean file-backed pages, including mmap() of files, "can be deleted by kswapd". Dirty anonymous pages are compressed into zRAM. Under pressure the low-memory killer kills by out-of- memory score [DOC §Types of memory, §LMK].
    • RSS counts shared and private pages; PSS splits shared pages evenly; USS counts private pages only. "RSS … is better for tracking changes in memory allocation" [DOC §memory footprint].
    • Consequence for §4: an mmap'd runtime's RSS includes reclaimable clean pages, while a pread runtime's page-cache pages are not in its RSS at all. Peak-RSS comparisons across runtimes therefore need an anon/file split.
3. Claim verdicts (my scope: C1, C4)
C1 — Hard ceiling. Verdict: NARROWED

Closest prior art, and how each one differs:

What died. "Streaming plus async prefetch plus partial residency under a user budget, CPU-only" is published (FlexInfer, built on llama.cpp; also STI and Hermes). "Pricing memory and auto-shrinking context" ships in llama.cpp --fit. "Running models larger than a phone's RAM" is published (PowerInfer-2, at 7–19 GB on 16–24 GB phones).

What survives (state it exactly):

An operator-set peak-RSS contract in which a pure planner prices *every* RSS contributor (KV at full ctx, LM head, scratch, ring, pins) and refuses infeasible configurations. Applied to dense, unquantized-beyond-GGUF (lossless relative to the GGUF), CPU-only inference on an unrooted commodity phone, with the guarantee verified by measured kernel VmHWM across a budget sweep. Shown at expansion factors (model file ÷ peak) of about 25× (13B in 317 MB) that no prior work reports on any device.

Evidence SipLLM has for the surviving form [SIPLLM]:

  • Budget sweeps with measured peak and zero violations:
    • bench/results/ram-budget-Ankits-MacBook-Air-2026-10-19.json (M3, /usr/bin/time -l, median of 3): TinyLlama at 128M gives 72.2 MB, 256M gives 210.1, 384M gives 334.0, 512M gives 481.3, 768M gives 644.9. SmolLM2-135M at 128M gives 100.3.
    • The same pattern holds on WSL2 Linux (ram-budget-Radhe-10ONTI4F-2026-08-24.json).
    • Phone (device-OnePlus-IV2201-2026-10-19.json, VmHWM at 5 Hz): budget 256M gives 200.9–201.0 MB, 512M gives 452.3–452.5, 1G gives 615.0–615.1, unlimited gives 117.9–118.1. All under budget.
  • Gap: the sweeps cover only TinyLlama and SmolLM2. The 8B/13B ratios are single points. The refusal path is unit-tested, not exercised on a device.

An open caveat to resolve before a paper [INFERENCE]. This does not falsify the RSS bound; it qualifies what the speed numbers mean.

  1. Streamed reads go through the page cache. SipLLM streams with buffered pread. A grep of src/ and include/llm/file_backing.h finds no O_DIRECT, F_NOCACHE or posix_fadvise(DONTNEED) [SIPLLM]. Streamed layers therefore also sit in the OS page cache, which is clean, reclaimable and outside RSS [DOC Android memory-management].
    • The RSS ceiling holds on the evidence above.
    • What is unproven is streamed *speed under real memory pressure*. When the file fits in free RAM, "streaming" is served from cache.
    • Effective read bandwidth computed from SipLLM's own data (streamed bytes per generated token × decode tok/s):
      • M3, TinyLlama, budget 0: 14,439 MB over 24 generated tokens, about 602 MB/token, at 10.77 tok/s, gives about 6.3 GB/s (ram-budget-…-2026-10-19.json; prefill's share of streamed_mb is ignored, so this slightly overstates).
      • **Phone, TinyLlama, budget 0, --fast: about 0.6 GB/token at 4.7 tok/s gives about 2.8 GB/s** (CHANGELOG on-device table).
      • M3, 8B: 4.92 GB × 0.55 tok/s gives about 2.7 GB/s [SIPLLM brief].
    • I did not look up storage specs for the M3 Air SSD or the IV2201's flash. If they are below these figures, the TinyLlama streaming speeds are page-cache speeds. The CHANGELOG itself says the phone is "compute-bound even when streaming", which is consistent with cache-served reads. The 8B point is the likeliest to be genuinely storage-bound.
    • FlexInfer avoids this with direct I/O plus cgroup limits; cgroup v2 memory.max charges page cache [FlexInfer p.5]. LLM in a flash benchmarks "without the aid of operating system caching" [p.7].
    • To resolve: (a) add posix_fadvise(DONTNEED) per consumed layer, or O_DIRECT / F_NOCACHE. (b) Add an external RAM clamp run (a memory-hog leaving MemAvailable ≈ budget, or cgroup on Linux) and show that tok/s and completion hold.
  2. Fairness against mmap runtimes. llama.cpp's mmap'd weights count in its RSS as clean file pages, while SipLLM's cached pages do not count in SipLLM's RSS. A raw VmHWM ratio therefore flatters SipLLM against mmap runtimes by up to the file size. Report RssAnon, RssFile and VmSwap (/proc/<pid>/status or smaps_rollup) for every runtime, plus system MemAvailable (§4.3 item 8).

Searched (for the OPEN part, nothing found): "operator memory budget LLM CPU phone peak RSS", "memory budget refuse infeasible inference", plus the papers above. No paper or system was found that both (i) guarantees a whole-process peak-memory ceiling chosen by the operator and (ii) reports measured peak RSS on a real low-RAM commodity phone.

C4 — Iso-memory frontier. Verdict: NARROWED (resident form DEAD; streaming form OPEN)

Closest prior art:

  • Dettmers & Zettlemoyer 2212.09720: at fixed total model bits (= RAM for resident weights), 4-bit is optimal across 19M–176B [p.1].
  • 2505.15030: on a laptop CPU with peak RSS measured, "heavily quantized large models consistently outperform smaller, high-precision models", with a ~3.5 BPW threshold [p.1, p.4].
  • 2603.26603: S25 Ultra, measured speed, energy and quality frontier across 0.5B–9B resident models. 3B is the named sweet spot. MoE (OLMoE-1B-7B) sits off the dense trend [p.10–11].
  • MELT Fig.10 (size vs accuracy on phones) [p.11]. PalmBench (quantize to 2–3 bits on <4 GB devices) [p.9].
  • Throughput vs memory budget for one model, with no quality axis: FlexInfer Fig.4 (7B–70B) [p.5], PowerInfer-2 Fig.10 (47B over 7–19 GB) [p.10].

What died: "At a fixed RAM, which (model size, quantization) maximizes quality?" is answered for resident weights (go bigger and lower-bit, down to about 3.5–4 BPW).

What survives: in SipLLM, RAM and model bits come apart, so the open question is:

At a fixed RAM budget B, over configurations {resident small model at precision p} ∪ {streamed or partly pinned larger model at precision p'}, what is the Pareto frontier of task quality vs tokens/s (and J/token) on a given device and storage?

No paper I read combines a quality axis with streamed configurations at a fixed RAM budget. CoEdge-RAG (2511.05915) came up in a search, but I did not read it and do not use it as evidence.

Prior expectation, to state before measuring [INFERENCE]: streamed decode is bounded by storage bandwidth. tok/s ≤ BW_storage ÷ (streamed bytes per token). SipLLM's own 8B point (4.92 GB, about 0.55 tok/s on M3) implies about 2.7 GB/s effective, and that may itself be page-cache-assisted (see the C1 caveat). On UFS phones the bound is lower. A streamed 8B will probably lose to a resident 1–3B on quality per second at any interactive threshold. It can win only where the small model fails outright (a quality cliff, e.g. GSM8K at 1.5B vs 14B in 2505.15030 p.4) and latency does not matter (batch or offline tasks). That makes the contribution a measured crossover map, not a claim that streaming wins.

[HYPOTHESIS, falsifiable] On the Dimensity 900 at B = 512 MB, there is a task set (multi-step math or code) where Llama-3.1-8B Q4_K_M streamed (pinned under B) beats every resident model that fits in 512 MB on exact-match accuracy per joule, and loses on accuracy per second. Test: models SmolLM2-135M/360M, Qwen2.5-0.5B, TinyLlama, Llama-3.2-1B and 8B streamed. A fixed eval (e.g. GSM8K-100, HumanEval-50). Measure VmHWM, tok/s, energy per §4, accuracy. Plot the Pareto front. Falsified if no streamed point is on the front for any metric.

4. Head-to-head protocol: SipLLM vs LiteRT-LM vs MediaPipe LLM Inference vs llama.cpp
4.1 What can actually be compared (blunt)
  • Bit-identical weights are impossible. LiteRT-LM and MediaPipe take only .litertlm, .task or .tflite made by LiteRT Torch from PyTorch [DOC MediaPipe guide]. SipLLM and llama.cpp take GGUF. Every comparison is therefore "runtime plus its quantizer". Quality must be measured per artifact, and the paper must say results are system-level, as 2603.23640 does [p.1].
  • Regimes differ. LiteRT-LM and MediaPipe are fit-only: they have no RAM knob, and the KV size appears to be fixed per artifact. The ekv1280/ekv4096 filenames suggest this [INFERENCE from the filenames]. The docs state it explicitly only for Gemma-3 1B's maxTokens. llama.cpp is fit, plus --fit ctx shrink, plus mmap paging. SipLLM has a ceiling. So report three tables, never one:
    • (A) iso-model, default settings: peak memory and speed as each ships.
    • (B) iso-memory: SipLLM --ram-budget set to each rival's measured peak (and to 50% and 25% of it), compare speed.
    • (C) minimum RAM to run at all: SipLLM's floor vs "does not run" for the others under a clamp.
  • GPU and NPU comparisons are impossible for SipLLM (Vulkan is a stub [SIPLLM]). Compare CPU backends only. Give rivals' GPU numbers as context, with GPU memory reported separately: LiteRT reports GPU memory apart from RSS [DOC Qwen2.5 card], whereas on Mali SipLLM's Vulkan buffers land in VmRSS [SIPLLM GPU evidence].
4.2 Models runnable in all four (checked 2026-10-23)

Llama-3.2-1Bonly llama3_2_1b_mixed_int4_gpu.litertlm (GPU) [DOC HF file list]yessupportedCPU comparison impossible with the published artifact (would need a reconversion).
Gemma-4-E2B/E4B, Gemma-3nLiteRT's flagship models—gemma4 on synthetic weights only; no gemma3n arch (src/model.cpp) [SIPLLM]Impossible today. Note that LiteRT's headline numbers are all on these models.
Qwen3-* (0.6B–14B)yesyesno qwen3 in arch_from_name [SIPLLM]Impossible today.
Phi-4-miniyesyesphi4 synthetic only [SIPLLM]Not credible today.

Quantization pairing: LiteRT dynamic_int8 (int8 weights, float activations [DOC TinyLlama card]) is closest to GGUF Q8_0. LiteRT int4/QAT is closest to Q4_0 (QAT GGUF where Google publishes one). Never pair LiteRT int8 with SipLLM Q4_K_M in one row without saying so. Report bytes on disk and bits per weight for each artifact.

4.3 What must be controlled (with the source for each control)
  1. Same device, same session, randomized interleave. Run order ABCD, DCBA and so on across runtimes to spread thermal drift [INFERENCE; drift documented by 2603.23640 p.1 and 2410.03613 p.5]. Force-stop third-party apps, airplane mode, fixed brightness, screen state fixed [2603.26603 p.5; MELT p.4].
  2. Thermal state. Cool down between runs until battery temperature is within 1 °C of baseline, or ΔT < 2 °C over 60 s [2603.23640 p.5]. Minimums seen: 60 s [ExecuTorch p.9], 200 s [2603.26603 p.6], reboot plus 10 min between rounds [2410.03613 p.5]. Report both a cold first-iteration value and the sustained plateau over ≥20 back-to-back iterations: phones lose 15–40% to throttling [2603.23640 p.1].
  3. Clocks. Unrooted phones cannot pin governors. Record scaling_cur_freq for each cluster before and after every run, and battery temperature (SipLLM's device script already does [SIPLLM]). Keep the battery at 80–100% for voltage stability [2603.26603 p.6].
  4. Threads and affinity. Equal thread counts, pinned to the same cores (taskset). LiteRT's own tip: "taskset f0" [DOC build-and-run]. ExecuTorch uses threads equal to performance cores [p.9]. On the Dimensity 900 (2×A78 + 6×A55) report 2, 4 and 8 threads. Big.LITTLE matters [2410.03613 p.2].
  5. Warm vs cold. Separate (i) load time, (ii) first run (XNNPACK weight-cache creation, mmap faults: "During the first run, the latency and memory usage may differ" [DOC gemma-4 card]; mmap cold start up to 20 s [2410.03613 p.16]), and (iii) warm steady state. One discarded warm-up [ExecuTorch p.9; 2603.23640 p.5].
  6. Prompt and decode lengths. Fixed token counts, not text. Use LiteRT's flags --benchmark_prefill_tokens / --benchmark_decode_tokens (1024/256 is their published setting [DOC gemma-4 card]). Use llama-bench -p/-n, and SipLLM's equivalent. Also 128/128 and 512/256 (PowerInfer-2 p.9, ExecuTorch p.9, 2410.03613 p.5). Greedy decoding, EOS ignored.
  7. Context / KV capacity. Equal KV capacity. The guide says that for Gemma-3 1B, MediaPipe's maxTokens "must match the context size built into the model" [DOC MediaPipe guide]. The ekv1280 filenames suggest the same holds for the TinyLlama and Qwen2.5 .task files [INFERENCE; verify by trying another maxTokens]. So set SipLLM and llama.cpp --ctx 1280 for those models. Note that SipLLM prices KV at full ctx [SIPLLM]. Report KV precision (SipLLM fp32 or --kv-q8; LiteRT unknown).
  8. Peak memory on Android (the core metric).
    • Primary: kernel high-water RSS, /proc/<pid>/status VmHWM, which equals getrusage ru_maxrss on Linux/Android. That is what LiteRT reports on Android [DOC gemma-4 card] and what SipLLM's phone script samples [SIPLLM]. For an app-hosted runtime (MediaPipe AAR), read VmHWM of the app process via adb shell cat /proc/$(pidof …)/status.
    • Mandatory split: at peak, read /proc/<pid>/smaps_rollup (or status RssAnon / RssFile / RssShmem, VmSwap). mmap runtimes (llama.cpp default; LiteRT embeddings [DOC gemma-4 card]) carry reclaimable file pages in RSS [DOC Android memory]. SipLLM's pread streaming carries its file data in the page cache, outside RSS [INFERENCE, §3]. Run llama.cpp both with and without --no-mmap.
    • System-level check: record /proc/meminfo MemAvailable and Cached before, at peak and after. Run the external clamp test (a memory-hog leaving MemAvailable ≈ B). That is the only test that compares "bounded memory" claims fairly across mmap, pread and resident runtimes.
    • Secondary: dumpsys meminfo <pkg> PSS and Graphics for app-based runtimes and any GPU run [DOC Android memory §RSS/PSS/USS].
    • Never mix counters: LiteRT macOS figures are phys_footprint and Windows figures are PrivateUsage [DOC gemma-4 card]. Neither is comparable with /usr/bin/time -l maximum RSS [INFERENCE]. On macOS, report both RSS and phys_footprint for every runtime.
  9. Energy. Gold standard: Monsoon with battery bypass, USB lanes off [MELT p.3–4]. Unrooted, acceptable: a BatteryManager current × voltage logger at 100 ms, 2 h idle baseline subtracted, trapezoidal integration, wireless ADB [2603.26603 p.6–7]. Rooted: /sys/class/power_supply at 100 ms [llm.npu p.10; 2410.03613 p.13]. Report J/token for prefill and decode separately. Caveat: 2603.23640 refused to report phone energy from BatteryManager [p.5]. State accuracy limits. SipLLM's streaming adds flash-read energy, so energy is required before any e-waste usefulness claim [INFERENCE].
  10. Repetitions and statistics. At least 10 runs (PowerInfer-2 p.9; PalmBench p.6). Best practice is 30 [2603.26603 p.5]. Report median with IQR or min/max [ExecuTorch p.9]. Test normality. Mobile data are non-normal, so use Friedman plus Wilcoxon with Holm correction [2603.26603 p.7–8]. Report device, Android version, runtime commit, artifact hash, quant, ctx, threads, affinity, temperatures and clocks, matching SipLLM's own Rule 1 reporting [SIPLLM CLAUDE.md].
  11. Quality. Because artifacts differ, run one fixed eval through each runtime's generation API: greedy exact-match on a small set plus greedy-output agreement with the HF reference. Speed without quality is meaningless across quantizers [2603.23640 p.1]. Example: Qwen2-7B GSM8K is 77.43 under llama.cpp group-32 quant but 22.26 under QNN per-channel INT4 on the same phone [PowerInfer-2 p.12 Table 7].
  12. How each runtime is executed. SipLLM, llama.cpp and **LiteRT-LM all run from adb shell** (litert_lm_main built with bazel build --config=android_arm64, pushed to /data/local/tmp, with --report_peak_memory_footprint) [DOC build-and-run]. **MediaPipe's documented Android path is an app (AAR / LlmInference Kotlin API)** [DOC Android guide]. It needs an APK harness, and its memory must be read from the app process. That makes it the weakest-controlled leg. Say so.
  13. Device floor. MediaPipe claims optimization for "Pixel 8 and Samsung S23 or later" [DOC]. The Dimensity 900 (2021) may fail. Record failures as data. LiteRT-LM publishes no minimum RAM.
4.4 Minimal first experiment (cheapest decisive test of the standing goal)

TinyLlama-1.1B and SmolLM2-135M on the OnePlus IV2201 (Dimensity 900). LiteRT-LM litert_lm_main CPU vs SipLLM (Q8_0 resident; --ram-budget equal to LiteRT's measured VmHWM, 512M and 256M) vs llama.cpp (mmap and --no-mmap). 1024/256 tokens, 4 threads pinned to the same cores, 10 runs plus a 20-iteration plateau, VmHWM plus RssAnon/RssFile plus MemAvailable, BatteryManager energy. MediaPipe follows once an APK harness exists. This produces table (A) and table (B) and settles "better than LiteRT" as a measured RAM claim or a measured RAM-plus-speed claim.

5. Top lessons for SipLLM, ranked by expected impact
  1. Prove streamed speed under memory pressure before publishing C1. The RSS ceiling holds. But streaming uses buffered pread with no cache bypass (include/llm/file_backing.h), and SipLLM's own data implies about 6.3 GB/s (M3) and 2.8 GB/s (phone) effective reads for TinyLlama. Those rates are likely page-cache speeds; storage specs were not checked.
    • Add posix_fadvise(DONTNEED) per consumed layer, or O_DIRECT / F_NOCACHE.
    • Add an external RAM-clamp run to scripts/device and scripts/bench_ram_budget.sh.
    • Report RssAnon and RssFile for every runtime so the ratio against mmap runtimes is fair. [FlexInfer p.5, LLM in a flash p.7, Android doc]
  2. Run the LiteRT-LM shell head-to-head now. Both of SipLLM's main benchmark models have official LiteRT artifacts, and litert_lm_main runs from adb shell with a peak-memory report using the same Android counter (ru_maxrss equals VmHWM). This is the cheapest path to the standing goal's missing evidence. (§4.4)
  3. **Reposition C1 against FlexInfer and llama.cpp --fit.** The novelty is the *contract* (all-contributor ledger plus refusal plus measured VmHWM sweep on an unrooted phone), not streaming, prefetch or partial residency. Extend the on-phone budget sweep beyond TinyLlama to 8B and 13B, and exercise the refusal path on device.
  4. Measure energy per token with the 2603.26603 BatteryManager pipeline. Without it, the e-waste framing has no number behind it (streaming adds flash-read energy).
  5. Test FlexInfer-style balanced per-layer pinning against whole-layer pinning in src/mem_plan.cpp and the loader ring. [HYPOTHESIS] At equal budget it raises tok/s by smoothing per-layer I/O [FlexInfer p.4 Fig.3, ablation p.6].
  6. Frame C4 as the streaming extension of Dettmers / 2505.15030, and expect a crossover map rather than a win. Build the Pareto harness (quality × tok/s × J/token at fixed B).
  7. Kernel headroom is known: an i8mm path (MNN tile table [MNN-LLM p.4 Table 2]; I8MM gives "twice the speed of the NEON dot product-based implementation" [DOC MediaPipe blog 2024]) and LUT kernels for ≤3-bit (T-MAC). These matter more for prefill (SipLLM at 66% of llama.cpp on phone [SIPLLM]) than for C1.
6. Open questions I could not resolve
  1. Phone RAM discrepancy: the brief says "~6 GB". The CHANGELOG says 8 GB, and bench/results/device-OnePlus-IV2201-2026-10-19.json records mem_total_mb: 7550 (consistent with 8 GB). The brief should be corrected.
  2. CHANGELOG competitor anchor (Gemma3-1B: MediaPipe 138/50 in 982 MB; LiteRT-LM 379/55 in 1009 MB) cites a model card that is now gated. Unverifiable today. The current overview shows Gemma3-1B S24U CPU at 177/33 tok/s with no memory figure.
  3. Does litert_lm_main load legacy .task files (TinyLlama, Qwen2.5 artifacts), or only .litertlm? If only the latter, TinyLlama needs a LiteRT Torch reconversion, which needs a 64 GB Linux host [DOC MediaPipe guide].
  4. Does MediaPipe LLM Inference load .litertlm (SmolLM2) on Android? The docs say Gemma .litertlm is "ready to use with the LLM Inference API for Android and Web". Generality is unknown.
  5. Memory counters used by PowerInfer-2, llm.npu, Hermes, 2410.03613 and 2603.26603 are not stated in the pages I read. Their memory numbers cannot be compared with VmHWM.
  6. KV precision inside LiteRT .task and .litertlm artifacts is undocumented in what I read. iso-KV comparisons are uncertain.
  7. Whether a Google-published QAT Q4_0 GGUF of Gemma-3-1B exists. Not checked; needed for the int4-vs-int4 pair.
  8. How much of SipLLM's measured streamed decode (M3 and phone) came from the page cache. It needs one run with a cache bypass or a clamp to answer.
  9. ExecuTorch's LiteRT CPU decode numbers (e.g. Qwen3-0.6B at 12 tok/s on S25U) are far below Google's model-card numbers for comparable models. Either configuration or version. Not resolved. Remeasure; do not quote.
Domain C: Pooled E-Waste & Distributed Weak Devices — (23 Source Records Analyzed)

Cluster C — pooled inference across weak/edge/home devices + e-waste and memory-economics framing

Local, untracked working file (2026-10-23). Never commit. Labels: [PAPER p.N] = the paper's own claim/number; [SIPLLM] = SipLLM fact from BRIEF.md or the repo source; [INFERENCE] = my reasoning; [HYPOTHESIS] = needs an experiment. PDFs live in /Users/mobile/Desktop/Dev/sipllm/.claude-artifacts/research/C-pooled-ewaste/papers/ (text dumps from pdftotext -layout in papers/txt/, page-split helper papers/pg.py; fetched READMEs in papers/repos/).

STATUS: complete for this pass (2026-10-23). Section order follows BRIEF.md: search log (§1), per-paper records (§2), framing evidence (§3), claim verdicts (§4), lessons (§5), open questions (§6). §0 is the SipLLM source check.

0. What SipLLM LAN pooling actually does (read from source, not from docs)

Source read: /Users/mobile/Desktop/Dev/sipllm/include/llm/lan.h, /Users/mobile/Desktop/Dev/sipllm/src/lan.cpp, /Users/mobile/Desktop/Dev/sipllm/src/runtime.cpp (lines 40-100, 380-420).

  • [SIPLLM] Topology: plain pipeline by contiguous layer range. Coordinator owns layers [0,a) plus tokenizer, embeddings, final norm and LM head; each worker owns one contiguous range; ranges must tile [0,n_layers) exactly (parse_nodes, runtime.cpp:388-395). Per hop only the residual stream crosses the wire (n x dim fp32), synchronous request/response over TCP (RemoteExecutor::run). One coordinator per worker at a time; no micro-batching, no pipelining across tokens or requests.
  • [SIPLLM] Per-device ceiling: each node builds its own Runtime with its own --ram-budget; the planner (plan_memory) is run with plan_cfg.n_layers = layer_end - layer_begin, the KV cache is allocated only for the node's layers, and a worker (layer_begin > 0) forces stream_lm_head = true (runtime.cpp:47-57, 99-100). A node whose budget is impossible throws "RAM budget impossible" at construction. So "each device under its own ceiling, refusing if infeasible" is real in the code.
  • [SIPLLM] Layer ranges are MANUAL (--nodes host:port:A-B,...). There is no partitioner, no profiler, no device selection, no load balancing, no re-partitioning, no fault tolerance (a dropped worker throws).
  • [SIPLLM] The handshake Fingerprint requires equality of: model file size, an FNV-1a hash over the full tensor table, n_layers, dim, ctx, KV precision, EFFECTIVE --fast (requested AND int8_kernels_available(), neon.cpp:73, which checks HWCAP_ASIMDDP), residency, layer range (lan.cpp:182-200). Consequences:
    • every node must hold the FULL GGUF file on its own storage (file size + whole tensor table are checked), not just its slice — a 7.87 GB 13B file on every 16-32 GB discarded phone;
    • a mixed pool of SDOT (ARMv8.2+dotprod) and non-SDOT (ARMv8.0, e.g. Cortex-A53/A73-era) phones is refused whenever --fast is on: the effective flag differs, so the handshake fails. --fast must be off on every node, which forfeits the int8 kernels on the strong nodes too. [INFERENCE] This hits exactly the e-waste mix.
  • [SIPLLM] Security: pre-shared token, constant-time compare, bounded lengths, no encryption (activations leak prompt content on the LAN).
  • [SIPLLM] Measurement status: verified ONLY over loopback on one Mac (identical output to single machine); never run across separate devices; no per-node peak RSS, tok/s, or network numbers exist (BRIEF.md). The worker does print getrusage peak RSS on disconnect (lan.cpp:353-356), so the instrument exists.
