2. Per-paper records (most relevant first)
prima.cpp — Prima.cpp: Fast 30-70B LLM Inference on Heterogeneous and Low-Resource Home Clusters
  • Cite: Zonghang Li, Tao Li, Wenjiao Feng et al., ICLR 2026, arXiv:2504.08791v3 (4 Jul 2026), https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.08791 ; code https://github.com/OpenCPIL/prima.cpp. Read: FULL main text (pp.1-11) + appendix A.1-A.3, A.8-A.19 (pp.12-19, 24-37); formula pages 17-20 skimmed; pp.20-23 (A.4-A.7) only grepped.
  • Problem: run 30-70B models on a household cluster (laptops, desktops, phones, tablets) whose AGGREGATE RAM+VRAM is smaller than the model, over Wi-Fi, mixed OS, without dedicated hardware [PAPER p.1-2].
  • Mechanism:
    • Built on llama.cpp (20K LoC added) [PAPER p.7]. Weights are mmap'ed; CPU-side layers are paged in on demand and evicted by the OS; only KV cache, compute buffers and GPU-resident layers are pinned [PAPER p.28].
    • Pipelined-ring parallelism (PRP): the layer sequence is cut into windows assigned round-robin to devices in a ring, k rounds per token; each device prefetches only one window per round so prefetched pages are not evicted before use (fixes the "prefetch-release conflict" of mmap prefetch) [PAPER p.3-4, A.1-A.2 pp.12-13].
    • Halda scheduler: an integer linear-fractional program over per-device window size w_m and GPU-layer count n_m, with a latency model covering compute, memory access, disk reload (OS-specific: macOS with/without Metal, Linux, Android incl. swap-out), and network; solved as a set of ILPs (HiGHS) with iterative device-set assignment; 10-12 ms for 4-32 devices [PAPER p.5-6, A.3 pp.13-20].
    • Automatic device selection: devices assigned <=1 layer are dropped [PAPER p.7, A.7 p.24].
    • Extras: speculative decoding, continuous batching, node join/drop, cross-LAN proxy [PAPER A.9, A.11, A.12].
  • Memory regime: NOT an operator-set ceiling. Halda's RAM constraint uses measured available memory d_avail (plus swap-out on Android) as a model input, and for device sets M1-M3 it deliberately plans RAM usage ABOVE available RAM ("devices should overload") and relies on mmap reclaim [PAPER p.5, eqs. 27-32 p.19]. Their headline memory metric is "memory pressure" = delta mem_available / mem_total, which EXCLUDES reclaimable page cache by definition [PAPER p.9 fn.11]; per-device footprints including page cache are only plotted (Fig. 9, p.25), and for exo/dllama 14-70B those are "estimated", not measured [PAPER p.24]. No peak-RSS ceiling is stated or verified. Experiments ran on idle systems except A.18 [PAPER p.35].
  • Hardware evaluated: main testbed D1-D4 (+D5, D6 in appendix): Mac M1 (2.4 GiB avail), Intel i9 laptop + RTX 3070 (4.1 GiB avail RAM, 8 GiB VRAM), i9 desktop + 2080 Ti (9.7 GiB, 11 GiB VRAM), Huawei Mate40 Pro Kirin 9000 (1.9 GiB avail, HarmonyOS, Termux), Honor Pad Dimensity 8100 (5.1 GiB, Termux), Mac Air i5 (6.8 GiB); Wi-Fi 320-610 Mbps, 3-7 ms [PAPER Table 3 p.7]. Aggregate RAM+VRAM 37 GiB for D1-D4 [PAPER p.7]. 10-node testbed in A.19 (8-32 GiB RAM machines) [PAPER p.36].
  • Headline numbers (theirs): Llama-3 70B Q4K TPOT 674 ms/token, TTFT 1793 ms on D1-D4; llama.cpp on D3 alone 10120 ms/token; exo and dllama OOM at 70B [PAPER Table 4 p.8]. Memory pressure <= 6% per device at 70B vs exo/dllama > 50% at smaller sizes [PAPER Table 5 p.9]. Speculative decoding: Llama 3.3-70B 442 ms/token, Qwen-2.5-32B 38 ms/token (26 tok/s) on a 4x Linux 8 GiB + 2x 4090 testbed [PAPER Table 10 p.26]. Energy per 1K tokens measured (PowerMetrics, NVIDIA counters, powercap, Ludashi): distributed run cuts total energy 57-90% vs single-device llama.cpp; cloud (8x RTX 6000 Ada) 28% lower than the local cluster [PAPER A.13 pp.29-30, Fig. 13]. Busy-system test: background TikTok/YouTube/Zoom/Work/Download raise TPOT 5-18%, a 3D game 66% [PAPER A.18 p.35]. Context: 70B TPOT flat for 1-4K ctx on the low-mem testbed, then rises or OOMs [PAPER A.15 p.33].
  • Limitations (theirs + mine): the phones are a Kirin 9000 Mate40 Pro and a Dimensity 8100 Honor Pad with 1.9 and 5.1 GiB AVAILABLE RAM [PAPER Table 3 p.7] (total RAM and release years are not given in the paper; [INFERENCE, not from paper] these are recent high-end SoCs, not discarded 2-3 GB devices); phones used only via Termux; idle-system default; OOM-freedom relies on the OS reclaiming page cache, so peak RSS (which counts file-backed resident pages) is unbounded by design [INFERENCE]; requires the FULL model file on every device's disk — verified in the repo README: every rank runs -m download/qwq-32b-q4_k_m.gguf and the Docker recipe copies the whole GGUF into each container (https://github.com/OpenCPIL/prima.cpp README, "Build, Download, and Test" and Docker sections, README lines ~218-253 as fetched 2026-10-23); the Docker recipe emulates devices with --memory=8gb --memory-swap=8gb cgroup limits; Halda's model is worst-case additive (sum of device latencies, not max) [PAPER A.3 p.13]; the 70B slow single-device baseline is llama.cpp thrashing, i.e. no comparison against an explicit-streaming single device.
  • Pre-empts SipLLM claims:
    • C2: PARTIAL-to-FULL on the headline. Pooling weak home devices (including phones) to run a model larger than their aggregate memory, with disk offload and prefetch, measured on real hardware, is done and published at ICLR 2026. What is NOT done: an operator-set per-device peak-RSS ceiling, measured peak RSS, discarded/low-end phones, CPU-only phone pools.
    • C1: NONE for the hard-ceiling part (they explicitly design the opposite: soft, OS-arbitrated residency).
    • C7: FULL as a mechanism (read-only mmap streaming of weights from disk is their core; they note downloads' "continuous writes contend with prima.cpp's read-only mmap" [PAPER p.35]). They do not argue endurance.
  • Lessons for SipLLM:
    • The prefetch-release conflict is SipLLM's reason for explicit pread into owned buffers; prima.cpp's own measurements (Fig. 14, p.31: at 16 GiB RAM the whole 25 GiB CPU share is reloaded during compute) are third-party evidence FOR SipLLM's design — cite it in docs/product/why-not-mmap.md.
    • A.18 is the strongest argument AGAINST a pinned ceiling: on Android/iOS, a process that yields page cache is less likely to be killed or to jank the foreground app. SipLLM's anonymous (owned) buffers are not reclaimable; under LMK they may get the process killed. This must be measured, not argued (see C2 experiment).
    • SipLLM needs at minimum an automatic layer-range partitioner (--nodes auto) using each worker's planner result (max pinnable layers at its budget) and a measured tok/s per node; Halda's device-dropping rule ("drop nodes that get <=1 layer") is a cheap heuristic to copy into src/lan.cpp / the CLI.
    • Report memory pressure (delta MemAvailable) next to peak RSS so results are comparable with prima.cpp.
  • Contribution opportunity: [HYPOTHESIS] On the same 3-4 low-RAM Android phones and the same model, SipLLM with per-node --ram-budget keeps every node's kernel peak RSS <= its ceiling AND is killed by LMK no more often than prima.cpp while a foreground app runs, at a stated tok/s cost. Falsified if peak RSS ever exceeds the ceiling, or if SipLLM gets LMK-killed where prima.cpp survives.
Galaxy — Galaxy: A Resource-Efficient Collaborative Edge AI System for In-situ Transformer Inference
  • Cite: Shengyuan Ye, Jiangsu Du, Liekang Zeng et al., IEEE INFOCOM 2024, arXiv:2405.17245v1, https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.17245. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1, 5-7 read in full; pp.2-4 and 8-10 grepped for memory budget, testbed, tables).
  • Problem: accelerate single-shot Transformer inference in a smart home by pooling trusted idle edge devices [PAPER p.1].
  • Mechanism:
    • Hybrid model parallelism: tensor parallelism for MHA (split by head) and MLP (split by row), sequence parallelism for the "connective" ops (LayerNorm, dropout, residual) [PAPER p.3-5].
    • Heterogeneity- and memory-aware workload planning: minimise straggler latency subject to l * (M_att * a_d/A + M_mlp * b_d/B) < Budget_d for each device d [PAPER eq.5 p.5]. Two-step heuristic: balance by compute capacity, then shift overflow from over-budget devices to devices with spare budget; if any device is still over budget, "Exit with Fail" — the devices "are not capable of accommodating the target model" [PAPER Alg.1 lines 23-24, p.6].
    • Tile-based overlap of ring AllGather / ReduceScatter with GEMMs [PAPER p.6-7].
  • Memory regime: ENFORCES an operator-set per-device budget in the planner, and REFUSES when infeasible. Budgets set by the experimenters: 1.5 GB for Nano-M (homogeneous); 1.5 / 1.2 / 0.7 GB for Nano-L / Nano-M / Nano-S (heterogeneous) [PAPER p.7]. BUT the budget constraint prices only weight blocks (eq.5) — no KV cache, activations, runtime base or scratch — and all weights stay resident (no streaming, no offload). Measured memory against the budget is not reported; OOMs for baselines are reported [PAPER Table IV p.8].
  • Hardware evaluated: Jetson Nano boards, CPU only by default (GPU in §IV-E), frequency modes to emulate heterogeneity (Nano-L 1.47 GHz, Nano-M 825 MHz, Nano-S 403 MHz) [PAPER Table II-III p.8].
  • Headline numbers (theirs): 1.3x-2.5x latency reduction vs Megatron-LM-style TP and SP baselines; models from 66M (DistilBert) to 2.7B (OPT-XL) parameters; GLUE QNLI classification, avg sequence length 284 [PAPER p.7-8, Table IV]. No decode/tok/s numbers (encoder-style single-shot workload).
  • Limitations: models fit in the aggregate memory; no autoregressive generation; budgets cover weights only; Jetson dev boards, not phones; no peak-RSS measurement.
  • Pre-empts SipLLM claims:
    • C2: PARTIAL — "each device under its own operator-set memory budget, planner refuses if infeasible" exists in the distributed setting (2024). Missing: the model being larger than aggregate memory, streaming, whole-process ledger, measured RSS, LLM decode.
    • C1: PARTIAL for "planner refuses an infeasible budget" (weights-only pricing, distributed, not single node).
  • Lessons for SipLLM: cite Galaxy as prior art for per-device budget + refusal; SipLLM's differentiator must be stated as "the ceiling covers the whole measured peak-RSS ledger (process base, scratch, KV at full ctx, ring, pinned layers), and is verified against kernel peak RSS", not "per-device budgets".
  • Contribution opportunity: [HYPOTHESIS] A weights-only budget (Galaxy eq.5) under-predicts real peak RSS on phones by a large, measurable margin; SipLLM's itemised ledger predicts it within X%. Test: run SipLLM with the ledger truncated to weights-only vs full, record /usr/bin/time peak RSS vs predicted, across ctx 512-4096.
TPI-LLM — TPI-LLM: Serving 70B-scale LLMs Efficiently on Low-resource Edge Devices
  • Cite: Zonghang Li, Wenjiao Feng, Mohsen Guizani, Hongfang Yu, arXiv:2410.00531v1 (1 Oct 2024; "under review"; published later as IEEE Trans. Services Computing 2025 per prima.cpp's reference list p.10, title "... on Low-resource Mobile Devices"), https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.00531 ; code https://github.com/Lizonghang/TPI-LLM. Read: FULL arXiv v1 (pp.1-19). The TSC version was NOT read.
  • Problem: single-user 70B inference on a few low-memory, CPU-only edge devices, keeping prompt data local [PAPER p.1-3].
  • Mechanism:
    • Tensor parallelism over attention heads and FFN rows; master holds embeddings and LM head; weight slices are pre-split and SHIPPED to workers (workers download only their slices) [PAPER Alg.1-2 p.5].
    • Star-based allreduce, justified by link latency (not bandwidth) dominating [PAPER Prop.1-2 p.5-6, A.1].
    • Sliding-window memory scheduler: a daemon thread preloads the next w blocks (attention/FFN) and frees used ones; steady-state conditions (Props. 3-4) for hiding disk I/O; optional retention of one FFN block every T [PAPER p.6-7, A.3-A.6].
    • Peak memory is predicted analytically: params-in-window x empirical scaling factor gamma (Prop.5, A.5 p.15-16).
  • Memory regime: reduces memory to a window of w blocks; no operator-set byte ceiling, no refusal; the "budget" is the window size w (count of blocks). How "peak memory footprint" was MEASURED is not stated anywhere in the paper [checked p.8-10, 18]. Emulated nodes were containers limited to 8 GB RAM + 4 GB swap [PAPER p.8], so values in Table 2 above 8 GB (e.g. 130 GB) for "scheduler disabled" cannot be measured footprints [INFERENCE].
  • Hardware evaluated: 8 emulated devices on 2x Xeon Gold 5220R via the Klonet emulator (8 logical cores, 8 GB RAM, 4 GB swap each; 300 Mbps, 1 ms) [PAPER p.8, A.7]; real testbed 4 laptops over Wi-Fi (MacBook Pro M1 8 GB, MacBook Air i5 8 GB, 2x Dell i7-1165G7 16 GB) [PAPER Table 7 p.18]. FP32 PyTorch, CPU only.
  • Headline numbers (theirs): Llama 2-70B FP32 on 8 emulated devices: 26.1 s/token, TTFT 29.4 s, 3.1 GB peak per device; Llama 3.1-70B 29.9 s/token, 11.3 GB [PAPER Table 1 p.8]. Llama 2-70B with N=2 devices: 3.7 GB per device [PAPER Table 2 p.8]. Real 4-laptop testbed: Yi-34B 29 s/token vs 55 s/token for Transformers+their scheduler on one device [PAPER Table 3 p.10].
  • Limitations: FP32 only, ~26-30 s/token at 70B (prima.cpp calls it "impractical" [prima.cpp p.2]); emulated devices; measurement method unspecified; TP needs 2 allreduces per layer, so link latency dominates on Wi-Fi.
  • Pre-empts SipLLM claims:
    • C2: PARTIAL — pooling low-memory CPU-only devices to run a model none could hold, with per-device streaming from disk and small per-device peak footprint, exists (2024). Missing: operator-set ceiling with refusal, quantized weights, phones, measured RSS method.
    • C1: PARTIAL for "run 70B in ~3 GB per device by streaming" (but no operator ceiling, no refusal, no measured-RSS method).
    • C7: FULL as mechanism (read-only weight streaming from disk with prefetch).
  • Lessons for SipLLM: (1) ship slices, not the full file, to workers (lan.cpp fingerprint currently forces the full file); (2) TPI-LLM's steady-state conditions (Prop.4: t_attn + t_allreduce >= tau_ffn, etc.) are a ready-made analytic check SipLLM's planner could print: "prefetch hides I/O iff compute(layer i) >= read(layer i+1)"; (3) TP over Wi-Fi is latency-bound — SipLLM's choice of pipeline is consistent with both TPI-LLM's analysis and prima.cpp's numbers (dllama ~150 ms per all-reduce [prima.cpp p.8]).
  • Contribution opportunity: [HYPOTHESIS] At equal per-device RAM (e.g. 512 MB-1 GB), int8/Q4 pipeline streaming (SipLLM) beats FP32 TP streaming (TPI-LLM) on Wi-Fi by >10x tok/s on the same phones because both bytes read per token and allreduce count drop. Test: same model (Llama-2-13B), same 3-4 phones, both systems, report tok/s and per-node peak RSS.
LOIP (formerly LIME) — LOIP: Collaborative LLM Inference with Offloading-based Pipeline Parallelism on Edge Devices
  • Cite: Mingyu Sun, Xiao Zhang, Shen Qu et al., arXiv:2512.21835v3 (22 Sep 2026; v1 Dec 2025 was titled "LIME: Accelerating Collaborative Lossless LLM Inference on Memory-Constrained Edge Devices"), https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.21835. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-3 and 10 read in full; pp.4-9 grepped for budget, scheduler and online-adaptation passages; result pages 11-49 are mostly figure/table pages, grepped pp.26, 42, 45-46).
  • Problem: run 13-70B unquantized models across heterogeneous memory-constrained Jetson devices where the aggregate memory may not hold weights + KV [PAPER p.1-2].
  • Mechanism:
    • Interleaved pipeline parallelism + per-device weight offloading to SSD; loading for the next stage overlaps compute and communication [PAPER p.2].
    • Offloading-aware cost model + DP allocation scheduler choosing layer assignment, offloaded layers and #stages [PAPER p.4-8].
    • Per-device budget Mem_i = "memory budget available for decoder weights and KV cache after accounting for other memory usage, including the operating system, runtime, and activations"; retained weights + loading buffer + peak resident KV must fit Mem_i [PAPER p.4-5, Table II p.6].
    • Online memory adaptation: token-count thresholds TS_ij at which more blocks are offloaded as KV grows (eq.8); KV cache temporarily moved to a peer with spare memory over the network [PAPER p.8-9].
    • UMA-aware loading with custom CUDA kernels [PAPER p.2].
  • Memory regime: operator/experimenter-set per-device budgets (emulated by "reserving" 3 GB / 5 GB / 1 GB on devices) [PAPER p.10]; planner-enforced; no measured peak memory vs budget reported (metric is ms/token only) [PAPER p.10].
  • Hardware evaluated: Jetson AGX Orin 64 GB, AGX Orin 32 GB, Xavier NX 16 GB (GPU, UMA), 100/200 Mbps links [PAPER Table III, p.10].
  • Headline numbers (theirs): up to 8.8x (single input) and 20.3x (concurrent) speedups over baselines; 70B on 2x Orin 64 + Orin 32 + NX 16: 1533.9 ms/token sporadic, 407.1 ms/token bursty [PAPER abstract, Table IV p.46].
  • Limitations: big Jetson boards (16-64 GB) with CUDA; budgets emulated by reservation; no CPU-only path.
  • Pre-empts SipLLM claims:
    • C2: PARTIAL — per-device memory budget + offloading + pipeline across devices for models larger than memory, with KV growth handled by evicting weights. Missing: low-RAM phones, CPU-only, measured RSS vs budget.
    • C5: PARTIAL (relevant to cluster owning C5) — KV growth under a fixed budget handled by offloading weights and moving KV to peers; KV entries beyond memory are evicted and recomputed for a baseline [PAPER p.10].
    • C6: PARTIAL — budgets on UMA devices count CPU and GPU allocations in one pool [PAPER p.1-3].
  • Lessons for SipLLM: LOIP's token thresholds are what SipLLM's planner avoids by pricing KV at full ctx; SipLLM could offer a "grow" mode (start with more pinned layers, unpin as KV grows) using the same ledger — but only if measured to help. Pass FlexGen ("search-based planner ... under a given memory budget" [PAPER p.1]) to the C1 owner as single-node prior art.
  • Contribution opportunity: [HYPOTHESIS] On CPU-only phones, pricing KV at full ctx (SipLLM) costs at most N pinned layers vs threshold-based unpinning (LOIP-style) and therefore at most Y% decode speed, while never exceeding the ceiling. Test: implement unpin-on-growth behind a flag, compare peak RSS and tok/s over a 4K-token generation.
LinguaLinked — LinguaLinked: A Distributed Large Language Model Inference System for Mobile Devices
  • Cite: Junchen Zhao, Yurun Song, Simeng Liu et al., arXiv:2312.00388v1 (Dec 2023; ACL 2024 demo per search result, not verified), https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.00388. Read: FULL (pp.1-12; references skipped).
  • Problem: distributed LLM inference across a user's trusted Android devices [PAPER p.1-2].
  • Mechanism:
    • torch.fx subgraph extraction -> ONNX sub-modules, int8 dynamic quantization via ONNX Runtime (CPU only) [PAPER p.8-9].
    • LP/MILP (Gurobi) model assignment minimising compute + transfer time, subject to per-device memory sum(M_mod * x) <= beta * M_a, where beta is "the maximum proportion of memory that each device can allocate for the inference task" and M_a is available memory [PAPER p.6].
    • Runtime load balancer moves "movable" overlapping sub-modules between adjacent devices; inactive sub-modules live on secondary storage and are loaded on demand [PAPER p.6-7]. ZeroMQ ROUTER-DEALER ring; residual-connection shortcuts [PAPER p.7-8].
  • Memory regime: per-device fraction-of-available-memory constraint (beta) in the planner, using DeepSpeed-profiled sub-module memory [PAPER p.6, p.9]. Not a measured ceiling; no memory measurements reported; models must fit in aggregate memory.
  • Hardware evaluated: 3x Google Pixel 7 Pro (Tensor G2, 12 GB) + 1x CUBOT X30 (Helio P60, 8 GB) [PAPER Table 1 p.8].
  • Headline numbers (theirs): BLOOM 1.1B/1.7B/3B; optimized assignment 1.11-1.61x throughput vs even split; multi- threading 1.73-2.65x; load balancing 1.29-1.32x; BLOOM-1.7B full precision 4.62 -> 3.59 s/token after balancing [PAPER p.9-12].
  • Limitations: models (<=3B) would fit on one 12 GB phone; 2-5 s/token; no memory evidence.
  • Pre-empts SipLLM claims: C2 PARTIAL (per-device memory fraction across phones; no ceiling verification, no model larger than aggregate memory).
  • Lessons: the beta knob is the closest phone-side precedent for a per-device operator limit; SipLLM should report the planner-predicted vs measured peak to show why a fraction-of-MemAvailable knob is insufficient.
  • Contribution opportunity: covered by the C2 experiment below.
EdgeShard — EdgeShard: Efficient LLM Inference via Collaborative Edge Computing
  • Cite: Mingjin Zhang, Jiannong Cao, Xiaoming Shen, Zeyang Cui, arXiv:2405.14371v1 (May 2024; IEEE IoT Journal 2024 per prima.cpp ref list), https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.14371. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-4 read; pp.6-9 grepped for memory, testbed and Table IV).
  • Problem: partition an LLM into shards across edge devices + cloud to cut latency / raise throughput [PAPER p.1-2].
  • Mechanism: profiling; joint device selection + layer partition via dynamic programming, with per-device memory budget Mem_j (a device cannot be assigned more than its budget) and a privacy constraint [PAPER p.3-6].
  • Memory regime: fits a fixed device budget; weights resident; full precision [PAPER Table I p.2, p.4].
  • Hardware: 12x Jetson AGX Orin 32 GB, 2x Orin NX 16 GB, 1x RTX 3090 server [PAPER Table III p.7].
  • Headline (theirs): Llama2-7B 75.88 ms/token vs 140.34 Edge-Solo; Llama2-70B runs on 11 AGX Orin + 1 RTX 3090 [PAPER Table IV p.8].
  • What it does NOT do / limitations: no streaming or offload; full-precision weights must fit the selected devices; Jetson boards (16-32 GB) plus a cloud GPU, not phones; no measured memory vs budget.
  • Pre-empts: C2 PARTIAL (memory-budgeted partitioning across devices; big devices, no streaming). C1/C7: NONE.
  • Lessons: DP partitioner over a layer chain with per-device memory caps is simple and directly applicable to SipLLM's contiguous-range topology (SipLLM's ranges are already contiguous, which is exactly EdgeShard's DP state).
  • Contribution opportunity: N/A beyond lesson 3 in §5 (an automatic --nodes auto DP using each node's own planner result as its memory cap).
Jupiter — Jupiter: Fast and Resource-Efficient Collaborative Inference of Generative LLMs on Edge Devices
  • Cite: Shengyuan Ye, Bei Ouyang, Liekang Zeng et al., IEEE INFOCOM 2025, arXiv:2504.08242v1, https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.08242. Read: PARTIAL (p.1 read; pp.2-9 grepped for memory budget, testbed, tables).
  • Mechanism: intra-sequence pipeline parallelism for prefill; outline-based pipelined decoding + speculative decoding for decode; planner sets transition cost T = +infinity when a device's memory budget is exceeded [PAPER p.5].
  • Memory regime: per-device budget in the planner; model resident (LOIP notes Jupiter "assumes the entire model remains resident" [LOIP p.10]).
  • Hardware: Jetson Xavier NX 8 GB, TX2 8 GB, Nano 8 GB [PAPER Table III p.7]. Llama2 7B/13B; up to 26.1x latency reduction; outline decoding slightly changes answer quality (GPT-4o-judged scores, Table VI p.9).
  • What it does NOT do / limitations: resident weights only; GPU (CUDA) Jetsons; outline-based decoding changes outputs (not lossless); no memory measurement vs budget.
  • Pre-empts: C2 PARTIAL (budgeted partitioning, resident weights). C1/C7: NONE.
  • Lessons: none specific beyond "budget-aware planners are standard in this literature"; output-changing decoding tricks would break SipLLM's golden-matrix rule, so not applicable.
  • Contribution opportunity: N/A.
MDI-LLM — Model-Distributed Inference for Large Language Models at the Edge
  • Cite: Davide Macario, Hulya Seferoglu, Erdem Koyuncu, arXiv:2505.18164v1 (May 2025), https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.18164. Read: FULL (pp.1-6).
  • Mechanism: layer partition across nodes (starter holds embeddings + head), "recurrent pipeline parallelism" over multiple concurrent samples, per-sample KV caches swapped in/out; TCP sockets, LitGPT/PyTorch [PAPER p.2-4].
  • Memory regime: just reduces per-device memory by splitting; measured total memory per node (VRAM+RAM shared) [PAPER Table I p.5]: NanoLlama-304M 2.15 GB on 1 node, 1.76 GB/node on 2, 1.56 GB/node on 3; Python libs alone 450 MB; 150-200 MB for HTTP/coordination [PAPER p.5].
  • Hardware: 3x Jetson TX2 8 GB, gigabit Ethernet; TinyLlama 1.1B in FP32 does not fit one board (4.6 GB params + runtime) [PAPER p.4-5].
  • Headline (theirs): generation rate rises with node count for multiple concurrent samples (Fig.3, p.5; no single tok/s headline); per-node memory falls with more nodes (Tables I-II, pp.5-6).
  • What it does NOT do / limitations: no ceiling, no offload/streaming; FP32 PyTorch; toy and 1.1B models; Jetson boards on wired Ethernet.
  • Pre-empts: C2 PARTIAL (minimal); C1/C7 NONE. A useful datum: Python runtimes spend 0.45-0.65 GB per node on libraries and coordination [PAPER p.5], which a C++ runtime does not [INFERENCE].
  • Lessons: report per-node memory split into model / KV / runtime overhead as they do (SipLLM's ledger already itemises this — print it per node in LAN mode).
  • Contribution opportunity: N/A.
Petals — Distributed Inference and Fine-tuning of Large Language Models Over The Internet
  • Cite: Alexander Borzunov, Max Ryabinin, Artem Chumachenko et al., NeurIPS 2023, arXiv:2312.08361v1, https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.08361. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-4, 8-10, appendix 15-17 via text; memory regime and results). The 2022 demo paper arXiv:2209.01188 was downloaded but not read (superseded).
  • Problem: run 50B+ models (Llama 2 70B, BLOOM 176B) by pooling volunteer GPUs over the Internet, with fault tolerance [PAPER p.1-2].
  • Mechanism: servers each hold a contiguous span of transformer blocks in GPU memory (8-bit weights), clients hold embeddings; dual attention caches for recovery from server failure; decentralized load balancing that places spans to maximise swarm throughput [PAPER p.2-7, A p.16].
  • Memory regime: fits a fixed device. "Normally, each server loads as many blocks as it can fit in its GPU memory, unless a user limits the number of blocks" [PAPER p.16] — the operator knob is a block count, not a byte ceiling. Aggregate swarm memory must hold the model.
  • Hardware: A100 servers, consumer GPUs (e.g. RTX 3070 in examples), geo-distributed real setup [PAPER p.7-10].
  • Headline (theirs): autoregressive generation >=10x faster than local RAM offloading; offloading upper bound ~0.18 tokens/s for BLOOM-176B over PCIe 4.0 x16 [PAPER p.10, App. B p.15].
  • What it does NOT do / limitations: GPU servers only; swarm must hold the whole model in aggregate GPU memory; no byte ceiling; not a LAN/phone system.
  • Pre-empts: C2 PARTIAL (pooling devices to run a model none could hold; GPU, Internet, resident weights). C1/C7 NONE.
  • Lessons: Petals' finding that for big models, shipping activations over a slow network beats swapping weights from RAM/SSD [PAPER p.2] is the core economic argument FOR pooling over single-device streaming; its fault-tolerant dual caches show what SipLLM's "a dropped worker throws" lacks.
  • Contribution opportunity: [HYPOTHESIS] On 2-3 GB phones there is a model size above which pooling N phones beats one phone streaming everything from its own flash (Petals' crossover, moved from GPUs/Internet to phones/Wi-Fi). Test: tok/s vs model size for 1, 2, 3, 4 phones with fixed per-node --ram-budget; report the crossover size.
3. Framing evidence (e-waste, embodied carbon, memory economics)
Junkyard Computing — Junkyard Computing: Repurposing Discarded Smartphones to Minimize Carbon
  • Cite: Jennifer Switzer, Gabriel Marcano, Ryan Kastner, Pat Pannuto; arXiv:2110.06870v2 (25 Oct 2022; published at ASPLOS 2023 per the brief — venue not re-verified from the PDF, which is the arXiv preprint), https://arxiv.org/abs/2110.06870. Read: FULL (pp.1-11; references skimmed).
  • Problem: can discarded smartphones serve as cloud compute, and when does reuse save carbon? [PAPER p.1]
  • Mechanism / method:
    • Computational Carbon Intensity CCI = (C_M + C_C + C_N) / lifetime ops, with C_M = 0 for reused devices [PAPER eq.1-2, p.4].
    • Benchmarks: GeekBench SGEMM / PDF / Dijkstra / memcpy on a PowerEdge R740, a 2007 ProLiant, a 2015 ThinkPad, a 2019 Pixel 3A and a 2012 Nexus 4 [PAPER Table 1, p.4]; power at load levels [PAPER Table 2, p.4].
    • Design study: cooling (phones throttle 40-50 C, shut off ~60-80 C), Wi-Fi limits (co-located Wi-Fi clusters infeasible beyond ~30 devices, citing Na et al.), batteries as UPS + carbon-aware "smart charging", battery replacement every 1.2-2.3 years [PAPER pp.5-7].
    • Prototype: 10 Pixel 3A phones (bought for $65 on eBay), Ubuntu Touch, Docker Swarm, DeathStarBench microservices over Wi-Fi [PAPER pp.1, 10].
  • Memory regime: N/A (microservices). Fig.1 shows popular Android phones' memory vs AWS T4g sizes [PAPER p.2]; the Nexus 4's compute subsystem includes "2GB RAM (Samsung K3PE0E00A)" [PAPER Table 3, p.6].
  • Headline numbers (theirs):
    • 1.5 billion smartphones sold per year, most decommissioned <2 years later; 60-70% neither thrown out nor recycled; 150 million discarded per year in the US [PAPER p.1].
    • Manufacturing = 70-85% of a smartphone's lifetime carbon [PAPER p.1]; 70-90% for phones and laptops, 20-50% for high-performance computing devices [PAPER p.3].
    • Pixel 3A needs 54 units to match one R740 on SGEMM; Nexus 4 needs 256 [PAPER Table 1, p.4]. Pixel 3A average power 1.54 W at the light-medium profile [PAPER Table 2, p.4].
    • Pixel 3A cloudlet vs c5.9xlarge after three years: 18.9x (SocialNetwork-Write), 9.8x (SocialNetwork-Read), 12.6x (HotelReservation) more carbon-efficient per query; cost $1,027.60 vs $40,404 over three years [PAPER p.11].
    • 3-year datacenter-scale CCI: smartphone 0.292 vs PowerEdge 0.598 mgCO2-e/gflop (SGEMM) [PAPER Table 4, p.9].
  • Limitations: no ML/LLM workload at all; light, non-CPU-bound microservices (6/10 phones barely used [PAPER p.11]); CCI assumes linear throughput scaling for microbenchmarks [PAPER p.5]; Wi-Fi scale ceiling ~30 devices; batteries are a recurring embodied-carbon and labour cost.
  • Pre-empts SipLLM claims: C2 framing PARTIAL — "pool discarded phones on a LAN to do useful cloud work, argued by embodied carbon" is published (2022/23). It does NOT pre-empt LLM inference, memory-bounded execution, or running a model no single phone can hold. C7: NONE.
  • Lessons for SipLLM: adopt CCI (gCO2e per generated token, C_M = 0 for reused phones, battery replacement term) as the paper's sustainability metric instead of inventing one; it needs measured energy/token, which SipLLM does not have (BRIEF.md: "Energy per token: never measured"). prima.cpp A.13 shows energy/token on phones is measurable with PowerMetrics/powercap/USB meters. Thermal throttling (sustained vs burst TDP) [PAPER p.6] will dominate decode on phones running 24/7 — measure sustained tok/s over 30+ minutes, not a 20-token burst.
  • Contribution opportunity: [HYPOTHESIS] A pool of N discarded 2-3 GB phones running a 7-13B Q4 model under SipLLM has lower gCO2e/token (CCI with C_M = 0) than a new 8 GB phone running the same model resident, over a 3-year horizon, despite lower tok/s. Test: measure W and tok/s per node with a USB power meter; plug into Junkyard eq.2.
Junkyard (2025 UCSD follow-up, grey literature) — Junkyard Computing: Repurposing Discarded Smartphones for Serverless Applications
  • Cite: Ajay Ramesh Ranganathan, Risab Sankar, Grant Cheng et al. (UCSD), PDF at https://kastner.ucsd.edu/wp-content/uploads/2025/06/admin/junkyard.pdf (ACM template with placeholder venue "2018", DOI "XXXXXXX" -> unpublished student report). Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-2, grep of pp.4-16).
  • Content: Kubernetes/Docker/Ray on Google Pixel Fold phones (12 GB RAM, Mali-G710) over Ethernet; grading jobs and a CV inference workload; "a small cluster of 4 phones can process 33 grading tasks in a minute" [REPORT p.1].
  • The one useful datum: the first listed limitation is "the lack of RAM on the phones ... 12GB of RAM should be enough ... For HPC workloads ... it is possible to run out of RAM very quickly"; swap did not help [REPORT p.13]. [INFERENCE] Even 12 GB phones hit RAM as the binding constraint in a phone cluster — supports SipLLM's premise, but this is a student report; cite only as anecdote.
Chasing Carbon — Chasing Carbon: The Elusive Environmental Footprint of Computing
  • Cite: Udit Gupta, Young Geun Kim, Sylvia Lee et al., arXiv:2011.02839 (HPCA 2021), https://arxiv.org/abs/2011.02839. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-7, 9; the mobile sections).
  • Numbers (theirs): manufacturing (capex) share of iPhone life-cycle emissions rose from 49% (iPhone 3GS, 2009) to 86% (iPhone 11, 2019) [PAPER p.2, Fig.2]; a second figure gives 40% (3GS) -> 75% (XR) [PAPER p.6, Fig.7] — the two numbers differ by device generation and figure, quote both with pages. Battery-powered devices: ~75% manufacturing, ~20% operational energy [PAPER p.5]. Amortizing a Pixel 3's manufacturing carbon needs ~3 years of continuous MobileNet inference — "beyond the typical smartphone lifetime" [PAPER p.2, Takeaway 6 p.6, p.7: MobileNet v3 on CPU 350 days, DSP ~1,200 days; "expected lifetime is three years (about 1,100 days)"].
  • Memory regime / hardware: N/A (carbon accounting; Apple product environmental reports, Pixel 3 measurements).
  • Limitations: relies on vendor LCA reports; mobile AI measured with MobileNet image classification, not LLMs.
  • Pre-empts SipLLM claims: NONE (C1-C7). Framing only.
  • Lessons: quote 49%->86% [p.2] and "~3 years to amortise" [p.2, p.7] as the reason second-life workloads matter.
  • Contribution opportunity: N/A (framing source).
Sustainable AI — Sustainable AI: Environmental Implications, Challenges and Opportunities
  • Cite: Carole-Jean Wu, Ramya Raghavendra, Udit Gupta et al., arXiv:2111.00364 (MLSys 2022), https://arxiv.org/abs/2111.00364. Read: PARTIAL (pp.4, 9-10 via text; targeted).
  • Numbers (theirs): "manufacturing carbon cost accounts for 74% of the total footprint of client devices", and client devices are under-utilised, making embodied carbon hard to amortise [PAPER p.9]; for Meta's large ML models, manufacturing carbon is roughly 50% of the location-based operational footprint [PAPER Fig.5 caption, p.4].
  • Memory regime / hardware: N/A (fleet-level carbon analysis at Meta).
  • Limitations: client-device numbers are cited from another source (its ref. [19]), not measured in this paper.
  • Pre-empts SipLLM claims: NONE. Framing only.
  • Lessons: adds "under-utilised client devices" — a pooled LLM service raises utilisation of devices already paid for (C_M sunk). [INFERENCE]
  • Contribution opportunity: N/A (framing source).
ACT — ACT: Designing Sustainable Computer Systems With An Architectural Carbon Modeling Tool
  • Cite: Udit Gupta, Mariam Elgamal, Gage Hills et al., ISCA 2022, pp.784-799 (citation details from search results; NOT verified from the paper). Read: NOT READ — the PDF was not obtained (only ACM/Meta landing pages; arXiv has ACT v3 2606.16889 from 2026, also not read). Do not cite ACT numbers from this file.
  • Secondhand only: Junkyard says ACT "only has estimates for ICs ... but does not consider all elements of the phone (e.g., battery, display, PCB, and chassis, which contribute ~1/3)" and that Gupta et al. describe the carbon benefits of "repurposing smartphones as an SSD storage system" [Junkyard p.3, p.5]. If the paper wants a "reuse" precedent from ACT, someone must read ACT §Reuse first.
CO2-Meter — CO2-Meter: A Comprehensive Carbon Footprint Estimator for LLMs on Edge Devices
  • Cite: Zhenxiao Fu, Chen Fan, Lei Jiang, arXiv:2511.08575v1 (Nov 2025; AAAI 2026 per search result, not verified), https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.08575. Read: PARTIAL (p.1 + targeted pp.6-7).
  • Numbers (theirs): unit-level embodied model for edge SoCs; on Jetson AGX Orin the GPU accounts for 1.91 kgCO2-eq and LPDDR5 DRAM 1.68 kgCO2-eq of embodied carbon, with 22.8% of embodied carbon "supporting LLM inference"; on RK3588, LPDDR5 DRAM adds 0.42 kgCO2-eq and 10.4% of embodied carbon is tied to LLM inference [PAPER p.6]. "Embodied carbon often dominates total emissions in edge SoCs" [PAPER p.7]. Faster LPDDR5 on RK3588 gives 2.3x speedup for a 50-token prompt at +27.5% embodied carbon [PAPER p.7].
  • Memory regime / hardware: N/A for memory policy; evaluated on Jetson AGX Orin, Orin NX, RK3588, RK3568 [PAPER p.6].
  • Limitations: an estimator (GNN energy predictor + unit-level embodied model); no reused-device scenario.
  • Pre-empts SipLLM claims: NONE.
  • Lessons: DRAM is a first-order embodied-carbon item on edge LLM hardware; CO2-Meter is a ready tool for SipLLM's sustainability section (operational + embodied per token).
  • Contribution opportunity: [HYPOTHESIS] A runtime that holds a model in a fixed small RAM budget lets an operator choose a device with less DRAM (or keep an old one) — quantify avoided DRAM embodied carbon with CO2-Meter's per-GB DRAM factor. Not tested.
GreenLLM — GreenLLM: Disaggregating Large Language Model Serving on Heterogeneous GPUs for Lower Carbon Emissions
  • Cite: Tianyao Shi, Yanran Wu, Sihang Liu, Yi Ding, arXiv:2412.20322v1 (Dec 2024), https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.20322. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-4, 11-15, 22, 48 via text).
  • What: reuse OLD datacenter GPUs (T4 2018, V100 2017) next to an A100 by disaggregating prefill/decode or running the speculative-decoding draft on the old GPU; up to 31.3-40.6% carbon saving at 90% SLO attainment [PAPER abstract, p.22]; cites an estimate of 1.2-5.0 million tons of generative-AI e-waste 2020-2030 [PAPER p.1, its ref. 46].
  • Memory regime / hardware: fits fixed devices (T4 16 GB, V100 16 GB, A100 40 GB [PAPER Table 1 p.4]); vLLM-based.
  • Limitations: datacenter GPUs; carbon savings mainly operational; old GPUs raise embodied share [PAPER p.22].
  • Pre-empts: the general claim "reusing old hardware for LLM inference to cut embodied carbon / e-waste" is published (datacenter GPU form). SipLLM's framing must be narrowed to old CONSUMER devices (phones/laptops), CPU-only, low RAM. C1-C7: NONE directly.
  • Lessons: its carbon model (embodied amortised over remaining lifetime + operational per request [PAPER eq.1, p.4]) is the datacenter analogue of Junkyard's CCI; cite both.
  • Contribution opportunity: N/A beyond the CCI hypothesis in the Junkyard record.
Global E-waste Monitor 2024 (UNITAR / ITU)
  • Cite: C.P. Baldé et al. (UNITAR SCYCLE / ITU), The Global E-waste Monitor 2024, released 20 March 2024, PDF https://ewastemonitor.info/wp-content/uploads/2024/03/GEM_2024_18-03_web_page_per_page_web.pdf (page numbers below are PDF page indices; the printed folios match on the pages checked, e.g. p.74). Read: PARTIAL (exec summary pp.7-16, chapter 3 pp.26-32).
  • Numbers (theirs): 62 billion kg of e-waste in 2022 (7.8 kg per capita); 22.3% (13.8 billion kg) documented as formally collected and recycled [GEM p.10, p.28]; up from 34 billion kg in 2010; projected 82 billion kg by 2030 with the documented rate falling to 20% [GEM p.16, p.26]; e-waste generation grew 5x faster than documented collection/recycling since 2010 [GEM p.16]. Small IT and telecommunication equipment (mobile phones, routers, PCs, ...) totalled 5 billion kg in 2022 [GEM p.27], of which ~1 billion kg (22%) was documented as collected [GEM p.28 figure]; small IT devices have low return rates "possibly because small IT devices contain personal data and consumers may therefore be reluctant to give them back" [GEM p.32].
  • Phone-count figures: WEEE Forum estimated ~5.3 billion of ~16 billion mobile phones would become waste in 2022 (International E-waste Day, Oct 2022). SOURCE QUALITY: only secondary coverage (AFP/WEF/Geneva Environment Network listing) was reachable; the WEEE Forum release itself was not opened. Use only with that caveat.
Smartphone replacement cycles
  • Counterpoint Research, "Memory crisis pushes global smartphone replacement cycles toward 4 years", 13 Aug 2026, https://counterpointresearch.com/en/reports/memory-crisis-pushes-global-smartphone%20replacement-cycles-toward-4-years (page fetched 2026-10-23): "Smartphone replacement cycles are extending to four years"; "higher memory and component costs drive up device prices"; expected to continue "through 2026 and 2027".
  • Secondhand only (search-result summaries, not opened): Counterpoint Nov 2025 — global cycle 43.4 months (2023), 43.0 (2024); Omdia via The Register (Apr 2026) — 4.2 years average device lifetime, up from 3.6 in 2020.
  • Junkyard (2022) instead says "most are decommissioned less than two years later" [Junkyard p.1] — the figures differ by definition (first owner vs device lifetime) and by year. [INFERENCE] Two-sided for SipLLM: longer cycles mean fewer phones discarded per year in the near term, but also that the installed base being kept is OLDER and lower-RAM, and that new phones are getting pricier because of memory — both strengthen the "make low-RAM devices useful" angle while weakening the "flood of free discarded phones" angle.
Memory and storage prices 2025-2026 (testing "RAM is expensive, storage is cheap")

All TrendForce figures are contract-price QoQ changes.

QuarterConventional DRAMNAND FlashSource (date)
4Q25 actual+45-50%(not in source)TrendForce press release, 26 Feb 2026, https://www.trendforce.com/presscenter/news/20260226-12937.html ; DRAM industry revenue $53.58B, +29.4% QoQ
1Q26 forecast+90-95% (PC DRAM >100%; server ~90%; LPDDR4X/LPDDR5X ~90%, "steepest increases on record")+55-60% (enterprise SSD +53-58%)TrendForce, 2 Feb 2026, https://www.trendforce.com/presscenter/news/20260202-12911.html
2Q26 forecast+58-63%+70-75%; mobile NAND (UFS/eMMC) "rise sharply", tightest NAND segmentTrendForce release of 31 Mar 2026 as republished by I-Connect007, https://iconnect007.com/article/149414/
3Q26 forecast+13-18% (server DRAM +13-18%)+10-15%TrendForce 9 Jul 2026 (server), https://www.trendforce.com/presscenter/news/20260709-13140.html ; conventional DRAM + NAND via Igor'sLAB 8 Jul 2026 citing TrendForce 3 Jul 2026 (secondary)
4Q26 forecast(number only in a chart image)+15-20% overall; enterprise SSD +23-28% (QLC for KV cache)TrendForce "4Q26 Memory Price Forecast", 24 Sep 2026, https://www.trendforce.com/research/download/RP260924PL
  • Stated causes: AI/data-centre demand, CSP long-term agreements, capacity shifted to HBM and server DRAM, NAND crowded out by enterprise SSD; "PC and smartphone vendors are reducing product capacities to curb NAND demand" (2Q26 release); consumer segments see small increases because notebook/smartphone shipments are falling (4Q26 page).
  • Spot snapshot (DRAMeXchange, fetched 2026-10-23): DDR5 16Gb (2Gx8) 4800/5600 session average $61.267 (last update Oct 23 2026); DDR4 16Gb 3200 $82.870; NAND wafer spot (last update Oct 12 2026): 512Gb TLC $18.833, 256Gb TLC $18.365, 128Gb TLC $9.967. [INFERENCE] Per GB, assuming each wafer quote is per die-equivalent of the stated density: DDR5 chip ~$61.27 / 2 GB = ~$30.6/GB; TLC NAND ~$0.29/GB (512Gb), ~$0.57/GB (256Gb), ~$0.62/GB (128Gb) -> DRAM roughly 50-100x NAND per GB at the component level. The 256Gb quote being almost equal to the 512Gb quote suggests legacy-part pricing or a unit convention I have not confirmed, so treat the range, not the top figure, as the number. Caveats: packaged chip vs die-equivalent wafer pricing, spot not contract, no controller/packaging for NAND.
  • Verdict on the premise: "storage is cheap" is NOT true in the absolute or trend sense — NAND contract prices rose roughly as steeply as DRAM through 1H26 (2Q26 NAND +70-75% vs DRAM +58-63%) and vendors are cutting device storage capacities. What survives is the RELATIVE claim: per GB, DRAM remains roughly 50-100x dearer than NAND at component spot prices (inference above), and on an existing (discarded) device the flash is already paid for. The paper must phrase it as "trade a relatively cheap, already-owned resource (flash bandwidth) for the scarcest one (DRAM)", not "storage is cheap". Also: on the device, flash CAPACITY becomes the constraint when every node stores the full GGUF (§0).
  • Earnings (secondary only, not primary): Micron FQ4-2026 (ended 3 Sep 2026) revenue $54.23B vs $11.32B a year earlier, GAAP gross margin 86.8% (Pulse 2.0 summary, 30 Sep 2026; Micron's own release not reached). Use only as colour.
Open-source systems without papers
  • distributed-llama (b4rtaz), README at https://github.com/b4rtaz/distributed-llama (fetched 2026-10-23): tensor parallelism over Ethernet; root node loads the model and forwards weights to workers; "The RAM usage of the neural network is split up across all nodes"; limitations: only 1, 2, 4 ... 2^n nodes, max nodes = number of KV heads, only Q40 weights with Q80 sync buffers (or F32) [README "Known Limitations", "Architecture"]. No disk streaming, no memory ceiling; prima.cpp measured ~150 ms per all-reduce on Wi-Fi and OOM at 70B on its testbed [prima.cpp p.8].
  • exo (exo-explore), README at https://github.com/exo-explore/exo (fetched 2026-10-23): automatic discovery, "topology-aware auto parallel" that "takes into account device resources and network latency/bandwidth", pipeline and tensor sharding, MLX backend, RDMA over Thunderbolt 5; macOS app or source on macOS/Linux; "Currently, exo runs on CPU on Linux"; placement previews report memory_delta_by_node [README "Features", "Run from Source (Linux)", API section]. No Android path; prima.cpp could not run exo on its phone because "it requires root access on the phone" [prima.cpp p.7]. Weights resident; partition by memory ratio (per prima.cpp p.3-4) — no disk offload, no ceiling.
  • prima.cpp README: "keeps its memory pressure below 10%"; Android/HarmonyOS via Termux; full GGUF on every device (see the prima.cpp record).
  • TPI-LLM code: https://github.com/Lizonghang/TPI-LLM returned 404 (raw README and GitHub API) on 2026-10-23 — could have moved; not verified further.
4. Claim verdicts (scope: C2 primary; C7, C1 where this cluster has evidence)
C2 — Pooled e-waste: bounded-RAM inference pooled across discarded/low-RAM devices on a LAN, each under its own ceiling, to run a model none could hold

Verdict: NARROWED. The headline idea is published; a narrow, measurable version survives, but SipLLM has no evidence for it yet and is behind prior art on scheduling.

What already exists (closest prior art first):

  1. prima.cpp (ICLR 2026) [PAPER pp.1-9]: pools weak home devices INCLUDING phones (Mate40 Pro with 1.9 GiB available, Honor Pad, via Termux) over Wi-Fi to run 30-70B models whose size exceeds the cluster's aggregate RAM+VRAM, by streaming weights from each device's disk (mmap + window prefetch), with an ILP scheduler (Halda) that picks layer windows per device under RAM/VRAM constraints and drops weak devices; measured TPOT, TTFT, energy, busy-system behaviour. -> "run a model none could hold, pooled across low-resource devices, larger than aggregate memory" is DONE.
  2. TPI-LLM (2024; IEEE TSC 2025) [PAPER pp.1-10]: CPU-only low-memory devices, per-device sliding-window streaming from disk, 70B at ~3.1 GB per device (method unspecified), but FP32 and ~26-30 s/token.
  3. Galaxy (INFOCOM 2024) [PAPER pp.5-7]: OPERATOR-SET per-device memory budgets (1.5/1.2/0.7 GB) enforced by the planner, which EXITS WITH FAIL if no allocation fits — i.e. "each device under its own ceiling, refuse if infeasible". Weights-only pricing, resident weights, models <= 2.7B.
  4. LOIP (2026) [PAPER pp.4-10]: per-device budget Mem_i net of OS/runtime/activations, weight offloading + interleaved pipeline, KV-growth thresholds; Jetson 16-64 GB boards.
  5. LinguaLinked (beta x available-memory constraint on phones) [PAPER p.6]; EdgeShard (Mem_j) [PAPER p.4]; Jupiter (T = +inf over budget) [PAPER p.5]; Petals (block-count limit per server) [PAPER p.16].
  6. Junkyard (2022/23) [PAPER pp.1-11]: pooling discarded phones on Wi-Fi for useful work, justified by embodied carbon — but not LLMs. GreenLLM (2024): reuse of old GPUs for LLM serving to cut carbon.

What none of them does (the surviving, narrower claim — every item is currently UNMEASURED for SipLLM):

  • (a) A per-node ceiling on the WHOLE measured peak RSS — process base, scratch, activations, KV priced at full ctx, streaming ring, pinned layers — verified against the kernel high-water mark on every node. Galaxy prices weights only (eq.5); LOIP budgets weights+KV after an estimated OS/runtime deduction; prima.cpp deliberately oversubscribes and reports Δ MemAvailable, not RSS; TPI-LLM does not say how it measured; none reports measured per-node memory vs its own budget.
  • (b) Explicit pread streaming into owned buffers instead of mmap/page cache — which prima.cpp's own Fig.14 (p.31) shows suffers the prefetch-release conflict (whole CPU share reloaded during compute at 16 GiB).
  • (c) Genuinely discarded / low-RAM (2-3 GB total) phones, CPU-only, quantized (Q4_K/Q8_0). prima.cpp's phones were a Kirin 9000 and a Dimensity 8100 device with 1.9 / 5.1 GiB available [PAPER Table 3 p.7] ([INFERENCE, not from paper] recent high-end SoCs); Galaxy/Jupiter/LOIP/EdgeShard used Jetson boards; LinguaLinked used a 12 GB Pixel 7 Pro and an 8 GB Cubot [PAPER Table 1 p.8].
  • (d) NOT a differentiator by itself: "an operator-set memory ceiling". Kernel-enforced ceilings already appear in this cluster's own evaluations: prima.cpp's Docker recipe runs each emulated device under --memory=8gb --memory-swap=8gb (README), and TPI-LLM's Klonet nodes are capped at 8 GB RAM + 4 GB swap [TPI-LLM p.8]. Anyone can run llama.cpp under a cgroup memory.max. What a cgroup does at the limit is reclaim page cache, thrash, or OOM-kill. What SipLLM's planner claims to add is pricing the whole ledger and REFUSING (or re-planning ctx / pinned layers / ring depth) BEFORE loading, so the run never reaches the kernel limit. That difference must be shown, not asserted (see the cgroup arm of the experiment below).

So the defensible statement is: "a pre-load planner that prices the whole-process peak-RSS ledger and refuses or re-plans before loading, giving per-node HARD ceilings verified by measured per-node peak RSS, on a pool of discarded 2-3 GB phones running a model larger than any one phone's RAM."

Where SipLLM is BEHIND prior art today [SIPLLM, from §0]:

  • Manual --nodes A-B layer ranges: no profiler, no partitioner, no device selection (prima.cpp Halda, LinguaLinked LP, EdgeShard/LOIP DP, Galaxy heuristic all automate this).
  • Strictly sequential request/response pipeline: no pipelining across rounds (prima.cpp PRP), no micro-batching (MDI-LLM, LOIP), so with N nodes only one node computes at a time. Per token the cost is the sum over nodes of (own layers' compute + own streaming reads) plus one network round trip per worker; pooling therefore adds RAM capacity, and adds speed only when the flash reads it avoids on each node outweigh the added hops. [INFERENCE]
  • Every node needs the full GGUF (TPI-LLM and LinguaLinked ship slices; prima.cpp also needs the full file).
  • --fast must match on every node, so a mixed SDOT / non-SDOT pool (typical of e-waste) runs without int8 kernels.
  • No fault tolerance (Petals, prima.cpp node drop/join). No encryption.
  • No multi-device measurement at all (loopback only).

The strongest adversarial point: prima.cpp A.18 [PAPER p.35] and §4.3 [PAPER p.9] argue that on phones the right policy is to YIELD memory (page cache the OS can reclaim) so the LLM is neither the cause of foreground jank nor the target of the low-memory killer. SipLLM's owned buffers are anonymous memory that LMK must kill the process to reclaim. A hard ceiling protects the OTHER apps only if it is set below what LMK tolerates; it does not make SipLLM unkillable. This is an empirical question that decides whether the C2 story is a strength or a liability.

Experiment that would discriminate (what to run on real discarded phones) [HYPOTHESIS]:

  • Hardware: 3-4 discarded Android phones with 2-3 GB RAM each, at least one ARMv8.0 core without dotprod (check asimddp in /proc/cpuinfo), plus their own eMMC/UFS; one Wi-Fi AP. Record each phone's flash sequential read MB/s.
  • Model: larger than any single phone's RAM (e.g. Llama-2-7B or 13B Q4_K_M, 3.8-7.9 GB) — ideally larger than the pool's combined RAM, so streaming is mandatory on every node.
  • Systems on the same phones, same GGUF, same prompt and ctx: SipLLM (--worker per phone with --ram-budget set to e.g. 60% of MemAvailable; --fast off everywhere, then on for the SDOT-only subset), prima.cpp (Termux, Halda automatic), and llama.cpp single-node mmap as the floor. TPI-LLM if its code can be found.
  • Cgroup arm (the cleanest test of what the planner adds over a kernel limit): on a Linux laptop node (Android needs root for cgroup control), run llama.cpp and prima.cpp under cgroup v2 memory.max (and memory.swap.max=0) equal to SipLLM's --ram-budget on the same model/ctx. Measure tok/s, major page faults (/usr/bin/time -v), memory.events oom_kill count and high/max events, and peak RSS. Prediction: SipLLM never touches the limit and has zero OOM kills with a stated tok/s; the cgroup-capped mmap runs either thrash (major faults per token >> layer count) or get OOM-killed as ctx grows. If they instead run at equal speed with no kills, the planner's value reduces to "refuses with an explanation", which is much weaker.
  • Per node, measure: kernel peak RSS (/usr/bin/time -v or the worker's getrusage line), ΔMemAvailable (prima.cpp's metric), per-node compute ms vs network wait ms (SipLLM already logs both), flash bytes read per token, decode tok/s, TTFT, energy (USB power meter -> J/token), and LMK kills / foreground frame drops while a foreground app (video playback) runs, as in prima.cpp A.18.
  • Falsifiable predictions: (P1) every SipLLM node's measured peak RSS <= its --ram-budget (any violation falsifies C1/C2 as stated); (P2) prima.cpp's per-node peak RSS exceeds the same number while its ΔMemAvailable stays low (shows the two metrics measure different things); (P3) SipLLM is LMK-killed no more often than prima.cpp under the foreground load — if it is killed more often, the hard-ceiling story for phones is weaker than the page-cache story and must be reported as such; (P4) pooled tok/s vs single-phone streaming tok/s: pooling must beat one phone streaming the whole model from its own flash, otherwise pooling only helps capacity, not speed (Petals' crossover argument, p.2).
C7 — Flash-friendly streaming (read-only, costs bandwidth not write endurance)

Verdict (this cluster's evidence): DEAD as a novelty; survives only as a framing sentence.

  • Every weight-offloading system in this cluster streams read-only weights from storage: prima.cpp (read-only mmap; "continuous writes contend with prima.cpp's read-only mmap of model weights" [PAPER p.35]), TPI-LLM (sliding-window loads from disk [PAPER p.6]), LOIP (weights staged on SSD [PAPER p.1-2]), LinguaLinked (inactive sub-modules on secondary storage [PAPER p.7]). None writes weights back. The property is inherent to weight streaming, not specific to SipLLM.
  • What remains true and citable: the swap-based fallbacks that streaming replaces DO write — TPI-LLM's Transformers baseline thrashes swap until OOM [TPI-LLM p.10]; prima.cpp's exo baseline "pinning model weights in memory can induce uncontrolled swapping" [prima.cpp p.23]. So "streaming avoids swap writes" is a fair contrast, but endurance was not measured by anyone here; claiming an endurance benefit needs a write-counter measurement (e.g. /proc/diskstats sectors written) of swap vs streaming. Also note the counter-pressure: NAND prices rose in 2026 (§3), so the "cheap storage" half of C7 must be stated relatively (per-GB vs DRAM), not absolutely.
C1 — Hard ceiling (evidence from this cluster only; the C1 owner has the main search)

Verdict from this cluster: NARROWED (not dead), with one important partial pre-emption.

  • Galaxy enforces operator-set per-device budgets and refuses infeasible configurations [PAPER p.6-7] — but prices weights only, keeps weights resident, does not measure memory, and is distributed. LOIP budgets weights + KV per device [PAPER p.4-5]. LinguaLinked uses a beta fraction of available memory [PAPER p.6]. TPI-LLM reports 3.1 GB per device for 70B but has no ceiling and no stated measurement method. prima.cpp explicitly rejects pinned residency.
  • Kernel-enforced operator ceilings (cgroup limits) are used as test harnesses by prima.cpp (README Docker recipe, --memory=8gb --memory-swap=8gb) and TPI-LLM (Klonet, 8 GB + 4 GB swap [PAPER p.8]); so "an operator-set ceiling" alone is not novel. The claim must be the pre-load planner: whole-ledger pricing + refusal/re-plan before loading, so the process never reaches a kernel limit (no reclaim thrash, no OOM kill).
  • None in this cluster combines: operator-set byte ceiling + itemised whole-process ledger + refusal + measured peak RSS <= ceiling, on CPU-only commodity hardware. Hand FlexGen (search-based planner under a memory budget, cited by LOIP p.1) and Lever 2605.16786 (flash-backed single-phone inference) to the C1 owner.
5. Top lessons for SipLLM (ranked by expected impact)
  1. Measure the pool before claiming anything (impact: decides C2 survival). Run the C2 experiment above, starting with 2 phones + 1 laptop and one model larger than any phone's RAM. The worker already logs peak RSS and compute ms; add per-hop network ms and flash bytes/token to the coordinator stats (src/lan.cpp RemoteExecutor already tracks wait_ms, bytes). Include the LMK/foreground-app test from prima.cpp A.18 and the cgroup memory.max arm (llama.cpp / prima.cpp capped by the kernel at the same ceiling) — that arm is what shows the planner adds something over a kernel limit.
  2. Re-position the claim around the ledger + measured RSS, not around "per-device budgets" (impact: avoids a reviewer kill). Cite Galaxy, LOIP, LinguaLinked, prima.cpp, TPI-LLM as prior art in docs/ and the paper; state the difference as "whole-process itemised ledger verified against kernel peak RSS" and quote prima.cpp's Fig.14 as third-party evidence for pread-over-mmap (docs/product/why-not-mmap.md).
  3. Automatic partitioning (impact: removes the largest functional gap vs prior art). Add --nodes auto: each worker reports its planner result at its own budget (max pinnable layers, predicted per-layer ms from a short profile, flash MB/s); the coordinator runs a DP over contiguous ranges (EdgeShard-style; SipLLM's contiguous-range topology is exactly that DP's state) minimising sum of per-node time + hops, and drops nodes that get <= 1 layer (prima.cpp's rule). Lives in src/lan.cpp + CLI; reuses plan_memory unchanged.
  4. Ship slices, relax the fingerprint (impact: makes e-waste pools possible at all). Let a worker hold only its layers' tensors (TPI-LLM Alg.2 / LinguaLinked style) and hash only the tensors it serves plus the shared config; and make --fast a per-node choice (the residual stream crossing the wire is fp32 regardless of each node's kernels, so [INFERENCE] arithmetic differences are limited to rounding inside a node — needs a golden check for output drift). Files: src/lan.cpp fingerprint()/describe_mismatch(), loader slice support.
  5. Overlap across nodes (impact: speed). A strictly synchronous chain leaves N-1 nodes idle; prima.cpp's PRP (k rounds, small windows) and micro-batching (MDI-LLM, LOIP) are the known fixes. For single-user decode on phones, at least prefetch the next layer on node i+1 while node i computes (SipLLM's per-node async prefetch already exists; ensure it is armed before the activation arrives).
  6. Adopt Junkyard's CCI (gCO2e/token with C_M = 0 for reused devices, battery term) and measure energy/token (USB meter / powercap, as in prima.cpp A.13). Without energy numbers the e-waste framing is rhetoric.
  7. Phrase the economics honestly: NAND prices rose as fast as DRAM in 1H26; the surviving argument is relative (~50-100x per GB at component spot, §3) plus "already owned flash on a discarded device".
6. Open questions I could not resolve
  • Does prima.cpp's per-node peak RSS (including page cache) ever exceed what an operator would set as a ceiling, and does LMK treat its file-backed pages differently from SipLLM's anonymous buffers on Android 12-14? Needs the experiment; no paper measured it.
  • TPI-LLM's memory-measurement method (the paper is silent; the code repo 404'd on 2026-10-23). The IEEE TSC 2025 version was not read and may differ from arXiv v1.
  • ACT (ISCA'22) and Hepti (ICS'24) were not read (no PDF obtained); EdgePipe (2110.14895), CoFormer (2508.20375) and the Wi-Fi broadcast-limits paper (2608.02341) were not read — the last may matter for >4-phone Wi-Fi pools (Junkyard already reports Wi-Fi infeasibility beyond ~30 co-located devices, p.7).
  • Exact 4Q26 conventional DRAM QoQ (only in a TrendForce chart image; secondary sources say +10-15%); WEEE Forum's primary release for the 5.3 billion phones figure; Micron's primary FQ4-2026 release.
  • Whether pooling beats single-phone streaming at all for decode on 2-3 GB phones: per token, pooling replaces one phone's flash reads of the non-resident layers by N network hops of dim x 4 bytes; on Wi-Fi with 3-7 ms links (prima.cpp Table 3) and ~100-300 MB/s eMMC on old phones, the crossover depends on model size — not measured anywhere for this device class.
  • Whether --fast mismatch across nodes actually changes outputs beyond rounding (would allow mixed pools); needs a golden-matrix run.
Domain D: KV-Cache Compression, Eviction & Quality Frontiers — (32 Source Records Analyzed)

Cluster D: residency theory, KV-cache memory, iso-memory quality, and the "bad RAM" probe

Local, untracked working file (2026-10-23). It is never committed. Scope: C3 (residency vs Belady), C4 (iso-memory frontier), C5 (KV eviction under a hard ceiling), static or verified peak-memory bounds (the C1 "contract" framing), and a short "bad RAM" probe.

Labels: [PAPER p.N] means the paper's own claim. [SIPLLM] means a SipLLM fact from the brief or the repo source. [INFERENCE] means my reasoning. [HYPOTHESIS] means it needs an experiment. PDFs are in /Users/mobile/Desktop/Dev/sipllm/.claude-artifacts/research/D-theory-kv/papers/ (with pdftotext -layout .txt twins). The page grep helper is /Users/mobile/Desktop/Dev/sipllm/.claude-artifacts/research/D-theory-kv/pgrep.py. Toy simulator: /Users/mobile/Desktop/Dev/sipllm/.claude-artifacts/research/D-theory-kv/sim/.

0. What SipLLM actually does in this cluster's area (read from source, not from the brief)
  • KV cache (include/llm/kv_cache.h) [SIPLLM]
    • The cache grows on demand by doubling from 64 positions, capped at max_ctx.
    • It has no eviction of any kind. The runtime stops generation when pos_ >= max_ctx (src/runtime.cpp:263) and rejects prompts that do not fit (runtime.cpp:241).
    • The planner prices KV at the full chosen ctx (src/mem_plan.cpp:144-145; header comment include/llm/mem_plan.h:21-22: "priced at the FULL chosen ctx (worst case) … so the contract holds no matter how long the conversation runs").
    • So C5 describes a capability SipLLM does not have today.
  • KV growth transient (possible hole in the contract) [INFERENCE]
    • KVCache::grow_to allocates the new nk/nv vectors (assign(per, 0), which touches the pages) before swap frees the old ones.
    • At the last growth step the live KV bytes are therefore old cap + new cap. grow_to doubles and then caps at max_ctx, so the old cap c is the largest 64·2^j below max_ctx and c ≥ max_ctx/2.
    • The transient is therefore between 1.5x and just under 2x the full-ctx KV the planner priced. It is exactly 1.5x only when max_ctx is 64·2^j.
    • Planner candidates step by 128 (mem_plan.cpp:94-101), and an explicit --ctx can be any value. Examples: ctx 1920 gives 1.53x; ctx 1152 gives 1.89x; --ctx 1500 gives 1.68x.
    • The only slack is fixed.base = 24 MB (mem_plan.cpp:52).
    • Example (my arithmetic): 8B-class model, 32 layers, kv_dim 1024, FP32, ctx 2048. Full KV is about 537 MB, so the transient adds about 268 MB, which exceeds the 24 MB slop. At ctx 1152 it would add about 89% of full KV. TinyLlama at ctx 512 (about 23 MB KV) stays inside the slop.
    • It also allocates during inference, which TFLM's contract explicitly forbids (record 18).
    • [HYPOTHESIS] Running at a tight --ram-budget with generation crossing ctx/2 will show /usr/bin/time -l peak RSS above the budget, unless the allocator happens to reuse pages.
  • Semantic / prefix cache (src/semantic_cache.cpp, include/llm/semantic_cache.h) [SIPLLM]
    • Structure: a radix tree over token ids, LRU list, leaf-first eviction until current_bytes_ <= max_budget_bytes. KV is stored as FP32 per node, [n_layers][len][kv_dim].
    • Enabled by --semantic-cache-budget BYTES (main.cpp:170, 286) and refuses Q8 KV (runtime.cpp:107-109).
    • **The budget is not in the mem_plan ledger** (no mention in mem_plan.cpp).
    • find_longest_prefix materializes full FP32 out_k/out_v copies of the matched prefix (semantic_cache.cpp:96-120) before injection. That is a second transient outside the ledger.
    • Minor code smell: the fallback branch of evict_until_fits (semantic_cache.cpp:45-55) detaches a non-leaf without subtracting its bytes and then breaks. It is probably unreachable, because every leaf is in the LRU list.
  • Residency policy [SIPLLM]
    • LayerLoader::plan_and_pin_layers pins the leading contiguous run of layers [0, target) under the byte ceiling (src/loader.cpp:186-231).
    • The rest stream through a 1–2 slot ring with sequential wrap prefetch (loader.cpp:89-93, 163-166).
  • Streaming I/O and the page cache [SIPLLM]
    • Reads use plain pread (include/llm/file_backing.h:43-55), with no POSIX_FADV_DONTNEED, O_DIRECT or F_NOCACHE. FileBacking::prefetch (fadvise WILLNEED) exists but its own docs say it is unused (docs/product/why-not-mmap.md:10).
    • scripts/bench_ram_budget.sh:101-102 warms the model before timing and records "cache_state": "warm" (line 155). The CHANGELOG scorecard header says "warm".
    • Consequence [INFERENCE]: on any machine whose free RAM exceeds the model file (certainly TinyLlama 669 MB on a 6 GB phone), "streamed" tok/s is largely page-cache memcpy speed, not flash speed. The system-wide footprint also includes the cached file pages, which RSS excludes.
    • For the M3 8B/13B runs the status is warm-labeled, cache-served status unknown. 4.92 GB per token at 0.55 tok/s is about 2.7 GB/s. That is close to plausible internal-SSD read speed and well below plausible memcpy speed, so the 8B figure may already be storage-bound, or bound by the single-reader pread pipeline. Not determinable without a cold run plus I/O counters.
    • This matters directly for the e-waste pitch: on a 2–3 GB device, the page cache cannot hold a 4.9 GB model.
