2. Per-paper records (most relevant first)
R1 Layered paging: "Cache Management for Mixture-of-Experts LLMs – extended version"
  • Cite: Angelopoulos, Marchal, Obrecht, Simon. arXiv 2509.02408 (Sep 2025). https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.02408
  • Read: PARTIAL (pp.1, 3-8, 11-15).
  • Problem: Competitive analysis of caching MoE experts across layers.
  • Mechanism:
    • Defines "ℓ-layered paging": the request sequence cycles through layers, one expert page per layer per round, with cache size k [PAPER p.5].
    • OPT is Belady's furthest-in-future rule [PAPER p.6].
    • Thm 1: any deterministic algorithm with a fixed per-layer cache partition has unbounded competitive ratio, for n≥2 and ℓ≥2 [PAPER p.7].
    • Thm 2: deterministic CR ≥ k−ℓ+1 [PAPER p.7].
    • Thm 3: CR(LRU) ≥ k when ℓ divides k+1 [PAPER p.7].
    • Thm 4: randomized CR ≥ max(H_n, log ℓ / …) [PAPER p.8].
    • Proposes Layered-LRU (LLRU) [PAPER p.12].
  • Memory regime: abstract cache of k pages. No RAM ceiling, no hardware.
  • Hardware: none (simulation). Traces from Mixtral (ℓ=32, n=8, 1000 prompts) and Llama-MoE (n=16, ℓ=32, 100 prompts) [PAPER p.12].
  • Their numbers:
    • Online policies sit up to about 2.5x above OPT in faults [PAPER p.13].
    • LLRU makes about 15% fewer faults than LRU and about 7% fewer than LRU-Dist on Llama-MoE at k=200 [PAPER p.14, Fig.5].
    • OPT-Dist (split cache) makes up to 3x more faults than shared OPT [PAPER p.14].
    • Authors' claim: "To our knowledge, this is the first study of competitive analysis in the context of LLM caching" [PAPER p.15].
  • Does NOT do:
    • Counts faults only: no wall-clock time, no prefetch overlap, no variable page sizes.
    • Single expert per layer (top-k is serialized into extra rounds) [PAPER p.12-13].
    • [INFERENCE] With n=1 (a dense model), the input is a fixed cyclic sequence and competitive analysis has nothing to measure.
  • Pre-empts:
    • C3: FULL for MoE experts (Belady/OPT plus competitive ratios for LLM caching). NONE for dense layers.
    • C5: NONE.
  • Lessons:
    • If SipLLM ever streams MoE experts, do not partition the expert cache per layer (Thm 1). Use a global pool with LLRU-style tie-breaking.
    • For the current dense loader.cpp, the paper confirms LRU is pathological on cyclic access (p.13): a leading-run static pin set is correct and LRU would be wrong.
  • Contribution opportunity: none in the theory itself. [HYPOTHESIS] A measured check of its fault model, i.e. whether wall-clock time follows faults once prefetch overlap exists, would be new, but only for MoE.
R2 "Who Should Own the Expert Cache? Kernel-Managed Tiering for Trillion-Parameter MoE Inference"
  • Cite: Si, Lin, Li, Zhang. arXiv 2608.12103v2 (Aug 2026). https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12103
  • Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-4).
  • Problem: Should the OS page cache or a user-space cache own the MoE expert tier?
  • Mechanism:
    • Router traces from three MoE models (E = 128, 256, 896).
    • Capacity is enforced by **three independent mechanisms: balloon, cgroup-v2 MemoryMax wall, and mem= boot** [PAPER p.1-2].
    • Separates the target capacity, MemAvailable, file-backed resident pages, mlocked anonymous pages, /proc/diskstats block reads, and logical expert accesses [PAPER p.2, "What capacity denotes"].
    • Policies compared under fair windows: static frequency pinning, LFU, LRU and Belady [PAPER p.3-4].
  • Memory regime: an operator-set ceiling enforced by the OS (cgroup or mem=), not by an application planner.
  • Hardware: GH200 node (1.45 TB expert pool replayed natively), plus Qwen3-30B-A3B replay on Darwin [PAPER p.1].
  • Their numbers:
    • At trillion scale LRU reaches about 70% of Belady's hit rate (44% vs 62%); static frequency gets 23% [PAPER p.4].
    • Belady bounds the remaining miss reduction at about one third of LRU's misses [PAPER p.2].
    • At an enforced equal-memory limit, a user-space oracle-pinned arena is only 1.09–1.11x faster than the page cache in the pread replay [PAPER p.1-2].
    • Balloon-based sizing can overstate low-capacity device traffic by about 2x [PAPER p.1-2].
  • Does NOT do: dense models; CPU-only edge devices; a per-allocation budget planner; quality (outputs are token-identical).
  • Pre-empts:
    • C3: FULL for MoE experts (residency vs Belady on real hardware under enforced caps).
    • C1: PARTIAL as methodology. Operator-set caps via cgroup or mem= with measured device traffic is the evaluation bar a reviewer will apply to SipLLM.
  • Lessons (high impact):
    • (a) Any SipLLM claim about the ceiling should be validated under cgroup-v2 memory.max = budget on Linux/Android. That cap charges page cache, unlike RSS. Report /proc/diskstats bytes per token alongside RSS.
    • (b) The fair C1 baseline is "llama.cpp --mmap under memory.max = budget", not llama.cpp unconstrained. This paper suggests kernel-managed residency comes within about 10% of owned buffers at equal enforced memory.
    • Files to change: scripts/bench_ram_budget.sh, scripts/bench.sh.
  • Contribution opportunity: [HYPOTHESIS] For dense models on a phone under memory.max = B, SipLLM's planner is faster than mmap+kernel by more than 10%, because LRU-like page reclaim is pathological on cyclic layer scans (R1 p.13).
    • Experiment: TinyLlama and 8B Q4 on the Dimensity 900 (Android cgroup v2 if accessible, else a Linux SBC).
    • Arms: SipLLM --ram-budget B vs llama.cpp mmap with memory.max=B.
    • Metrics: tok/s, diskstats bytes per token, and OOM-kill rate.
R3 "Towards MoE Deployment: Mitigating Inefficiencies in Mixture-of-Expert (MoE) Inference"
  • Cite: Huang, Ardalani, Sun, Ke, et al. (Meta). arXiv 2303.06182 (2023). https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.06182
  • Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-4, 8-10).
  • Problem: MoE inference latency and memory.
  • Mechanism:
    • "Expert Buffering": hot experts are kept in a fixed, limited GPU buffer and the rest sit in CPU memory [PAPER p.2].
    • LIFO eviction [PAPER p.8].
    • Load balancing.
  • Memory regime: a fixed GPU buffer size per device (an operator-chosen cache size). Not a whole-process ceiling.
  • Hardware: 8x V100 32 GB, 700 GB host [PAPER p.4].
  • Their numbers: static memory down up to 1.47x [PAPER p.1]. Fig.12(b): LIFO beats FIFO and, with load balancing, gives a miss rate "very close to Belady's MIN" [PAPER p.9].
  • Does NOT do: dense layers; CPU-only operation; quality under memory limits.
  • Pre-empts: C3 FULL for experts. It is the earliest Belady comparison for LLM expert caching that I found (2023).
  • Lessons: [INFERENCE] LIFO/MRU-like policies are the classical answer for loop-like references. SipLLM's static pin of a leading run is the dense analogue and is already miss-optimal (section 3, C3).
  • Contribution opportunity: none for C3.
R4 "Reproducible Evaluation of MoE Expert Caching: Replay Semantics, Workload Contamination, and Operating Regimes"
  • Cite: Yu Zhang. arXiv 2608.07911v4 (Aug 2026). https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07911
  • Read: PARTIAL (p.1 abstract, pp.2, 5-6, 9, 15-18 via grep).
  • Problem: How fragile are policy-vs-Belady comparisons?
  • Mechanism:
    • Trace-driven, event-atomic simulator over OLMoE-1B-7B (64 experts), Qwen3-30B-A3B (128 experts) and one 40-expert model [PAPER p.1, p.6].
    • Compares LRU, LFRU, Least-Stale, LFU, static, Belady and Belady-forced-admit [PAPER p.5].
  • Memory regime: simulated capacity only.
  • Hardware: simulation, with traces from MLX/llama.cpp commits [PAPER p.6].
  • Their numbers:
    • Inconsistent replay inflates recency policies by 27–29% [PAPER p.1-2].
    • After correction, the gap to the offline optimum is 44.2–45.9%; a forced-admission oracle attributes 84–97% of it to furthest-next-use knowledge [PAPER p.1].
    • A causal next-use predictor recovers −11.4% of the gap, i.e. it is worse than its baseline [PAPER p.1].
  • Does NOT do: systems timing; dense models.
  • Pre-empts: C3 FULL for experts. It also shows that a "gap to Belady" headline is itself easy to overstate.
  • Lessons: if SipLLM ever publishes residency-vs-Belady numbers, follow its reporting checklist: replay semantics, workload diversity, and union-to-capacity ratio.
  • Contribution opportunity: none.
R5 InfiniGen: "Efficient Generative Inference of Large Language Models with Dynamic KV Cache Management"
  • Cite: W. Lee, J. Lee, Seo, Sim. OSDI 2024 [venue from memory, not shown in the arXiv PDF I read]. arXiv 2406.19707. https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.19707
  • Read: PARTIAL (pp.1, 5-6, 9, 11).
  • Problem: KV offloading to CPU makes PCIe transfer the bottleneck.
  • Mechanism:
    • Speculatively prefetches only the important KV entries, using partial-weight query/key rehearsal in the previous layer.
    • Keeps the KV cache pool on CPU.
    • §4.4: "we extend the design to incorporate a user-defined memory size limit … when the size of the CPU memory reaches a user-defined limit, the KV cache pool manager selects a victim KV entry for eviction". Victim policies: counter-based, FIFO or LRU [PAPER p.9].
  • Memory regime: an operator-set ceiling on the KV pool, with eviction.
  • Hardware: RTX A6000 48 GB plus Xeon Gold 6136 with 96 GB DDR4-2666, PCIe 3.0 [PAPER p.9].
  • Their numbers:
    • At an 80% KV limit: FIFO pushes OPT-13B WikiText-2 perplexity from 10.55 to 30.99. LRU and Counter equal the no-limit perplexity across OPT-6.7B/13B/30B and Llama-2-7B/13B [PAPER p.11, Table 2].
    • Up to 3.00x over prior KV management [PAPER p.1].
  • Does NOT do:
    • Tests only the 80% limit, perplexity at 2048 tokens, and a few lm-eval tasks.
    • GPU+CPU offloading, not CPU-only.
    • The ceiling covers the KV pool alone, not process RSS or weights.
  • Pre-empts: C5 FULL in mechanism: a KV pool with a victim policy under a user-set limit, with the quality cost reported. PARTIAL for C1-style whole-process ceilings.
  • Lessons:
    • If SipLLM adds KV eviction, FIFO (which equals StreamingLLM without sinks) is the worst choice. Counter- or importance-based victims cost almost nothing at a mild limit.
    • The implementation would go in kv_cache.h (slot indirection) plus transformer.cpp attention gather.
  • Contribution opportunity: [HYPOTHESIS] At limits far below InfiniGen's 80%, inside a single host-RSS ceiling shared with weights, there is an interior optimum split between KV bytes and pinned-layer bytes that maximizes quality per second. See C5 verdict.
R6 SGLang / RadixAttention: "SGLang: Efficient Execution of Structured Language Model Programs"
  • Cite: Zheng, Yin, Xie, Sun, et al. NeurIPS 2024 [venue from memory, not shown in the arXiv PDF I read]. arXiv 2312.07104. https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.07104
  • Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-2, 4-6, 8).
  • Problem: Reuse KV across requests that share a prefix.
  • Mechanism:
    • A radix tree maps token sequences to KV; LRU evicts the least recently used leaf first, so ancestors are reused until they become leaves [PAPER p.4].
    • Per-node reference counts protect the running batch [PAPER p.4].
    • Cached tokens share the same pool as running requests; there is no fixed cache pool [PAPER p.4].
    • Cache-aware scheduling: Thm 3.1, DFS order gives the optimal hit rate offline when the cache is at least the max request length [PAPER p.6]. Achieves 96% of optimal hit rate on average [PAPER p.8].
  • Memory regime: bounded by the GPU KV pool. No separate operator-set cache budget.
  • Hardware: mostly AWS G5 NVIDIA A10G 24 GB, plus some A100 80 GB [PAPER p.7].
  • Their numbers: 50–99% hit rates on its benchmarks [PAPER p.8].
  • Does NOT do: CPU-only operation; host-RAM budgets; quantized cached KV.
  • Pre-empts: SipLLM's semantic cache design is FULLy pre-empted: radix tree plus LRU leaf-first plus byte bound, the same algorithm (semantic_cache.cpp:36-56 vs SGLang p.4). C5: PARTIAL (lossless prefix-cache eviction, not lossy KV-token eviction).
  • Lessons:
    • Do not present the semantic cache as novel.
    • Engineering debts relative to SGLang: (a) the cached KV is outside the mem_plan ledger; (b) FP32-only (runtime.cpp:107); Q8 rows would make the cache about 3.8x smaller (34 B vs 128 B per 32 values); (c) the out_k/out_v copy-then-inject doubles transient memory, where zero-copy or a direct write into KVCache would avoid it.
  • Contribution opportunity: none that is research-grade.
R7 vLLM / PagedAttention: "Efficient Memory Management for Large Language Model Serving with PagedAttention"
  • Cite: Kwon, Li, Zhuang, Sheng, et al. SOSP 2023 [venue from memory, not shown in the arXiv PDF I read]. arXiv 2309.06180. https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.06180
  • Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-2, 4, 8).
  • Problem: KV fragmentation and reservation waste in serving.
  • Mechanism:
    • Block-paged KV with a block table.
    • When free blocks run out, all-or-nothing eviction of whole sequences (gang-scheduled groups), FCFS order (latest-arrived preempted first). The evicted KV is either swapped to CPU RAM, bounded by the GPU KV size, or recomputed [PAPER p.8, §4.5].
    • The paper notes that eviction "typically" uses furthest-next-access heuristics, but because all blocks of a sequence are accessed together it evicts whole sequences [PAPER p.8].
  • Memory regime: a fixed GPU KV block pool with lossless eviction at request granularity.
  • Hardware: NVIDIA A100 [PAPER p.1].
  • Their numbers: 2–4x throughput at the same latency [PAPER p.1]. Existing systems use only 20.4–38.2% of KV memory for token states [PAPER p.2].
  • Does NOT do: token-level lossy eviction; CPU-only operation; single-stream edge use.
  • Pre-empts: C5 PARTIAL. "KV as a pool with an eviction policy under a fixed memory limit" exists here at sequence granularity. SipLLM is single-stream, where that granularity is meaningless.
  • Lessons: none direct. For a SipLLM server with several sessions, recompute-on-preempt is the RAM-free option.
  • Contribution opportunity: none.
R8 H2O: "Heavy-Hitter Oracle for Efficient Generative Inference of Large Language Models"
  • Cite: Z. Zhang, Sheng, Zhou, Chen, et al. NeurIPS 2023 [venue from memory, not shown in the arXiv PDF I read]. arXiv 2306.14048. https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.14048
  • Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-5, 7-9).
  • Problem: KV memory grows with sequence length.
  • Mechanism:
    • Keeps a fixed KV budget (for example 20% [PAPER p.4]) made of heavy hitters (by accumulated attention) plus recent tokens, and evicts one token per step.
    • Formulated as dynamic submodular maximization with a guarantee [PAPER p.1, p.3].
    • "Belady's Algorithm is optimal for standard cache, but not necessarily for KV cache" … "once evicting important KVs, it could destroy the performance" [PAPER p.2 fn.1, p.5].
  • Memory regime: a fixed KV token budget (operator-set fraction), lossy.
  • Hardware: A100 80 GB and T4 16 GB, implemented on FlexGen [PAPER p.7-9].
  • Their numbers: 20% heavy hitters give up to 29x throughput over DeepSpeed Zero-Inference / Accelerate and 3x over FlexGen (OPT-6.7B/30B) [PAPER p.1].
  • Does NOT do: CPU-only; whole-process ceiling; joint planning with weights.
  • Pre-empts:
    • C5: FULL for "KV eviction under a fixed budget with quality reported".
    • C3: it also explains why Belady does not transfer to lossy KV eviction. That is a useful sentence for the paper.
  • Lessons: a token-budget eviction mode would sit in kv_cache.h. The quality cost depends on the model (see R10).
  • Contribution opportunity: none beyond the C5 survivor.
R9 StreamingLLM: "Efficient Streaming Language Models with Attention Sinks"
  • Cite: Xiao, Tian, Chen, Han, Lewis. ICLR 2024. arXiv 2309.17453. https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.17453
  • Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-5, 7-9, 15).
  • Problem: Window attention collapses once text exceeds the cache.
  • Mechanism: keep 4 "attention sink" initial tokens plus a rolling window (for example 4+1020) [PAPER p.2, p.5]. The cache size is fixed.
  • Memory regime: a fixed-size KV (constant memory) [PAPER p.9].
  • Hardware: A6000 [PAPER p.9].
  • Their numbers: stable language modeling up to 4M tokens [PAPER p.2]; up to 22.2x faster than sliding-window recomputation [PAPER p.2, p.9].
  • Their limitation: does not extend context or long-term memory [PAPER p.15].
  • Pre-empts: C5 FULL for "bounded KV with a fixed eviction rule". llama.cpp's context shift is the CPU-runtime version (R11).
  • Lessons: the cheapest way for SipLLM to stop refusing at ctx-full (runtime.cpp:263) is n_keep sinks plus a window. It is lossy for retrieval (R10).
  • Contribution opportunity: none.
R10 KV-compression benchmark: "KV Cache Compression, But What Must We Give in Return? A Comprehensive Benchmark of Long Context Capable Approaches"
  • Cite: Yuan, Liu, Zhong, et al. EMNLP Findings 2024 [venue from memory, not shown in the arXiv PDF I read]. arXiv 2407.01527. https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.01527
  • Read: PARTIAL (pp.1, 3-6).
  • Problem: Quality cost of KV compression at aligned ratios.
  • Mechanism:
    • Evaluates KIVI, FlexGen-INT4, StreamingLLM, H2O, InfLLM, LLMLingua2, Mamba and RWKV on LongBench plus needle-in-a-haystack.
    • Token-dropping methods are modified to linear compression for fairness [PAPER p.6].
  • Memory regime: compression ratio only.
  • Hardware: not material.
  • Their numbers (Table 2, p.5), shown as LB avg / needle:
    ModelMethodRatioLB avgNeedle
    Llama-3-8B-Instructbaseline1x45.2100
    Llama-3-8B-InstructKIVI-2bit5.05x44.3100
    Llama-3-8B-InstructKIVI-4bit3.11x45.3100
    Llama-3-8B-InstructStreamLLM4x34.422.3
    Llama-3-8B-InstructStreamLLM8x30.318.0
    Llama-3-8B-InstructH2O8x43.4100
    Mistral-7BH2O8x40.480.0
  • Does NOT do: memory ceilings; speed on CPU.
  • Pre-empts: C5: it answers "what quality does KV reduction cost" for quantization and eviction at fixed ratios.
  • Lessons (high):
    • Under a hard ceiling, quantize KV before evicting KV. Quantization at 3–5x is near-lossless; window eviction destroys retrieval; importance eviction depends on the model.
    • SipLLM's --kv-q8 (kv_cache.h Q8_0) is the right first lever. The semantic cache should accept it (runtime.cpp:107).
    • The planner should try kv_q8 before capping ctx (mem_plan.h:15-19 order).
  • Contribution opportunity: see the C5 survivor.
R11 llama.cpp server: context shift and RAM prompt cache (software; no paper)
  • Cite: ggml-org/llama.cpp, local checkout commit 7d442abf5c62 (2026-05-11). Read: FULL, for the sections below.
  • Context shift: tools/server/server-context.cpp:2172-2229. When n_tokens + 1 >= n_ctx, it keeps n_keep tokens (+BOS), discards n_discard (default n_left/2), and calls llama_memory_seq_rm plus llama_memory_seq_add to shift positions. Toggled by the --context-shift flag (common/arg.cpp:1345-1351).
  • RAM prompt cache: --cache-ram N sets the "maximum cache size in MiB", default 8192 (common/arg.cpp:1322-1327, common/common.h:594).
    • Whole-prompt KV states are reused by longest common prefix (tools/server/server-task.cpp:2036-2050).
    • Eviction is FIFO: "cache size limit reached, removing oldest entry" (server-task.cpp:2088-2097).
  • Memory regime: an operator-set MiB ceiling on the prompt cache; a fixed n_ctx with lossy shift.
  • Pre-empts:
    • C5: PARTIAL. A CPU runtime already has bounded KV with eviction (context shift) and an operator-set host-RAM budget with eviction (prompt cache).
    • SipLLM's semantic cache: PARTIAL (byte-budgeted host-RAM prefix reuse; FIFO and whole prompts instead of a radix tree with LRU).
  • Lessons: SipLLM's radix design is finer-grained than llama.cpp's FIFO list. That is an engineering edge, not a research claim.
R12 k-bit scaling: "The case for 4-bit precision: k-bit Inference Scaling Laws"
  • Cite: Dettmers, Zettlemoyer. ICML 2023 [venue from memory, not shown in the arXiv PDF I read]. arXiv 2212.09720. https://arxiv.org/abs/2212.09720
  • Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-4).
  • Problem: For a fixed number of total model bits, what precision and size maximize zero-shot accuracy?
  • Mechanism:
    • More than 35,000 experiments: OPT, BLOOM/BLOOMZ, Pythia, GPT-2; 19M–176B parameters; 3–16 bit [PAPER p.1-2].
    • Accuracy is the mean of LAMBADA, PIQA, HellaSwag and Winogrande, plus perplexity on The Pile CommonCrawl [PAPER p.4].
  • Memory regime: iso-model-bits (a proxy for memory and for latency in the memory-bound regime) [PAPER p.2].
  • Hardware: not an on-device study.
  • Their numbers: "4-bit precision is almost universally optimal for total model bits and zero-shot accuracy" [PAPER p.1]. The exception is BLOOM-176B, where 3-bit is slightly but not significantly better [PAPER p.4]. Block size 64–128 is best [PAPER p.2].
  • Does NOT do:
    • Treats latency as proportional to bits and does not measure it.
    • Every model is assumed resident; there is no streaming, no residency axis and no RAM ceiling below model size.
    • Uses 2022-era models trained on few tokens (see R12b).
  • Pre-empts: C4 FULL for the (size × bits) axis at fixed memory when the model fits. NONE for the residency axis.
  • Lessons: in the paper, do not claim "larger quantized beats smaller resident" as new. Cite this and R13.
  • Contribution opportunity: see the C4 survivor.
R12b "Scaling Laws for Precision" (short record)
  • Cite: Kumar, Ankner, Spector, et al. arXiv 2411.04330 (2024). Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-2, 5, 9).
  • Finding: post-training-quantization degradation increases with pretraining data, "eventually making additional pretraining data actively harmful" [PAPER p.1, p.5]. Fit on 465 runs, up to 1.7B parameters and 26B tokens [PAPER p.1].
  • [INFERENCE] Modern small models are heavily overtrained (TinyLlama: 1.1B parameters on trillions of tokens, per its model card; not verified here). Dettmers' 4-bit optimum may therefore shift upward for exactly the models SipLLM runs. That uncertainty is why a fresh measured frontier would carry information.
R13 "A Systematic Evaluation of On-Device LLMs: Quantization, Performance, and Resources"
  • Cite: Song, Liu, Lin, et al. arXiv 2505.15030v5 (Mar 2026). https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15030
  • Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-4, 6-8).
  • Problem: Capability, efficiency and resource use of llama.cpp quantizations on consumer CPUs.
  • Mechanism: Qwen2.5 and Llama-3 at 0.5B–14B; q2_k through fp16 in llama.cpp; throughput and power measured on a laptop. Capability is evaluated on a GPU because accuracy is platform-independent [PAPER p.3-4].
  • Memory regime: fits a 16 GB device; no ceiling.
  • Hardware: Core i7-1360P, 16 GB DDR5-5600, NVMe; llama-cpp-python [PAPER p.3].
  • Their numbers:
    • "Heavily quantized large models consistently outperform smaller, high-precision models, with a performance threshold at ∼3.5 effective bits-per-weight" [PAPER p.1, p.7].
    • Example: 14B q3_k scores 89.01 on GSM8K vs 3B fp16 at 80.89 [PAPER p.4, Table 1].
    • Decode is memory/communication-bound for large models and compute-bound for small ones [PAPER p.4].
  • Does NOT do: streaming or partial residency; RAM ceilings below model size; quality per second as a single objective.
  • Pre-empts: C4 FULL for the CPU-device version of the (size × bits) question, when models fit.
  • Lessons: reuse its BPW framing. Q4_K at 4.5 BPW and Q6_K at 6.5625 BPW are on the good side of the 3.5 threshold [PAPER p.8].
  • Contribution opportunity: see the C4 survivor.
R14 MCAP / NVE: "Deployment-Time Layer Profiling for Memory-Constrained LLM Inference"
  • Cite: Anurita Das. arXiv 2604.21026v2 (Apr 2026). https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.21026
  • Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-4, 13-17).
  • Problem: Choose per-layer precision and residency at load time for a memory budget.
  • Mechanism:
    • A 60-second profiler produces per-layer importance.
    • A three-tier pager: GPU (hot), CPU RAM (warm, LRU), SSD (cold, mmap), with PMI-clustered prefetch [PAPER p.13-14].
    • Three modes [PAPER p.15-16]:
      • A: paged, all layers, quality unchanged.
      • B: hot-only, layers skipped.
      • C: hot-only + AWQ.
  • Memory regime: an operator-set GPU VRAM budget (1–24 GB). CPU RAM is unbounded (the warm tier holds everything).
  • Hardware: NVIDIA T4 16 GB and A10G (Modal cloud) [PAPER p.17].
  • Their numbers:
    • Llama-3.2-3B in 2 GB at 2.31 tok/s; Llama-3.1-8B in 4 GB at 1.25 tok/s [PAPER p.1].
    • Table 6 [PAPER p.16]:
      • 3B at 2 GB (36% of layers active): paged scores 100%, hot-only 0% (below the floor).
      • 8B at 4 GB: paged 88%.
      • An "empirical cliff" appears near 50% active layers for layer skipping.
    • The ">99.6% hit rate" is warm-up amortization, as the authors themselves note [PAPER p.15, Table 5].
    • n=8 task suite, with an explicit CI caveat [PAPER p.17].
  • Does NOT do: host-RAM ceilings; CPU-only operation; measured RSS; competitive analysis.
  • Pre-empts:
    • C4: PARTIAL. A memory budget picks among {stream all layers, skip layers, quantize} with quality reported. That is an iso-memory quality frontier with a residency axis, but for GPU VRAM.
    • C1: PARTIAL (budget-driven placement, not a host-RSS guarantee).
    • C3: NONE (uses LRU on cyclic layers, which R1 says is pathological).
  • Lessons:
    • (a) "Streaming keeps full quality while skipping collapses below about 50% of layers" is a data point to cite in favour of SipLLM's streaming-over-pruning design.
    • (b) Its tiny n and GPU-only setting leave the CPU-RAM version of the frontier unmeasured.
  • Contribution opportunity: the C4 survivor (host RSS, CPU, phones, quality per second).
R15 FlexGen: "High-Throughput Generative Inference of Large Language Models with a Single GPU"
  • Cite: Sheng, Zheng, Yuan, Li, et al. ICML 2023 [venue from memory, not shown in the arXiv PDF I read]. arXiv 2303.06865. https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.06865
  • Read: PARTIAL (pp.5-6).
  • Problem: Throughput-oriented offloading across GPU, CPU and disk.
  • Mechanism:
    • Weight placement as percentages wg/wc/wd at layer granularity, i.e. "assign 50% of the tensors in a layer to the GPU", so every layer is partially resident [PAPER p.5].
    • Zig-zag block schedule with overlap. Thm 4.1: I/O complexity within 2x of optimal [PAPER p.5].
    • Linear-program policy search with peak-memory constraints per device [PAPER p.6, Eq.1].
  • Memory regime: fits device capacities, but "sometimes a strategy from the policy search can run out of memory. In this case, we manually adjust the policy" [PAPER p.6]. The memory model is explicitly not a guarantee.
  • Hardware: a single NVIDIA T4 16 GB, 208 GB CPU DRAM, 1.5 TB disk [PAPER p.1-2].
  • Pre-empts:
    • C3: PARTIAL (an I/O-optimality result for an offloading schedule; spread residency).
    • C1: NONE. It is a counter-example: a planner without a guarantee. That strengthens the contrast with SipLLM's refuse-or-guarantee planner, provided SipLLM closes the holes in section 0.
  • Lessons: spreading residency across layers (instead of a leading run) is prior art. See R16 and the C3 survivor.
R16 ChunkFlow: "Communication-Aware Chunked Prefetching for Layerwise Offloading in Distributed Diffusion Transformer Inference"
  • Cite: Meng, Liu, Li. arXiv 2605.11335 (May 2026). https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11335
  • Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-3, 6).
  • Problem: Layerwise offloading exposes prefetch time when per-layer compute is short.
  • Mechanism: chunked prefetch, plus "partial parameter residency": keep a subset of chunks of each layer resident, which "recovers overlap" when compute is too short to hide a full prefetch [PAPER p.6, §3.3]. The resident working set is two layers under full offload [PAPER p.3].
  • Memory regime: a tunable GPU residency fraction.
  • Hardware: 2x H100 over PCIe; diffusion transformers [PAPER p.6].
  • Their numbers: up to 1.28x step-time speedup over SGLang's layerwise offload; up to 49% lower peak GPU memory [PAPER p.1].
  • Pre-empts: C3 PARTIAL. "Spread residency to hide prefetch" exists, at chunk granularity on GPU.
  • Lessons: directly supports Lesson 4 (spread pinning in loader.cpp).
R17 Cao, Felten, Karlin, Li: "A Study of Integrated Prefetching and Caching Strategies"
  • Cite: ACM SIGMETRICS 1995 (Princeton TR-479-94 listing says so, per search result). PDF: https://homes.cs.washington.edu/~karlin/papers/sigmetrics.pdf
  • Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-2).
  • Problem: The offline-optimal combined prefetch/replace schedule for a known reference string.
  • Mechanism:
    • Model: a known reference sequence, one disk, fetch time F, cache of k blocks; elapsed time = references + stalls [PAPER p.2].
    • Four rules [PAPER p.2]:
      1. Prefetch the next missing block.
      2. Replace the block referenced furthest in the future.
      3. Do no harm.
      4. First opportunity.
    • The "conservative" strategy is within 2x of optimal elapsed time, and the bound is tight [PAPER p.1].
  • Memory regime: abstract cache.
  • Pre-empts: C3. This is the correct theory frame for dense SipLLM: stall time, not miss count. It predates LLMs by three decades.
  • Lessons: for a cyclic layer string the reference sequence is known exactly, so these offline rules are implementable online. [INFERENCE] Rule 2 on a cyclic string evicts the most recently used non-pinned layer, which is what a ring does.
  • Contribution opportunity: see the C3 survivor.
R18 TensorFlow Lite Micro: "Embedded Machine Learning on TinyML Systems"
  • Cite: David, Duke, Jain, Janapa Reddi, et al. MLSys 2021 [venue from memory, not shown in the arXiv PDF I read]. arXiv 2010.08678. https://arxiv.org/abs/2010.08678
  • Read: PARTIAL (pp.3, 5-6).
  • Problem: Inference on microcontrollers without dynamic allocation.
  • Mechanism:
    • "We require applications to supply a fixed-size memory arena … If an allocation takes up too much space, we raise an application-level error".
    • "Allocations only occur during the interpreter's initialization phase. No allocation … is possible during model invocation."
    • A two-stack head/tail arena, where crossing pointers indicate lack of capacity [PAPER p.6, §4.4-4.4.1].
    • Models compile into the binary as data arrays [PAPER p.6, §4.3.1]. Typical targets have a few MB of flash and at most about 1 MB of SRAM [PAPER p.3].
  • Memory regime: an operator-set ceiling with refusal at init (the TinyML version of C1's contract).
  • Hardware: microcontrollers.
  • Pre-empts: C1 PARTIAL. The contract shape "operator supplies the budget, the runtime refuses at setup, nothing allocates during inference" exists, for small non-LLM models.
  • Lessons: SipLLM's contract should match TFLM's no-allocation-during-invoke rule. Today KVCache::grow_to and the semantic cache allocate mid-inference (section 0). In budget mode, preallocate the planned KV once (it is priced at full ctx anyway). Files: kv_cache.h, runtime.cpp.
  • Contribution opportunity: C1, narrowed: "the TFLM arena contract, extended to models larger than RAM by streaming weights, for LLMs on phones". That belongs to the C1 owner.
R19 PolyQ: "Codesigning End-to-End Quantization Framework for Scalable Edge CPU LLM Inference"
  • Cite: Oh, Jang, Chen, et al. arXiv 2607.14618 (Jul 2026). https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14618
  • Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-2, 6-7).
  • Problem: Fractional average-bit budgets on CPUs.
  • Mechanism:
    • Per-channel bits from {2, 3, 4, 8, 16} under a user average-bit budget.
    • Example: "a 3.9 GB memory budget for Llama3-8B" [PAPER p.2].
    • A peak footprint model counting all live buffers: quantized storage, FP16 residue, KV, activations, scratch, metadata and the reorder buffer [PAPER p.7, §5.3].
  • Memory regime: maps a budget to a configuration, but peak is modeled, not measured RSS [PAPER p.7, Fig.8 caption]. It does not refuse configurations.
  • Hardware: Ryzen 9 9950X, Ryzen 7 7840U, Intel N250 [PAPER p.6].
  • Their numbers: perplexity improves 2.4–32.1% over prior methods at 3 bits; Llama2-13B at B=2.6 has a 4.98 GiB modeled peak [PAPER p.1, p.7].
  • Pre-empts:
    • C4: PARTIAL (a within-model quality-vs-bits frontier at a memory budget on edge CPUs).
    • C1: PARTIAL (an itemized footprint model similar to SipLLM's ledger, without enforcement or refusal).
  • Lessons: a reviewer will compare SipLLM's ledger to this one. SipLLM's differentiator is enforcement plus measured RSS plus models larger than RAM.
R20 EDEN: "Enabling Energy-Efficient, High-Performance Deep Neural Network Inference Using Approximate DRAM"
  • Cite: Koppula, Orosa, Yağlıkçı, et al. MICRO 2019 (venue per the brief's seed; not shown in the arXiv PDF). arXiv 1910.05340. https://arxiv.org/abs/1910.05340
  • Read: PARTIAL (p.1, abstract and intro).
  • Problem: Run DNNs on DRAM operated below spec (reduced voltage and latency), which raises bit error rate.
  • Mechanism:
    1. Retrain the DNN against the target device's error model.
    2. Map each DNN data type to an approximate-DRAM partition matching its tolerance, under a user-specified accuracy target [PAPER p.1].
  • Memory regime: none (it is about reliability).
  • Hardware: multi-core CPUs, GPUs and accelerators, using error models from real approximate DRAM devices [PAPER p.1].
  • Their numbers: within 1% accuracy, average DRAM energy falls 21% (CPU) and 37% (GPU); average CPU speedup 8% (max 17%) [PAPER p.1].
  • Does NOT do: LLMs (2019, CNN-era); old or defective consumer DRAM. It concerns deliberate under-volting of healthy DRAM.
  • Pre-empts: none of C1–C7. It is the answer to the "bad RAM" literal reading (section 3, bad RAM).
R21 RangeGuard: "Efficient, Bounded Approximate Error Correction for Reliable DNNs"
  • Cite: Ko, Yeon, Ko, Kim. arXiv 2605.04563 (May 2026). https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.04563
  • Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-2, 4).
  • Problem: DRAM errors (multi-bit, rising with density) break DNNs.
  • Mechanism: protects per-value range identifiers rather than raw bits, within the existing 2 B per 32 B parity budget [PAPER p.1-2].
  • Their numbers:
    • "an unprotected Llama-3.2-1B model suffers significant accuracy loss from just a single bit flip at critical positions". BF16 exponent flips create huge outliers [PAPER p.1-2].
    • RangeGuard is robust up to BER 1e-6, with small degradation at 1e-5 [PAPER p.2].
    • LLMs are more vulnerable than CNNs because of attention, residual and normalization amplification [PAPER p.1-2].
  • Pre-empts: none. It is evidence against "tolerate errors in weight storage" being free for LLMs.
One-line records (read, but secondary or redundant)
  • FlashMoE (Kim, Lee, Han, Yoo, Kim; arXiv 2601.17063; PARTIAL pp.1-5): SSD-offloaded MoE on a desktop. Belady 86% hit vs LRU 73%, about 1.9x more I/O for LRU [PAPER p.3]. Its ML policy beats LRU/LFU by up to 51% [PAPER p.1]. C3 FULL for experts.
  • SeqMoE (Wang, Wang, Gong, et al.; arXiv 2609.12978; PARTIAL p.1-6): Belady, Belady-8 (8-step oracle) and Belady-F8 (predicted) vs LRU/LFU [PAPER p.5]; a probabilistic Belady policy [PAPER p.6]. C3 FULL for experts.
  • Not All Models Suit Expert Offloading (arXiv 2505.16056, ICLR 2026; PARTIAL pp.1-5): defines segment-cache best hit rate (SCH). It states that the clairvoyant furthest-next-use algorithm bounds any cache's hit rate [PAPER p.5]. C3 FULL for experts.
  • Mixture of Cache-Conditional Experts (Skliar, van Rozendaal, et al., Qualcomm; TMLR 2025; arXiv 2412.00099; PARTIAL pp.1-3): only a subset of experts fits in device DRAM; cache-aware routing "surpasses" the oracle bound by changing routing. Cache misses fall over 50% at 0.1–3% perplexity cost and <0.1% accuracy cost; 2x on mobile [PAPER p.1]. Relevant to C4 (quality traded for residency on phones) and C3 (Belady is only a bound for fixed routing).
  • vAttention (Prabhu, Nayak, Mohan, et al.; ASPLOS 2025 [venue from memory, not shown in the arXiv PDF I read]; arXiv 2405.04437; PARTIAL greps pp.1-12): CUDA-VMM demand paging of contiguous virtual KV, deferred reclamation, eager allocation; preempts requests when out of memory (p.7). Allocation-only paging, the contrast case in C5's wording. A100.
  • SnapKV (Li, Huang, Yang, et al.; arXiv 2404.14469; PARTIAL p.1): compresses the prompt KV using an observation window; 3.6x speed and 8.2x memory at 16K; 380K context on one A100-80GB [PAPER p.1]. C5 FULL-family (eviction at a fixed budget).
  • KIVI (Liu, Yuan, Jin, et al.; ICML 2024 [venue from memory, not shown in the arXiv PDF I read]; arXiv 2402.02750; PARTIAL pp.1-2): 2-bit asymmetric KV (per-channel K, per-token V, full-precision residual window) [PAPER p.1-2]; 2.6x lower peak memory including weights [PAPER p.1].
  • KVQuant (Hooper, Kim, Mohammadzadeh, et al.; NeurIPS 2024 [venue from memory, not shown in the arXiv PDF I read]; arXiv 2401.18079; PARTIAL p.1): pre-RoPE per-channel keys, non-uniform types, dense-and-sparse outliers; <0.1 perplexity degradation at 3-bit; 1M context for LLaMA-7B on one A100-80GB [PAPER p.1].
  • Quest (Tang, Zhao, Zhu, et al.; ICML 2024 [venue from memory, not shown in the arXiv PDF I read]; arXiv 2406.10774; PARTIAL pp.1-3): query-aware page selection that "retains all of the KV cache and selects part" [PAPER p.2]. It reduces reads, not memory, so it is irrelevant to a RAM ceiling except as a bandwidth lever.
  • Pisarchyk & Lee "Efficient Memory Management for Deep Neural Net Inference" (Google/TFLite GPU; arXiv 2001.03288; PARTIAL pp.1-3): intermediate-tensor buffer sharing is NP-complete [PAPER p.2]; lower bound plus greedy-by-size/breadth; up to 10.5x vs naive and 11% vs SOTA [PAPER p.1]. It computes the arena size statically, but minimizes: there is no operator ceiling and no guarantee for models larger than RAM.
  • Badshah & Sajjad "Quantifying the Capabilities of LLMs across Scale and Precision" (arXiv 2405.03146): ABSTRACT-ONLY. Covers 7B–70B; 4-bit larger models beat smaller high-precision ones "under similar memory requirements" [PAPER p.1].
  • Husom et al. "Sustainable LLM Inference for Edge AI" (arXiv 2504.03360): ABSTRACT-ONLY. 28 Ollama quantized models on a Raspberry Pi 4 (4 GB), energy measured with a hardware tool [PAPER p.1]. Relevant to SipLLM's missing energy/token metric; flag to the energy owner.
  • Linux kernel parameters (kernel.org Documentation/admin-guide/kernel-parameters.txt, fetched 2026-10-23; FULL for these entries):
    • memmap=nn[KMG]$ss[KMG] marks a region reserved, for example memmap=64K$0x18690000; Grub2 needs $ escaped.
    • memtest=<passes> "fills the memory with this pattern, validates memory contents and reserves bad memory regions that are detected".
    • memmap=nn without @ equals mem=nn, which caps the maximum address. That is the mechanism R2 uses to enforce capacity.
  • X3 (cross-cluster flag for C1): memopro (PyPI, version 0.1.10, released 2026-10-23; https://pypi.org/project/memopro/). Read via the WebFetch summarizer only, so the raw README must be verified.
    • Claims "within a set memory ceiling and without changing the results".
    • "A budget that cannot be kept is refused before starting."
    • Weights are "read again from their original files" per layer.
    • Covers macOS Apple silicon, Linux CPU and NVIDIA.
    • Vendor numbers: Qwen2.5-7B generation on an M1 8 GB, etc.
    • Python, not zero-dependency C++. If accurate, it is the closest software prior art to C1 found anywhere in this cluster. Pass it to the C1 owner.
3. Claim verdicts
C3 (residency science vs Belady): DEAD as stated. Only a narrowed engineering remnant survives.

Experts (MoE). Fully pre-empted, several times over:

  • Belady comparisons: Huang 2023 [R3 p.9]; FlashMoE [p.3]; SeqMoE [p.5]; SCH [2505.16056 p.5]; Si 2026 on real hardware under cgroup or mem= caps [R2 p.4]; Zhang 2026 evaluation methodology [R4].
  • Competitive ratios and layered-paging lower bounds: Angelopoulos 2025 [R1 pp.7-8], which explicitly claims to be the first.
  • SipLLM has no MoE support, so this literature does not apply to it at all.

Dense layers (what SipLLM does). [INFERENCE] The Belady question is trivial:

  • The reference string is a fixed cycle of L equal-size layers per token, known in advance.
  • In any window of one token, each layer is referenced exactly once. A reference hits only if that layer occupied a slot since its previous reference, a full cycle earlier.
  • So hits per token are at most the number of slots that keep the same layer for a whole cycle, which is at most k, and misses are at least L−k.
  • Static pinning of any k layers with a bypass ring achieves exactly L−k, so it is Belady-optimal in transfers.
  • LRU without bypass misses all L whenever the cache holds fewer than L layers. R1 p.13 notes LRU's cyclic pathology.
  • Because the future is known, there is no online/offline gap to measure, and competitive analysis is vacuous.
  • Note: SipLLM's ring slots cost RAM. With B layer-sized slots in total and R ring slots, misses equal L−(B−R). Each extra ring slot costs one transfer per token, bought for overlap.

What is left is a stall-time problem, not a paging problem.

  • Frames: Cao et al. 1995 [R17 p.2]; FlexGen Thm 4.1 [R15 p.5].
  • Spreading residency across layers to recover overlap is already published: FlexGen per-layer percentages [R15 p.5]; ChunkFlow partial residency [R16 p.6].
  • Narrowed remnant [HYPOTHESIS]:
    • Claim: on CPU at batch 1, under a hard byte ceiling, interleaved (spread) pinning beats SipLLM's leading-run pinning (loader.cpp:186-231) at equal RAM when the fetch/compute ratio F/c is low (fast storage, slow cores, partial residency).
    • Toy model [INFERENCE] (/Users/mobile/Desktop/Dev/sipllm/.claude-artifacts/research/D-theory-kv/sim/budget_matched_out.txt), budget-matched:
      • F/c=5 (phone-like): 13–27% lower time per token at 16–20 of 22 layers resident.
      • F/c=2: about 21% at 11–16 of 22.
      • Deep-streaming regime (few layers resident): about 0–2%. The M3 8B extreme (F/c≈20): 0% except when nearly fully resident.
    • Experiment: add --pin-placement {lead,spread} with default lead, so output stays byte-identical (Rule 3). Measure decode tok/s and peak RSS on the Dimensity 900 at budgets that pin 25/50/75/90% of TinyLlama and 8B layers, cold cache.
    • Falsified if spread is less than 5% better at every budget.
    • This is an engineering improvement, not a paper contribution, because the "spread" idea is prior art.
C4 (iso-memory frontier): NARROWED.

Closest prior art:

  • Dettmers & Zettlemoyer [R12 p.1-2]: at fixed total model bits, 4-bit with a larger model wins.
  • Song et al. [R13 p.1, p.4]: on a laptop CPU, heavily quantized large models beat small high-precision ones, with a threshold near 3.5 BPW.
  • Badshah & Sajjad (abstract only).
  • PolyQ [R19]: within-model bits vs quality at a memory budget on CPUs.
  • MCAP [R14 p.16]: a GPU-VRAM budget choosing among paging, layer skipping and AWQ, with quality reported.
  • Cache-conditional experts [2412.00099]: quality traded for DRAM residency on phones.

What differs:

  • Every iso-memory study above assumes the model fits, so memory equals model bits.
  • Streaming breaks that identity: at a fixed host RSS of B, SipLLM can run a model whose file is 25x larger than B [SIPLLM: 13B in 317 MB].
  • The axis "quality and tok/s at a fixed host-RSS ceiling, including streamed-larger-than-RAM points, on CPU-only phones" was not found in any source. MCAP is closest but bounds GPU VRAM and leaves CPU RAM unbounded [R14 p.13].
  • [INFERENCE] Kumar et al. [R12b] suggest the 4-bit optimum may move for heavily trained modern small models, so the answer is not derivable from Dettmers.

Surviving claim [HYPOTHESIS]:

  • Claim: at fixed host-RSS budgets B ∈ {256 MB, 512 MB, 1 GB, 2 GB} on the Dimensity 900, cold cache, there exist budgets where a streamed larger model (for example 8B Q4 at budget B) has higher task accuracy per second than the best fully resident model that fits B (for example a 0.5–1.5B at Q4–Q8). Otherwise the crossover never occurs at usable speeds.
  • Grid: models of 0.5/1.1/3/8B × Q4_K/Q6_K/Q8_0 × budgets.
  • Metrics: accuracy on a fixed small suite (for example GSM8K-100, MMLU-200, HellaSwag-200, chosen before running), decode tok/s, TTFT, and peak RSS from /usr/bin/time -l or /proc VmHWM.
  • Objective: accuracy at a deadline (tasks completed correctly per hour) as well as accuracy alone.
  • A negative result is publishable as a measured frontier: "streaming never wins on quality per second below X GB/s of storage".
C5 (KV eviction under a hard ceiling): NARROWED. The mechanism is fully pre-empted (InfiniGen §4.4, H2O, StreamingLLM, vLLM, llama.cpp). The survivor (joint KV-vs-pinned-layers planning under one host-RSS ceiling) is unbuilt in SipLLM.

Closest prior art:

  • InfiniGen §4.4 [R5 p.9, Table 2 p.11]: a KV pool with a user-defined memory limit, a victim policy (counter, FIFO or LRU), and perplexity reported at an 80% limit.
  • Fixed-budget lossy eviction with quality: H2O [R8 p.4] and StreamingLLM [R9 p.5].
  • vLLM [R7 p.8]: lossless sequence-granularity eviction (swap or recompute) from a fixed block pool.
  • llama.cpp [R11]: context shift and the --cache-ram MiB-bounded FIFO prompt cache, in a CPU runtime.
  • Quality cost at fixed ratios: R10 Table 2.

What differs:

  • SipLLM implements no KV eviction at all. It refuses or stops at ctx (section 0). C5 describes unbuilt work.
  • None of the above prices KV eviction or quantization jointly with weight residency under one host-RSS ceiling.
  • InfiniGen bounds only the CPU KV pool, and only at 80%. H2O's budget is a fraction of tokens.

Surviving claim [HYPOTHESIS]:

  • Claim: under one ceiling B, the planner should choose among (ctx, KV precision FP32/Q8, KV eviction budget, pinned layers). For long contexts the quality-per-second optimum is interior: some KV bytes are worth more as Q8 KV than as pinned layers, and vice versa.
  • Experiment: TinyLlama and 8B at a fixed B. Sweep KV mode {FP32 full, Q8 full, Q8 + sink-window eviction at 50/25%} against the pinned layers the remaining budget buys. Measure LongBench-subset/needle accuracy and tok/s.
  • The prediction from R10: Q8 full dominates eviction.
  • The survivor is a planner contribution, not a KV-algorithm contribution.
Bound / contract framing (C1 support, inside this cluster's scope)
  • No formally verified or statically proven peak-memory bound for LLM inference was found (queries 6–7).
  • Nearest prior art:
    • TFLM's fixed arena with init-time refusal and no allocation during invoke [R18 p.6] (MCU, small models).
    • Static arena planners that minimize rather than guarantee [Pisarchyk & Lee].
    • PolyQ's modeled, unenforced CPU footprint [R19 p.7].
    • FlexGen's LP with memory constraints that "sometimes … can run out of memory" [R15 p.6].
    • OS-enforced caps used as experimental controls [R2].
    • memopro (unverified vendor page) claims budget, refusal and per-layer re-reads [X3].
  • Two holes in SipLLM's own guarantee found in source [INFERENCE] (section 0):
    1. The KV grow_to transient (1.5x to just under 2x KV, depending on ctx).
    2. The semantic cache outside the ledger, plus FP32 out-copies.
  • A third, system-level caveat: RSS excludes the page cache that pread streaming fills, and every published SipLLM number is warm-cache.
"Bad RAM" literal reading: real, but a different direction from SipLLM
  • "Tolerate errors in weight storage" is a real research line:
    • EDEN [R20 p.1]: deliberately under-volted, healthy DRAM with retraining; CNNs; DRAM energy down 21–37%.
    • RangeGuard [R21 p.2] shows LLMs are fragile: one BF16 exponent flip at a critical position causes significant accuracy loss, and protection needs ECC-style metadata.
    • I found no source evaluating LLMs on old or defective consumer DRAM.
  • "Need less of it" is the operational route: memtest= / memmap=nn$ss (kernel-parameters.txt) map faulty regions out, leaving a smaller-RAM machine. SipLLM's bounded-RAM planner is a natural fit for such a machine, but that is C1, not a new claim.
  • Speculative bridge [HYPOTHESIS], one sentence for future work, not a claim:
    • Streamed layers are re-read from flash every token, so a DRAM bit flip in a streamed weight buffer lives for at most one token. Pinned layers and the KV cache keep the corruption.
    • Q4_K/Q8_0 quants have no per-weight exponent, but their per-block fp16 scales do.
    • Test: fault injection into ring vs pinned buffers vs KV, measuring output divergence.
4. Top lessons for SipLLM (ranked by expected impact)
  1. Make the streaming numbers cache-honest before any paper claim. [INFERENCE] Evidence: section 0; R2 pp.1-2.
    • Every published streamed tok/s is warm page cache (bench_ram_budget.sh:101-102, 155), and pread without DONTNEED/F_NOCACHE leaves the model in the page cache.
    • On a real 2–3 GB device the file cannot stay cached. Speed will equal flash bandwidth, and the e-waste story depends on that number.
    • Actions:
      • (a) Report cold-cache runs (PURGE=1 already exists in scripts/bench.sh:31; on Android, echo 3 > /proc/sys/vm/drop_caches as root, or a cgroup).
      • (b) Run under cgroup-v2 memory.max = budget and log /proc/diskstats bytes per token vs expected layer bytes.
      • (c) Optionally posix_fadvise(POSIX_FADV_DONTNEED) / F_NOCACHE after consuming each streamed layer (include/llm/file_backing.h), as a guarded flag.
  2. Benchmark against the strongest baseline: llama.cpp mmap under an OS cap at the same budget.
    • R2 found kernel-managed residency within 9–11% of user-pinned buffers at equal enforced memory, for MoE.
    • For dense cyclic scans, kernel LRU-like reclaim should thrash (R1 p.13), which would show SipLLM's planner advantage cleanly. [HYPOTHESIS] Or it may show none.
    • Files: scripts/bench*.sh.
  3. Close the contract holes (section 0). They matter because TFLM-style contract language is the C1 story.
    • (a) KV growth transient: in budget mode, allocate the planned KV once at load, since it is priced at full ctx anyway, or price (c + ctx)/ctx, which is up to about 2x (kv_cache.h, mem_plan.cpp:144-161).
    • (b) Put --semantic-cache-budget plus its transient copies in the ledger (mem_plan.cpp, runtime.cpp:105-114, 213-228).
    • (c) Add a regression test: tight budget, long generation crossing ctx/2, asserting VmHWM ≤ budget.
  4. Prefer KV quantization over eviction (R10 Table 2 p.5; R5 Table 2 p.11).
    • Let the semantic cache store Q8 rows (runtime.cpp:107).
    • Have the planner try --kv-q8 before capping ctx (order in mem_plan.h:15-19).
    • If eviction is ever added, use sinks plus counter/importance victims, never FIFO.
  5. **Spread pinning in loader.cpp:plan_and_pin_layers** behind a default-off flag. Expected 10–25% decode gains only in the partial-residency, low-F/c regime (toy model). Prior art: R15, R16.
  6. Run the C4 frontier experiment (section 3). It is the most plausible *novel measurement* in this cluster.
  7. Drop C3 and the semantic cache as research claims.
    • C3 is pre-empted for experts and trivial for dense layers.
    • The semantic cache is SGLang's RadixAttention algorithm (R6 p.4).
    • In the paper, one sentence with the dense miss-optimality argument and a citation to R1/R17 is enough.
5. Open questions I could not resolve
  • Does the KV grow_to transient actually show in peak RSS? The allocator or OS could defer page commit of assign(per,0), but zero-filling touches the pages. I have not run it.
  • What is the Dimensity 900's cold-cache streaming throughput for TinyLlama and 8B Q4? This determines F/c and therefore whether Lesson 5 or the C4 crossover is reachable.
  • Is the memopro vendor page accurate (mechanism, measured RSS)? It was read only through a summarizer. If accurate, it pre-empts much of C1 in a Python stack.
  • Is there an older systems paper on "MRU/static pinning is optimal for cyclic layer scans" in the DNN-offloading literature (vDNN, SwapAdvisor, ZeRO-Infinity)? Not searched, since it is out of cluster scope. A proceedings browse could find it; it would not change the C3 verdict.
  • What quality does H2O/StreamingLLM-style eviction cost at very small KV budgets on small (≤1.5B) models? All quality numbers I read are for 7–8B models (R10) or OPT/Llama-2 (R5, R8).
  • Do Q4_K/Q8_0 GGUF weights tolerate random DRAM bit flips better than BF16? No source found; RangeGuard covers BF16.
  • 2508.06978 ("SSD offloading … considered harmful in energy efficiency") was not read. It may undercut the storage-for-RAM economic bet. Pass to the C7/energy owner.
Domain E: Mobile GPU, Unified Memory Architecture & Shaders — (31 Source Records Analyzed)

Cluster E: LLM inference on small, integrated and mobile GPUs (Vulkan first) on unified-memory devices

Local, untracked working file. Never commit. Written 2026-10-23.

Labels: [PAPER p.N] is the paper's own claim. [SRC file:line] or [ISSUE #N] is a repo file or GitHub item I read verbatim (via gh api or the local clone). [SIPLLM] is a SipLLM fact from the brief, the repo or .claude-artifacts/vulkan/. [INFERENCE] is my reasoning. [HYPOTHESIS] needs an experiment.

PDFs are under /Users/mobile/Desktop/Dev/sipllm/.claude-artifacts/research/E-mobile-gpu/papers/. Web snapshots are under .../E-mobile-gpu/web/. Page numbers come from per-page pdftotext extraction (.../E-mobile-gpu/tools/findpage.sh), so they are PDF page indices, not printed folios.

0. Bottom line (read this first)
  1. The premise "Vulkan offload is unique" is false. llama.cpp ships a Vulkan backend (ggml/src/ggml-vulkan/), and ExecuTorch runs Llama 3.2 1B on a Mali GPU through its Vulkan backend (Arm learning path). Every leading published Android GPU LLM engine was evaluated on OpenCL, not Vulkan: ML Drift (LiteRT lineage), Transformer-Lite, MNN-LLM, HeteroInfer, and MLC-LLM as measured in arXiv 2410.03613. Vulkan is the more portable API on Android, since OpenCL is not part of the platform (TFLite GPU paper p.7). On Mali it is also the API with the worst published LLM results.
  2. C6 verdict: NARROWED. This is a design, not a result. vulkan_matmul() never dispatches and mem_plan.cpp has no GPU lines, so nothing about C6 is measured. The coordinator must not put it in a paper as a finding. Budgeting GPU-visible weight buffers inside a memory budget, with weights streamed from storage into memory the GPU reads without a second copy, already exists for DNNs on Jetson (SwapNet: operator budget, O_DIRECT into cudaMallocManaged). For LLMs it exists on Apple UMA (LLM in a flash: MTLStorageModeShared weight cache at a fixed half-model DRAM fraction). What survives: a hard, planner-enforced peak-RSS ceiling that prices Vulkan allocations on a commodity Android phone GPU, with per-token layer streaming into mapped Vulkan memory, verified against VmHWM. I found no prior art for that combination.
  3. The clause "so even tiny GPUs help" is dropped from the narrowed C6. The evidence today is negative for Mali-G68-class GPUs. SipLLM's own spike puts the untuned GPU upper bound (matmul only) at 17.7 tok/s against an end-to-end CPU prefill of 47. Published and community data agree:
    • Mali-G720 and G78 are slower than the CPU on both phases under MLC-LLM.
    • RK3588 Mali-G610 with llama.cpp Vulkan runs prompt at 3.3 vs 35.9 tok/s on CPU.
    • llama.cpp Vulkan segfaults on Vulkan-1.1 Mali drivers as of 2026-10-21. There is no precedent for a fast fp16 Vulkan kernel on G68-class Mali.
    • ML Drift's ~800 tok/s Mali-G715 prefill uses int8 instructions (p.5). [INFERENCE] The arithmetic says it probably requires them (§3.4).
    • The only fp16 Vulkan Mali precedent is llama.cpp PR #15800: 150 GFLOPS on a G715. G715 has 4× G68's fp16 rate per core per clock [Arm sheet: 512 vs 128 ops/cycle], and the PR states neither core count nor clock. [INFERENCE] Scaled per core and per clock, it implies no more than the ~35 GFLOPS the SipLLM spike already reaches on G68 MC4. Three routes remain open:
    • (a) CPU+GPU co-execution, which needs no new kernel;
    • (b) an OpenCL int8-dot path, since G68 silicon has int8 dot and only the Vulkan driver hides it;
    • (c) streamed prefill, which is compute-bound, so a ring of Vulkan memory can pay off there (§6.1). All three are [HYPOTHESIS].
  4. Media and games are not evidence of LLM capability (§9). Video decode runs on separate codec hardware (Intel VDBox, NVDEC, VideoToolbox, V4L2 m2m codecs; VLC on Android uses MediaCodec). Graphics keeps its working set in on-chip tile memory with compressed textures and framebuffers, and uses fixed-function texture, blend and pixel units. LLM decode streams every weight once per token (≈3.6 FLOP/byte at Q4_K). Mali shared memory is not on-chip SRAM (Arm Best Practices p.92). SipLLM's own f16vec4 benchmark did not reach Arm's documented 2× fp16 rate.
1. [SIPLLM] Current state: code and phone measurements
1.1 Code (/Users/mobile/Desktop/Dev/sipllm/src/vulkan.cpp, include/llm/vulkan_backend.h)
  • make VULKAN=1 defines LLM_HAVE_VULKAN and **links -lvulkan** (Makefile:94-96). It does not dlopen. The default build is a stub that always runs the CPU matmul.
  • With Vulkan compiled in, VkCtx creates an instance (API 1.1), picks the first integrated or discrete GPU, and reports its name. A CPU rasterizer such as llvmpipe is refused as "not a GPU".
  • vulkan_matmul() never dispatches. The body is a comment: "Full compute dispatch would go here". It always falls through to the CPU matmul() and returns false. The only shader is shaders/matmul.comp: fp32, one invocation per output row, no tiling, no quantized weights. It is embedded as shaders/matmul_spv.h (glslc output turned into a C array by od).
  • The header comment still names a "POCO X6 Pro's Mali-G615". That is stale relative to the Dimensity 900 / Mali-G68 measurements below.
  • src/mem_plan.cpp has no GPU line items (grep finds none for vulkan or gpu).
  • tools/vk_prefill_spike.cpp, tools/vk_probe.cpp and the shaders shaders/peak_flops.comp, q4k_gemm.comp (v1) and q4k_gemm_v2.comp are not on the current branch (release/v0.6.0). They live on local branch **issue-67-vulkan** (head 5eb1982, 2026-10-19), which I read with git show only. The built binaries and SPIR-V sit in build-android/ and build-android/spv/. The numbers below are reproducible from that branch, not from the release tree.
  • From the SPIR-V (my scan of OpExecutionMode and OpVariable): q4k_gemm_f16 and q4k_gemm_f32 ("v1") use local_size 64 with two workgroup (shared-memory) arrays, and both declare the Float16 capability. q4k_gemm_v2 uses local_size 64 with no shared memory and no Float16.
1.2 Device probe (vulkan/probe-2026-10-19.txt, probe2-...)

Device: Mali-G68 MC4. Vulkan api 1.1.177, driver 0x8001000, vendor 0x13b5. Limits:

  • maxComputeSharedMemorySize 32 KB, maxComputeWorkGroupInvocations 512.
  • maxStorageBufferRange 256 MB, minStorageBufferOffsetAlignment 64.
  • timestampPeriod 76.9 ns.
  • Subgroup size fixed at 16 (min = max = 16), with arithmetic, ballot and shuffle ops.

Features and extensions present:

  • shaderInt16=1, shaderInt64=0.
  • fp16 and int8 arithmetic plus 16- and 8-bit storage.
  • VK_EXT_subgroup_size_control, VK_KHR_buffer_device_address.
  • VK_EXT_external_memory_dma_buf, VK_KHR_external_memory_fd, AHardwareBuffer.

Absent: VK_KHR_shader_integer_dot_product, VK_KHR_cooperative_matrix, **VK_EXT_external_memory_host, VK_EXT_memory_budget**, VK_KHR_maintenance4.

Memory:

  • One heap, 7516 MB, flagged DEVICE_LOCAL.
  • Type 0 = DEVICE_LOCAL | HOST_VISIBLE | COHERENT (0x7).
  • Type 1 = DEVICE_LOCAL | HOST_VISIBLE | HOST_CACHED (0xb, not coherent).
  • Type 2 = DEVICE_LOCAL | LAZILY_ALLOCATED (0x11), which is "not allowed for buffer".
  • Storage buffers report memoryTypeBits=0x3. Every memory type a storage buffer can use on this driver is host-visible.

Accounting (256 MB per test):

  • Host-visible allocation (A): VmRSS 24.7 → 287.9 MB. The whole increase is RssFile (18.3 → 281.4 MB). dumpsys meminfo shows "GL mtrack" 263,536 kB, and TOTAL PSS rises by the same amount.
  • "Device-local" B is also a host-visible type, because type 2 is excluded for buffers. It shows the same +263 MB in VmRSS.
  • **Immediately after vkFreeMemory, VmRSS is still 289 MB. By the next sample, 2 s later ("[A: freed +2s]"), it is back to baseline.** VmHWM keeps the 289 MB peak.

[INFERENCE] On this phone, any GPU buffer SipLLM allocates counts against VmRSS and VmHWM in full, from the moment of allocation. Memory freed by the driver is released lazily, within ≤2 s. The probe did not sample between 0 and 2 s.

1.3 Compute (peak*, spike1-3)
  • peak and peak2 were broken runs. Both took about 5 s whatever the iteration count (0.0 to 3.5 GFLOPS). **The ~200 GFLOPS peak rests on peak3 alone**: f32 165 to 223, f16 199 to 200 GFLOPS (median by timestamps).
  • f16 was no faster than f32, even though the benchmark used packed fp16.
    • shaders/peak_flops.comp on issue-67-vulkan runs 8 independent vec4 FMA chains per thread, and with -DF16 the same chains as **f16vec4**. So the f16 run did request packed fp16.
    • Arm documents 2× for packed fp16: "A Valhall core can perform 32 FP32 FMAs ... per clock" (Valhall shader core doc 102203, p.10). "A single 16-wide warp maths unit can therefore perform 32x fp16/int16 operations per clock cycle, or 64x int8 operations per clock cycle" (p.11). The Best Practices guide adds that "Modern GPU architectures use packed f16x2 instructions to improve arithmetic performance" (101897, p.95).
    • [INFERENCE] G68 MC4 peak = 4 cores × 32 FMA × 2 FLOP × f = 256·f GFLOPS (fp32) and 512·f (packed fp16). The measured 223 GFLOPS fp32 implies f ≈ 0.87 GHz (the clock was not recorded). Packed fp16 should then reach ≈445 GFLOPS, and int8 dot (OpenCL only on this driver) ≈890 GOPS.
    • The measured ~200 GFLOPS fp16 is half the documented rate.
    • [HYPOTHESIS] The cause is one of: (a) the driver compiler not emitting packed FMA.v2f16 for this shader; (b) a lower GPU clock during the f16 run (the runs were back to back); (c) a limit not in Arm's public docs. Test with the Mali Offline Compiler on peak_flops.comp -DF16 (it reports per-pipeline cycles) and a devfreq clock log during each run.
    • On the documented peaks, the v1 spike's 34.7 GFLOPS is ~16% of fp32 peak and ~8% of packed-fp16 peak.
  • v1 Q4_K GEMM (shared-memory tiles, fp16 storage):
    • TinyLlama shapes at B=128 (spike3, warm): 31.4 / 3.9 / 83.0 / 88.0 ms for q,o / k,v / gate,up / down. That works out to ≈ 11.3 GFLOP per layer in 324.5 ms ≈ 34.7 GFLOPS (~17% of the 200 GFLOPS measured peak).
    • The matmul-only prefill upper bound is 17.7 tok/s.
    • Numerical error is ~4e-4.
  • v2 (no shared memory, fp32): ~7 GFLOPS, error ~1e-6, at most 3.7 tok/s.
  • **Caveat 1: the spike's cpu_fast column is not a valid CPU baseline.** It reaches only 11 to 15 tok/s for matmul alone, below the engine's end-to-end CPU prefill (SipLLM 47; llama.cpp pp128 57.77 at 4 threads, llamabench-cpu-pp-t4). The runs also started at very different big-core clocks: spike1 1.14 GHz, llama-bench CPU 1.43 GHz, spike3 2.4 GHz. The battery was at 20 to 22%.
  • The conclusion still holds, for a more robust reason. GPU matmul-only time is an upper bound on GPU end-to-end throughput, so GPU ≤ 17.7 tok/s < CPU 47 tok/s.
  • The GPU time includes no weight-upload cost: "with weight upload" changed 15.2 → 15.1. Upload is about 1 ms per matrix, small compared with compute at B ≥ 64.
  • [SIPLLM, git show issue-67-vulkan:shaders/q4k_gemm.comp] The ~4e-4 error appears in both v1 variants. That matches the source: activations X are stored as fp16 (uvec2 = 4×fp16), and weights are dequantized to fp16 into shared memory. -DACC32 changes only the multiply-add to fp32. The error comes from fp16 storage, not accumulation. v2 keeps X and weights in fp32, giving ~1e-6.
1.4 llama.cpp Vulkan on the same phone (llamabench-vulkan-gpu-2026-10-19.txt, ext/llama-vulkan-build.md)
  • Build 585080d (2026-05-20). ggml_vulkan reports: Mali-G68 MC4, uma 1, fp16 1, warp 16, shared 32768, int dot 0, matrix cores none.
  • -ngl 99 gives Segmentation fault (exit 139) within 1.94 s. VmHWM reaches 1,315,888 kB.
  • [INFERENCE] The pattern is consistent with the pre-1.2 Mali crash class in §3.12: api 1.1.177, no external_memory_host, segfault at load. Two tests would decide it: (a) a tombstone or backtrace (ggml_vk_create_buffer → null function pointer), and (b) whether -ngl 0 or -dev none with the Vulkan build also crashes, as reported in [ISSUE #30920].
