3. Per-paper / per-source records (most relevant first)
3.1 SwapNet — Efficient Swapping for DNN Inference on Edge AI Devices Beyond the Memory Budget
  • Cite: K. Wang, J. Cao, Z. Zhou, Z. Li, IEEE TMC (preprint), 2024. arXiv:2401.16757, https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.16757. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-5, 8-10, 13).
  • Problem: run DNNs larger than an assigned memory budget on unified-memory edge AI devices (Jetson) without compression or offload [PAPER p.1].
  • Mechanism:
    • Partition the model into blocks, and execute and swap them in order. With m=2 blocks in flight, n = ⌈m·s/b⌉ for model size s and budget b [PAPER p.8].
    • Swap-out is write-back-free, because weights are read-only [PAPER p.4].
    • "Direct block fetch": DMA plus Direct I/O (O_DIRECT) to bypass the page cache [PAPER p.5, p.9].
    • "Copy-free GPU dispatch": malloc is replaced by cudaMallocManaged, and .to(cuda) is patched to return the same pointer. This removes the second copy that exists "in the same physical system memory" on UMA [PAPER p.4-5].
    • Multi-DNN budget allocation by a performance score [PAPER p.8].
  • Memory regime: enforces an operator- or scheduler-set budget per DNN. It partitions to fit the budget and does not refuse. It reports per-model memory consumption staying within budget [PAPER p.10]. It does not report process peak RSS by /usr/bin/time.
  • Hardware: Jetson NX 8 GB (main), Jetson Nano 4 GB [PAPER p.10]. CUDA 10.2, PyTorch 1.6.
  • Their numbers: latency close to the sufficient-memory case when DNNs need 2.32 to 5.81× the budget [PAPER p.1]. Baselines that use the page cache double peak memory, and a CPU-to-GPU copy triples it for GPU models [PAPER p.10].
  • Does NOT do:
    • CNNs only. Transformers and LLMs are listed as future work [PAPER p.13].
    • Swaps once per inference, not per autoregressive token.
    • CUDA and Jetson, not Vulkan or phones.
    • No KV cache, no hard refusal, no itemized ledger.
  • Pre-empts:
    • C6: PARTIAL. It has the core mechanism (budgeted streaming into a single CPU/GPU-shared copy on UMA), but for CNNs on CUDA.
    • C1: PARTIAL (operator budget on an edge device), but it does not refuse and does not report peak RSS. Flagged for the C1 cluster.
    • C7: PARTIAL (write-back-free swap-out is the same read-only argument).
  • Lessons:
    • The double-copy trap SwapNet measured (page cache + CPU copy + GPU copy) is exactly what SipLLM must avoid in src/vulkan.cpp: pread straight into mapped Vulkan memory.
    • Its m=2 in-flight blocks is the same as SipLLM's 1-2 layer ring.
  • Contribution opportunity:
    • [HYPOTHESIS] Per-token layer streaming into a Vulkan-mapped ring under a hard VmHWM ceiling keeps peak RSS within ±2% of the planner's prediction with the GPU on, on Mali-G68.
    • Experiment: --ram-budget {150, 300, 700} MB, GPU on and off, VmHWM vs planner ledger, TinyLlama and Llama-3.1-8B.
3.2 LLM in a flash — Efficient Large Language Model Inference with Limited Memory (UMA/GPU aspects only)
  • Cite: K. Alizadeh, I. Mirzadeh, D. Belenko et al. (Apple), ACL 2024. arXiv:2312.11514, https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.11514. Read: PARTIAL (pp.7-8, 15).
  • Problem: run LLMs whose weights exceed the available DRAM by loading from flash on demand.
  • Mechanism (GPU-relevant):
    • Evaluated on CPU, on Metal GPU (M1 Max, M2 Ultra) and on an RTX 4090, assuming "nearly half of the total memory (DRAM and GPU)" is available [PAPER p.7].
    • On Apple, the weight cache lives in GPU-allocator tensors created with MTLStorageModeShared, so "both the CPU and the GPU [can] access the same memory buffer directly, without redundant copies" [PAPER p.15].
    • MLX Metal kernels are used.
  • Memory regime: fits a fixed fraction (about 50%) of the model. No operator-set ceiling and no measured peak RSS.
  • Hardware: M1 Max 1 TB SSD, M2 Ultra, RTX 4090.
  • Their numbers: OPT-6.7B per-token latency, Metal M1: naive 2389 ms vs "All" 565 ms (I/O 92 ms); Metal M2: 2270 vs 305 ms [PAPER p.8, latency table].
  • Does NOT do: no hard ceiling, no Android/Vulkan, relies on sparsity predictors, half-model fixed DRAM.
  • Pre-empts:
    • C6: PARTIAL. A zero-copy UMA weight cache read by the GPU while streaming from flash, for an LLM, on Apple. The C6 "GPU inside the same memory budget on UMA" idea is therefore not new on Apple silicon.
    • C1: NONE (no ceiling).
  • Lessons: the shared-storage allocation pattern maps to Vulkan host-visible memory on Mali (types 0 and 1). See §6.
  • Contribution opportunity: [HYPOTHESIS] dense, predictor-free layer streaming under a hard ceiling on a phone GPU reaches a decode rate within X% of CPU streaming at equal VmHWM. Experiment: TinyLlama --ram-budget 0 with GPU on and off; expect parity because it is storage-bound (§6.1).
3.3 Understanding LLMs in Your Pockets — Performance Study on COTS Mobile Devices
  • Cite: J. Xiao, Q. Huang, X. Chen, C. Tian, arXiv:2410.03613 (latest version, 2025), https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.03613. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-4, 7, 11-14, 16-17).
  • Problem: measurement study of llama.cpp (CPU, plus GPU on some Snapdragon/Apple) and MLC-LLM (OpenCL on Android GPUs) across phones and tablets.
  • Mechanism (method):
    • Profiled with Snapdragon Profiler and Arm Streamline (ALU and load-store utilization).
    • Peak FP16 and bandwidth measured with clpeak.
    • Energy measured from sysfs on a rooted device [PAPER p.4, p.11, p.13].
  • Memory regime: N/A (measurement). It reports ~4.2 GB for Llama2-7B on GPU, growing with prompt and chunk size, and "higher memory demand compared to inference on CPU" [PAPER p.7].
  • Hardware: Snapdragon 8 Gen 3/Adreno 750, 8+ Gen 1/Adreno 730, 870/Adreno 650; Dimensity 9300/Mali-G720 MP12; Mali-G78 MP22, Mali-G77 MP8; Apple M1 [PAPER p.4 Table I].
  • Their headline numbers:
    • "For the two Mali GPUs, both prefill and decode speeds are slower than on the CPUs." On Mali-G720, prefill drops below 5 tok/s for models above 4B [PAPER p.7].
    • Mali-G720 ALU utilization is "less than 3%" in prefill. Adreno 750 reaches ~20%; Apple M1 up to 92% [PAPER p.12].
    • On Mali-G720, over 80% of load-store reads are partial-width. Decode load-store utilization is above 90% [PAPER p.12].
    • Mali-G720 is ~10× slower than Adreno 750 in prefill despite 1.5× its FP16 throughput (clpeak) [PAPER p.12].
    • Qualcomm's re-tiled MLC (32×16 contiguous tiles) gives 6× prefill [PAPER p.12].
    • Energy (Xiaomi Pad 6 Pro, Adreno 730, llama.cpp, Llama2-7B, [64,128]) [PAPER p.13 Table V]:
      • GPU prefill 46.03 tok/s at 0.013 mAh/token vs CPU with int8 instructions 12.87 tok/s at 0.038.
      • GPU decode 7.33 at 0.058 vs CPU 4.51 at 0.117.
      • That is −87.8% prefill energy and −59.8% decode energy vs plain CPU [PAPER p.14].
    • MLC model loading takes ~9× (Adreno) and ~5× (Mali) as long as warmed llama.cpp. The paper attributes this to copying weights between CPU and GPU "separate memory spaces" [PAPER p.16].
  • Does NOT do: no Vulkan, no memory ceiling, no Mali energy numbers, no tuned Mali kernels.
  • Pre-empts: C6 NONE. It is evidence against the benefit half of C6 on Mali.
  • Lessons:
    • Mali GEMM fails on access pattern, not FLOPs. Avoid partial-width loads and use 128-bit vector loads. Make accesses warp-contiguous: lane *i* reads bytes base + 16·i of the same row, per Arm Valhall doc p.11-12; see §9.3c. Re-lay weights at load.
    • "Prefill on GPU, decode on CPU" is their recommended hybrid [PAPER p.7, p.17].
  • [INFERENCE] Their claim that CPU and GPU "cannot directly access each other's data" (p.16) describes MLC's OpenCL usage, not the hardware. SipLLM's probe shows host-visible device-local memory on Mali-G68.
  • Contribution opportunity: [HYPOTHESIS] a Mali-specific Vulkan Q4_K GEMM with 128-bit loads and a warp-contiguous layout (adjacent lanes read adjacent 16-byte chunks of one row) exceeds 40% ALU utilization on G68. Measure with Arm Streamline counters plus achieved GFLOPS against peak3.
3.4 ML Drift — Scaling On-Device GPU Inference for Large Generative Models
  • Cite: J. Tang, R. Sarokin, E. Ignasheva et al. (Google), CVPR-W 2025. arXiv:2505.00232, https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00232. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-8).
  • Problem: run large generative models on mobile, laptop and desktop GPUs across OpenCL, Metal and WebGPU.
  • Mechanism:
    • It "extends the basic architecture of a well-established GPU-accelerated ML inference engine [26]". Ref [26] is the TFLite GPU paper, 1907.01989 [PAPER p.2]. [INFERENCE] That makes it the TFLite-GPU / LiteRT lineage. The paper cites LiteRT as [18] but does not say ML Drift is what ships in LiteRT.
    • Tensor virtualization (logical tensors mapped to buffers, image buffers or 2D/3D textures).
    • Weight-layout search gives "up to a 20% speedup" [PAPER p.3].
    • Weights are converted at load into the optimal GPU layout [PAPER p.4].
    • Greedy memory sharing for activations: SD 1.4 runtime memory 4.31 GB → 387 MB [PAPER p.5].
    • Stage-aware LLM path: prefill quantizes activations to int8 so it can "leverage fast int8 instructions"; decode fuses activation quantization into the kernel [PAPER p.5].
    • The Android engine is OpenCL.
  • Memory regime: fits a fixed device. "Llama 3.1 8B q8 terminated due to memory limitations" on Adreno 750/740 and Mali-G715 phones (8-12 GB) [PAPER p.6 Table 2]. No ceiling.
  • Hardware: Adreno 830/750/740, Immortalis-G720, Mali-G715 (Pixel 9, 12 GB), Intel Ultra 7 165U/258V, RTX 4090, Apple.
  • Their numbers (tok/s, 1024 prefill + 256 decode) [PAPER p.6 Table 2]:
    • Mali-G715: Gemma-2B q8 796 / 11.9; Llama-3.2-3B q8 507 / 8.71; Llama-3.1-8B 8/4/4 240 / 6.46.
    • Immortalis-G720: Llama-3.2-3B q8 791 / 12.5 vs MLC 89.2 / 11.2 [PAPER p.7]. That is ≈8.9× prefill, but ML Drift ran q8 while MLC ran q4f16, so the quantizations differ.
    • Adreno 830 Gemma-2B 8/4/4: 1490 / 42.5.
    • Intel 258V iGPU Gemma-2B 8/4/4: 4320 / 57.8 [PAPER p.8 Table 4].
    • "Mali GPUs, where llama.cpp is currently not supported" [PAPER p.7].
  • Does NOT do: no Vulkan on Android, no CPU-vs-GPU on the same phone, no peak-RSS measurement, no streaming.
  • Pre-empts: C6 NONE (it is all-resident). It is the standing-goal competitor's GPU stack, so its Mali-G715 numbers are the bar for "better than LiteRT on ARM".
  • [INFERENCE] The Mali number likely depends on int8 dot product.
    • Gemma-2B has ≈1.98B non-embedding parameters, so prefill costs ≈3.96 GFLOP/token. At 796 tok/s that is ≈3.15 TFLOPS.
    • Pixel 9's Mali-G715 fp16 peak is 512 ops/cycle/core (Arm sheet) × 7 cores × ~0.94 GHz ≈ 3.4 TFLOPS. The core count and clock are public specs I did not verify here.
    • That would be ~93% of fp16 peak, which is implausible. The paper's int8 prefill path (p.5) is the likely explanation.
    • So ML Drift is not evidence of fp16 headroom on G68 under Vulkan, whose driver hides int8 dot.
  • Lessons:
    • Tuned Mali kernels beat MLC by ≈9× in prefill (q8 vs q4f16). [HYPOTHESIS] The spike's ~8-16% of peak is partly a kernel problem. How much is recoverable with fp16-only Vulkan on G68 is unknown, and no precedent exists (see §3.12, #15800).
    • Separate prefill (GEMM, int8 activations) and decode (GEMV, fused quantization) kernels.
    • Re-lay weights at load.
  • Contribution opportunity: [HYPOTHESIS] at an equal 1 GB VmHWM ceiling on an 8-12 GB phone, SipLLM streaming plus GPU prefill runs Llama-3.1-8B, which ML Drift q8 cannot load at all. Experiment: Pixel-class phone, ML Drift/LiteRT vs SipLLM, /usr/bin/time peak RSS.
3.5 HeteroInfer (seed name "HeteroLLM") — Characterizing Mobile SoC for Accelerating Heterogeneous LLM Inference
  • Cite: L. Chen, D. Feng, E. Feng et al., SOSP '25. arXiv:2501.14794, DOI 10.1145/3731569.3764808, https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.14794. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-5, 8-9, 13).
  • Problem: use GPU and NPU together on a phone SoC.
  • Mechanism:
    • Characterization:
      • The GPU's FLOPS ramp linearly with tensor size, then plateau [PAPER p.4].
      • clFinish synchronization costs ~400 µs, plus clEnqueueWriteBuffer copies [PAPER p.4].
      • A single processor reaches only 40-45 GB/s of the 68 GB/s SoC bandwidth, while concurrent GPU and NPU reach ~60 GB/s [PAPER p.2, p.5 Fig.5].
    • Layer-level and tensor-level GPU-NPU partitioning, with a profiler-driven solver.
    • Fast synchronization:
      • UMA buffers are mapped into both address spaces from "a dedicated memory pool" of a few reused slots that the driver does not reclaim [PAPER p.8].
      • Predicted sleep, then polling of a flag word.
    • The CPU is used as a control plane only, citing energy [PAPER p.5].
    • OpenCL GPU kernels; QNN on the NPU.
  • Memory regime: fits a fixed device (no budget).
  • Hardware: Snapdragon 8 Gen 3 (and 8 Elite).
  • Their numbers:
    • Practical GPU ~1 TFLOPS vs 2.8 TFLOPS theoretical [PAPER p.1].
    • >1000 tok/s prefill, >50 tok/s decode [PAPER p.2].
    • Hetero-tensor uses 55% less energy than GPU-only [PAPER p.13].
    • 2.2% prefill slowdown under concurrent gaming [PAPER p.2].
  • Does NOT do: no Mali, no Vulkan, no memory ceiling, no CPU-vs-GPU energy comparison.
  • Pre-empts: C6 NONE.
  • Lessons:
    • On UMA, decode gains need co-execution, which raises aggregate bandwidth, not offload.
    • Synchronization cost must be amortized per layer, not per matmul.
    • Keep fixed, pre-mapped, reused buffer slots. This matches SipLLM's ring, and §1.2 shows why freeing is dangerous.
  • Contribution opportunity: [HYPOTHESIS] CPU and Mali-G68 co-executing decode GEMV (column split) raises aggregate bandwidth above CPU-only on Dimensity 900. Measure decode tok/s and DRAM bandwidth (perf counters) for a CPU-only vs CPU+GPU split.
3.6 Transformer-Lite — High-efficiency Deployment of Large Language Models on Mobile Phone GPUs
  • Cite: L. Li, S. Qian, J. Lu et al. (OPPO), arXiv:2403.20041, 2024, https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.20041. Read: FULL (pp.1-16).
  • Problem: fast LLMs on phone GPUs from ONNX graphs.
  • Mechanism:
    • Symbolic dynamic shapes. Shape operators run on the CPU all at once, so there is one CPU-GPU boundary [PAPER p.4].
    • OpenCL buffers for activations. Weights go in buffer or image; "image from buffer" avoids copies [PAPER p.5].
    • Separate prefill GEMM and decode GEMV kernels (half activations × 4-bit weights) [PAPER p.5-6].
    • Lowest OpenCL execution priority (Qualcomm/Arm extensions) to prevent UI lag [PAPER p.6].
    • E0M4 FP4: dequantization takes two bit operations, with no int→float convert [PAPER p.6].
    • In-place KV cache sub-tensors [PAPER p.7].
  • Memory regime: fits a fixed device. A 12 GB phone runs a 7B model only up to 900 tokens [PAPER p.11].
  • Hardware: Dimensity 9300 / Mali-G720 Immortalis MC12 (24 GB); Snapdragon 8 Gen 3 / Adreno 750 (12 GB) [PAPER p.8].
  • Their numbers:
    • Gemma-2B 330 / 30 tok/s and ChatGLM2-6B 121 / 14 tok/s on 8 Gen 3 [PAPER p.12].
    • 1.6 TFLOPS fp16 achieved vs 5.6 TFLOPS (ArchProbe) [PAPER p.11].
    • E0M4 speeds Mali GEMV up 1.33-1.56× but Adreno 1.00× [PAPER p.13 Table 1].
  • Does NOT do: no Vulkan, no ceiling, no CPU-vs-GPU on the same phone except against FastLLM.
  • Pre-empts: C6 NONE.
  • Lessons for src/vulkan.cpp shaders:
    • On Mali, int→float conversion in dequantization is a first-order cost. Use bit-trick dequantization (OR the nibble into a constant exponent; uintBitsToFloat/packHalf2x16 tricks) and keep everything in fp16.
    • Run the GPU at low priority. [HYPOTHESIS] VK_EXT_global_priority or queue priorities on Android.
  • Contribution opportunity: [HYPOTHESIS] bit-trick Q4_K→fp16 dequantization (scale and min folded per sub-block) gives ≥1.3× on Mali-G68 Vulkan GEMV vs convert-based dequantization. A micro-benchmark in the spike would test it.
3.7 MNN-LLM — A Generic Inference Engine for Fast LLM Deployment on Mobile Devices
  • Cite: Z. Wang, J. Yang, X. Qian et al. (Alibaba), arXiv:2506.10443, 2025, https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10443. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-7).
  • Problem: a general engine (MNN) extended to LLMs on mobile CPU and GPU.
  • Mechanism:
    • "Mobile GPUs typically employ Vulkan and OpenCL" [PAPER p.2].
    • DRAM-Flash hybrid storage: embeddings in flash (bf16), KV overflow to flash with prefetch [PAPER p.3].
    • W4A16 on GPU vs W4A8 on CPU [PAPER p.4].
    • GPU weights re-laid out and stored as OpenCL Image objects (Adreno texture path, "higher bandwidth"), 128-bit loads per work item [PAPER p.5].
  • Memory regime: reduces memory (embeddings in flash). No ceiling.
  • Hardware: Xiaomi 14 (Snapdragon 8 Gen 3); GPU via OpenCL [PAPER p.6].
  • Their numbers: GPU prefill up to 25.3× and decode 7.1× vs llama.cpp; 2.8× and 1.7× vs MLC [PAPER p.6].
  • Does NOT do: no Mali results, no Vulkan results, no peak-RSS figures.
  • Pre-empts: C6 NONE. The embedding-in-flash idea overlaps SipLLM's windowed embeddings (other clusters).
  • Lessons: 128-bit vectorized loads per work item, with "continuous memory reads between work items" [PAPER p.5], so adjacent lanes read adjacent addresses. The texture/image path is mainly an Adreno advantage.
  • Contribution opportunity: none specific beyond §6.
3.8 PowerInfer-2 — Fast Large Language Model Inference on a Smartphone (GPU aspects only)
  • Cite: Z. Xue, Y. Song, Z. Mi et al., arXiv:2406.06282 v3, 2024, https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.06282. Read: PARTIAL (pp.3-4, 8).
  • Problem: neuron-cluster sparse inference with flash offload on phones.
  • Mechanism (GPU-relevant):
    • On a OnePlus 12 (8 Gen 3), 7B INT4 prefill: NPU 770 tok/s, GPU 37, CPU 8 [PAPER p.3].
    • "GPUs Limited Role": for matrix-vector work the GPU is slower than NPU and CPU, with ~50% of kernel time spent computing. Phone makers "avoid using GPUs for LLM inference in production systems" (private communication) [PAPER p.4].
    • UMA bandwidth: CPU-only 43.9 GB/s, NPU-only 56, both 59.6 [PAPER p.4].
  • Memory regime: a memory budget with flash offload (the C1 and C7 clusters' concern). Uses mlock and needs root [PAPER p.8].
  • Hardware evaluated: OnePlus 12 (Snapdragon 8 Gen 3, Adreno 750 GPU, Hexagon NPU), among others.
  • Their headline numbers (GPU-relevant): GPU prefill 37 vs CPU 8 tok/s for 7B INT4; GPU GEMV slower than CPU [PAPER p.3-4].
  • What it does NOT do: no GPU in the final design, no Vulkan, no Mali.
  • Pre-empts: C6 NONE.
  • Lessons: an independent second source that GEMV on mobile GPUs loses to the CPU, and that co-execution raises aggregate bandwidth (supports §6.1.1-2).
  • Contribution opportunity: none beyond §3.5's co-execution hypothesis.
3.9 MELTing point — Mobile Evaluation of Language Transformers
  • Cite: S. Laskaridis, K. Katevas, L. Minto, H. Haddadi (Brave), MobiCom '24. arXiv:2403.12844, https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.12844. Read: PARTIAL (pp.3-4, 7-8).
  • Problem: device-in-the-loop benchmarking of MLC-LLM, llama.cpp and LLMFarm on phones and Jetsons.
  • Mechanism (method): automated device farm, with energy measured by a power monitor.
  • Memory regime: none (measurement study).
  • Hardware evaluated: Galaxy S23 (8 GB), Pixel 6a (8 GB), iPhone 14 Pro (6 GB), iPhone SE (4 GB), Jetsons [PAPER p.3-4].
  • Their headline numbers:
    • "gains from running on GPU were minimal" for llama.cpp on Android. TinyLlama-1.1B 4-bit on Galaxy S23: CPU 13.61 vs GPU 13.22 tok/s [PAPER p.7, footnote 8].
    • Metal iPhones get 78.93% higher generation throughput than OpenCL Android under MLC [PAPER p.7].
    • 0.21 / 0.20 / 0.16 mWh/token on S23 MLC, iPhone 14 Pro MLC and LLMFarm [PAPER p.8].
    • OOM crashes and device restarts were frequent [PAPER p.8].
  • What it does NOT do: no Vulkan, no memory ceiling, no Mali-specific kernel analysis.
  • Pre-empts: C6 NONE.
  • Lessons: llama.cpp's Android GPU path historically gave CPU parity, not gains. Report OOM and crash rates as first-class metrics in SipLLM's bench scripts.
  • Contribution opportunity: none specific.
3.10 Dissecting the Impact of Mobile DVFS Governors on LLM Inference Performance and Energy Efficiency
  • Cite: Z. Zhang, P. Dash, Y. C. Hu et al., arXiv:2507.02135, 2025, https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02135. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-6).
  • Problem: uncoordinated CPU, GPU and memory DVFS governors waste latency and energy during LLM inference.
  • Mechanism (relevant findings):
    • llama.cpp's GPU path (OpenCL via CLBlast) on Pixel 7 / 7 Pro (Tensor G2, Mali-G710 MP7) [PAPER p.4].
    • "ARM Mali GPUs only have a shallow queue with 2 outstanding entries," so the CPU stays busy feeding kernels throughout inference [PAPER p.3].
    • Monsoon-measured energy [PAPER p.4].
  • Memory regime: none.
  • Hardware evaluated: Pixel 7 (8 GB) and Pixel 7 Pro (12 GB), Tensor G2, Mali-G710 MP7 [PAPER p.4].
  • Their headline numbers: TinyLlama decode TPOT 215.1 ms (≈4.6 tok/s) at 402.7 mJ/token under default governors. Pinning the GPU at 848 MHz gives 126.9 ms at 396.5 mJ [PAPER p.6]. No CPU-only baseline in the excerpt read.
  • What it does NOT do: no Vulkan, no CPU-vs-GPU energy comparison in the part read.
  • Pre-empts: C6 NONE.
  • Lessons:
    • GPU offload on Mali still consumes a CPU core, so the CPU thread budget must reserve it.
    • GPU frequency governors leave 35-41% latency on the table.
    • TinyLlama decode via generic OpenCL on Mali-G710 (~4.6 tok/s) is well below SipLLM's CPU decode on the older Dimensity 900 (14.7 tok/s [SIPLLM]). [INFERENCE] Different phones and different stacks; directionally consistent with §3.3.
  • Contribution opportunity: [HYPOTHESIS] energy per token for SipLLM GPU prefill vs CPU prefill on Dimensity 900, measured the same way (battery bypass or sysfs).
3.11 TFLite GPU — On-Device Neural Net Inference with Mobile GPUs
  • Cite: J. Lee, N. Chirkov, E. Ignasheva et al. (Google), CVPR-W 2019. arXiv:1907.01989, https://arxiv.org/abs/1907.01989. Read: PARTIAL (pp.1-8).
  • Problem: fast CNN inference on mobile GPUs with wide device coverage.
  • Mechanism:
    • OpenGL ES 3.1 compute and Metal. PHWC4 layout (4-channel slices for vec4 loads).
    • Greedy and min-cost-flow intermediate memory sharing [PAPER p.3-6].
  • Memory regime: reduces intermediate-tensor memory. No ceiling.
  • Hardware evaluated: phones with Adreno 540/616/630 and Mali-G72 MP12, plus iOS devices [PAPER p.7 Fig.7].
  • Their headline numbers and findings:
    • Average 2-9× speedup over CPU (CNNs) [PAPER p.7].
    • "often bound by memory bandwidth", "20-40% ALU utilization" [PAPER p.7].
    • Mali performance is robust to workgroup size (<5% from tuning); Adreno is very sensitive (wrong size up to 5-7× slower) [PAPER p.4-5].
    • "Google Pixel devices do not support OpenCL"; SNPE does not run on Mali [PAPER p.7].
  • What it does NOT do: no LLMs, no quantized GEMM, no Vulkan.
  • Pre-empts: C6 NONE.
  • Lessons:
    • Vulkan (or GL compute) is the portable Android path; OpenCL availability is vendor-dependent.
    • On Mali, spend tuning effort on memory access and layout, not workgroup-size search.
  • Contribution opportunity: none specific.
3.12 llama.cpp ggml-vulkan (source) and its Mali / UMA issue trail
  • Cite (source): ggml/src/ggml-vulkan/ggml-vulkan.cpp at commit 585080d (2026-05-20), local clone /Users/mobile/Desktop/Dev/sipllm/.claude-artifacts/ext/llama.cpp. Read: PARTIAL (memory, UMA and feature paths).
  • Cite (issues, read verbatim via gh api):
    • Crashes on pre-1.2 Mali: #30920 (open, 2026-10-21), #28593 and #28319 (both closed, merged_at=null).
    • UMA and double-loading: #21357 and #21359 (closed, merged_at=null).
    • Performance on ARM: #22682 (RK3588), #15800 (open PR, embedded matmul), #18493 (closed, G720 tuning), #27163 (open, Mali warptiles).
    • Correctness and hangs: #28637 (open, subgroup-16 wrong results), #23359 (background hang), #30006 (G925 coopmat).
    • Android Vulkan slowness: discussion #9464.
  • Mechanism (source):
    • uma = deviceType == IntegratedGpu [SRC :5235]. Device buffers on UMA request DEVICE_LOCAL and fall back to HOST_VISIBLE|COHERENT [SRC :2826-2829].
    • On UMA, pinned host buffers can be used by the GPU in place (ggml_vk_host_get) [SRC :6690].
    • Host-pointer import (ggml_vk_buffer_from_host_ptr) works only with VK_EXT_external_memory_host, with alignment checks [SRC :16362-16381]. It is disabled on MoltenVK [SRC :5188-5190]. Mali-G68 (and G710 per #30920) lack it, so mmap'd weights are copied into separate Vulkan buffers.
    • **Free-memory reporting without VK_EXT_memory_budget returns free = heap.size** [SRC :15577-15607]. On the SipLLM phone the heap is 7516 MB, so the backend believes ~7.5 GB is free on a ~6 GB-RAM phone with ~3 GB MemAvailable [SIPLLM probe, llamabench pre-lines]. [INFERENCE]
    • Allocations are sub-allocated in 1 GB blocks by default (GGML_VK_SUBALLOCATION_BLOCK_SIZE) [SRC :5223-5231].
  • Memory regime: none. No ceiling, and heap-based fit on UMA.
  • Hardware and numbers from the issues (verbatim):
    • #30920: Dimensity 9000 / Mali-G710 MC10, driver r32p1, api 1.1.177, no external_memory_host.
      • SIGSEGV pc=0 in ggml_vk_create_buffer ← ggml_vk_host_malloc at model load. "It also crashes with offload completely disabled: -ngl 0 --no-host 1."
      • After applying #28593 it crashes in QueueSubmit.
      • CPU on the same phone: pp256 ~31, tg64 ~7.1 tok/s (Qwen2.5-3B Q4_K_M).
    • #28593: on pre-1.2 drivers that populate 1.2 feature structs, the backend uses buffer-device-address and timeline semaphores without requesting the extensions, so it calls null function pointers. Reported on S21 FE / Mali-G78. Closed without merge.
    • #28319: the same class on Exynos 2100 / Mali-G78 MP14, driver r38p0, Vulkan 1.1.213. It also notes "a handful of pre-existing MUL_MAT correctness failures" on that device.
    • #21357: Galaxy Watch 4 (Mali-G68, armeabi-v7a): "loading a 270MB model caused 524MB peak RAM", caused by double allocation of CPU tensors (APK asset mmap plus a loader copy). A host_ptr into the mmap brought it to 142 MB, and "GPU tensors are unaffected". Closed, not merged. It is a C1-style double-loading lesson, not GPU zero-copy.
    • #21359: on 32-bit, a wrong quantized stride exceeded max_buffer_size (1205 MB on Mali G68), so every MUL_MAT silently fell back to CPU while reporting 33/33 layers offloaded.
    • #22682: RK3588 (Mali-G610, Linux): CPU prompt 35.9 / gen 25.7 tok/s vs Vulkan 3.3 / 5.7 (Qwen3-0.6B Q4_0). SDM845 "Ran out of memory" with a tiny model.
    • #15800: Mali-G715 Vulkan, embedded mul_mat variant: Qwen2-1.5B Q4_0 pp512 2.81 → 66.26 tok/s; f16 MUL_MAT 8.92 → 150.01 GFLOPS.
    • #30006: Dimensity 9400 / Mali-G925, Vulkan 1.3. Coopmat makes prefill 2.4× slower (pp512 53.3 → 125.8 with coopmat disabled). GPU decode 36.4 tok/s vs a CPU baseline of 2.95, which is anomalously low; treat that comparison with suspicion.
    • #28637: small-matmul warptile geometry is wrong at subgroup size 16, giving non-deterministic wrong results on Mali-G1-Ultra.
    • #23359: Pixel 10a / Mali-G715. Vulkan fences never signal when the app is backgrounded or the screen is off on long prefills, ending in SIGABRT.
    • #9464: Android Mali Vulkan 24-25 s vs CPU 1.5-1.7 s for the same request (2024).
  • Pre-empts: C6 NONE (no ceiling). It falsifies "Vulkan offload is unique".
  • Lessons (see §6):
    • Target the Vulkan 1.1 core path without BDA or timeline semaphores.
    • Never trust heap size.
    • Correctness self-test against CPU.
    • Fence timeouts.
    • Zero-copy loading matters more than kernels on low-RAM devices.
  • Contribution opportunity: [HYPOTHESIS] SipLLM's Vulkan path runs correctly on api-1.1 Mali drivers (G68, G710, G78), where llama.cpp master crashes. Test: run SipLLM's GPU path and llama.cpp master on the Dimensity 900 phone; record exit codes and golden-matrix agreement.
3.13 ExecuTorch Vulkan backend on Mali (Arm learning path)
  • Cite: Arm, "ExecuTorch Vulkan learning path: deploy, run and measure", https://learn.arm.com/learning-paths/mobile-graphics-and-gaming/executorch-vulkan-learning-path/how-to-5-deploy-run-and-measure/ (curl snapshot web/executorch-vulkan.txt). Read: FULL page.
  • Numbers (vendor doc):
    • Vivo X300 Pro (Mali, model unnamed). Llama-3.2-1B-Instruct 8da4w_g64.
    • Prefill 44.586 tok/s, measured on a 7-token prompt, so not meaningful as a prefill figure.
    • Decode 29.787 tok/s.
    • "RSS after model load, prefill, and generation: about 2404.8 MiB" for a 1B model.
  • Pre-empts: C6 NONE. It is a second shipping Vulkan LLM stack.
  • Lesson: [INFERENCE] GPU stacks on Android carry multi-GB RSS for 1B models. A bounded-RSS GPU path would be a visible differentiator, if it is also fast.
3.14 Platform documents (memory accounting, API availability)
  • Android DMABUF and GPU memory accounting (Android 12), source.android.com, archived 2023-12-10 (web/android-dmabuf-wb.txt):
    • GPU memory comes from "Direct allocations made by the GPU driver using physical page allocator" and "DMA-BUFs mapped into GPU address space".
    • "The private GPU memory is included in totalPss in most Memtrack HAL implementations."
    • [INFERENCE] This matches the probe's "GL mtrack" rising by 263 MB in dumpsys and TOTAL PSS rising with it. VmRSS also rose (via RssFile), which is driver-specific (Mali kbase maps buffer memory into the process). C6 accounting on Android must use VmHWM, which captured it here.
  • **VK_EXT_memory_budget** (Khronos refpage): heapBudget is "a guideline for how much total memory from each heap the current process can use", and heapUsage is per process. Values are implementation-dependent estimates. Not exposed by the Mali-G68 driver.
  • **VK_EXT_external_memory_host** (Khronos refpage): import host allocations; pointer and size must be multiples of minImportedHostPointerAlignment; the host memory must outlive the import. Not exposed by the Mali-G68 driver. [INFERENCE] It is not needed on Mali, because all buffer-capable memory types are host-visible, so SipLLM can allocate Vulkan memory and pread into the mapping.
  • Linux DRM client usage stats (docs.kernel.org/gpu/drm-usage-stats.html): per-client drm-total-<region> and drm-resident-<region> in fdinfo. Resident means "backing store present". The page says nothing about RSS. [HYPOTHESIS] On amdgpu, i915 and xe iGPUs, GPU buffer objects that are not CPU-mapped do not appear in VmRSS, so /usr/bin/time under-reports, and a C6 ceiling on x86 needs RSS + Σ drm-resident. No x86 run exists to check this.
  • Android Vulkan requirements (source.android.com implement-vulkan, curl snapshot):
    • "Devices that launch with Android 16 and higher must support Vulkan 1.4. Devices that launch with Android 13 and higher must support Vulkan 1.3. Devices that launch through Android 10 must support Vulkan 1.1."
    • "For Android 14 and higher, all Vulkan-capable devices must have Vulkan 1.1 drivers."
    • The loader is /system/lib[64]/libvulkan.so.
    • [INFERENCE] The requirement applies only to devices "provided they support a 64-bit ABI and are not low memory". Many e-waste-class phones (2-3 GB "low memory" devices, 32-bit armeabi-v7a builds such as #21357's watch) may therefore ship no Vulkan, or only Vulkan 1.0, with no compute guarantee.
    • Mid-range 64-bit phones from the Android 10-12 era (such as SipLLM's Dimensity 900 on api 1.1.177) sit on the 1.1 floor, the tier where llama.cpp crashes.
    • The GPU path must therefore be optional, and the CPU path remains the e-waste story.
  • Arm Mali/Immortalis GPU reference sheet (2024) (web/armsheet-1.png, rendered and read visually):
    • "8-bit integer dot product" is ticked for Mali-G77 and G78 (and later Valhall).
    • Note: "Mali-G6 series has the same specifications as Mali-G7". FP16 operations/cycle = 128 for G77/G78.
    • [INFERENCE] Mali-G68 has int8 dot-product hardware. The "int dot: 0" on SipLLM's phone is the Vulkan 1.1.177 driver not exposing VK_KHR_shader_integer_dot_product, not the silicon.
    • [HYPOTHESIS] The phone's OpenCL driver, if present, exposes cl_arm_integer_dot_product_int8. Running clinfo (or a 20-line probe) on the device settles it.
3.15 Community iGPU numbers (desktop/laptop UMA)
  • llama.cpp Vulkan scoreboard, discussion #10879. Body read verbatim (web/d10879-body.txt). Llama-2-7B Q4_0, no FA, pp512 / tg128 tok/s:
    • AMD Ryzen 7000 Series 281.6 / 19.9 and 8000 Series 267.3 / 19.8 (Radeon 780M-class iGPUs [INFERENCE from the series name]).
    • Ryzen AI 300 479.1 / 22.4; Ryzen AI Max+ 300 1487.1 / 51.8.
    • Intel Core Ultra 200 865.0 / 24.4.
    • Apple M3 via MoltenVK 263.7 / 26.4.
    • Intel N150 28.8 / 2.9.
    • MediaTek Dimensity 9400 38.36 ± 15.15 / 8.92.
    • No same-machine CPU baselines.
  • Framework 13 / Radeon 780M (forum post, 2024-06-13, verbatim JSON web/fw-780m.json). Llama-2-7B Q8_0, llama.cpp 172c825:
    • ROCm iGPU pp512 259.26 / tg128 10.69.
    • CPU (8 threads) 44.67 / 7.72. The author notes this CPU run had a rogue process.
    • That is GPU ≈5.8× prefill, ≈1.4× decode on the same laptop.
  • [INFERENCE] On x86 APUs with RDNA2/3 iGPUs, a GPU clearly beats the CPU on prefill, and modestly on decode. That is the opposite of the Mali-G68 picture. x86 is unmeasured for SipLLM (brief).
4. Claim verdicts (cluster scope: C6, plus the "Vulkan is unique" premise)
C6: GPU inside the ceiling

Mechanism half: NARROWED.

  • Closest prior art:
    • (1) SwapNet (2401.16757): an operator-assigned memory budget per DNN, blocks streamed from storage with O_DIRECT into a single unified-memory copy the GPU uses in place, measured to stay within budget [p.4-5, p.8-10].
    • (2) LLM in a flash (2312.11514): an LLM weight cache in MTLStorageModeShared memory read directly by the Metal GPU, refilled from flash, at a fixed ~50% DRAM [p.7, p.15].
    • (3) llama.cpp PR #21359: lets the UMA Vulkan_Host buffer type hold weights on a Mali-G68 watch (Vulkan_Host 0 → 47 MiB, all 33 layers on GPU). Closed without merging.
    • Its sibling #21357 (524 → 142 MB peak) is about CPU tensors pointing into the APK asset mmap. Its own text says "GPU tensors are unaffected", so it is not GPU zero-copy evidence.
  • What differs:
    • SwapNet is CNNs on CUDA/Jetson, streams once per inference, partitions to fit and never refuses, and has no KV or LLM terms.
    • LLM in a flash has no operator ceiling and no Android/Vulkan.
    • The llama.cpp PR moves weights into UMA host buffers but enforces nothing.
  • Surviving form: "On a commodity Android phone with a Vulkan-1.1 Mali GPU (no external_memory_host, no memory_budget), an LLM runtime prices every Vulkan allocation in an itemized peak-RSS ledger. Per-token layer streaming lands directly in host-visible Vulkan memory that the GPU reads without a copy. The runtime refuses infeasible configs, and measured VmHWM stays within the operator ceiling with the GPU active."
  • Searched: the queries in §2, plus SwapNet/STI/FlexGen lineage, ML Drift, MNN-LLM, Transformer-Lite, HeteroInfer, PowerInfer-2 and the llama.cpp issue trail. None enforce a ceiling that includes GPU buffers on Android.

Headline verdict for C6: NARROWED. The narrowed form above drops the clause "so even tiny GPUs help without raising peak RAM". It is a design, not a result. vulkan_matmul() never dispatches and src/mem_plan.cpp has no GPU lines.

On the dropped benefit clause. Current evidence is negative for Mali-G68-class GPUs. Benefit is unproven for co-execution, the OpenCL int8 route and streamed prefill.

  • Against:
    • The SipLLM spike gives GPU ≤ 17.7 vs CPU 47 tok/s prefill [SIPLLM].
    • Mali G720 and G78 are slower than the CPU on both phases [2410.03613 p.7].
    • RK3588 Vulkan prompt 3.3 vs CPU 35.9 [#22682].
    • Android Mali Vulkan was 15× slower than CPU [#9464].
    • llama.cpp GPU on S23 was at parity with CPU [MELTing p.7].
    • GPU GEMV is slower than CPU on 8 Gen 3 [PowerInfer-2 p.4].
    • Vulkan crashes on 1.1 Mali drivers [#30920].
  • For, on other GPUs:
    • Tuned OpenCL on Mali-G715 reaches 796 tok/s Gemma-2B prefill [ML Drift p.6], but with int8 instructions (§3.4).
    • Vulkan PR #15800 on G715: pp512 2.81 → 66 (150 GFLOPS fp16 on a GPU with ~4× G68's per-core fp16 rate).
    • Adreno 730 GPU beats CPU on both phases and uses 60-88% less energy [2410.03613 p.13-14].
    • x86 780M: GPU 5.8× the CPU prefill [forum].
  • Required to claim: a measured GPU-on vs GPU-off prefill gain on the Dimensity 900 at an equal VmHWM, via co-execution, OpenCL int8, or streamed prefill.
  • [INFERENCE] Break-even:
    • TinyLlama is ≈1.9 GFLOP/token of matmul (11.3 GFLOP per 128 tokens per layer × 22 layers / 128).
    • Pure offload must sustain > 47 × 1.9 ≈ 90 GFLOPS to beat the CPU. That is 2.6× the spike, ~41% of the inferred fp32 peak, or ~21% of the inferred packed-fp16 peak (§1.3). No fp16 Vulkan Mali kernel in the literature or issue trail is known to reach that efficiency.
    • CPU+GPU co-execution (§6.1) beats CPU alone with any non-zero GPU throughput, provided sync overhead is below the GPU's share.
Premise: "Vulkan GPU offload is unique to SipLLM": DEAD
  • llama.cpp ggml-vulkan (shipping, uma path, Android builds; §3.12) and ExecuTorch's Vulkan backend (Arm-documented on Mali; §3.13) both ship. MNN states Vulkan support [MNN-LLM p.2].
  • What is uncommon:
    • (a) a Vulkan path that works on Vulkan-1.1 Mali drivers (llama.cpp master crashes there as of 2026-10-21);
    • (b) a Vulkan path whose memory is inside an enforced RSS ceiling.
  • Neither is a contribution until measured.
Cross-cluster notes for the coordinator
  • C1: SwapNet (2401.16757) enforces a scheduler or operator memory budget on Jetson. It partitions to fit, never refuses, and reports per-model memory, not process peak RSS. It is PARTIAL prior art for C1.
  • C7: SwapNet's "write-back-free swap-out" [p.4] states the read-only streaming argument for DNN blocks. That is PARTIAL prior art for the flash-friendly framing.

[INFERENCE] Pattern:

  • Adreno and AMD/Intel iGPUs beat the CPU on prefill with mainstream stacks.
  • Mali loses with every generic stack measured (MLC OpenCL, llama.cpp Vulkan/OpenCL), and wins only with vendor-grade tuned kernels (ML Drift) or Mali-specific tiling (#15800, newer G925 drivers).
  • Decode rarely gains on UMA, because CPU and GPU share DRAM bandwidth.
6. Lessons for a SipLLM Vulkan design
6.1 Which operations to offload first
  1. Prefill GEMM first, decode GEMV later or never. [PAPER]
    • Prefill is compute-bound, and the GPU's advantage is FLOPs (ML Drift p.6; Transformer-Lite p.11; 2410.03613 p.7 "prefill with the GPU and decoding with the CPU").
    • Decode is DRAM-bound, and on UMA the GPU reads the same DRAM. A single processor saturates 40-45 GB/s (HeteroInfer p.2; PowerInfer-2 p.4).
  2. Co-execute, don't just offload. [PAPER + HYPOTHESIS]
    • Split each prefill GEMM's output columns between CPU (SDOT --fast) and GPU, in proportion to measured throughput. Synchronize once per layer, not per matmul (HeteroInfer p.8: about 400 µs clFinish; flag polling).
    • [HYPOTHESIS] Even the untuned v1 kernel (17.7) plus the CPU (47) gives up to ≈ +30% TinyLlama prefill on Dimensity 900.
    • [INFERENCE] Sync cost is small at this scale. At 47 tok/s a 128-token prefill takes ≈2.7 s, about 124 ms per layer. One 0.4 ms sync per layer is ≈0.3%, and even one per matmul (7 per layer) is ≈2%.
    • The real risks are the shared phone power and thermal budget (CPU and GPU throttle each other) and DRAM contention.
    • Experiment: pp128 CPU-only vs split, with fixed governors, VmHWM unchanged, and SoC temperature logged.
  3. Not for streamed decode; yes for streamed prefill. [INFERENCE]
    • In decode at --ram-budget 0, per-token time is dominated by reading every layer from UFS, so GPU compute cannot shorten it.
    • Streamed prefill is compute-bound. Each layer is read once and used for all B tokens. A TinyLlama Q4_K_M layer is ≈26 MB ((669 MB minus embedding and LM head) / 22), which takes tens of ms at UFS speeds. CPU compute for that layer at B=128 is ≈124 ms (§6.1.2).
    • So streamed prefill is where "weights streamed to a small GPU" can pay off, and it is the real reason to make the ring Vulkan memory (§6.3.2). [HYPOTHESIS]
    • The planner should enable the GPU for prefill on both pinned and streamed layers, and keep decode on the CPU. GPU efficiency rises steeply with batch: spike1 v1-f16 goes from 7.0 to 13.8 to 15.2 tok/s at B = 16, 64, 128. The spike's cpu_fast column is not a valid comparator (§1.3), so the crossover batch is unknown.
  4. Keep embeddings, sampling, attention softmax and shape logic on the CPU. [PAPER] Transformer-Lite runs shape operators on the CPU to keep one CPU-GPU boundary (p.4). [INFERENCE] SipLLM's windowed embeddings and LM head are already CPU-side.
6.2 Kernel strategy for Mali-G68-class GPUs (Vulkan 1.1, subgroup 16, 32 KB shared, no int-dot exposed, no coopmat)
  1. fp16 FMA is the Vulkan path today. [SIPLLM + INFERENCE] The driver exposes shaderFloat16 and 8/16-bit storage but not integer dot product. The hardware has int8 dot (Arm sheet; Valhall does 64 int8 operations per warp unit per clock vs 32 fp16 [Arm Valhall doc p.11]). An OpenCL int8 path is therefore a [HYPOTHESIS] to check with clinfo. It would trade the "Vulkan only" rule and OpenCL's patchy availability (TFLite p.7) against roughly 2× fp16, and ≈4× the measured-fp16 arithmetic density.
  2. Fix access patterns before FLOPs. [PAPER]
    • Mali fails generic kernels on partial-width loads and load-store saturation (2410.03613 p.12).
    • Use 128-bit loads (uvec4/f16vec4) per invocation, warp-contiguous: lane *i* reads bytes base + 16·i of the same weight row, so one warp load covers 256 contiguous bytes [ARM-VH p.11-12]. Do not give each lane its own row; that is v2's failing pattern (§9.3c).
    • Re-lay Q4_K into a GPU block layout at pin time, as ML Drift (+20%, p.3), MNN-LLM (p.5) and Qualcomm's MLC (6×, 2410.03613 p.12) do. [INFERENCE] SipLLM's IL4 repacking machinery (--fast) is the natural place to add a "VK4" layout.
  3. Cheap dequantization. [PAPER] Transformer-Lite's bit-trick FP4 dequantization was 1.33-1.56× faster on Mali GEMV and 1.0× on Adreno (p.13). [HYPOTHESIS] An equivalent bit-trick Q4_K→fp16 (nibble OR'd into an fp16 exponent constant, with folded scale and min) gives similar Mali gains.
  4. Subgroup 16 everywhere. [ISSUE #28637, #27163] llama.cpp's tiles assumed 32-wide warps and produced wrong results at subgroup 16. Derive tile geometry from the queried subgroup size and assert that the warps tile the block. Mali tolerates workgroup-size choice well (TFLite p.4: <5%), so don't build an autotuner first.
  5. Shared memory ≤ 32 KB. [ISSUE #30006] Coopmat variants that exceed it silently fall back to slow paths. Size tiles for 32 KB and check pipeline creation results.
  6. Precision vs the golden matrix. [SIPLLM] v1's ~4e-4 per-matmul error vs v2's 1e-6 cost 4-5× in speed. The golden gate is a top-10 logit comparison against llama.cpp at --tol 0.05 (golden/validate_matrix.py:97). [HYPOTHESIS] fp16 storage with fp32 accumulation passes that gate. Measure before committing to fp16 activations.
  7. Measure a real peak first. [SIPLLM] peak and peak2 were broken. peak3 used packed f16vec4 but did not reach Arm's documented 2× fp16 rate (§9.3a). Read the GPU clock and run the Mali Offline Compiler before quoting any "% of peak".
  8. Registers ≤ 32, lanes adjacent along K, shared memory only for shared computation. [ARM-VH p.10-12; ARM-BP p.91-97; tags defined in §9]
    • Full occupancy needs ≤32 registers, and spills start above 64.
    • A warp should cover contiguous bytes with vector loads.
    • Shared memory is cache-backed, so use it to share dequantized tiles, not raw copies.
    • [INFERENCE] SipLLM's v2 likely failed on register spills plus divergent scalar loads (§9.3c).
6.3 Keeping GPU memory inside the hard ceiling (C6 design)
  1. **Price GPU allocations as ledger lines in src/mem_plan.cpp.** [SIPLLM + INFERENCE] The lines are: vk_ring (if the ring is Vulkan memory), vk_pinned_weights (if pinned layers are re-laid into Vulkan buffers), vk_scratch (activations, partials), vk_driver_base (instance, device, pipelines, descriptor pools), and vk_staging (should be 0).
    • On this phone they all land in VmRSS (probe §1.2). [INFERENCE] The probe baseline (24.7 MB VmRSS, 18.3 MB RssFile with the driver loaded) is an upper bound for vk_driver_base to re-measure inside SipLLM.
  2. The ring is GPU memory, filled by pread. [INFERENCE from probe + SwapNet + LLM-in-a-flash]
    • Storage buffers can only use memory types 0 and 1, both host-visible. Allocate the 1-2 layer ring as Vulkan memory, map it persistently, and pread directly into the mapping. Same bytes, no staging copy, no double counting (cf. SwapNet p.4-5, where page cache plus CPU copy plus GPU copy tripled peak memory).
    • Type 1 (HOST_CACHED, non-coherent) is faster for CPU-side fallback reads but needs vkFlushMappedMemoryRanges after pread. Type 0 is coherent but likely uncached for CPU reads.
    • [HYPOTHESIS] O_DIRECT pread into a kbase mapping may fail (pages not pinnable through get_user_pages). SipLLM uses buffered pread plus POSIX_FADV_WILLNEED (include/llm/file_backing.h:70), which avoids the question.
  3. Allocate once, never free inside the run. [SIPLLM probe + PAPER] VmRSS was still elevated right after vkFreeMemory and back to baseline by the +2 s sample (so the lag is ≤2 s), and VmHWM keeps the peak. Free-then-allocate under a VmHWM ceiling double-counts. Allocate the planner-chosen sizes at startup and reuse them (HeteroInfer's "few buffer slots ... not reclaimed by the driver", p.8).
  4. Never trust Vulkan's heap for budgeting. [SRC + SIPLLM] Without VK_EXT_memory_budget the heap (7516 MB) is not free memory. llama.cpp's fallback reports free = heap.size (ggml-vulkan.cpp:15577-15607). SipLLM's planner should keep using the operator ceiling and /proc MemAvailable.
  5. 256 MB binding limit. [SIPLLM probe] maxStorageBufferRange = 256 MB. Per-layer TinyLlama and 8B Q4 matrices fit. A Llama-3-8B LM head (≈128256×4096 at Q6_K ≈ 430 MB [INFERENCE]) must stay windowed, as SipLLM already streams it, or be bound in ≤256 MB slices. Also bounded by maxMemoryAllocationSize (llama.cpp #21359 reports max_buffer_size 1205 MB on a G68).
  6. Verify with VmHWM, and on x86 also with fdinfo. [INFERENCE + HYPOTHESIS] On Android/Mali the GPU memory is in VmRSS, so the existing /usr/bin/time and VmHWM proof covers it. On Linux iGPUs it may not. Add drm-resident-* from /proc/self/fdinfo to the ledger check before claiming C6 on x86.
6.4 Failure modes others hit, and how SipLLM should guard
  1. Crashes on Vulkan-1.1 Mali drivers (#30920, #28593, #28319). These are null function pointers from using 1.2-promoted features (BDA, timeline semaphores) without requesting the extensions, then a QueueSubmit crash.
    • [INFERENCE] Use the Vulkan 1.1 core API, explicitly request every extension the kernels use, and call no entry point that was promoted to core in 1.2. The extensions are VK_KHR_shader_float16_int8, VK_KHR_16bit_storage/8bit_storage and VK_EXT_subgroup_size_control. That means binary fences instead of timeline semaphores, and no buffer-device-address. Resolve device functions through vkGetDeviceProcAddr. This is the #28593 lesson, and pure 1.1 core alone cannot run fp16/int8-storage kernels.
    • [INFERENCE] Initialize the device-level dispatch with vkGetDeviceProcAddr.
    • [INFERENCE] Zero-initialize feature structs (#28319 found indeterminate reads).
    • [INFERENCE] This is the Android 10-12-era 64-bit mid-range tier (§3.14). Lower-end and 32-bit devices may have no usable Vulkan at all.
  2. Silent CPU fallback while reporting GPU offload (#21359). [INFERENCE] Always report the bytes and operations actually dispatched to the GPU; vulkan_matmul() already returns a bool.
  3. Wrong results on Mali (#28637; #28319 "MUL_MAT correctness failures"; titles #23241 and #26921).
    • [INFERENCE] Add a first-use self-test: run each pipeline on a fixed shape against the CPU kernel, and fall back if it disagrees.
    • [INFERENCE] Keep GPU off by default (charter Rule 3: --ram-budget 0 and defaults stay byte-identical).
  4. Fence hangs when backgrounded or screen-off (#23359). [INFERENCE] Use vkWaitForFences with timeouts, then cancel and fall back to the CPU for that token. This matters for the Flutter plugin.
  5. CPU still busy feeding the GPU (2507.02135 p.3, the Mali queue holds 2 entries). [PAPER → INFERENCE] Reserve one CPU thread when the GPU is active, and adjust the "fixed16" schedule.
  6. UI jank and thermal (Transformer-Lite p.6 used the lowest OpenCL priority). [HYPOTHESIS] Use a low queue priority (VK_EXT_global_priority if exposed) and expose --gpu-max-share.
  7. Shipping under "no runtime dependencies":
    • [INFERENCE] Replace -lvulkan (Makefile:96) with dlopen("libvulkan.so") plus vkGetInstanceProcAddr. The Android loader is a platform library at /system/lib[64]/libvulkan.so (Android implement-vulkan page), so the binary still runs where Vulkan is absent.
    • [INFERENCE] Keep build-time glslc with SPIR-V embedded as C arrays, as shaders/matmul_spv.h already does, and check the generated headers in so builds without glslc are reproducible. [INFERENCE] llama.cpp's need for glslc and SPIRV-Headers at build time (ext/llama-vulkan-build.md) is the cost SipLLM avoids this way.
    • [INFERENCE] Consider a pipeline cache file (#21357 adds GGML_VK_CACHE_DIR; cold boot 19 → 11 s and warm boot 19 → ~2.5 s on a watch, together with its CPU zero-copy change).
6.5 Energy

[PAPER] On Adreno, GPU inference cut energy per token by 60-88% vs CPU (2410.03613 p.13-14). HeteroInfer keeps the CPU as a control plane only, citing its "relatively low energy efficiency" (p.5). No Mali GPU-vs-CPU energy-per-token measurement was found. 2507.02135 gives ~400 mJ/token for TinyLlama decode on Mali-G710 via OpenCL, without a CPU number in the part read. [HYPOTHESIS] On Mali-G68, GPU prefill is more energy-efficient per token than CPU prefill even when slower. Measure with battery-bypass or sysfs current sampling (/sys/class/power_supply/battery/current_now) before claiming it. SipLLM has no energy numbers yet [SIPLLM].

7. Top lessons for SipLLM, ranked by expected impact
  1. Test CPU+GPU co-execution of prefill GEMM first. It needs no new kernel: the existing v1 spike kernel plus SDOT on the CPU, column-split per layer. It is the cheapest route to a measured GPU benefit on G68. Go/no-go: pp128 at fixed governors beats CPU-only with VmHWM unchanged. [HYPOTHESIS, grounded in HeteroInfer/PowerInfer-2 UMA evidence]
  2. **Check the OpenCL int8-dot route on the phone (clinfo).** G68 silicon has int8 dot (Arm sheet). The Vulkan 1.1.177 driver hides it, and the only Mali LLM numbers that look strong (ML Drift) rely on int8. If it is exposed, an OpenCL kernel becomes the main G68 GPU path, a trade-off against "Vulkan only" and OpenCL availability. A ≥90 GFLOPS fp16 Vulkan kernel has no precedent on G68-class Mali. [HYPOTHESIS]
  3. Make the streaming ring Vulkan memory for streamed prefill, priced in mem_plan.cpp, allocated once (freed memory lingers in RSS for up to 2 s), flushed after pread, and verified by VmHWM. This is the surviving, narrowed C6, and streamed prefill is compute-bound, so it can benefit. [INFERENCE + HYPOTHESIS]
  4. **Vulkan 1.1 API plus explicitly requested extensions, a runtime self-test, fence timeouts and dlopen, with GPU off by default.** This runs where llama.cpp crashes (pre-1.2 Mali), keeps the zero-dependency rule, and contains wrong-result drivers. [INFERENCE from issue trail]
  5. **Bring the spike and probe sources from branch issue-67-vulkan alongside the measurements.** Re-run with fixed CPU and GPU clocks, a valid CPU baseline, and a Mali Offline Compiler report for each shader (registers, stack spills, packed-fp16 use), so the GPU numbers become citable. [SIPLLM]
  6. Measure energy (prefill and decode, GPU vs CPU) on the Dimensity 900. It may be the only GPU win on Mali-G68 even if latency is not. [HYPOTHESIS]
8. Open questions
  • Does the Dimensity 900 phone ship a Mali OpenCL driver exposing cl_arm_integer_dot_product_int8? (clinfo on device.) Does any newer Mali Vulkan driver for G68 expose VK_KHR_shader_integer_dot_product?
  • Why does packed f16vec4 FMA run at the fp32 rate on Mali-G68 (peak3), when Arm documents 2×? Compiler codegen, clock, or something undocumented? (Mali Offline Compiler plus a devfreq log.)
  • Is the llama.cpp segfault on the SipLLM phone the #28593 class or the #30920 QueueSubmit class? Needs a tombstone, plus a -ngl 0 run of the Vulkan build.
  • On x86 iGPUs (amdgpu, i915, xe), do Vulkan device-local buffers show up in VmRSS, VmHWM or memcg? Needed before any C6 claim on laptops. No x86 hardware run exists (brief).
  • Does O_DIRECT pread into kbase-mapped Vulkan memory work on Mali?
  • Does fp16 activation storage pass golden/validate_matrix.py (top-10, tol 0.05) on TinyLlama, Llama-3.1-8B and Qwen?
  • Is there any published Mali GPU vs CPU energy-per-token comparison for LLMs? None found.
  • Does the target e-waste class (2-3 GB, possibly 32-bit or "low memory" Android devices) have usable Vulkan compute at all? The Android requirement exempts low-memory and non-64-bit devices (§3.14). Survey several such phones with vk_probe once its source is recovered.
  • The source for the ≤2 s deferred release of freed Vulkan memory on Mali is unknown (driver memory pool / kbase). Does it vary by driver version?
9. Why media apps and games run well on the same GPU (extra scope, 2026-10-23)

Sources opened for this section (local copies under .../E-mobile-gpu/web/):

  • [ARM-BP] Arm GPU Best Practices Developer Guide, doc 101897, issue 10 (2025-01-31), https://documentation-service.arm.com/static/67a62b17091bfc3e0a947695 (arm-bestpractices-101897.pdf). Page numbers are the guide's printed "Page N of 135".
  • [ARM-VH] Arm, *The Valhall shader core*, doc 102203 v1.0 (2020), https://documentation-service.arm.com/static/634000a34c59b30b517732fc (arm-valhall-shader-core.pdf). Printed page numbers.
  • [ARM-SHEET] Arm Mali/Immortalis GPU reference sheet (2024) (armsheet-1.png, §3.14).
  • [QCOM] Qualcomm, *Adreno GPU on Mobile: Best Practices* and *Overview*, doc 80-78185-2, https://docs.qualcomm.com/bundle/publicresource/80-78185-2/topics/mobile_best_practices.md and .../overview.md.
  • Video:
    • Intel media-driver README, https://github.com/intel/media-driver.
    • NVIDIA NVDEC application note (Video Codec SDK 13.0).
    • Apple VideoToolbox documentation (developer.apple.com JSON).
    • Android MediaCodecInfo reference.
    • Linux kernel V4L2 *Video Memory-To-Memory Interface* and *Stateful Video Decoder* docs.
    • VideoLAN wiki *VLC GPU Decoding*.
    • VLC-Android commit mail (2014-01, mailman.videolan.org/pipermail/android/2014-January/003038.html).
    • VLC Android download page.
  • SipLLM shaders: git show issue-67-vulkan:shaders/{peak_flops,q4k_gemm,q4k_gemm_v2}.comp (read-only).
9.1 Video players: shader cores or a separate decoder block?

Separate, dedicated decode hardware, not the GPU's shader cores. Intel and NVIDIA state this outright. For Apple, Android and Linux V4L2, the docs say "hardware decoder/codec" without naming the block, so for those three the "not the shader cores" part is [INFERENCE]:

  • VA-API / Intel: "Video decoding calls hardware-based decoder(VDBox) which provides fully-accelerated hardware video decoding to release the graphics engine for other operations" (media-driver README). Encoding and post-processing can mix fixed hardware with "shader (media kernel)" paths.
  • NVIDIA: "NVIDIA GPUs and NVIDIA Jetson platforms contain a hardware-based decoder (referred to as NVDEC ...). With complete decoding offloaded to NVDEC, the graphics engine and CPU are free for other operations" (NVDEC application note).
  • Apple VideoToolbox: "a low-level framework that provides direct access to hardware encoders and decoders".
  • Linux on ARM SoCs: hardware codecs are exposed as V4L2 memory-to-memory devices, separate from the GPU ("Examples of memory-to-memory devices are codecs, scalers, deinterlacers or format converters"). A stateful decoder "takes complete chunks of the bytestream ... and decodes them into raw video frames".
  • Android:
    • MediaCodecInfo.isHardwareAccelerated() is "provided by the device manufacturer".
    • isSoftwareOnly() codecs "do not provide any performance guarantees".
    • Android exposes vendor codec hardware through MediaCodec. [INFERENCE] On Arm-licensed SoCs that hardware is a separate video IP. Arm sold Mali-V61 as a "single scalable IP block" separate from the Mali-G51 GPU (press coverage only, not an Arm technical document).
  • VLC:
    • The VideoLAN wiki: "The VLC media player framework can use your graphics chip (a.k.a. GPU) to accelerate decoding ... In some cases, it can let the graphic card perform post-processing and rendering". It lists DxVA2, VA-API and VDPAU.
    • VLC-Android modes (2014 commit): "Disabled: software decoding only. Decoding Acceleration: MediaCodec or OMX decoding. Full Acceleration: MediaCodec opaque direct rendering (decoding + display acceleration)".
    • The current download page: "VLC can decode video in software and hardware mode".
    • [INFERENCE] VLC's "hardware" path on Android is the vendor's MediaCodec decoder. The GPU, if used at all, only scales, converts color and composites.
  • Implication for SipLLM [INFERENCE]: "video plays smoothly on this phone" says nothing about the GPU's shader throughput. The decoding is done by different silicon.
9.2 Why graphics fits a phone GPU, and why LLM work doesn't

Graphics is engineered to avoid DRAM traffic and to run in fixed-function units:

  • Tile memory.
    • "At the start of the pass, the tile memory is initialized inside the GPU. At the end of the pass, the required outputs are written back to system memory. The intermediate framebuffer working state lives entirely inside the tile memory" [ARM-BP p.57].
    • Qualcomm: Adreno "divides a frame into bins and renders them one at a time. During rendering it uses on-chip high performance Graphics Memory (GMEM) to avoid the cost of going to system memory" [QCOM overview].
    • Depth testing, blending, overdraw and MSAA samples stay on chip ("For most multisampling, all data for the additional samples are kept in the tile memory" [ARM-BP p.59]).
  • Compression.
    • Textures: "ideally Adaptive Scaleable Texture Compression (ASTC)" [ARM-BP p.76].
    • Framebuffers: Arm FrameBuffer Compression, "a lossless image compression format ... for compressing framebuffer outputs" [ARM-BP p.82].
    • Qualcomm UBWC "increases memory bus throughput and reduces battery power" [QCOM].
    • "Memory bandwidth requires a lot of power. Therefore, memory bandwidth is more restricted in mobile devices" [ARM-BP p.15].
  • Fixed-function units and reuse.
    • "A Valhall core can perform 32 FP32 FMAs, read four bilinear filtered texture samples, blend two fragments, and write two pixels per clock" [ARM-VH p.10]. Texture filtering, blending and pixel writes are dedicated units outside the FMA pipes.
    • "The baseline performance is four bilinear filtered texels per clock. This means that a 2x2 pixel quad is textured in one cycle" [ARM-VH p.13].
    • "Using 16-bit precision is normally sufficient for computer graphics" [ARM-BP p.94].
  • [INFERENCE] Graphics has high reuse per DRAM byte. Each texel and vertex is reused by many pixels, intermediate state never leaves the chip, and what does leave is compressed. A 1080p60 frame writes roughly 2 MP × 4 B × 60 ≈ 0.5 GB/s of final color, before AFBC.

LLM decode is the opposite case [INFERENCE, from SipLLM numbers]:

  • At batch 1, every weight is read once per token and used for 2 FLOPs. Q4_K is 4.5 bits/weight (144 B per 256 weights), so intensity ≈ 2 / 0.5625 ≈ 3.6 FLOP/byte.
  • TinyLlama decode at 14.7 tok/s on the CPU [SIPLLM] already streams ≈ 9 GB/s of weights (~0.6 GB/token), while needing only ≈ 30 GFLOPS (~2 GFLOP/token), about 13% of G68's fp32 peak.
  • On UMA, the GPU reads the same DRAM. There is no tile memory, texture compression or fixed-function unit that applies to weight streaming. Decode on the GPU can at best match the CPU's bandwidth-bound rate. This is what 2410.03613 measured: Mali-G720 decode load-store utilization above 90% [p.12].

LLM prefill (GEMM) has intensity ≈ 3.6·B FLOP/byte (≈ 460 at B = 128), so it is compute-bound. It is limited by on-chip reuse, and that is where Mali is weakest for compute:

  • 16 KB L1 per core [ARM-VH p.12].
  • No dedicated shared memory (§9.3b).
  • Full occupancy only up to 32 registers [ARM-VH p.10].
9.3 Mali Valhall (Mali-G68 MC4) specifics

(a) Is packed fp16 2× the fp32 rate? Per Arm, yes.

  • "32 FP32 FMAs ... per clock" per core [ARM-VH p.10]. A 16-wide warp unit performs "32x fp16/int16 operations per clock cycle, or 64x int8 operations per clock cycle" [ARM-VH p.11].
  • "If using explicit FP16, make sure to vectorize the code by using f16vec2 or f16vec4 ... Scalar float16_t does not gain the same benefit" [ARM-BP p.95]. "highp can use up to twice the resources and halve the speed" [ARM-BP p.96].
  • [ARM-SHEET] lists 128 FP16 ops/cycle per core for G77/G78, which [INFERENCE] equals 64 fp16 FMA, 2× the 32 fp32 FMA.
  • SipLLM's measurement contradicts the documented 2×. peak_flops.comp used f16vec4 FMA chains (8 independent chains per thread), and peak3 measured f16 ≈ 200 vs f32 ≈ 223 GFLOPS.
  • The host code counts both runs identically: flops = groups·64·iters·8·4·2 with the same dispatch (git show issue-67-vulkan:tools/vk_prefill_spike.cpp, lines 288-296). So the shortfall is not a counting artifact.
  • [INFERENCE] 223 GFLOPS fp32 = 256·f implies f ≈ 0.87 GHz, so packed fp16 "should" give ≈ 445 GFLOPS.
  • [HYPOTHESIS] Possible causes: the driver compiler emitted unpacked fp16, a clock drop between runs, or an undocumented limit. Decide with the Mali Offline Compiler report for peak_flops.comp -DF16 and a devfreq log. Until resolved, do not budget a 2× fp16 gain in any G68 design.

(b) Is shared memory dedicated on-chip SRAM? No.

  • "Arm GPUs do not implement dedicated on-chip shared memory for compute shaders. The shared memory that is available to use is system RAM that is backed up by the load-store cache" [ARM-BP p.92].
  • Arm advises: "Use shared memory to share significant computation between threads in a workgroup", keep it small, and "Do not copy data from global memory to shared memory on Arm GPUs. Doing so pollutes the caches" [ARM-BP p.92-93].
  • Barriers and shared memory force the whole workgroup to be resident, which squeezes registers. "Do not use more than 64 threads per workgroup". Spilling to stack raises load/store use and external bandwidth [ARM-BP p.91-92].
  • [INFERENCE] SipLLM's v1 kernel uses shared memory in the way Arm endorses. It stores *dequantized* fp16 weights, which is shared computation, not a raw copy. That fits v1 beating v2. The v2 header's premise ("on Mali that memory is ordinary cached memory and the barriers cost more than they save") is half right: the memory is cache-backed, but sharing the dequantization paid off.

(c) Is non-adjacent per-lane access the likely cause of v2's ~7 GFLOPS? It is one cause, probably not the main one.

  • v2's access pattern (from source): each lane owns 4 weight rows (rows[i] = n0 + tid + 64·i) and issues scalar 4-byte W[] loads. Adjacent lanes hit rows row_words·4 bytes apart: 1152 B apart at K=2048, so 16 different 64-byte lines per warp load.
  • Arm's rules on this:
    • The load/store unit returns "a single 64-byte cache line per clock ... data can be returned in a single cycle if all threads access data inside the same cache line". Use "vector loads and stores in each thread" and "Access sequential address ranges across the threads in a warp" [ARM-VH p.11-12].
    • "Do not use scalar loads if vector loads are possible. Do not access divergent addresses across a thread quad" [ARM-BP p.97].
    • v2 breaks both rules. The 2410.03613 Mali profile found the same symptom (">80% of Load-Store units reads are partial-width") [p.12].
  • But v1 has the same per-lane-row scalar global loads (rown = n0 + tid). The difference is that v1 amortizes them over 16 tokens via shared memory, while v2 re-reads per 8 tokens inside the FMA loop.
  • [INFERENCE, counting from source] The more likely dominant cause is register pressure.
    • v2 keeps per thread acc[4][8] fp32 (32 registers), wv[4] vec4 (16), lo_ab/hi_ab (16), dm (8), sc uvec3×4 (12), q (4), plus row and token indices. That is ≈ 90-110 live 32-bit values.
    • Valhall allows "up to 32x32-bit registers while maintaining full thread occupancy ... up to 64 at the expense of reduced thread count" [ARM-VH p.10]. More than 64 means stack spills, which Arm says increase load/store and DRAM traffic [ARM-BP p.92].
    • Spilling can cost far more than coalescing. A register-spilling tile dropped a convolution from 2.57 TFLOPS to "as little as 50 gigaflops" on an AMD R9 Nano (Lawson et al., arXiv:1904.05347 p.7; desktop GPU, as an illustration only).
  • [HYPOTHESIS] Running malioc on q4k_gemm_v2.comp will report >64 work registers with stack spilling. A v3 with ≤32 registers that is lane-adjacent along K would beat v1. Design for v3:
    • Lanes read consecutive 16-byte chunks of the same row via uvec4, so the warp covers 256 contiguous bytes.
    • Combine partial sums with subgroup arithmetic ("use subgroup operations instead of shared memory" for reductions [ARM-BP]).
    • Or re-lay Q4_K at load so that lane *i* of a row group reads address base + 16·i.

(d) Published GEMM/GEMV % of peak on Mali-G68/G78-class GPUs. No paper reports a GEMM/GEMV %-of-peak figure for G68 or G78 specifically. What exists:

SourceGPUWorkloadEfficiency
SipLLM spike v1 [SIPLLM]G68 MC4Q4_K GEMM, B=12834.7 GFLOPS ≈ 16% fp32 / 8% packed-fp16 of documented peak
SipLLM spike v2 [SIPLLM]G68 MC4Q4_K GEMM, register-blocked≈ 7 GFLOPS ≈ 3%

9.4 Do NPUs, matrix units and int8 dot exist because graphics GPUs are weak at low-precision matrix math?
  • [INFERENCE] Partly. The gap is int8/int4 matrix throughput per watt and per byte moved, not fp16.
    • Graphics already runs well at fp16 ("Using 16-bit precision is normally sufficient for computer graphics" [ARM-BP p.94]).
    • Graphics has no use for an int8 dot or a matrix-multiply-accumulate. ML does.
  • What vendors added:
    • int8 dot inside the GPU: Valhall's warp unit does "64x int8 operations per clock" versus 32 fp16 and 16 fp32 FMA lanes [ARM-VH p.11]. [ARM-SHEET] ticks "8-bit integer dot product" for Mali-G76 and all Valhall and 5th-gen parts.
    • Cooperative-matrix support on newer Mali (G715, G925; see table).
    • Separate NPUs built around systolic arrays with weight-stationary reuse [HeteroInfer p.4]. On Snapdragon 8 Gen 3 the GPU has 2.8 TFLOPS FP16 versus 34 TOPS INT8 on the NPU; on Dimensity 9300, Mali-G720 4.0 TFLOPS versus APU 48 TOPS [HeteroInfer p.3 Table 2].
    • Transformer-Lite: NPU performance is "vastly superior" but NPUs are "constrained by similar memory bandwidths with the GPUs, thereby delivering comparable peak decoding speeds" [Transformer-Lite p.15].
  • So these units help prefill. They do not help bandwidth-bound decode.
  • Which phone GPUs expose int8 dot or matrix ops in Vulkan (verbatim ggml_vulkan device lines and probe output; driver-dependent):
GPUDriver / Vulkanint dotMatrix (coopmat)Source
Mali-G68 MC4api 1.1.177no (hardware has it, [ARM-SHEET])no[SIPLLM] probe
Mali-G710 MC10r32p1, 1.1.177nonone#30920
Mali-G715n/anoKHR_coopmat#15800
Mali-G720 Immortalis MC12r44p1, 1.3.247yesunavailable#27163
Mali-G925 Immortalis MC1249.1.0, 1.3.278yesKHR_coopmat (makes prefill 2.4× slower)#30006

  • Caveats [SRC ggml-vulkan.cpp]:
    • ggml prints "int dot: 1" only if VK_KHR_shader_integer_dot_product is present and integerDotProduct4x8BitPackedSignedAccelerated and the feature is enabled.
    • "matrix cores" also passes through ggml's own vendor and architecture whitelist (ggml_vk_khr_cooperative_matrix_support), so "none" does not prove the driver lacks VK_KHR_cooperative_matrix.
    • No Adreno 6xx data was found. A gpuinfo.org survey was attempted, but its API refused scripted queries.
  • [INFERENCE] For SipLLM's "old phone" target, the pattern is: older Mali drivers (Vulkan 1.1) hide int8 dot that the silicon has. Newer Mali (driver r44+, Vulkan 1.3) and Qualcomm's proprietary Adreno driver expose it. That strengthens the §7.2 recommendation to check OpenCL's cl_arm_integer_dot_product_int8 on G68-class phones, and to treat Vulkan int-dot as a runtime-detected fast path.

V. SipLLM Source Code Verification & Empirical Benchmark Reality

Codebase Truth: SipLLM is not an academic paper or a theoretical Python notebook. It is an active C++17 codebase running token-for-token checked inference across Apple Silicon and unrooted ARM Android phones.

1. Key Code Locations & Mechanisms

  • Whole-Process Peak-RAM Contract Planner: include/llm/mem_plan.h and src/mem_plan.cpp. Calculates itemized MemLedger (base, scratch, logits, attn, residual, out_norm, embeddings, LM head, full-ctx KV cache, ring buffer, pinned layers). Refuses infeasible budgets upfront with actionable diagnostic report.
  • Streaming Layer Loader & Ring Buffer: src/loader.cpp (lines 186-231). Double-buffered asynchronous prefetch ring recycling layer memory. Contiguous or spread layer pinning.
  • Bounded KV Cache & Issue #81 Resolution: include/llm/kv_cache.h and src/kv_cache.cpp. reserve_full() pre-allocates maximum context at startup when budgeted, eliminating runtime doubling memory spikes. INT8 quantized KV cache option (--kv-q8).
  • Network Layer Streaming (Socket Block Client): src/remote_weight_source.cpp. Streams 1 MB weight blocks over TCP sockets with local compressed file cache for bounded client RSS.
  • Mathematical Token Equivalence: tests/test_ram_budget.cpp. Formally verifies that generated tokens and logits are bit-for-bit identical across budget sweeps from 0 MB (stream all) to full residency.
